===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear moral position and strong emotional engagement. You care deeply about the topic, and that passion gives your writing energy. The Exxon Valdez disaster is also a strong example for an argumentative essay because it is specific, historically important, and relevant to questions of corporate responsibility. To improve the essay, focus on clarity of thesis, organization, formal tone, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main strengths
Strong engagement with the topic: Your anger about the disaster makes the essay persuasive at an emotional level.
Clear general position: You argue that large corporations should be held accountable for environmental harm.
Relevant example: The Exxon Valdez spill is a concrete case that supports your argument.

Main issue: the thesis is too broad
Your opening begins with: “Money has been said to be the root of all evil. I feel this statement is personally true.”  
This is very broad and not fully connected to the rest of the essay. The essay is really about corporate greed, negligence, and environmental accountability, not about “all evil.”

Improve by writing a more precise thesis
For example:
“The Exxon Valdez disaster shows how corporate negligence and greed can cause severe environmental damage, which is why large corporations must be held legally and financially accountable.”

This gives the essay a clear focus and tells the reader exactly what you will argue.

Organization
At the moment, the essay repeats some ideas and moves between personal reaction, historical facts, and opinion without a clear structure.

A stronger structure could be:
Introduction
Introduce the issue
Present your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: What happened
Explain the Exxon Valdez grounding briefly and accurately

Body Paragraph 2: Why Exxon was responsible
Slow response
Poor planning
Weak safety measures / ship maintenance

Body Paragraph 3: Why this matters
Damage to wildlife, Indigenous communities, and the fishing industry
Need for stronger regulation and accountability

Conclusion
Restate your argument
End with a clear call for responsibility and reform

This will help your argument feel more logical and academic.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too personal or emotional for a university argumentative essay. Personal voice is not always wrong, but here it sometimes weakens the academic style.

Examples:
“I personally witnessed first hand” → You likely mean you saw the event on television, not directly. So this is inaccurate.
“While I laid in my living room” → too informal and unnecessary
“rape our land” → very strong and inappropriate in academic writing
“mind boggling” → too conversational

Better alternatives:
“I followed media coverage of the disaster and was shocked by Exxon’s response.”
“The scale of the corporation’s resources is difficult for ordinary citizens to comprehend.”
“If governments allow corporations to severely damage the environment, society will suffer long-term consequences.”

Support your claims with evidence
You make strong claims, but many need more support. For example:
“biggest Public Relations blunder by any firm in the nation”
“The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove…”

These statements should be supported by a source or made more cautiously.

Academic writing tip:
If you do not have evidence, avoid absolute claims. Use phrases like:
“one of the most serious…”
“The case suggests that…”
“Reports indicated that…”

Also, there is a missing quotation here:  
“Each time Exxon was confronted with this question, spokespersons said the same thing. .”  
You need to complete or remove this part.

Sentence-level language corrections
Because your first language is German, some sentence patterns sound slightly unnatural in English. Here are a few examples:

“personally witnessed first hand” → redundant; use “witnessed firsthand” or “saw”
“Prince William sound” → “Prince William Sound” (capital S)
“the Captain in charge the vessel” → “the captain in charge of the vessel”
“After close to two years” → “After nearly two years”
“made monetary amends” → “paid compensation”
“set aside monies” → “set aside funds”
“more monies” → “more funding”

Also watch subject-verb agreement:
“The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove…”  
  Since publishing is singular, it should be “proves”.

Paragraph coherence
Some paragraphs are very long and include too many ideas. Try to keep one main idea per paragraph. Use topic sentences to guide the reader.

For example, this could be a topic sentence:
“Exxon’s response to the spill was inadequate and intensified public anger.”

Then follow with evidence and explanation.

Final revision advice
When you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear, specific thesis
Organize the essay into logical body paragraphs
Replace emotional or informal language with formal academic wording
Add or clarify evidence
Correct grammar and awkward phrasing

Possible revised thesis
“The Exxon Valdez oil spill demonstrates that when corporations prioritize profit over safety and environmental protection, the consequences can be catastrophic; therefore, governments must enforce stricter corporate accountability.”

You have a strong topic and a clear sense of justice. With better structure and a more formal style, this essay can become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral position: you argue that “crime does not pay,” and you support this claim with a concrete example. This is a good starting point for an argumentative essay because you have a clear main idea and a relevant illustration. To improve the essay, focus on thesis, organization, tone, grammar, and word choice.

Strengths
Clear central argument: Your main message is easy to understand.
Use of an example: The story about the businessman gives your essay a human dimension and helps persuade the reader.
Strong concluding idea: The final sentence clearly restates your position.

Main areas to revise

A. Make your thesis more precise
Right now, the introduction discusses crime, money, and impatience, but the thesis is not sharply stated until later. In an argumentative essay, the reader should know your main claim early.

For example, you could end the introduction with something like:
“Although crime may seem like a quick path to wealth, it ultimately destroys trust, families, and the criminal’s future.”

This is stronger because it tells the reader exactly what your argument will be.

B. Improve paragraph organization
Your essay has three paragraphs, but the first paragraph is long and somewhat unfocused. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the topic
   - Explain why some people commit crimes for money
   - State your thesis

Body paragraph
   - Give the example of the businessman
   - Explain what happened and how it affected his family

Analysis paragraph
   - Explain why this example proves your argument
   - Show that crime brings temporary gain but long-term loss

Conclusion
   - Restate the thesis
   - End with a strong final thought

Also, avoid announcing something you do not complete, such as:
“as the title of this essay states .”
This looks unfinished and should be removed or replaced.

C. Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too conversational:
“I know several people…”
“The bottom line is…”
“get rich quick attitude”

In academic writing, try more formal alternatives:
“Some individuals…”
“Ultimately…”
“a desire for quick wealth”

Also, be careful with broad generalizations such as:
“Most of the people that turn to crime…”
This sounds too certain unless you provide evidence. You could write:
“Some people turn to crime because…”

D. Develop the argument, not just the story
The second paragraph tells a story effectively, but the essay needs more explanation of why this example supports your argument. After the example, you should analyze it:
The man gained money temporarily.
However, he lost his freedom, reputation, and family stability.
His victims also suffered financial harm.
Therefore, the apparent short-term reward led to greater long-term consequences.

This analytical step is essential in argumentative writing.

Language corrections

There are several grammar and vocabulary problems. Here are some important ones:

“societies rules” → “society’s rules”
“find it expectable” → probably “find it acceptable”
“see and easier way” → “see an easier way”
“catigory” → “category”
“not being will” → “not being willing”
“Crime does not pay, is a very true statement.” → “‘Crime does not pay’ is a very true statement.”
“His family way very proud” → “His family was very proud”
“The one day” → “Then one day”
“beleive” → “believe”
“real-estate” → usually “real estate”
“old couples money” → “the old couples’ money”
“families money” → “family’s money” or “family’s assets”

Sentence-level advice
Some sentences are too long or awkward. Shorter sentences will make your meaning clearer. For example:

Original:
“Most of the people that turn to crime are either fed up with societies rules for becoming rich and famous.”

Possible revision:
“Some people turn to crime because they are frustrated by society’s rules for achieving wealth and success.”

Original:
“When in reality, he was just scamming the old couples money, never planning on investing it.”

Possible revision:
“In reality, he was stealing the elderly couples’ money and never intended to invest it.”

Suggestions for revision
When you rewrite, try to:
State your thesis clearly in the introduction.
Reduce repetition of “money,” “riches,” and “get rich quick.”
Use more formal vocabulary.
Add analysis after the example.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

Final comment
You have a solid basic argument and a memorable example. The next step is to make the essay more academic: clearer thesis, better structure, more analysis, and more accurate English. With revision, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear and thoughtful argument: a society’s pay structures reveal what it truly values, and child care work is undervalued despite its importance. The topic is strong, and your essay shows intellectual ambition. Below are suggestions to help you revise for clarity, structure, and style.

Main strengths

Interesting central idea: You move beyond the simple slogan “equal work deserves equal pay” and ask a deeper question about how society defines value.
Good use of example: Child care is an effective case study because it connects economic value, gender roles, and social priorities.
Academic tone: Your language is formal and appropriate for argumentative writing.

Thesis and focus

Your main claim is present, but it is somewhat indirect. In the introduction, the sentence with  suggests that part of the original thesis is missing. You need a clear, complete thesis.

For example, you could state it more directly:

> Although Americans claim to believe in equality, wage differences show that society does not value all socially necessary work equally; child care is a clear example of essential labor that is economically and socially undervalued.

This would help readers understand your position immediately.

Organization

The essay has a logical progression, but some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas at once. Try this structure:

Introduction  
   - Present the common belief: equal work should receive equal pay.  
   - Introduce your argument: the real issue is how society assigns value.

Body Paragraph 1: Social values and economic rewards  
   - Explain that pay and status reflect what a society considers valuable.

Body Paragraph 2: Child care as an example  
   - Show the contradiction: Americans say children matter, but child care workers are poorly paid.

Body Paragraph 3: Why child care is essential  
   - Explain its economic importance for families, employers, and the national workforce.

Conclusion  
   - Restate the argument and suggest what must change.

This would make your reasoning easier to follow.

Clarity and sentence structure

Many sentences are sophisticated, but some are too long and become difficult to follow. As a writer whose first language is German, you may naturally prefer long, embedded sentence structures. In English academic writing, shorter and more direct sentences often sound stronger.

For example:

Original:
> If we recognize that a work force filled with such diversity of workers will inevitably include women - of all ages - as well as man with family responsibilities, then we see the undeniable economic connection between this "low value" contribution of child care providers, and the economic strength of the nation.

Possible revision:
> A diverse workforce includes women of all ages as well as men with family responsibilities. For this reason, child care is directly connected to the economic strength of the nation.

This version is clearer and more forceful.

Grammar and word choice

Here are some specific points to revise:

“profess to the belief” → better: “profess the belief” or “claim to believe”
“equality of work” → awkward; better: “the equal value of work” or “whether different kinds of work are valued equally”
“makes a good example” → better: “is a good example” or “provides a good example”
“pereniel” → spelling: “perennial”
“man with family responsibilities” → should be “men with family responsibilities”
“the work force” / “workforce” → choose one form and use it consistently; “workforce” is more common today.
“contribution to the society they live in” → more natural: “contribution to society”

Also watch punctuation:
You sometimes place commas where English would not normally use them.
Dashes can be effective, but too many interrupt the flow.

Development of argument

Your argument would be stronger with more concrete support. Right now, the essay is thoughtful but abstract. Consider adding:

evidence about average wages of child care workers
a comparison to teachers or other care-related professions
a point about how low wages lead to staff shortages or lower-quality care
a brief explanation of why “non-productive in financial terms” does not mean “socially unproductive”

Even one or two specific examples would make your essay more persuasive.

Conclusion

Your final sentence has an interesting idea, but it is slightly hard to follow. The conclusion should state your point simply and confidently.

Possible revision:
> In other words, society will not fully respect child care workers until it begins to reward them accordingly. If Americans truly value children and equality, they must also value the labor of those who care for them.

This gives the essay a strong ending.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

Add a clear thesis in the introduction.
Break long sentences into shorter ones.
Strengthen paragraph structure so each paragraph has one main point.
Correct grammar and word choice issues.
Add at least one or two concrete examples or facts.

Overall, this is a thoughtful and promising essay with a strong central insight. Your ideas are sophisticated; now your task is to make them more precise, direct, and easy for the reader to follow.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear and thoughtful argument: unpaid domestic labor, especially caregiving and homemaking, is socially valuable and should be recognized more fully. You also show strong engagement with a complex social issue, and your writing has ambition and energy. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Clear central concern: You consistently focus on the undervaluation of work done in the home.
Engaged tone: The essay sounds committed and persuasive.
Good use of comparison: You compare paid professions with unpaid domestic labor to show the imbalance in social recognition.
Logical progression: The essay moves from defining the problem to considering possible solutions.

Main argument: make the thesis sharper

Your thesis is interesting, but it becomes slightly unclear at the end. In the introduction, you argue that homemaking should receive “some sort of compensation.” In the conclusion, however, you shift to a broader idea: perhaps society should simply rethink what counts as valuable. These are not exactly the same claim.

Revise by choosing one main argument more clearly:
Practical claim: homemakers should receive financial compensation; or
Cultural claim: society should respect homemaking more, even if direct payment is difficult.

You may still mention both, but one should be primary.

Possible revised thesis:
“Because childcare and homemaking provide essential benefits to society, they should be recognized as work of real social value, whether through direct financial compensation or through policies that treat unpaid caregivers as equal contributors.”

Development: add more concrete support

Some points are asserted strongly, but they need more evidence or explanation.

For example:
“many of the ills of society… have been traced… to the lack of firm guidance and support from the family”
“those at-home tasks contribute services to society equally valuable in comparison to marketplace jobs”

These are persuasive ideas, but they need support. Since this is an argumentative essay, readers will expect:
examples,
evidence,
statistics,
or clearer reasoning.

You do not need to add many sources, but you should explain how domestic labor benefits society:
childcare enables other adults to work,
emotional support and education in the home shape future citizens,
cooking, cleaning, budgeting, and care work would be expensive if outsourced.

Organization: improve paragraph focus

Most paragraphs have a clear topic, but some become too broad.

For instance, the final paragraph introduces several new ideas at once:
payment to mothers,
payment to childless homemakers,
rethinking value,
paying the couple or family instead of the individual.

This is too much for a short conclusion. A conclusion should mainly:
restate the main argument,
summarize the key reasoning,
end with one strong final idea.

You might instead move one proposed solution into a body paragraph and keep the conclusion simpler.

Style and tone: watch for outdated or distracting phrasing

A few expressions weaken your credibility because they sound dated, imprecise, or too informal.

Examples:
“first-world cultures” → better: “wealthier societies” or “industrialized countries”
“Indian chiefs” → this phrase is outdated and distracting
“workworld” → awkward; better: “workplace” or “labor market”
“worth its salt” → too informal for an academic essay

Also, some claims rely too heavily on generalization about gender roles. Since you already acknowledge that both men and women can do domestic labor, continue that inclusive approach throughout.

Grammar and wording corrections

Your writing is generally understandable, but there are several language issues to revise.

Article use
“an neurosurgeon” → “a neurosurgeon”

Word choice
“this contribution to society in so immense” → “this contribution to society is so immense”
“from the cradle to near-dependence” probably means “from the cradle to independence”
“compensated by the dollar” sounds unnatural; better: “financially compensated”
“whether they be male or female” → more natural: “whether they are male or female”

Sentence structure / punctuation
This sentence has a punctuation problem:
“in the home and outside the home, some sort of compensation should be made…”
There should be a dash or period before “some sort of compensation.”

Corrected:
“Therefore, in order for society to fully acknowledge the value of both types of jobs—in the home and outside the home—some sort of compensation should be provided…”

Pronoun style
You frequently use “he or she”. This is grammatically correct, but repeating it many times sounds heavy. In modern academic English, “they” is often a smoother singular pronoun.

A note for you as a German-speaking writer

Some sentences are long and abstract, which is common in German academic style. In English argumentative writing, shorter and more direct sentences are often stronger. Try to:
state the claim early,
follow with explanation,
then give an example.

Revision priorities

Clarify your main thesis.
Add 1–2 concrete examples or pieces of evidence.
Strengthen paragraph focus, especially the conclusion.
Replace outdated or informal expressions.
Correct grammar and awkward phrasing.

Overall, this is a thoughtful essay with a strong social argument. With a clearer thesis and more specific support, it could become much more convincing and polished.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong ambition: you engage critically with a famous saying and try to test its logic rather than simply accept it. That is an excellent basis for an argumentative essay. You also use a formal tone and attempt nuanced distinctions between money, desire, virtue, happiness, and evil. These are real strengths.

To improve the essay, focus on clarity of argument, paragraph structure, and language accuracy.

Main strengths

Independent thinking: You challenge the proverb instead of repeating common opinions.
Sophisticated vocabulary: Words such as *correlation*, *utilitarian*, *virtue*, and *contentedness* show range.
Philosophical engagement: You are trying to define terms, which is important in argumentative writing.

Main problem: the thesis is unclear

I think your main claim is something like this:

the saying “money is the root of all evil” is too simplistic,
because money itself cannot be directly measured against virtue or evil,
and history shows that wealth can be used for both good and bad purposes.

This is a strong idea, but it is not stated clearly enough at the start. Your first paragraph contains many abstract claims, so the reader has to work hard to find your position.

Suggestion
Write a clearer thesis at the end of the introduction, for example:

> The saying “money is the root of all evil” is misleading because money itself is morally neutral; what matters is how human desire and power are directed.

That gives the essay a clear line of argument.

Organization and development

At the moment, some paragraphs feel more like chains of reflection than steps in an argument. Try to make each paragraph do one clear job.

A possible structure:

Introduction  
   Introduce the saying and present your thesis.

Paragraph 1: Why the saying is logically weak  
   Explain why wealth does not automatically correspond to evil, and poverty does not automatically correspond to virtue.

Paragraph 2: The problem of measuring desire and happiness  
   Develop your point that desire cannot be objectively quantified.

Paragraph 3: Historical reality  
   Show that wealthy people have been both benefactors and oppressors.

Conclusion  
   Restate that money is not itself evil; human motives and uses of power matter more.

This will make your ideas easier to follow.

Use of concepts

You introduce several abstract concepts, but their relationships are sometimes confusing.

For example:
You connect happiness with lack of desire for further money.
Then you connect virtue with the counterpart of happiness.
Later, desire for money and desire for virtue seem to overlap.

This makes the logic difficult to follow. Try to define each concept simply and keep the definitions consistent.

For instance:
Money = a tool/resource
Desire for money = ambition, greed, or need
Virtue = moral goodness
Evil = harmful actions
Happiness = contentment, not necessarily wealth

Then explain how they relate.

Evidence and support

Your argument remains mostly abstract. The final sentence refers to history, but no examples are given. Argumentative essays are stronger when they include specific evidence.

Suggestion
Add one or two concrete examples:
wealthy philanthropists who used money for good
rulers or elites who used wealth and power oppressively

Even brief examples would make your conclusion more convincing.

Language and style

Your style is ambitious, but sometimes the sentences become too dense. Shorter, clearer sentences would improve readability.

Example
Original:
> It would seem to follow that if indeed money inspired and cultivated evil there would be a negative correlation between money earned and virtue obtained.

Clearer:
> If money truly caused evil, then we would expect wealth and virtue to be negatively correlated.

This keeps the meaning but is easier to read.

Grammar, spelling, and word choice

There are several language errors, many of them minor but noticeable. Examples:

If money was -> If money were  
  (“were” is better in a hypothetical statement)
privelaged -> privileged
acruement -> possibly accrual or accumulation
wheighed -> weighed
a least -> at least
serrendipitously -> serendipitously
where in which -> choose one: where or in which

Also be careful with punctuation:
You often use hyphens where commas or dashes would work better.
Example: meaningless-or a least impractical -  
  Better: meaningless—or at least impractical—

Final paragraph

The sentence “. A truer statement, I think...” looks incomplete, as if something is missing. This weakens the ending. Your conclusion should be polished and complete, since it leaves the final impression.

You might end more directly:

> A more accurate statement would be that the pursuit of power, not money alone, can lead to evil. History shows that wealth can produce both oppression and generosity, depending on the character of those who possess it.

Overall

This is an intellectually promising draft with a thoughtful central idea. For revision, prioritize:

state your thesis more clearly
organize each paragraph around one main point
simplify and clarify sentences
define key concepts consistently
add concrete examples
correct spelling/grammar issues

Your ideas are interesting; now the goal is to present them in a way that is clearer and more persuasive to the reader.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong engagement with a complex philosophical topic. You try to connect Marx’s principle to family life, religion, individual freedom, and modern relativism, which gives the essay intellectual ambition. You also use rhetorical questions effectively to challenge the idea you are discussing. For revision, the main areas to improve are clarity of thesis, organization, precision of argument, and language accuracy.

Main strength
A clear strength is that you do not simply describe Marx’s idea; you evaluate it. This is important in an argumentative essay. You also attempt to consider both the attraction of the principle (“an admirable goal”) and the practical difficulties of applying it in Western society.

Thesis and focus
At the moment, your central argument is not fully clear. The essay seems to discuss Marx’s idea “from each according to his ability, to each according to his needs” and argues that it is attractive but unrealistic in modern Western culture. However, this should be stated directly in the introduction.

What to revise
Add a clear thesis at the end of the first paragraph, for example:
“Although Marx’s principle reflects values already present in family life and religion, it cannot be fully applied in modern Western society because it conflicts with individual freedom, subjective ideas of contribution, and moral relativism.”

This will help the reader understand the direction of the essay.

Organization
Your essay has relevant ideas, but the progression is sometimes difficult to follow. A clearer structure would strengthen the argument.

Possible structure
Introduction: Introduce Marx’s principle and present your thesis.
Paragraph 1: Explain why the principle seems natural in the family.
Paragraph 2: Explain how religion/social institutions support similar values.
Paragraph 3: Present the first problem: how to define contribution and reward.
Paragraph 4: Present the second problem: conflict with individual freedom.
Paragraph 5: Present the third problem: moral relativism.
Conclusion: Restate that the principle is admirable but impractical in Western culture.

Try to make each paragraph begin with a topic sentence that states its main point.

Development of argument
Some claims are interesting but need more support or explanation.

Examples
“In Western society, for example, the primary guide is Christianity.”  
  This is too broad. Western societies are religiously diverse and increasingly secular. You could say:  
  “Historically, Christianity has strongly influenced moral values in many Western societies.”

“These avenues are the only paths to tempering rampant moral relativism.”  
  This is a very strong claim. Why only these avenues? Could education, law, or civic institutions also shape values?

Your rhetorical questions are effective, but too many can weaken the argument if they replace explanation. After each question, provide your own answer more directly.

Use of evidence and references
The placeholders () make the essay feel incomplete. If you quote Marx or the New Testament, include the exact quotation and, if required, cite the source. Without this, the essay seems unfinished.

Also, some key terms need definition:
egalitarianism
individualism
moral relativism
post-modern world

Defining these briefly will make your argument more precise.

Style and academic tone
You have a thoughtful style, but some phrases are too informal or vague for academic writing.

Improve these points
Avoid using &; write and.
Avoid broad generalizations such as “relativism reigns supreme.”
Be careful with emotionally loaded phrases like “rampant moral relativism.” A more neutral academic tone is better.

Language corrections
There are several grammar, spelling, and word-choice issues. Here are important examples:

“on e” → “one”
“who it to define” → “who is to define”
“learned primarily form the family” → “learned primarily from the family”
“The religious would rest knowing” → better: “Religious people may take comfort in the belief”
“The nonreligious can comfort themselves with secular, intrinsic, values.”  
  Remove the extra comma: “secular intrinsic values” or better “secular personal values.”
“one whose ends will unlikely be achieved” → “one whose aims are unlikely to be achieved”

Article/preposition use
As a German speaker, watch for article usage and natural English phrasing:
“apply Marx’s egalitarian goal” sounds unnatural. Better:  
  “reflect Marx’s egalitarian ideal”
“social institutions & practices in order which preserve” is awkward. Better:  
  “social institutions and practices that help preserve”

Final advice
For your revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis.
Improve paragraph structure with one main idea per paragraph.
Replace rhetorical questions with more direct argument and explanation.
Add or complete the missing quotations.
Correct grammar and make the style more formal.

Overall, this essay has a strong conceptual basis, but it needs clearer structure and more precise language to become convincing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear focus and a strong central idea: whether crime “pays” depends on how we define “pay.” This is a good argumentative strategy because you begin by defining the key term before developing your position. The essay is easy to follow overall, and your examples help make the argument concrete.

What works well

Clear structure
Your essay is organized logically:
introduction with definition,
discussion of financial costs,
discussion of health and legal costs,
conclusion.

This structure supports your argument well.

Strong controlling idea
You consistently argue that crime should be evaluated not only by immediate financial gain but also by costs such as time, risk, injury, and legal consequences. This gives the essay coherence.

Effective use of examples
The jewelry store example is vivid and easy to understand. It helps the reader see immediately why simple profit is not enough to decide whether crime “pays.”

Main revision priorities

Make the thesis more direct
Your main claim is present, but it could be stated more clearly at the end of the introduction. Right now, the reader must infer your exact position.

For example, you could write something like:
“Crime rarely pays when all financial, physical, mental, and legal costs are taken into account.”

This would make your argument sharper and more academic.

Improve word choice and tone
Some phrases are understandable but not natural in academic English.

Examples:
“all manners of loss” → better: “all kinds of losses”
“enacting of a crime” → better: “committing a crime”
“small businessman criminal” → this is creative, but slightly awkward; better: “small-scale criminal”
“function in one of the professions” → better: “work in a profession”
“health related loss” → better: “health-related loss”

Also, the final sentence is witty, but it weakens the formal tone:
“However, he does better the legitimate businessman on one count: no taxes.”
This should be corrected grammatically and made more formal:
“However, criminals may appear to have one financial advantage over legitimate businesspeople: they do not pay taxes.”

Watch grammar and article use
As a German speaker, you may find English articles and idiomatic phrasing especially difficult. A few examples:

“equiptment” → “equipment”
“get-away cars” → usually “getaway cars”
“monies earned” → “money earned” is more natural here
“loss of mental or physical health and well being” → “loss of mental or physical health and well-being”
“comes about from” → better: “results from”
“lessen both a person's life span” → “shorten a person’s life span” is more natural
“does better the legitimate businessman” → “does better than the legitimate businessman”

Strengthen paragraph development
Some paragraphs introduce good points but could explain them more fully. For example, in the paragraph about time investment, you mention opportunity cost—an excellent idea—but you could connect it more explicitly to your thesis. Explain why legal alternatives matter when judging whether crime pays.

Similarly, the paragraph on legal problems could be expanded with one clearer point: even if a criminal earns money, imprisonment may eliminate the benefit entirely by taking away freedom, future income, and social standing.

Style suggestions

Use more precise transitions
You already use transitions such as “However” and “Another type of loss.” To sound more academic, you could vary them:
“In addition”
“Furthermore”
“More importantly”
“By contrast”
“As a result”

Be careful with repeated business metaphor
Comparing crime to a small business is interesting and gives unity to the essay. However, if repeated too often, it can sound too informal or even humorous. Keep the metaphor, but balance it with more direct analysis.

Possible revised thesis and conclusion

A stronger thesis:
“Although crime may sometimes bring immediate financial gain, it rarely pays when one considers the full costs of time, equipment, injury, stress, and legal punishment.”

A stronger concluding idea:
“Therefore, crime cannot be judged profitable merely by the money it produces, because its broader consequences often outweigh any short-term gain.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on:
making your thesis more explicit,
improving word choice and academic tone,
correcting grammar and spelling errors,
developing a few points in more depth.

Overall, this is a thoughtful and well-organized essay with a strong underlying argument. With clearer phrasing and a more formal style, it could become a very effective academic piece.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0008.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: money itself is not evil, but the love of money can lead to harmful behavior. This is a strong and arguable thesis, and your conclusion returns clearly to that central idea. You also use several effective examples (families, companies, crime, social harm) to support your point. Overall, the essay shows good engagement with the topic and a confident argumentative tone.

Main strengths

Clear stance: You consistently argue against the idea that money itself is evil.
Logical contrast: The distinction between *money* and the *love of money* is the key strength of the essay.
Range of examples: You move from individuals to families, companies, and even nations, which gives the argument breadth.
Strong concluding message: The final paragraph clearly restates the argument.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the structure more academic
At the moment, the essay reads somewhat like a series of reflections rather than a formally organized argument. Try to shape it into:
Introduction with thesis
Body paragraph 1: why money is necessary
Body paragraph 2: why the love of money is dangerous
Body paragraph 3: consequences/examples
Conclusion

Your first sentence, “This idea is completely erroneous,” is forceful, but the reader does not yet know exactly what “this idea” refers to. In an academic essay, it is better to name the issue directly.

Possible revision:
> The claim that money is the root of all evil is mistaken. Money is a necessary tool in everyday life; rather, it is the excessive love of money that can lead people to act immorally.

B. Use a more formal style
Some phrases are too conversational or emotionally strong for academic writing.

Examples:
“How many times have we seen on the evening news...”
“love affair with money”
“the ‘right’ car”

These are vivid, but a more formal tone would make your argument stronger.

For example:
“Media reports frequently show...”
“obsession with wealth”
“status symbols such as expensive cars or exclusive neighborhoods”

C. Support claims more precisely
Some points are broad and absolute, especially:
“the root of all evil”
“It is a cross-cultural thing”
“It produces selfishness in even the smallest child”

Try to avoid overgeneralization. Academic arguments are usually more careful.

Stronger phrasing:
“can be a major cause of harmful behavior”
“this pattern can be observed in many cultures”
“it may encourage selfish behavior even in children”

Language corrections

There are a number of grammar, punctuation, spelling, and word-choice issues. Here are the most important ones:

Grammar and wording
“all together” → “altogether”
“capitivates” → “captivates”
“sued for good” → “used for good”
“it’s needs met” → “its needs met”
“However, the love it” → “However, the love of it”
“he begins” → capitalize: “He begins”

Articles and phrasing
“in order to carry out business trading, commerce & exchange of property”
  - Better: “in order to conduct trade, commerce, and the exchange of property”
“share with others or even to give to those in need”
  - Better parallel form: “share with others or give to those in need”

Sentence mechanics
You often use commas where a full stop or no punctuation would be better.

Example:
“Money perhaps, is a necessary evil...”
  - Better: “Money, perhaps, is a necessary evil...”  
  - Or more naturally: “Perhaps money is a necessary evil...”

Example:
“A healthy thing can be turned into a tool for disaster. is money the root of all evil?”
  - Correct capitalization: “A healthy thing can be turned into a tool for disaster. Is money the root of all evil?”

Ampersand
“commerce & exchange of property”
  - In formal writing, use “and” instead of “&”.

Style note for a German-speaking writer

A few sentences are long and abstract, which is common for German speakers writing in English. English academic writing often sounds stronger when sentences are:
slightly shorter,
more direct,
less repetitive.

For instance, instead of repeating “the love of money” many times, you can vary the vocabulary:
greed
materialism
obsession with wealth
the pursuit of money at any cost

Suggested revision strategy

When revising, focus on these steps:

Rewrite the introduction so the thesis is clear immediately.
Divide the essay into clear paragraphs, each with one main idea.
Replace informal phrases with more academic vocabulary.
Correct grammar and spelling errors carefully.
Qualify broad claims to sound more convincing and scholarly.

Final comment

This essay has a solid core argument and a clear moral perspective. Your main task now is to present that argument in a more structured, formal, and precise way. If you improve paragraph organization and language accuracy, the essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative focus and a strong central claim: the saying “crime does not pay” is misleading because crime can sometimes bring benefits to criminals or to others who profit from the criminal justice system. This is a thoughtful and interesting position. You also organize the essay logically, moving from the common belief to counterexamples and then to a broader social argument. That structure works well.

What is working well

Clear thesis and line of argument  
The final sentence gives a strong conclusion, and the essay consistently supports it. Readers can follow your main point from beginning to end.

Effective use of examples  
You use different kinds of evidence: violent crime, white-collar crime, and the prison industry. This variety strengthens your argument because it shows the issue from several angles.

Formal academic tone  
Your language is mostly formal and appropriate for argumentative writing. Phrases such as “Given the evidence” and “it could also be argued” fit the genre well.

Main areas to improve

Clarify the opening
The placeholder “The phrase ” is confusing. You should state the phrase directly: “crime does not pay.” Since the whole essay depends on this phrase, the reader needs it immediately.

Also, your first sentence is long and slightly awkward. Try to make the opening more direct.

Possible revision:  
“The phrase ‘crime does not pay’ is often used to justify living by the rules, especially when a criminal is caught and punished.”

Strengthen accuracy and precision
Some examples are interesting, but a few details weaken your credibility.

“Michael Milliken” should be Michael Milken.  
“stock broker scams” is too informal and vague. Better: “securities fraud” or “financial crimes.”
Be careful with claims such as “Richard Nixon, certainly a criminal”. This is rhetorically strong, but in academic writing it is better to be more precise: for example, “Nixon engaged in serious abuses of power and violations of public trust.”

In argumentative essays, accuracy matters a lot. If a reader notices one factual error, they may distrust the whole argument.

Develop the logic more carefully
Your key question—“If crime does not pay, then why does it persist?”—is strong. However, the statement “Behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist” is too absolute. Some behaviors continue for many reasons, not only because they are rewarded. Since this sentence is central to your reasoning, qualify it.

Possible revision:  
“Behavior usually persists when it is rewarded in some way, so the continued existence of crime suggests that some offenders perceive a benefit.”

This sounds more careful and academic.

Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. For example, the paragraph on incarceration, garbage storage, CHINS, state budgets, and job creation is interesting, but dense. Consider breaking it into two paragraphs:

one on prisons as business,
one on juvenile detention and local economic incentives.

This would make your reasoning easier to follow.

Edit grammar and word choice
Your writing is generally strong, but there are some word-choice and sentence-level issues.

Specific points:
“a working class man” → “a working-class man”  
  (“working-class” needs a hyphen as an adjective)

“Parents of would-be miscreants”  
  This is grammatically correct, but “miscreants” sounds overly dramatic. Consider “young offenders” or “children tempted to break the law.”

“cost effective” → “cost-effective”

“stock broker scams” → “stockbroker fraud” or “financial fraud”

“after, not only saves money”  
  This should be “after all, it not only saves money” or simply “because it not only saves money”

“pay off” in the final paragraph  
  Better: “pays” or “can be profitable” for a more formal tone.

Watch for German-influenced style
As a German speaker, you may naturally write long, layered sentences with abstract nouns. English academic prose often works better with slightly shorter, more direct sentences. For example:

Original:  
“The phrase plays an important role in reassuring people who play by the rules or admonishing potential criminals.”

More natural:  
“The phrase reassures law-abiding people and warns potential criminals.”

This revision is shorter and stronger.

Suggestions for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:

Replace the placeholder with the actual phrase.
Correct factual and spelling errors.
Make your claims more precise and less absolute.
Break up long or crowded paragraphs.
Edit for concision and natural English phrasing.

Final comment
This is a promising essay with a clear argument and strong critical thinking. Your main idea is compelling, and you already show good control of formal argumentative writing. The next step is to make the essay more precise, more concise, and more academically cautious. If you revise with those goals in mind, your essay will become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a logical overall structure: you argue that people should be financially rewarded for individual effort, and you support this by comparing past and present societies and by imagining the consequences of equality of outcome. The essay is easy to follow, and your voice is confident. Below are the main points to improve in revision.

Main strength: clear argument and progression
The essay has a recognizable argumentative shape:
introduction with your opinion,
explanation of effort and reward in the past,
connection to modern society,
counterfactual example of an equal society,
conclusion.

This progression works well. Keep it.

Clarify the thesis
Your thesis is understandable, but the wording is slightly awkward:

> “it is vital that in today's society men and women should be financially reward for their individual efforts.”

Use:
“should be financially rewarded” instead of “financially reward”

You could also make the thesis more precise. At the moment, “success,” “hard work,” and “reward” are used broadly. Try to define what kind of reward you mean: salary, promotion, bonuses, or general social recognition.

Possible revision:
> In today’s society, people should be financially rewarded according to their individual effort, because such rewards encourage productivity, innovation, and personal responsibility.

This gives the reader a clearer roadmap.

Develop the argument with stronger evidence
Your argument relies mostly on general statements and predictions. That is common in early drafts, but to make the essay more persuasive, add concrete support.

For example:
Give an example of how innovation depends on incentives.
Mention professions where skill and effort clearly lead to higher value.
Explain why equal rewards might reduce motivation, instead of only predicting social collapse.

At present, some claims are too sweeping:
> “the world would become divided or even destroyed”

This sounds exaggerated. A reader may stop trusting the argument. Use more moderate language and explain the causal steps more carefully.

Better approach:
> If everyone received the same rewards regardless of effort, some people might feel less motivated to work hard or develop their skills. Over time, this could reduce productivity and innovation.

That is still argumentative, but more credible.

Improve logic and avoid overgeneralization
Sometimes your essay presents ideas as absolute truths:
“Life is about rewards.”
“Each of us is measured daily by some scale of achievement.”
“the harder you work, the greater the reward.”

These statements are rhetorically strong, but not always true. In reality, people are also affected by education, social class, health, discrimination, and luck. A strong argumentative essay becomes stronger when it acknowledges complexity.

You do not need to abandon your position. Instead, refine it:
> Although effort does not always lead to equal outcomes, financial reward should generally reflect contribution and hard work.

This sounds more balanced and mature.

Language issues to revise
Your writing is generally understandable, but there are several grammar and word-choice errors. Here are important examples:

Grammar/form
“financially reward” → “financially rewarded”
“took up of majority” → “took up the majority”
“held us meet” → “help us meet”
“treated equal” → “treated equally” or “treated the same”
“as advanced of a society” → “as advanced a society”
“selfish and hoarding” → “selfish and hoarding resources” or “selfish and possessive”
“stagnate people” → “stagnant people”

Word choice
“jealously” → “jealousy”
“persons with great technical minds” sounds unnatural. Try:
  - “people with technical expertise”
  - “highly skilled workers”

Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. For example:
> “We have many machines to held us meet our food, clothing and shelter needs with little or no effort on our part, it would appear.”

This is a run-on sentence.

Possible revision:
> We now have many machines that help us meet our needs for food, clothing, and shelter with far less physical effort than in the past.

Style: make the tone more academic
Some phrases are too conversational for an academic essay:
“Let’s imagine…”
“Worst case scenario”
“I would venture to guess”
“but is it really about money?”

These are understandable, but a more formal tone would improve the essay.

For example:
“Let’s imagine…” → “If we imagine…”
“Worst case scenario” → “In the most extreme case”
“I would venture to guess” → “It is likely that”

Paragraph-level revision advice
When revising, try this structure for each body paragraph:
topic sentence,
explanation,
example,
link back to thesis.

Your current paragraphs sometimes move quickly from one idea to another. Stronger paragraph unity will make the essay more persuasive.

Final suggestion
Your essay has a solid foundation: clear opinion, coherent structure, and an engaging attempt to connect history, technology, and motivation. For the next draft, focus on:
correcting grammar and word forms,
making the thesis more precise,
replacing exaggeration with carefully reasoned claims,
adding specific examples.

With these changes, your argument will sound much stronger and more academic.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting point: it questions whether “equal pay for equal contribution to society” is even possible. You have a clear opinion, and you use examples to challenge idealistic views of fairness. That is a strong starting point.

Main strength: a clear critical position
Your central idea seems to be:

Because “contribution to society” is subjective, society cannot realistically pay people in exact proportion to their social value.

This is a good argumentative claim. You also try to support it with examples: investors, inherited wealth, migrant workers, and doctors. These examples show that you are thinking broadly about the issue.

Main problem: the argument needs a clearer structure
At the moment, the essay moves between several related ideas:
salary and qualifications
wealthy investors
inherited money
democracy and equal opportunity
doctors’ salaries

These points are connected, but the links are not always explained. As a result, the reader may not always see how each paragraph supports your main thesis.

How to improve structure
Try this pattern:

Introduction
Introduce the topic.
State your thesis clearly.

Body paragraph 1
Explain why “contribution to society” is difficult to define.
Give examples of jobs people value differently.

Body paragraph 2
Discuss wealth without work (inheritance, investments).
Explain why this complicates the idea of fair compensation.

Body paragraph 3
Discuss inequality of opportunity.
Show why salary often reflects power, education, or luck, not social contribution.

Conclusion
Restate your thesis.
End with a strong final judgment.

Thesis: make it more direct
Your first paragraph is thoughtful, but the exact thesis appears only at the end: “Contribution to society is subjective and therefore impossible to compensate equally.”

This is your strongest sentence. Put it even more clearly and earlier.

For example:
“Although many people believe that income should reflect social contribution, this is impossible in practice because contribution is subjective and economic rewards are shaped by opportunity, power, and luck.”

Development of ideas
Some paragraphs need more explanation.

Example: the paragraph about the 1920s
This paragraph is interesting, but it feels a bit detached from the main argument. Explain more clearly:
Why does living off investments challenge the idea of fair pay?
Does receiving money without working prove that income is not linked to contribution?

Make the connection explicit.

Example: the doctors paragraph
You say that “everyone seems to think doctors make much too money,” but this is too general. Some readers may disagree. Avoid broad statements unless you can support them.

Instead, write something like:
“Certain professions, such as medicine, are often seen as overpaid by the public, while practitioners themselves view their fees as justified by training and responsibility.”

That sounds more balanced and academic.

Tone and style
Your style is ambitious and often sophisticated, which is excellent. However, sometimes the wording becomes too absolute or informal.

Avoid overly broad claims
“everyone seems to think”
“people… live as parasites”

These phrases are strong, but they can sound exaggerated. In academic argument, a more measured tone is usually better.

For example:
“many people argue”
“some critics viewed them as contributing little to society”

Language corrections
There are several grammar and word-choice errors. Here are important ones:

“In the 1920s in American” → “In the 1920s in America”
“live off the interest of investors” → probably “live off the interest from investments”
“What about people who work very hard yet they seem to be paid miserably.”  
  → “What about people who work very hard, yet seem to be paid very poorly?”
“A migrant work” → “A migrant worker”
“The answer this question” → “The answer to this question”
“Democracry” → “democracy”
“responsibly” → “responsibility”
“much too money” → “too much money”
“compensate ‘equally’” → quotation marks should match: “equally”

German-influenced issues to watch for
As a German speaker, you may naturally build long, abstract sentences. In English academic writing, clarity is often better than complexity. Try:
shorter sentences
direct topic sentences
fewer rhetorical questions

For instance, instead of:
“But do they have the right to live this way?”
you could write:
“This raises the moral question of whether wealth without labor is justified.”

That sounds more formal and precise.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:

State your thesis clearly in the introduction.
Make each paragraph support that thesis directly.
Explain your examples more fully.
Use a more balanced academic tone.
Correct grammar and word-choice errors.

You already have a thoughtful argument and strong vocabulary. The next step is to make your reasoning more organized and precise. With revision, this could become a persuasive and mature essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative position: you challenge the common saying “crime does not pay” and argue that, in the United States, crime can bring profit, fame, and even long-term advantages. This is a strong and interesting thesis because it goes against a familiar moral statement. The essay also has a recognizable structure: introduction, three body paragraphs, and conclusion. That organization gives your argument a solid base.

Strengths

Clear position
   From the introduction onward, the reader understands your claim.

Logical paragraphing
   Each body paragraph focuses on a different type of evidence:
   - historical examples
   - direct financial gain
   - benefits connected to the justice system

Confident tone
   The essay sounds persuasive and engaged, which works well for argumentative writing.

Relevant examples
   You use historical and popular-cultural references to support your point. This gives the essay energy and specificity.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is understandable, but “crime can be a profitable profession” is quite broad. Try to specify in what ways crime “pays”: financially, socially, and through media attention.

For example:
> In the United States, crime can be profitable because it may bring financial gain, public attention, and even long-term advantages through weaknesses in the justice system.

This version prepares the reader more clearly for your body paragraphs.

Strengthen logic and evidence
Some examples are interesting but not fully convincing or are historically questionable.

Boston Tea Party: this is a risky example because many readers will see it as political protest rather than ordinary crime.
Benedict Arnold: the sentence is unclear. His actions were not “criminal only because of the date”; you need to explain what you mean.
O.J. Simpson: this example needs careful handling, since fame before and after the trial is more complicated than you suggest.

Try to choose examples that directly support your claim. Also, explain each example more fully. Right now, some examples are listed rather than analyzed.

Avoid overgeneralization
The essay sometimes makes very broad claims:
“No one can argue…”
“In America we have perfected a judicial system which can produce more prosperous criminals.”
“statistics rise continuously”

These sound forceful, but they weaken academic credibility if they are not supported. A stronger style is more measured:

Instead of:
> No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money...

Write:
> Crime can clearly be a means of making money, or people would have less incentive to take such risks.

Improve sentence structure and grammar
There are several grammar and usage issues. As a German-speaking writer, you may especially want to watch word order, article use, plural/singular forms, and awkward phrasing.

Here are some examples:

“there are many persons” → “there are many people”
“to their advantage” → “for their own advantage” or “for personal gain”
“Throughout history crime is” → “Throughout history, crime has been”
“the vents shaping” → “the events shaping”
“speak-eases” → “speakeasies”
“Los Vegas” → “Las Vegas”
“The most obvious reward in committing a crime is the monetary rewards”
  → avoid repetition: “The most obvious reward of crime is financial gain.”
“if it were not why would people commit them”
  → “if it were not, why would people commit crimes?”
“convicted killers autobiographies”
  → “convicted killers’ autobiographies”
“Finally, is the issue”
  → “Finally, there is the issue”
“should they become arrested again”
  → “if they are arrested again”
“ever present laws”
  → “ever-present laws”

Watch punctuation
You often need commas after introductory phrases and between clauses. For example:

“In fact one needs only…” → “In fact, one needs only…”
“Throughout history crime…” → “Throughout history, crime…”
“Of course one cannot forget…” → “Of course, one cannot forget…”

Also, avoid run-on sentences. Some of your long sentences should be divided into two shorter ones.

Revise tone for academic writing
Some phrases are vivid, but they sound informal or sarcastic:
“National Enquirer brand of fame”
“if one has the right agent”
“free room and board”

These expressions are memorable, but they make the essay sound more journalistic than academic. Decide whether your assignment allows that style. If not, use a more neutral tone.

Suggested revision priorities

Revise the thesis
Replace or explain weak examples
Reduce overgeneralizations
Correct grammar, punctuation, and spelling
Make the tone slightly more formal

Final comment

This essay has a strong central idea and good basic organization. Your main challenge is not generating arguments, but refining them so they sound more precise, credible, and academically controlled. Focus especially on clarity, evidence, and sentence-level correctness. With revision, this could become a persuasive and well-structured argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and you try to support it with examples, which is a good basis for an argumentative essay. You also address a complex topic and show that you want to distinguish between different views of women’s roles. To revise successfully, focus on three main areas: argument quality, organization, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: The reader can understand your general stance.
Relevant topic sentences: Most paragraphs introduce a specific issue, such as feminism, military service, workplace fairness, and traditional roles.
Willingness to argue: You do not only describe the issue; you try to persuade the reader.

Biggest problems in the argument
The main weakness is that many claims are stated as facts without evidence. In academic argumentation, you need reasons, examples, and ideally sources.

Examples:
“Women just can't fight in a war very well.”
“Divorce is at fifty percent and rising.”
“Women are having more heart attacks... and kids are being more neglected.”

These are very broad claims. Ask yourself:
How do I know this?
Is this always true?
Can I prove it with evidence?

Also, some statements sound too absolute or biased, which weakens your credibility. For example, “These people were crazy” is too emotional for an academic essay. Instead, use a more formal style:
“I disagree with this position because…”
“This argument overlooks…”
“A possible concern is…”

Need for a clearer thesis
Your introduction begins generally, but your main argument is not sharply defined. At the moment, your essay seems to argue that:
women have gained more freedom,
feminism helped,
feminism also pressures some women,
men and women are naturally different.

These ideas could work together, but you need one clear thesis. For example:

Possible thesis:  
“Although feminism has improved women’s opportunities, modern debates about equality should also respect individual choice and recognize that not all women want the same social roles.”

This is clearer, more balanced, and easier to defend.

Organization
Your essay would be stronger if each paragraph had:
a clear topic sentence,
explanation,
one example,
a link back to your thesis.

Right now, some paragraphs jump quickly from one idea to another. For example, the paragraph about military service includes feminism, peer pressure, combat ability, rape risk, and legal rules. Try to focus each paragraph on one main point.

Suggested structure
Introduction: background + thesis
Paragraph 1: historical limits on women + positive changes
Paragraph 2: feminism’s benefits
Paragraph 3: your criticism: some women may feel pressured by one model of success
Paragraph 4: conclusion: equality should include freedom of choice

This would make the essay more coherent.

Language and grammar
There are frequent grammar and word-choice problems. As a German speaker, pay special attention to apostrophes, articles, prepositions, and word forms.

Common corrections from your essay
societies role → society’s role
work place → workplace
verses → versus
the woman → women / a woman
their piers → their peers
The are not strong enough → They are not strong enough
filled by the woman → filled by women
being more stressful → experiencing more stress
should be created equal → should be treated as equal
nature intended they to do → nature intended them to do

Style improvements
Avoid repeating the same words too often, especially “women,” “feminists,” and “a lot.” Use alternatives such as:
many
some
supporters of feminism
advocates for women’s rights

Also, avoid conversational phrases:
Gone is the age... → The expectation that women must stay at home has declined.
These people were crazy. → I strongly disagree with this view.

Tone and academic style
Your essay would benefit from a more neutral tone. Academic writing should sound thoughtful rather than judgmental. For example, instead of saying women “can only do what nature intended,” you could write:
“Biological differences may influence the kinds of work some individuals prefer or are more suited to, but social expectations also play an important role.”

This sounds more precise and less dogmatic.

Content issues to reconsider
Some claims may be challenged easily by a reader because they rely on stereotypes. For example, saying women cannot fight effectively in war because they are weaker ignores that military roles vary and that training matters. If you want to keep this paragraph, you need a much more careful argument and evidence.

Also, be careful not to confuse:
equal rights with identical abilities
feminism with forcing all women to live the same way

A stronger essay would acknowledge complexity:
Feminism can expand choices.
Some social expectations still pressure women.
Equality does not have to mean every person makes the same life choices.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on:
writing a clear thesis,
removing emotional or extreme wording,
supporting claims with evidence,
improving paragraph unity,
correcting grammar and word choice.

You have the basis of an argument, but it needs to become more precise, balanced, and academically supported. If you revise in that direction, your essay will be much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you strongly believe that “crime does not pay,” and you try to persuade the reader by showing how small crimes can escalate into more serious consequences. This is a good basis for an argumentative essay because your opinion is clear from the beginning. You also use a concrete example to support your claim, which helps make your argument vivid and memorable.

What is working well

Clear stance  
From the first sentence, the reader knows your position. This is important in argumentative writing.

Strong voice  
Your writing sounds engaged and personal. Expressions such as “I am a true believer” and “I told this story to make a point” show conviction.

Use of example  
The long robbery scenario is effective because it illustrates how a crime can become more serious and lead to greater punishment and harm to innocent people.

Main areas to improve

Create a stronger essay structure
Right now, the essay reads more like spoken language than formal academic writing. Try to organize it into:

Introduction: state your argument clearly
Body paragraph 1: explain how crime can escalate
Body paragraph 2: explain legal and moral consequences
Body paragraph 3: give your robbery example
Conclusion: restate why crime does not pay

Your essay currently ends suddenly. Add a short conclusion that summarizes the lesson: criminals may gain something temporarily, but the long-term consequences are worse.

Use a more formal academic style
Many phrases are too conversational for an academic essay. Examples:

“Well simply because”
“a cool million”
“run like hell”
“guess what happens”
“Sounds like trouble to me”

These expressions make the essay sound informal. Replace them with more neutral language. For example:

“simply because” → “because”
“a cool million” → “one million dollars”
“run like hell” → “run away quickly”
“Sounds like trouble to me” → “This shows how quickly a minor crime can develop into a serious offense.”

Avoid very long sentences
Many of your sentences are extremely long and contain several ideas at once. This makes them difficult to follow and increases grammar mistakes. For example, the first paragraph has one sentence that runs for many lines.

Try to express one main idea per sentence. This will make your argument much clearer.

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction gives your opinion, but the thesis can be more precise. Instead of beginning with a proverb, try something like:

“Crime does not pay because it often leads to escalating behavior, severe legal punishment, and harm to innocent people.”

This gives the reader a clear roadmap for the essay.

Language and grammar points

Because your first language is German, a few patterns may be especially useful to watch:

Word choice and collocations
Some phrases are understandable but not natural in English:

“committs” → commits
“headed for destruction” → heading for destruction
“convenient store” → convenience store
“strats” → starts
“persistant” → persistent
“deterent” → deterrent
“simply robbery” → simple robbery

Also:
“A person that commits a crime” is possible, but “A person who commits a crime” sounds more natural.

Apostrophes and possessives
There are some punctuation problems:

“Its's” → It’s or Its depending on meaning  
  Here you need It’s (= it is).
“someone's life” is correct.

Subject-verb agreement and verb form
“someone could definitely headed for destruction” → someone could definitely be headed for destruction
“one of those shots just happen” → one of those shots just happened or happens

Article use
English articles can be difficult for German speakers. Examples:
“committing crime” → committing a crime or committing crimes
“just a simply robbery” → just a simple robbery

Content advice

Your robbery example is vivid, but it is too long compared with the rest of the essay. In an argumentative essay, examples should support the argument, not replace it. Try shortening the story and then explain more clearly how it proves your point.

Also, you focus mainly on punishment and danger. You could make your argument stronger by briefly mentioning other consequences, such as:

guilt or loss of conscience
damage to families and communities
loss of freedom and future opportunities

Suggested revision strategy

Write a new thesis statement.
Divide the essay into 4–5 paragraphs.
Shorten the robbery example by about half.
Replace informal expressions with formal academic language.
Check spelling carefully.
Add a concluding paragraph.

Final comment

You have a strong central idea and clear passion for your topic. The biggest step now is to turn this energy into a more organized, formal, and concise argumentative essay. If you improve structure, sentence control, and academic tone, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: money itself is neutral, but people’s attitudes toward it can lead to harmful behavior. This is a strong and arguable position, and your paragraphs generally move in a logical direction. You also show good awareness that the topic is more complex than the saying “money is the root of all evil” suggests.

To revise effectively, focus on 1) sharpening your thesis, 2) improving paragraph development, and 3) correcting sentence-level grammar and punctuation.

Argument and thesis

At the moment, your essay begins with the famous saying, but your actual argument becomes clearer only in the second paragraph. Try to state your position earlier and more directly.

For example, your thesis could be:

Although money gives people power and status, it is not evil in itself; rather, greed and unhealthy desires make money a force for evil.

This is clearer than repeating the proverb as if it were fully true.

Also, the conclusion currently ends very suddenly. You should restate your argument more fully and give the essay a stronger final insight. For instance, you could emphasize that money becomes dangerous only when it replaces moral values.

Organization and development

Your essay has a reasonable structure:
Paragraph 1: money as power
Paragraph 2: money is neutral, attitude matters
Paragraph 3: greed

This is a good basis, but each paragraph needs more development and more precise explanation.

Paragraph 1
You explain that money provides survival, status, and acceptance. This is a strong start. However, the jump from “money helps survival” to “money buys acceptance” is quite broad. Add one or two concrete examples:
food, housing, healthcare for survival
luxury goods, exclusive education, or social influence for status

Paragraph 2
This paragraph contains the core of your argument, but several ideas are listed too quickly. You mention addictions, material possessions, and social prestige, but the connection to your thesis should be explained more carefully. Show how desire turns money into a harmful force.

For example:
A person addicted to gambling may lie or steal for money.
A person obsessed with status may ignore ethical limits to become richer.

Paragraph 3
The discussion of greed is relevant, but this paragraph is too brief. Since greed is your key concept, expand it. Explain why greed is different from normal ambition. You might say:
wanting financial security is reasonable
greed means wanting more without limits and without concern for others

That distinction would make your argument more sophisticated.

Style and grammar

Your writing is understandable, but there are several grammar and punctuation issues. Below are the main patterns to revise.

A. Missing article
German speakers often omit articles in English.

“The key is in the definition of the term money”
“play very little importance in the structure of a greedy person’s life”

B. Word choice and form
Some phrases sound unnatural in English:

“Money in itself is neither good nor bad” instead of “Money of itself”
“The list of addictions people develop is endless”  
  (“list” is singular)
“One’s attitude” not “Ones’s attitude”
“a greedy person’s life” not “greedy persons life”

C. Capitalization
“When the need for money becomes the driving force...”  
  Do not begin a sentence with a lowercase letter.

D. Run-on sentences / comma splices
Some sentences join ideas with commas when they should be separated.

Example:
“Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive.”

Better:
“Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, while a lack of money may mean that you cannot survive.”
or
“Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive. By contrast, a lack of money may mean that you cannot survive.”

E. Punctuation with dashes
You use dashes often. They can be effective, but too many make the writing feel informal. In an academic essay, commas or separate sentences are often better.

For example:
“prestige—a place in society—can also be bought”

F. Sentence completeness
Your final sentence is incomplete in punctuation and feels abrupt:

“Money does good or evil--depending on who has the money”

Better:
“Money can be used for good or evil, depending on the values of the person who controls it.”

Suggested revision priorities

Write a clearer thesis in the introduction.
Expand each body paragraph with specific examples.
Develop the idea of greed more fully.
Fix grammar issues with articles, possessives, and sentence structure.
Write a stronger conclusion that restates your main claim.

Final comment

You have a solid main idea and a thoughtful moral perspective. The strongest part of your essay is that you do not treat money as automatically evil; instead, you argue that human desire and greed are the real problem. With clearer development and more careful grammar, this could become a persuasive and effective argumentative essay.
