Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and a strong position: you support some goals of feminism but believe traditional dating customs and chivalry have declined. This gives your argument energy and makes the reader understand what matters to you. For revision, the main areas to improve are focus, tone, evidence, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear stance: The reader can easily identify your main claim.
Personal engagement: Your own experience gives the essay authenticity.
Acknowledgment of complexity: You do not reject all feminist ideas; this makes the argument more nuanced.

Main issue: define the thesis more precisely
At the moment, your argument shifts between several ideas:
feminism has had positive effects,
gender roles in marriage have changed,
modern dating is confusing,
chivalry has declined.

These points are related, but the essay needs one central thesis. For example:

*Although feminism has improved women’s social and economic opportunities, modern dating has become less clear because traditional expectations of chivalry no longer apply in the same way.*

This kind of thesis is more balanced and academic.

Use evidence, not only personal opinion
Right now, the essay relies heavily on personal experience and reports from friends. Personal examples are useful, but in an argumentative essay they are usually not enough. Readers may ask:
Is this true for most people, or only for your social circle?
Are feminists really responsible for the change, or are there other causes?

To strengthen your argument, add evidence such as:
surveys about dating expectations,
research on gender roles,
historical comparisons of courtship norms,
studies on attitudes toward paying, opening doors, or consent.

Also, be careful about cause and effect. The essay often suggests that feminism caused the decline of chivalry, but this is not proven. Other factors may include changing social values, economic pressures, and new ideas about equality.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some sentences make broad claims about “women,” “men,” or “feminists.” This weakens the essay because it sounds unfair and too absolute.

Examples:
“women in general are offended…”
“because of the feminists in this country”
“Men were gentlemen in the 60’s”

These statements ignore differences among individuals and time periods. Try using more cautious language:
*some women may prefer…*
*certain feminist arguments have been interpreted as…*
*many people remember the 1960s as…*

This will make your argument sound more credible and academic.

Tone and word choice
Your essay sometimes uses emotionally charged or conversational phrases such as:
“Believe me”
“this is bunk!”
“you men out there”

These create a strong voice, but in academic writing they can sound too informal or confrontational. Try a more neutral style. For example:
*In my view, this assumption is inaccurate.*
*Many people still value traditional gestures of courtesy.*

Also be careful with serious terms such as “date rape” and “sexual harassment.” In the essay, they are used rhetorically, which may seem dismissive. If you mention them, do so carefully and respectfully.

Organization
Your paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but the structure could be clearer. Consider this outline:

Introduction  
   Present the topic and thesis.

Positive achievements of feminism  
   Equal pay, career opportunities, freedom of choice.

Changes in dating culture  
   Explain how expectations have become less clear.

Why this creates confusion  
   Discuss traditional gestures versus modern equality.

Conclusion  
   Restate your balanced position: support equality, but preserve mutual courtesy.

This structure will help the reader follow your reasoning more easily.

Language and grammar points
Because your first language is German, a few English expressions need attention:

“opposite-sexed dinner date” → unnatural; better: *a date of the opposite sex* or simply *their date*.
“in the realm of date” → *in the realm of dating*.
“stigmatism” → *stigma*.
“allowing the ‘glass ceiling’ to be broken through” → *helping women break the glass ceiling*.
“Dutch treat” → understandable, but *split the bill* is more common today.
Be careful with long sentences. Several sentences are too long and contain too many interruptions in brackets or dashes. Shorter sentences will improve clarity.

Also check punctuation:
Avoid combining too many clauses with commas.
Use quotation marks consistently.
“60’s” and “90’s” are better written as 1960s and 1990s in formal writing.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear, balanced thesis.
Replace generalizations with careful, precise claims.
Add outside evidence.
Use a more formal and respectful tone.
Improve paragraph structure and sentence clarity.

You have a strong starting point and a clear sense of what you want to say. If you make the argument more precise and academic, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: crime has serious consequences not only for victims, but also for offenders and society. You show awareness of several dimensions of the topic—financial, moral, and social—which is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Clarify the thesis and introduction
Your opening is interesting, but the argument is not fully clear because the phrase you refer to is missing (). I assume you mean “crime does not pay.” If so, state this directly and present your main claim in one sentence.

For example:
The saying “crime does not pay” is true because crime creates financial, moral, and social costs for offenders, victims, and the wider community.

This would give the essay a clearer direction from the beginning.

Improve organization
At the moment, each paragraph presents a different consequence of crime, which is good, but the essay needs a stronger structure. A simple argumentative structure would be:

Introduction: explain the phrase and state your thesis  
Body paragraph 1: financial costs to society and taxpayers  
Body paragraph 2: emotional and moral costs to the offender  
Body paragraph 3: costs to law-abiding citizens and communities  
Body paragraph 4: long-term consequences such as stigma, employment, and housing  
Conclusion: restate the argument and summarize why crime does not pay

Right now, the essay ends suddenly. Add a short conclusion to give the piece a finished and persuasive ending.

Make the argument more precise
Some points are too general or slightly overstated. Academic argumentation is stronger when it is precise.

Examples:
“for the most part … the resulting emotion is remorse and sorrow”  
  This is too absolute. Not all criminals feel remorse. You could write:  
  - “Many offenders may experience remorse and sorrow, especially when confronted with the consequences of their actions.”

“the label of ‘criminal’ will be affixed to them for the rest of their lives and there is no escape”  
  This is powerful, but too absolute. A more balanced version would be:  
  - “A criminal conviction can continue to affect a person’s opportunities for many years, especially in employment and housing.”

Try to avoid claims that sound universal unless you can prove them.

Work on sentence structure and punctuation
Many sentences are long and run together. This makes the essay difficult to read. In English academic writing, shorter sentences with clear punctuation are usually more effective.

For example, this sentence is too long:
“The individual who commits a crime, if and when they are caught will in most cases be faced with the loss of his or her freedom.”

A clearer version:
“An individual who commits a crime and is caught will, in most cases, lose their freedom.”

Also remember:
Start a new sentence instead of joining many ideas with commas.
Capitalize the first word of every sentence.
Avoid comma splices and missing commas after introductory clauses.

Grammar and language points
Here are some recurring issues to revise:

a) Word choice
“to often overlooked” → “too often overlooked”
“freinds” → “friends”
“acquantances” → “acquaintances”
“oppurtunity” → “opportunity”
“their is no escape” → “there is no escape”
“so to will” → “so too will”

b) Formal style
Some phrases are slightly informal:
“daily paper” → “newspaper”
“we hear stories” is acceptable, but in a formal essay you might prefer “media reports often describe…”

c) Pronouns and agreement
You shift between “they,” “his or her,” and “the individual.” Try to be consistent. Modern academic English often accepts singular they:
“A person who commits a crime may lose their freedom.”

d) Articles and phrasing
“the one's own freedom” is incorrect. Use “one’s own freedom” or better: “their own freedom.”

Develop stronger support
Your essay would be more persuasive with examples or evidence. For instance:
mention prison costs for taxpayers,
explain how crime affects property values,
discuss how a criminal record can limit employment.

You do not need many statistics, but even one concrete example in each paragraph would strengthen the argument.

Suggested revision strategy
When revising, focus on these steps:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Add a conclusion.
Break long sentences into shorter ones.
Correct spelling and grammar errors.
Replace absolute statements with more careful wording.
Add one example to each body paragraph.

Final comment
Your essay has a solid main idea and shows good awareness of the wider consequences of crime. The biggest improvements will come from clearer structure, more precise language, and better sentence control. With revision, this could become a much stronger and more convincing argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear voice and strong personal engagement with the topic. You sound confident, and the contrast between “organized” and “unorganized” crime gives the paper a basic structure. However, for an argumentative academic essay, you need a clearer thesis, more careful reasoning, and a more formal style. Right now, the essay reads more like a personal reflection than a persuasive academic argument.

Main issue: unclear argument
Your central claim seems to be that organized crime is more profitable and more protected than unorganized crime. This is an interesting idea, but it is not stated clearly enough in the introduction. The first paragraph is very personal and does not tell the reader exactly what you will argue.

What to revise:
End the introduction with a direct thesis statement.
Example:  
  *“While unorganized crime is often impulsive and easier to punish, organized crime is more profitable and harder to eliminate because it relies on money, networks, and corruption.”*

This would help the whole essay stay focused.

Organization and development
You do separate the essay into two parts: unorganized crime and organized crime. That is helpful. But the development within each paragraph is uneven.

Paragraph on “unorganized crime”
You call robbery, assault, rape, and theft “petty offenses.” This is a serious problem. In English, “petty offenses” usually means minor crimes, such as small-scale theft or vandalism. Assault and rape are not petty offenses. This weakens your credibility.

Also, some claims are too broad:
“These people are usually somewhat less educated”
“believe what they do is essential for survival”
“these criminals just are not that good”

These statements need evidence, and in academic writing you should avoid sweeping generalizations.

Paragraph on organized crime
This section is more developed and more convincing, but again many claims are presented as facts without support. For example:
police are “willing to overlook illegal acts”
officials protect organized criminals for money
billions of dollars are turned over every year

These may be possible arguments, but you need sources, evidence, or specific examples. If not, the reader may see them as opinions rather than proof.

What to revise:
Use one main point per paragraph.
Support each point with evidence, examples, or course material.
Avoid unsupported claims about police, criminals, or society.

Tone and academic style
Your tone is often too informal for an academic argumentative essay. Examples:
“save the best for last”
“MONEY!”
“BIG sum of money”
“trying to figure me out”

Also, the final paragraph becomes autobiographical. The section beginning “My name is Thomas Vessely…” does not support your argument and should be removed. In academic writing, readers do not need your age, major, or personal introduction unless the assignment specifically asks for it.

What to revise:
Replace conversational phrases with formal ones.
Avoid all caps and dramatic punctuation.
Remove the personal self-introduction at the end.

Evidence and logic
A strong argumentative essay needs a combination of:
claim
reason
evidence
explanation

Your essay often has the claim and reason, but not enough evidence. For instance, the gambling statistic (“$30 per person”) could be useful, but you need to explain:
where the number comes from
why it matters
how it supports your thesis

You also make a moral argument indirectly, but it is not fully developed. Are you arguing that organized crime is more dangerous? More tolerated? More difficult to stop? More profitable? Choose the main focus and build your paragraphs around it.

Language and grammar
Your English is generally understandable, but some sentences sound unnatural or imprecise. Here are a few examples:

“educated on both sides of the track”  
  Better: *“familiar with both sides of the issue”*  
  (“Both sides of the tracks” is an idiom, but your use here is unusual.)

“The persons must be patient and will to deal with others.”  
  Better: *“Such individuals must be patient and willing to work with others.”*

“These things only happen with money and with money, a person can have ANYTHING they want.”  
  Better: *“Such systems depend heavily on money, which can be used to gain influence and protection.”*

“not exclusive to”  
  Better: *“not limited to”*

Also watch article use and word choice, which can be difficult for German speakers:
*“a 21-year-old male”* rather than *“a 21 year old male”*
*“throughout almost every country”* rather than *“in every facet of (almost) every country”*

Suggested revision strategy
Write a new thesis at the end of the introduction.
Remove the final autobiographical section.
Reorganize the body into clear argument points:
   - unorganized crime: impulsive, less profitable, easier to detect
   - organized crime: structured, profit-driven, supported by corruption
Replace emotional or informal language with formal academic language.
Add evidence from your course, readings, or reliable sources.
Avoid overgeneralizations and define key terms carefully.

Final comment
You have a strong starting idea and a confident writing voice. The next step is to turn this into a true academic argument by making your thesis precise, supporting your claims with evidence, and using a more formal tone. If you revise in that direction, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argument and personal position: you want to show that crime should have consequences and that, in your experience, “crime doesn’t pay.” The personal narrative is effective because it gives the argument emotional force. The essay is also easy to follow overall because it moves in chronological order from the burglary to the court case and final reflection.

What works well

Strong personal voice
   - The essay feels sincere and emotionally convincing.
   - The repeated reference to your father’s saying creates a clear theme.

Effective organization
   - The story develops step by step: childhood belief → burglary → investigation → court process → conclusion.
   - This makes the essay readable and coherent.

Clear sense of purpose
   - Your main point remains consistent throughout the essay: justice matters, and criminal acts should lead to punishment.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more explicit

Right now, the essay begins as a story, but the argument is not fully stated at the start. In an argumentative essay, readers should know your position early.

For example, your introduction could end with a sentence like:

This experience taught me that although justice can be slow and painful, crime should have real consequences and the legal system must protect victims.

That would make your central claim clearer from the beginning.

Separate narrative from argument more clearly

Much of the essay is narrative, which is valuable, but argumentative writing usually also includes reflection and explanation. After important events, add 1–2 sentences explaining what they prove.

For example, after describing the possible plea bargain, you could explain:

This showed me that the legal system can sometimes focus too much on efficiency and not enough on the victim’s suffering.
Punishment is important not only for the criminal, but also because it acknowledges the harm done to victims.

These sentences would strengthen the essay as an argument, not just a personal story.

Improve grammar and sentence structure

There are several grammar problems that sometimes interrupt the flow. Below are some important examples:

“my mother and father always taught me the stealing was wrong”  
  → “my mother and father always taught me that stealing was wrong”

“I always believed them and they saying that”  
  → “I always believed them and what they said”

“Upon return from the doctor’s office”  
  → “Upon returning from the doctor’s office”

“before I could call for police help”  
  → “before I could call the police”

“my husbands firearms”  
  → “my husband’s firearms”

“it was our home they stolen property came from”  
  → “that the stolen property came from our home”

“prosecutors office”  
  → “the prosecutor’s office”

“they would probably would not serve any time”  
  → “they probably would not serve any time”

“what my father told be”  
  → “what my father told me”

“two men and one women”  
  → “two men and one woman”

As a German speaker, pay special attention to:
word order
articles (a/the)
possessives (husband’s, prosecutor’s)
that-clauses in English

Check punctuation and plural forms

Some small errors make the essay look less polished:

CD’s → CDs  
policemen is possible, but police officers is more natural and modern
Add commas carefully, but do not overuse them in long sentences

For example, this sentence is too long:

“This upset me, because I felt like they were getting away with a crime and that I was the one that was being punished…”

You could divide it into two sentences for clarity.

Use a more formal academic tone in places

Because this is an argumentative essay, some phrases are too conversational:

“I mean everything”  
  This is emotional and understandable, but in formal writing you could write:
  - “Nearly everything had been taken.”

“those awful people”  
  Better:
  - “the offenders” or “the burglars”

This does not mean you must remove emotion completely, but a slightly more formal vocabulary will make your argument stronger.

Strengthen the conclusion

Your conclusion returns nicely to the phrase “crime doesn’t pay,” but you can make it more analytical. Instead of ending only with your personal feeling, connect your experience to a broader claim.

Suggested revision priorities

Add more explanation of why your experience supports your argument.
Correct grammar and sentence structure.
Make the tone slightly more formal.
Revise the conclusion to emphasize the broader significance.

Final comment

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that feminism originally aimed at equality, but that some extreme forms of modern feminism have damaged the movement’s public image. This gives the paper a strong argumentative focus. You also show engagement with history and with contemporary debate, which is a good basis for revision.

What is working well

Clear thesis and line of argument  
The essay consistently returns to the idea that feminism should mean equality, not female dominance. This gives the reader a clear sense of your main claim.

Personal voice and engagement  
Your statement “I would call myself a feminist” makes your position concrete and helps the reader understand your perspective.

Use of contrast  
You effectively contrast “original” feminism with “modern” misuse. This is a useful argumentative structure.

Main areas to improve

Make the argument more precise and balanced
At the moment, some claims are too general or emotionally loaded. For example:

“The concept many people today have of a ‘feminist’ is that of a big chunky lesbian who hates all men.”
“Feminists are becoming anarchists.”

These statements sound stereotypical and unsupported. In academic argumentation, broad claims about “many people,” “outsiders,” or “feminists” need evidence. Otherwise, the reader may feel you are replacing one stereotype with another.

How to revise:  
Distinguish between mainstream feminism and radical or fringe groups.
Avoid claiming that “feminists” as a whole behave in one way.
Use cautious language: “some critics view…,” “certain radical groups…,” “in some public debates…”.

Strengthen evidence
The essay makes many assertions, but provides almost no evidence. For instance, you mention Susan B. Anthony, pay inequality, sex crimes, and domestic violence, but you do not explain or support these points with examples.

How to revise:  
Add:
one historical example of early feminism,
one example of a major positive achievement of feminism,
one concrete example of a controversial or extreme feminist position that has influenced public opinion.

This will make your argument much more persuasive.

Improve organization
Your essay has a general structure, but some paragraphs are too long and combine several ideas. For example, the fourth paragraph discusses equal pay, voting rights, achievements of feminism, and extreme groups all at once.

A stronger structure might be:
Introduction: define your view of feminism and present thesis.
Historical background: explain the original goals of feminism.
Achievements: show what feminism has accomplished.
Problem: explain how some extreme voices have harmed the movement’s image.
Conclusion: restate your balanced position.

Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence.

Use a more academic tone
Some phrasing is informal or overly forceful, for example:
“dirty words”
“home free”
“a bunch of whining women”
“fight tooth and nail”

These expressions sound conversational rather than academic.

Try replacing them with more neutral alternatives:
“have acquired negative connotations”
“avoid criticism”
“are sometimes dismissed as complaining”
“argue aggressively”

Sentence-level grammar and style
There are several language issues, many of which are common for German-speaking learners.

a. Capitalization
English requires capitalization at the start of sentences:
“by this I do not mean…” → “By this I do not mean…”

b. Articles and singular/plural nouns
“A women is…” → “A woman is…”
“The equality of woman…” → “The equality of women…”
“to an extremes” → “to extremes” or “to an extreme”

c. Word choice
“innately whole” sounds unnatural here. You might say “fully empowered” or “able to participate fully in society.”
“moot argument” may not mean what you intend. Perhaps “weak argument” or “minor issue.”

d. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and would be stronger if divided.

Example:
> “There is a problem though, the problem is that one can see why the statement this essay is commenting on exists.”

Possible revision:
> “There is, however, a problem. One can understand why the statement discussed in this essay exists.”

Example of a more academic thesis
Your introduction could end with something like:

> Feminism originally sought political and social equality for women; although the movement has achieved major progress, its public image has sometimes been damaged by extreme voices that do not represent its central goals.

This version is clear, balanced, and suitable for academic writing.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these three priorities:

Reduce generalizations and stereotypes.
Add specific evidence and examples.
Use a more formal academic tone.

You have a strong central idea, but to make the essay convincing at university level, you need more precision, support, and careful wording. The goal is not only to express a strong opinion, but to persuade readers through balanced reasoning.

Your essay has a clear central idea: the Women’s Liberation Movement was harmed less by feminists themselves than by how outsiders perceived and stereotyped them. That is a strong argumentative claim, and it gives your essay a clear direction. You also show good awareness of how public opinion can distort a movement. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Main strengths
Clear position: Your thesis is understandable: radical feminists were not the real problem; public perception was.
Relevant topic: You focus on stereotypes and media/public reaction, which is a promising angle for argumentation.
Attempt at historical development: You move from the nineteenth century to the 1960s and then to the present, which gives the essay structure.

Main issues to revise

Your thesis appears most clearly here:  
“I contend that the fault does not lie with the radical members of the movement, but rather, in the perception of those observing the movement.”

This is a strong sentence, but it comes rather late. Try to state your argument more precisely at the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although outspoken feminists were often blamed for damaging the Women’s Liberation Movement, the movement was actually weakened more by public stereotypes and selective attention to its most radical figures.*

This version is more specific and argumentative.

B. Improve paragraph structure
Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try to organize each paragraph around one clear point:
Introduction: stereotypes form around social movements.
Historical example: early feminism was judged through a few visible radicals.
1960s example: media/public focused on extreme images instead of ordinary women.
Conclusion: the movement’s reputation suffered because of outsiders’ perceptions.

Add stronger topic sentences so the reader knows the purpose of each paragraph.

C. Use evidence more carefully
Your essay names several figures, but some examples are questionable or need explanation:
Emily Dickinson is not usually discussed as a feminist activist in the Women’s Liberation Movement.
George Sand was French, as you mention, but then you use her in an essay specifically about America.
Stein is unclear. Do you mean Gloria Steinem? If so, use the full name.

Also, some claims are broad and need support:
“Women’s Movement in America has been fighting a losing battle”
“it was again destined for failure”

These are strong statements, but they need evidence or a more careful wording. Otherwise they sound exaggerated.

D. Develop the argument more fully
At the moment, the essay repeats the same point several times: people notice radicals more than ordinary feminists. To make the argument more convincing, explain how this happens:
through media attention?
through stereotypes about gender roles?
through fear of social change?

If you explain the mechanism, your argument will become deeper and more persuasive.

Language and style

A. Grammar and word choice
There are several sentence-level errors. Here are some important ones:

“Whenever a movement is began” → *Whenever a movement begins* / *Whenever a movement is begun*
“People normally associated with politics, religion, or art” → better: *People involved in politics, religion, or art*
“oppostion” → *opposition*
“therfore” → *therefore*
“in modern standards” → *by modern standards*
“and and” → remove one *and*
“promote” → *promoted*
“readiliy” → *readily*
“the public it was being aimed” → *the public at which it was aimed* / more naturally: *the public it was aimed at*

B. Punctuation
Do not separate subject and verb with a comma:
“This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem…”  
Correct: *This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists made it seem…*

Be careful with commas after introductory phrases and around interruptions.

C. Tone and precision
Some expressions are too informal or vague for an academic essay:
“So what am I getting at?” is conversational. Replace it with something more formal, such as:
  *The central point, then, is that…*
“wild, disgruntled zealots” is vivid, but if this is your own wording, it sounds emotionally loaded. If you mean that this was the stereotype, make that clear.

Suggestions for revision
When revising, try this plan:
Rewrite the introduction so the thesis is clear at the end.
Check your examples for accuracy and relevance.
Make each paragraph support one reason why public perception harmed the movement.
Replace informal phrases with academic language.
Proofread carefully for spelling, verb forms, and punctuation.

Example of a stronger sentence
Original:  
*The good of their cause is undermined by the perception of the radicals.*

Revised:  
*The legitimacy of their cause is often undermined because the public judges the entire movement by its most radical members.*

This is clearer and more formal.

Overall, your essay has a solid argumentative core, but it needs clearer organization, more accurate evidence, and careful language revision. With these changes, your argument could become much more convincing.

Your essay has a clear topic and a confident voice. Main strengths

2) Engaged tone  
The writing sounds committed and persuasive, which is important in argumentative writing.

3) Historical awareness  
You connect women’s suffrage with later feminist movements, which helps show continuity.

Main areas to improve

A. Strengthen the argument
At the moment, your essay is more descriptive than argumentative. You state that America “continues to set an example,” but you do not fully prove this claim.

To make the essay more argumentative:
state your main thesis more clearly at the end of the introduction
give specific evidence for your points
explain why the examples support your conclusion
consider a counterargument

For example, if you argue that feminism improved opportunities for women, include concrete evidence such as:
expanded access to higher education
legal changes in employment discrimination
women entering politics or professions previously closed to them

Then explain how these changes show the impact of feminism.

B. Clarify the thesis
Your essay begins globally, but then discusses only the United States. That is fine, but the thesis should match the scope.

This is stronger because it is specific and balanced.

C. Develop paragraphs more fully

For example, the paragraph about Betty Friedan and Gloria Steinem introduces important figures, but the sentence with  shows that a key idea is missing. Language and style corrections

Word choice / phrasing

  “Women’s access … has expanded”

  - “at the World Conference on Women”
  - “in the voting booths of America” sounds awkward; better: “at the ballot box” or “in elections”

Spelling / names
“Gloria Steinam” → “Gloria Steinem”
“board rooms” → usually “boardrooms”

Tone and academic style

“battle cries”
“preached the same sermon, from the same pulpit”
“slammed in the faces”

create vivid imagery, but they can sound dramatic rather than analytical. In argumentative academic writing, aim for a balance between passion and precision.

For example:

“preached the same sermon” → “advocated the same central principle”

correcting grammar and word choice

With these changes, your essay will become much more convincing and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a logical basic structure: you reject the statement “Money is the root of all evil,” explain why, admit that money can be connected to some evil actions, and end by arguing that individual choice is more important. This is a solid argumentative foundation. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengthen the thesis

Your central claim is understandable, but it could be stated more clearly and directly. In the introduction, you begin with the wording of the phrase, but the sentence *“I believe this corrected statement to be true”* is slightly confusing because the reader has to work out what “this corrected statement” means.

A clearer thesis would be something like:

Although the love of money can lead people to do harmful things, money itself is not the root of all evil; rather, evil comes from human choices and moral failings.

This version makes your argument precise and sets up the essay more effectively.

Improve the introduction

Your first sentence focuses on correcting the phrase, which is interesting, but the opening could be smoother. Also, “wold” should be would.

You might revise the introduction so it:
introduces the common saying,
explains the difference between *money* and *love of money*,
presents your thesis.

For example:
Many people say that money is the root of all evil, but this statement is too simplistic. Money itself is neutral; it is the love of money and the choices people make that can lead to evil actions.

Develop your reasoning more fully

Your body paragraphs make good points, but some ideas need more explanation.

Paragraph 2
You argue that if money were evil, then everyone with money would be evil. This is a strong point, but the sentence *“Most money is attained in honest ways”* is too broad. Be careful with absolute claims such as “most” unless you can support them.

Also, this sentence has a language problem:
If one is attaining money honestly, how can honestly be associated with evil?

Better:
If someone earns money honestly, how can money itself be considered evil?

Paragraph 3
This paragraph is effective because you acknowledge the other side. That makes your essay more balanced. Still, you can make the logic more precise:
Money is not the cause of evil.
The desire for money may motivate evil actions.

That distinction is important.

Paragraph 4
This paragraph lists other causes of evil, but it feels somewhat disconnected. Try to connect it more clearly to your thesis:
Greed, dishonesty, and a lack of moral values—not money itself—are often the true causes of evil actions.

Also, punctuation in the list needs revision. Instead of:
attitudes, morals (lack of), greed;
write:
attitudes, greed, and a lack of morals

Use more precise and natural English

There are several places where your wording sounds understandable but not fully natural. Some examples:

worth-while → worthwhile
was in “If money was the root…” → better were in formal hypothetical writing
attaining money → more natural: earning money or acquiring money
honestly be associated with evil → awkward phrasing

Also, avoid repetition of evil in nearly every sentence when possible. You can vary with:
wrongdoing
harmful actions
immoral behavior
corruption

Pay attention to style and tone

Some sentences are too long and could be clearer if divided. For example:

I have to agree that many times these worth-while causes are sometimes being used to satisfy an individual's greed; but I am sure that most if not all funds donated are used for the purposes intended in those agencies and programs which are reputable.

This is difficult to read because it contains too many ideas. You could split it:

It is true that some charitable causes are sometimes used to satisfy an individual’s greed. However, reputable organizations generally use donated funds for their intended purposes.

This is shorter, clearer, and more formal.

Make the conclusion stronger

Your conclusion has a good final idea: individual choice matters most. However, it would be stronger if it briefly restated your argument before ending.

For example:
In conclusion, money itself is not the root of all evil. Although the desire for money can encourage greed and dishonesty, evil ultimately comes from the moral choices individuals make.

Grammar and mechanics to revise

Please check the following:
spelling: wold → would
conditional form: If money was → If money were
article use: the many agencies, groups and programs is fine, but watch consistency
comma use in lists
sentence length and semicolon use
avoid unnecessary capitalization in quoted phrases unless required

Final advice

Your essay already has:
a clear opinion,
relevant examples,
a logical progression.

For revision, focus on:
writing a clearer thesis,
making the distinction between *money* and *love of money* more precise,
using more natural academic English,
shortening long sentences,
strengthening transitions between paragraphs.

You have a solid argument here. With clearer phrasing and more precise language, this could become a strong and persuasive essay.

Feedback Report

You have a clear central idea: money is necessary, but it should not control people. Your essay shows thoughtful reflection and uses contrasts effectively, such as “use money” versus “be used by money.” The piece also has a strong moral viewpoint, which suits an argumentative essay. For revision, focus on three main areas: clarity of argument, organization, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear topic: You stay focused on the relationship between money and power.
Engaging tone: The opening is strong and immediately presents a real-world perspective.
Interesting contrasts: Phrases like “Some use it to live and others live to use it” are memorable and effective.
Thoughtful conclusion: Your final paragraph clearly expresses your overall position.

Main revision priorities

A. Make your thesis more precise
Right now, your essay discusses several related ideas: money, power, corruption, work ethic, and success. These ideas connect, but your main argument could be stated more clearly in the introduction.

For example, you could revise your thesis to something like:
Although money is necessary in modern society, it becomes dangerous when people treat it as the source of power and personal worth.

This gives the reader a clear expectation for the rest of the essay.

B. Improve paragraph structure
Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try using this pattern:
Topic sentence
Explanation
Example
Link back to the thesis

For instance, your third paragraph talks about working hard for money and appreciating it more. That is a good point, but it would be stronger with a concrete example.

Example:
People who earn their own salary often learn discipline and value money more carefully.
By contrast, people who have always had easy access to wealth may spend it carelessly.

This makes the argument more persuasive.

C. Develop the argument more logically
At times, the essay moves too quickly from one point to another. For example:
Paragraph 1: money is necessary
Paragraph 2: power corrupts
Paragraph 3: effort increases appreciation
Paragraph 4: different attitudes toward success

These are all relevant, but the links between them should be clearer. Use transition phrases such as:
However
In addition
As a result
By contrast
Therefore

Language and grammar issues

There are several grammar, spelling, and word-choice problems. Since you are a German-speaking writer, pay special attention to apostrophes, article use, word forms, and sentence structure.

Common corrections from your essay
“have some where to sleep” → “have somewhere to sleep”
“currupts” → “corrupts”
“Once ones basic” → “Once one’s basic”
“begats” → “begets”
“cicle” → “cycle”
“sence” → “sense”
“sucess” → “success”
“heir” → probably “on” in “rate success on more substantive accomplishments”
“indespencable” → “indispensable”

Grammar points to review
Possessives:  
  “ones basic needs” should be “one’s basic needs.”
Parallel structure:  
  “power or the power to influence” is repetitive. You might write “wealth or influence.”
Pronoun agreement:  
  “If one has to work hard for their salary” is common in informal English, but in formal writing you could revise to:  
  “If people have to work hard for their salaries, they are more likely to spend money wisely.”
Quotation use:  
  You write: “Absolute power currupts absolute.”  
  The standard quotation is “Absolute power corrupts absolutely.”

For example:

Suggested revision plan

Add examples or explanations to support each claim.

Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

Final comment
This essay has a strong core argument and several insightful observations about the role of money in society. Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: money itself is not evil, but the desire for money can lead people away from moral values. This is a strong and arguable thesis, and your conclusion returns to that idea effectively. You also use a clear moral framework, which gives the essay unity. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Main strengths
Clear central argument: You consistently argue that money is neutral, while people’s attitudes and actions can be evil.
Logical progression: The essay moves from defining the saying, to discussing moral danger, to explaining that money can also be used for good.
Effective conclusion: The final sentence clearly answers the essay question.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your opinion is understandable, but it would be stronger if stated more directly in the introduction.

For example:
“Money is not evil in itself; rather, the desire for wealth and power can lead people to act immorally.”

This gives the reader your exact claim from the beginning.

B. Develop the argument with more explanation
Some paragraphs make strong claims but need more support. For example, you say that the pursuit of money can make family and friends unimportant and can lead to gambling. These are possible consequences, but you should explain why and how this happens.

Try adding:
a brief example,
a real-life situation,
or a clearer cause-and-effect explanation.

For instance:
“When people measure success only by wealth, they may begin to neglect relationships, honesty, and community because financial gain becomes their highest priority.”

This sounds more analytical and academic.

C. Consider a broader perspective
Your essay relies heavily on a Christian viewpoint. That is acceptable if the assignment allows a religious argument, but you should make sure the essay still addresses a wider audience. You might show that this issue is not only religious but also ethical and social.

For example:
“Many religious traditions, as well as secular moral systems, warn against greed because it can damage both individuals and society.”

This makes your argument more inclusive and academically persuasive.

Organization
Your essay is generally easy to follow, but topic sentences could be stronger. Each paragraph should begin with a sentence that clearly states its main idea.

Example revisions:
Paragraph 3: “From a Christian perspective, a good life is centered on God rather than material wealth.”
Paragraph 4: “When people become obsessed with money, they may lose sight of relationships and moral limits.”
Paragraph 6: “At the same time, money can be a powerful tool for improving people’s lives.”

This helps the essay feel more structured.

Language and grammar
There are several grammar, word choice, and spelling issues. Many may come from direct translation or spelling uncertainty. Below are important patterns to revise.

A. Missing or incorrect words
“you always want more that you have” → “you always want more than you have”
“can be lead into gambling” → “can be led into gambling”
“to educated the uneducated” → “to educate the uneducated”

B. Spelling
“christian” → “Christian”
“subservant” → “subservient”
“mear” → “mere”

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are repetitive or awkward.

Original:
“It is the person whose judgements, decisions, and actions that can be evil.”

Better:
“Rather, it is people’s judgments, decisions, and actions that can be evil.”

Original:
“Money can provide opportunities to educated the uneducated which will enable them to be able to provide a better life for themselves and their families.”

Better:
“Money can provide educational opportunities, enabling people to build better lives for themselves and their families.”

D. Punctuation
Be careful with commas joining sentences. For example:
“I think not, it is all in how it is used…”  
This is a comma splice.

Better:

Avoid very absolute statements unless you support them.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a consistent argument: you argue that “crime does pay” by comparing criminal work with ordinary employment. The structure is easy to follow because you organize the body around three advantages: job security, income, and early retirement. This gives your essay a logical progression. For revision, however, you need to strengthen the language accuracy, improve the academic tone, and develop the argument more critically.

Argument and critical thinking

Your main claim is clear, but the essay currently sounds as if it genuinely supports crime. In an academic argumentative essay, this can be risky unless you clearly show that you are analyzing an idea rather than endorsing it. At the moment, the argument is one-sided and ignores major counterarguments, such as imprisonment, violence, fear, social harm, addiction, and the instability of criminal life.

To improve this, acknowledge the weaknesses in your own argument. For example, after explaining the financial “advantages,” you could discuss the serious risks and ask whether money alone means that crime “pays.” This would make your essay more thoughtful and persuasive.

Also, some claims are too broad:
“A criminal will always be employed.”
“The average criminal can work one hour a day and make $300.”
“The criminal can retire a lot faster than the worker.”

These statements need evidence. In academic writing, unsupported generalizations weaken your credibility.

Organization

Your essay has an introduction, body, and conclusion, which is good. Each paragraph focuses on one point. That said, your topic sentences could be more precise. For example:
“Let’s take time to compare…” is conversational.
“Then if we think about the work day…” is also informal.

Use more formal transitions and topic sentences, such as:
“First, crime may appear to offer greater job security than conventional employment.”
“Second, criminals may seem to earn more in less time.”
“Finally, one could argue that crime allows earlier retirement.”

Your conclusion is very short. Rather than simply repeating your opinion, briefly summarize the main points and reflect on the broader meaning of the comparison.

Style and academic tone

The essay is understandable, but the style is too informal for academic writing. Phrases such as:
“I beg to differ”
“Let’s take time”
“I’d definitely say”

sound conversational. Try to use a more neutral, formal tone. For example:
“Contrary to the common saying, one might argue that crime can be financially rewarding.”
“A comparison between criminal activity and legal employment suggests…”
“Based on these factors, one could conclude…”

Also, be careful with wording. Some expressions sound awkward or unnatural in English, for example:
“hard working person” → “hard-working person” or “law-abiding worker”
“holds down a job” is understandable but slightly informal
“set for life” is informal

Grammar and language issues

There are several grammar and word-choice problems. These are important to revise.

Common errors:
it’s / its:  
  “Being a criminal has it’s advantages” → its advantages  
  (“It’s” = “it is”)

whether / rather:  
  “concerned with rather or not” → concerned with whether or not

Sentence error:  
  “There is never a lack of work are a means for continuing employment.”  
  This is unclear. You could write:  
  “There is always some opportunity for illegal profit.”

cost / costs:  
  “insurance will costs them” → insurance will cost them

number agreement:  
  “a specific numbers of hours” → a specific number of hours

pronoun consistency:  
  You switch between “he or she,” “they,” and “his/her.” Choose one strategy and use it consistently. In modern academic English, singular they is usually best.

verb form:  
  “If he/she invest half this money” → If they invest half this money

Article and phrasing issues
As a German speaker, you may want to watch article use and idiomatic phrasing. For example:
“laid off work” → laid off
“make enough profit to pay the employees” → acceptable, but earn enough profit or remain profitable enough to pay employees sounds more natural.

Evidence and logic

Your use of numbers makes the essay feel concrete, but the figures seem invented and may not be realistic. If you use statistics, they should come from a source. Otherwise, present them as hypothetical examples:
“For instance, if a worker earns…”
“If a criminal were able to steal…”

Also, your logic assumes that illegal income is easy, regular, and safe. This is the biggest weakness in the essay. A stronger argument would either:
provide evidence for these claims, or
admit that the financial benefits are offset by serious risks.

Revision priorities

Adopt a more formal academic tone.  
Correct grammar and word-choice errors.  
Avoid unsupported generalizations.  
Add counterarguments and address risks of crime.  
Revise unclear sentences and improve transitions.

Final comment

You have a strong central idea and a clear comparative structure, which is a good foundation. Your next step is to make the essay sound more academic, accurate, and balanced. If you revise for tone, grammar, and critical depth, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0028.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim: for many poor urban youths, crime appears financially attractive because legal opportunities are limited and the risk of punishment seems low. This is a strong argumentative topic, and your essay has energy and a clear sense of purpose. To revise effectively, focus on (1) strengthening the logic and evidence, (2) reducing sweeping generalizations, and (3) improving grammar and style.

Argument and organization

What works
Your thesis is easy to identify: “crime pays” under certain social conditions.
The essay follows a logical general pattern: examples → causes → weak policing → weak punishment → conclusion.

What needs revision
At the moment, much of the argument depends on assumptions rather than proof. You often state that many young people with expensive clothes and cars must have obtained them through crime, but this is not necessarily true. Readers may question your reasoning immediately.

How to improve
Replace rhetorical questions with direct claims supported by evidence.
Explain why crime appears attractive using facts: unemployment, school inequality, underfunded neighborhoods, incarceration rates, recidivism, or wage comparisons.
Add at least 2–3 concrete sources or examples. For instance:
  - statistics on youth unemployment,
  - data about sentencing for nonviolent crime,
  - research on the relationship between poverty and crime.

A stronger thesis might be:

> In many low-income urban communities, crime can appear more profitable than legal work because of limited economic opportunity, weak social support, and inconsistent punishment.

This is more precise and less absolute than “crime pays.”

Tone and audience awareness

Your current tone is forceful, but sometimes it becomes overgeneralized or stereotypical, which may weaken your credibility. For example, the opening suggests that seeing young people in nice cars and clothes is proof of criminal activity. This risks sounding biased and unfair.

Why this matters
In academic argument, readers trust you more when you sound careful, balanced, and precise.

Suggestions
Avoid broad claims about “urban America,” “inner-city youth,” or “criminals” unless you define these groups and provide evidence.
Be careful with emotionally loaded words like “thug.” If you use such language, explain that it reflects a stereotype, not your own label.
Acknowledge complexity: not all poor youths turn to crime, and not all visible wealth comes from illegal activity.

For example, instead of:
> In many cases the answer is simple: crime.

you could write:
> In some cases, illegal activity may provide access to material goods that legal employment does not easily provide.

This sounds more academic and persuasive.

Development of ideas

Several paragraphs repeat the same basic point: crime is profitable and legal work is unattractive. Try to deepen the discussion instead of repeating it.

You could develop the essay with this structure:
Economic pressure: low wages, few jobs, poor schools.
Social influence: visible success of people engaged in crime.
Weak deterrence: low arrest rates or short sentences for some offenses.
Counterargument: crime also carries high risks—violence, trauma, prison, long-term exclusion from legal work.
Conclusion: crime may appear to pay in the short term, but society must create better alternatives.

Including a counterargument will make your essay more sophisticated.

Grammar, wording, and sentence-level issues

There are several language errors, but most are easy to fix.

Specific corrections
wether → whether
come → comes  
  (“The only danger that exists for a criminal comes from…”)
refuse → refuses  
  (“The public calls for more police but refuses…”)
Remove extra punctuation: “. .”
an act of crime → a crime
placed in jail for non-violent crime → jailed for a nonviolent crime
no en in sight → probably no end in sight
McDonalds → McDonald’s

Style issues
Avoid overusing he or she / his or her. It sounds repetitive. You can often use:
  - they / their (accepted in modern academic English), or
  - plural nouns like young people, offenders, citizens.
Some sentences are too long and could be divided for clarity.
Use hyphens consistently: nonviolent or non-violent—choose one form and keep it consistent.

Strongest revision priorities

If you only have time for a few changes, do these first:

Rewrite the introduction so it does not assume luxury goods automatically come from crime.
Add evidence instead of relying mainly on opinion.
Reduce stereotypes and absolute language to sound more academic.
Correct grammar and spelling errors throughout.
Add a counterargument to show critical thinking.

Final comment

You have an interesting and debatable thesis, and the essay shows confidence and strong engagement with the topic. To become more convincing, however, you need to move from assertion to evidence-based argument. If you revise with a more careful tone, stronger support, and more precise language, your essay will be much more effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear overall topic—the social effects of computers—and it tries to balance benefits and harms. You also make a definite claim in the introduction: computers have made life physically easier but mentally harder. That gives the essay direction. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, better supported, and more consistent in tone.

Strengths
Clear topic: You stay focused on computers and society.
Debatable thesis: The idea that computers help physically but create mental difficulties is arguable and interesting.
Organized structure: Introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion are present.
Attempt at nuance: You do not simply say computers are “good” or “bad”; you argue both.

Main issues to revise

A. Make the thesis sharper
Your thesis is understandable, but “physically easier” and “mentally harder” are still broad. Try to define exactly what you mean.

For example:
Do computers reduce physical labor through automation?
Do they increase mental dependence, reduce understanding, or create anxiety?

A stronger thesis could be:
“Although computers have reduced physical labor and increased efficiency, they have also created intellectual dependence and social anxiety because many users rely on systems they do not fully understand.”

This version is more specific and easier to support.

B. Improve paragraph development
Some body paragraphs list ideas instead of developing one argument fully. Each paragraph should have:
a clear topic sentence,
explanation,
examples or evidence,
a link back to the thesis.

For instance, in paragraph 2 you mention office work, factory automation, and calculators. These points are related, but they need explanation. How exactly do computers make work easier? What kinds of jobs are eliminated? What are the consequences?

C. Use evidence, not generalizations
Many claims are too broad:
“computers make errors all the time”
“mathematical models… are absolutely riddled with errors”
“almost everyone has at least one simple computer”
“everyone already knows”

These statements need support or qualification. In academic writing, avoid absolute language unless you can prove it. Use more cautious phrasing:
“can produce errors when data or instructions are incorrect”
“many people rely on calculations they do not fully understand”
“computers are widely used in everyday life”

Also avoid unsupported claims about “2nd & 3rd world countries.” This sounds outdated and inappropriate. Use respectful and current language such as “lower-income countries” only if relevant and supported.

D. Clarify your logic
Some arguments are interesting but not fully clear. For example, you say computers make “needless” computations and therefore create mental problems. Explain the connection more directly:
Is the problem overreliance?
Is it loss of mathematical understanding?
Is it the misuse of data?
Is it false confidence in computer-generated results?

Similarly, in paragraph 4, job loss is presented under “mental effects,” but it is mainly an economic or social effect. You can still discuss the emotional consequences, but the connection should be clearer.

Style and academic tone

Avoid informal or conversational phrases
Examples:
“catch-22”
“etc”
“everyone already knows”
“what he should do”

These reduce the academic tone. Replace them with more formal language:
“paradox”
give specific examples instead of “etc”
avoid appeals to common knowledge

Avoid gendered pronouns
You repeatedly use “he” to refer to a general person. Use:
“they”
“the user”
“the programmer”

Example:
Original: “if that person knew what he should do…”
Better: “if that person understood the process better…”

Language corrections

Because you are a German-speaking writer, some sentence structures may feel slightly heavy or indirect in English. Try to simplify.

Grammar and wording examples
“1940's” → “1940s”
“there have been many technological advances and also there has been”  
  → awkward repetition; revise to  
  “there have been many technological advances, as well as a tremendous impact on society.”
“other type functions” → “other types of functions”
“Due to this errors frequently exist” → “Because of this, errors frequently occur”
“ramifications about what they are doing” → “ramifications of what they are doing”
“if computers are good or bad” → “whether computers are beneficial or harmful”

Also watch punctuation and spacing; there are several double spaces and semicolon/comma problems.

Suggested revision strategy
Rewrite the thesis so it is more specific.
Reorganize the body into two or three clear arguments:
   - physical/economic benefits,
   - intellectual dependence and misuse,
   - social/emotional consequences.
Add concrete examples for each paragraph.
Remove exaggerated statements and use more careful academic wording.
Proofread for grammar and formal style.

Model revision of one sentence
Original:
“The computer has made life easier from a physical standpoint and harder from a mental standpoint.”

Possible revision:
“Computers have reduced physical labor and increased efficiency, but they have also encouraged dependence on technology and reduced users’ understanding of the processes behind the results.”

Overall, you have a solid starting point and a strong central idea. The next step is to make your reasoning more precise and academic.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: computer knowledge is important in modern life. You also try to support this idea with examples from work and banking, which is a good strategy for an argumentative essay. The topic is relevant, and your conclusion returns to the main claim. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Main strengths
Clear central argument: You argue that computer literacy is necessary for success today.
Use of examples: The manager in the auto-parts company is a concrete example, which helps readers understand your point.
Logical overall structure: Introduction → body examples → conclusion.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your main claim is understandable, but it could be stronger and more specific. Right now, “successful life” is a bit broad.

Current idea:  
“Today, having knowledge of how the computer operates is considered a necessary component of leading a succesful life.”

Possible revision:  
“Today, basic computer literacy is essential for professional success, financial security, and full participation in modern society.”

This version is more specific and prepares the reader for the body paragraphs.

B. Develop each paragraph more fully
Your body paragraphs are quite short. In an argumentative essay, each paragraph should usually contain:
a clear topic sentence,
explanation,
evidence or example,
connection back to the thesis.

For example, the banking paragraph introduces an important point, but it needs more explanation. How exactly do computers affect banking? Why does this mean people need computer knowledge? Explain the connection more clearly.

C. Improve coherence and transitions
Some ideas feel abrupt. For instance, “A vivid illustration of this can be found by examining the work” is not very natural in English. Also, the shift from workplace technology to banking could be smoother.

Use clearer transitions such as:
In the workplace,
Another important area is banking,
As a result,
Therefore,
In conclusion,

D. Use more formal and accurate wording
Some phrases are slightly awkward or imprecise. For example:
“the internet working of large corporations” → probably “the internal networks of large corporations” or “the operations of large corporations”
“put all of their inventory on computer” → “computerized their inventory system”
“viewed as a defecit” → “viewed as a liability” or “seen as unable to meet the company’s needs”

In academic writing, try to choose precise vocabulary rather than conversational phrasing.

Grammar and language issues

A. Spelling
There are several spelling errors:
twenthieth → twentieth
succesful → successful
auto-pasts → auto-parts
defecit → deficit

Be careful: spelling mistakes can weaken the credibility of an argument.

B. Articles and word choice
As a German speaker, you may find English article usage difficult. A few examples:
“on computer” → on a computer / using computers
“examining the work” → examining the workplace / looking at the workplace

C. Sentence structure and punctuation
Some sentences need commas or restructuring:
“Because of this hacking has become quite a problem.”  
  → “Because of this, hacking has become quite a problem.”

“People also need to be more aware of their accounting statements and check carefully each month to see no errors have been made.”  
  → “People also need to review their bank statements carefully each month to make sure no errors have been made.”

D. Verb tense and phrasing
Try to keep tense usage consistent and natural:
“Millions of dollars transfer from one bank to another.”  
  → “Millions of dollars are transferred from one bank to another every day.”

This passive form sounds more natural here.

Content suggestions for revision
To make the essay more convincing, add at least one more developed argument. For example:
education,
communication,
applying for jobs online,
accessing government or health services.

You can also strengthen your existing examples by explaining consequences more fully. For instance, in the workplace paragraph, you could discuss how technological change requires employees to update their skills continuously.

Possible revised structure
Introduction: importance of computers + clear thesis  
Body paragraph 1: computers in the workplace  
Body paragraph 2: computers in banking and personal finance  
Body paragraph 3: computers in daily life or education  
Conclusion: restate argument and emphasize lifelong learning

Final advice
Your essay has a solid basic idea, but it needs more development, clearer connections, and language correction. For revision, focus on:
strengthening the thesis,
expanding each body paragraph,
improving transitions,
correcting spelling and phrasing.

You already have the foundation of a good argumentative essay. With more detail and more careful editing, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: computers have brought many benefits, but they have also made society less personal and too dependent on machines. This is a strong argumentative idea because it presents both advantages and disadvantages while still showing your own opinion. The essay is easy to follow overall, and your examples about ATMs, banking, cars, and medical technology help make the topic concrete.

What is working well

Clear overall argument  
You consistently argue that computers improve efficiency but may reduce human interaction and independence. This gives the essay focus.

Good use of examples  
Examples such as ATMs, typewriters, and safety technology support your points and make the discussion more realistic.

Strong personal voice  
Phrases like “I feel” and “I fear” show engagement with the topic. This gives the essay energy and makes your position clear.

Main areas to improve

Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction presents the topic, but the main argument could be stated more directly and formally.

Current idea:
computers changed lives in good and bad ways

A stronger thesis could be:
Although computers have made modern life faster and more efficient, they have also reduced human interaction and increased people’s dependence on technology.

This version is clearer and sets up the structure of your body paragraphs.

Use a more formal academic style
Some phrases sound too conversational for an academic essay. For example:

“Not to mention…”
“EVERYTHING”
“a bunch of wires”
“I feel afraid”

Try using more formal wording:
Furthermore
many aspects of modern life
technology
I am concerned that

Also, avoid excessive capitalization for emphasis. In academic writing, emphasis should come from word choice, not capital letters.

Improve paragraph structure
Your body paragraphs have good ideas, but they would be stronger if each began with a clear topic sentence and developed one main point.

For example:

Paragraph 1: Benefits of computers  
efficiency  
cheaper access  
banking and daily tasks  
medical/safety improvements

Paragraph 2: Negative effects of computers  
reduced human interaction  
job loss  
overdependence on machines  
danger when technology fails

Right now, some ideas in the last paragraph are repeated or move too quickly from one point to another.

Be careful with grammar and word choice
There are several language problems, some of which may come from direct translation or spelling difficulties. Here are important corrections:

people's life styles → people’s lifestyles
has been quite a new invention → is still a relatively new invention
live transpire much quicker → life move much faster
We now have ATM → We now have ATMs
are able to proceed with the same transactions → are able to perform the same transactions
impersonalized → made the world more impersonal
Interraction → Interaction
obstical → obstacle
an on-coming obstical is approaching → an oncoming obstacle is near
comes many changes → come many changes
in some instances to function → in some instances, to function effectively

Check sentence punctuation
You sometimes separate ideas with commas where a full stop or no comma is needed.

Examples:
These changes resulting from the invention of the computer, have been, I feel both bad and good.  
Better: These changes resulting from the invention of the computer have been, in my view, both positive and negative.

While computers have brought about improvements, in my opinion, they also have "impersonalized" the world.  
Better: While computers have brought about many improvements, they have also made the world more impersonal.

Try not to interrupt sentences too often with commas around phrases like “I feel” or “in my opinion.”

Advice especially relevant for a German-speaking writer

A few sentences are understandable but slightly unnatural in English because of word choice or sentence structure. In English academic writing:
prefer simple, direct sentence structure
avoid very long sentences with many inserted phrases
use specific verbs like *perform, reduce, increase, improve* instead of more vague expressions

Also, English usually sounds stronger when you make the subject clear:
Computers have made daily tasks more efficient
rather than
We are able to complete our tasks much better

Suggested revision goals

Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Separate benefits and drawbacks more clearly in the body paragraphs.
Replace informal phrases with more academic vocabulary.
Correct spelling and grammar errors.
Add a short concluding sentence that restates your argument clearly.

Example of a stronger concluding idea

In conclusion, computers have greatly improved modern life by making communication, work, and services more efficient. However, society must be careful not to sacrifice human interaction and independent thinking in exchange for convenience.

Overall, your essay has a solid foundation and a clear argument. With more formal language, cleaner grammar, and a sharper structure, it could become a strong academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral purpose and a sincere voice. You communicate strong values—peace, equality, and respect for human difference—and this gives the piece emotional strength. You also choose an interesting angle: instead of naming a scientific invention, you argue that cultural awareness and the rejection of prejudice are among the most important developments of the 20th century. That is an original and thoughtful idea.

What is working well

Clear personal position  
   You make your opinion visible from the beginning: you value humanity and believe people should live in peace. This helps the reader understand your purpose.

Relevant topic  
   Your focus on racism, discrimination, and cultural learning fits the broad question of what has significantly influenced people’s lives.

Serious and reflective tone  
   The essay sounds engaged and sincere. It does not feel superficial.

Main areas to improve

Stronger thesis and focus
Your introduction says that “the most significant discovery or invention will depend on the individual,” but then you move to your own choice. This makes the thesis a little unclear. Try to state one direct argument.

For example:
Stronger thesis: *One of the most significant developments of the 20th century was the growth of cultural awareness and the rejection of prejudice, because it changed how societies understand equality, human dignity, and peaceful coexistence.*

This makes your argument more precise.

Clarify the link to “discovery or invention”
The prompt seems to ask for a “discovery or invention.” Your essay treats social progress as a kind of discovery. That can work, but you need to explain it more clearly. For instance, you could argue that the 20th century led people to “discover” the harmful effects of prejudice and the value of cultural diversity.

Without this explanation, some readers may feel you are not fully answering the question.

Organization and paragraph development
Your essay has good ideas, but the body paragraphs are quite broad. Try to develop each paragraph around one clear point.

A possible structure:
Paragraph 1: Introduce the argument and define what you mean by this “discovery.”
Paragraph 2: Explain how prejudice and discrimination were widely accepted in the past.
Paragraph 3: Show what changed in the 20th century (civil rights movements, women’s rights, anti-racist education, multicultural awareness).
Paragraph 4: Explain why this change is significant for humanity.
Conclusion: Restate the main argument.

Also, add specific examples. At the moment, the essay stays very general. Mentioning the civil rights movement, anti-apartheid struggles, or women’s rights would make your argument much stronger.

Sentence control and punctuation
Several sentences are too long or joined incorrectly with commas. This creates run-on sentences. For example:

*“The opportunity to learn about the vast world around us is opening up, the availability to study cultural difference and reflect the knowledge on ourselves is improving.”*

This should be divided into two sentences or joined more carefully.

Possible revision:
*The opportunity to learn about the vast world around us is increasing. The ability to study cultural differences and reflect on this knowledge is also improving.*

Word choice and grammar
There are a number of language errors, many of them involving spelling, word form, or common English confusions. Here are some important corrections:

world wide → worldwide
peoples lives → people’s lives
discriminitive → discriminatory
more then → more than
excepted → accepted
enought → enough
breed stereotypes → deeply rooted stereotypes  
  (“breed” is the wrong word here; perhaps you meant “bred” or “deep-rooted”)
burried → buried
forms are presently being held → probably forums are presently being held
avalibility → availability
cultural difference → cultural differences
existance → existence

A few phrases are also slightly unnatural in English:
*“reflect the knowledge on ourselves”* → reflect on this knowledge ourselves / apply this knowledge to ourselves

Style and precision
Some statements are very broad, for example:
*“racism is almost inherent in today’s society”*
*“prejudice and discrimination are not consciously learned but they must be unlearned”*

These are interesting ideas, but they need either explanation or more careful wording. Academic writing is stronger when claims are precise.

For example:
*Racist attitudes are often reinforced by social institutions, media, and education.*
*Because prejudice is learned through social experience, it can also be challenged and unlearned.*

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis that directly answers the prompt.
Explain why this counts as a discovery or major development.
Add specific historical examples.
Break long sentences into shorter, clearer ones.
Correct spelling and grammar errors carefully.

You have a meaningful central idea and a compassionate perspective. With clearer structure, more concrete support, and closer attention to language accuracy, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically strong.
