Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and some strong ideas. You argue that television changed people’s lives by making information immediate, connecting cultures, and providing entertainment that can teach lessons. These are good argumentative points, and your enthusiasm for the topic is clear. The next step is to make the essay more organized, precise, and grammatically controlled.

Strengths

Clear thesis/topic: You answer the prompt directly by choosing television and explaining why it matters.
Relevant arguments: You discuss news, global connection, and entertainment/learning. This gives your essay enough material.
Good critical thinking: You do not only praise television; you also mention limits such as distortion and glorified violence. This makes the essay more balanced.

Main areas to improve

A. Organization and paragraph structure
Your essay has good ideas, but some sentences are very long and contain several different points. This makes your argument harder to follow.

Try this structure:
Introduction: introduce television and your main claim.
Body paragraph 1: television spreads news instantly and affects public reaction.
Body paragraph 2: television connects cultures and helps create a “global village.”
Body paragraph 3: television entertains and can teach lessons, though it also has risks.
Conclusion: restate why television is important.

Also, begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence.

Example:
“One of television’s greatest contributions is the speed with which it delivers news.”
“Television has also helped connect people from different cultures.”
“In addition, television entertains people while also shaping their understanding of life.”

B. Sentence length and punctuation
This is the biggest language issue in your essay. Many sentences are run-on sentences: several ideas are joined with commas, dashes, or semicolons in ways that are not correct in formal academic English.

For example, this sentence is too long:
> “While previously people had to wait days... this allows them to make their own, informed judgment...”

Break long sentences into two or three shorter ones. This will make your ideas much stronger.

Also avoid:
& → use and
too many dashes
informal phrasing like well
repeated commas where a full stop is better

C. Style and academic tone
Your tone is generally thoughtful, but sometimes it becomes conversational:
“well, at least everybody who watches”
“people are going around acting like Superman”

These expressions are vivid, but they sound informal. In an academic essay, use a more neutral style.

For example:
“well, at least everybody who watches” → “at least all regular viewers”
“acting like Superman” → “confusing fiction with reality”

D. Grammar and wording
A few grammar and vocabulary choices need revision.

Articles and word choice
“An invention of the 20th century...” is possible, but “One invention of the 20th century...” sounds more natural.
“the instantaneous communication of ideas” is slightly awkward. Better: “the immediate transmission of ideas, images, and news.”

Prepositions and phrasing
“from across town all the way to Beijing” is understandable, but a bit informal.
“discuss ideas” is fine, but “exchange ideas” is more natural here.

Specific corrections
“this allows” after a comma should often begin a new sentence: “This allows...”
“a reaction is necessary- such as the gathering...” → punctuation problem. Better: “If a reaction is necessary, such as organizing opposition to a law, it can happen immediately.”
“speak & act” → speak and behave
“through television entertainment” → through television entertainment programs
“making life seem the good guy always wins” → making it seem as if the good guy always wins
“lives thru dangerous situations” → survives dangerous situations
“this sense of reality is not so distorted” is unclear. Maybe: “However, television does not usually distort reality so completely that viewers cannot distinguish fiction from real life.”

Development of argument
Some points would be stronger with more explanation or examples.

For instance:
When you say television allows people to react politically, give a concrete example.
When you say it connects cultures, explain how seeing foreign customs can reduce misunderstanding.
In the entertainment paragraph, explain more clearly how television teaches lessons.

At the moment, some ideas are interesting but not fully developed.

Suggested revision of your first sentence
Original:
> “An invention of the 20th century that I think has significantly changed people's lives is television.”

Improved:
> “One invention of the twentieth century that has significantly changed people’s lives is television.”

Final advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Shorten long sentences
Use formal punctuation and avoid “&”
Organize each paragraph around one main idea
Make your examples more specific
Keep an academic tone

You already have a solid foundation and a clear argument. If you improve the structure and sentence control, this will become a much more persuasive and polished essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: the microwave was a revolutionary invention that changed cooking and modern life. You also use an extended sea/storm metaphor at the beginning, which shows creativity and confidence. The main task now is to make your argument more precise, more academic, and grammatically cleaner.

Strengths

Clear thesis and opinion: You strongly argue that the microwave transformed food preparation.
Good sense of structure: Introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion are all present.
Strong engagement with the topic: The essay has energy and a persuasive tone.
Good use of comparison: You contrast traditional cooking with microwave cooking effectively.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the argument more academic and specific
At the moment, the essay relies heavily on dramatic language (“storm,” “marvel,” “slaving in the kitchen,” “bewitched”) and broad claims (“revolutionized life”). In an argumentative essay, you need more precise reasoning and, ideally, examples or evidence.

For example, instead of:
“Never before has such an invention revolutionized a science like this.”

You could write:
“Few kitchen inventions have changed everyday food preparation as dramatically as the microwave oven.”

Also, some claims are too absolute:
“All these things had one thing in common: FIRE.”
This is effective rhetorically, but your essay would be stronger if you explained why that matters:
“Earlier cooking methods relied on external heat sources such as fire, gas, or electricity, whereas microwaves heat food internally by exciting water molecules.”

B. Improve paragraph development
Each paragraph should have:
a clear topic sentence,
explanation,
supporting example,
link back to the thesis.

Your second body paragraph is your strongest because it focuses on time-saving and convenience. Develop it further with examples:
reheating leftovers,
preparing simple meals quickly,
safer use for children compared with open flames.

C. Use a more formal tone
Some expressions are too informal or exaggerated for academic writing:
“they would have thought you crazy”
“under the influence of heavy drugs”
“slaving in the kitchen”

Replace them with more neutral language:
“they would likely have considered it impossible”
“it reduced the amount of time required for meal preparation”

Language corrections

There are many grammar, spelling, and word-choice problems. Below are some important ones.

Spelling / word form
culunary → *culinary*
consistant → *consistent*
Progress has move slow → *progress has moved slowly*
rythm → *rhythm*
showed little changed → *shown little change*
twentyth → *twentieth*
comodity → *commodity*
dizzing → *dizzying*
differents perspectives → *different perspectives*

Grammar
“the constant rhythm of the waves have showed”  
  → “the constant rhythm of the waves has shown”
“As History progressed”  
  → “As history progressed”  
  (*history* is not usually capitalized)
“Modern times saw gas and electric ranges as to staple”  
  → “In modern times, gas and electric ranges became staples.”
“always used to prepare the fool of mankind”  
  → “always used to prepare the food of mankind”
“A mother who works, can come home can whip up something”  
  → “A working mother can come home and prepare something”
“The microwave is excellent for re-heating making it easier”  
  → “The microwave is excellent for reheating, making it easier”

Articles and phrasing
German speakers often have difficulty with articles and sentence flow in English. Watch for phrases like:
“at the dawn of time” (acceptable, but very dramatic)
“in the 20th century” (good)
“essential nowadays” → better: “essential in modern life” or “an essential household appliance today”

Style and word choice

Your opening metaphor is interesting, but it is also quite long. Because of that, the main idea arrives late. Consider shortening it so that your thesis becomes clear sooner.

For example:
“For centuries, cooking methods changed only gradually. However, in the twentieth century, the invention of the microwave oven transformed the way food is prepared.”

This is simpler and more direct.

Also, be careful with repetition:
You repeat “Throughout History” and “break from tradition” ideas several times.
Try to vary the wording and add new points instead.

Suggested revised thesis

A stronger thesis could be:
“The microwave oven revolutionized cooking because it introduced a completely new heating method, reduced cooking time, and made food preparation more convenient for modern households.”

This thesis is clear and gives you three body paragraph ideas.

Final advice

When revising, focus on:
clarity before drama,
formal academic tone,
specific supporting points,
careful proofreading for spelling and grammar.

You have a strong central idea and an engaging voice. If you make the language more precise and the argument more structured, this essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a personal, engaging voice. The example of your family and the story about your great-uncle make the writing relatable and help support your argument. You also show awareness of an opposing view, which is an important feature of argumentative writing. For revision, focus on making your main argument clearer, organizing the essay more strongly, and correcting grammar and word-choice issues.

Strengthen the thesis and overall argument
At the moment, the essay discusses microwaves, but the main claim is not stated strongly enough. The first sentence is very broad (“many advances in science and modern technology”), while the rest of the essay focuses only on one appliance.

This gives the reader a clear argument to expect.

Improve organization
Your essay currently reads more like a short personal reflection than a fully developed argumentative essay. A clearer structure would help:

Suggested structure
Introduction
General topic: technology and daily life
Narrow focus: microwave ovens

Body paragraph 1
Main point: microwaves save time in busy households
Evidence: both parents work; family members share cooking duties

Body paragraph 2
Main point: microwaves make food preparation easier
Evidence: vegetables prepared in advance and cooked quickly

Body paragraph 3
Counterargument: some people think microwaves are unnecessary
Example: great-uncle’s opinion
Response: although not everyone likes them, they are very useful for many families

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Brief final comment on why microwaves remain important

Adding a short conclusion is especially important. Right now the essay ends with the anecdote, but it should end by reinforcing your argument.

Develop the counterargument more effectively
It is good that you include another perspective. However, the uncle’s story should support a larger point. After presenting his opinion, explain why you still disagree, for example:

This turns the anecdote into an argument rather than just a story.

There are several grammar problems, many of which are common for learners of English. Here are some important ones:

Missing apostrophes
peoples lives → people’s lives
left overs → leftovers
they' re → they’re

Verb forms
we each take turns cook → we each take turns cooking / we take turns cooking
who are in their late eighties recieved → who are in their late eighties received

Double forms / repetition
the point is, is that → the point is that

Article and phrase choice
in the “average” family for both parents to work → in the average family, both parents work
take the time out → take the time
addition to their household is understandable, but appliance in their household or part of their household sounds more natural

Improve punctuation
You often need commas after introductory phrases and between clauses. For example:

For instance, a microwave is a brilliant addition...
In today’s society, it is common in the average family for both parents to work.
On the other hand, there are people...
In fact, I have a little story...

Also avoid unnecessary spaces, such as before punctuation or inside contractions.

Some phrases are too conversational for an argumentative essay:
brilliant addition
I have a little story to tell
the long and short of the story
pop them into the microwave

These are vivid, but in academic writing you should sound slightly more formal. For example:
a useful appliance
The following example illustrates this point
In conclusion
place them in the microwave

Spelling and word choice
Watch for spelling errors:
recieved → received
micro-wave → usually microwave

writing a strong thesis,
organizing ideas into clear paragraphs,
adding a conclusion,
making the style more formal,
correcting grammar and punctuation.

Your essay has a clear opinion, a sensible topic, and several relevant examples. You explain why ATM machines are useful in daily life, especially for convenience, safety, and saving time. What works well

Clear argument

Practical examples

Awareness of counterargument

Main areas to improve

Strengthen the thesis and focus

Your introduction gives your opinion, but the thesis could be more direct and academically phrased. At the moment, the first sentence is long and slightly awkward.

For example, instead of:
“An invention of the 20th century which I feel has significantly changed people's lives is the introduction of Bank-cash machines or Automatic teller machines.”

You could write:
“One of the most important inventions of the twentieth century is the automated teller machine (ATM), because it has made access to money faster, safer, and more convenient.”

This version is clearer and also introduces the main reasons you will discuss.

Improve organization

Your essay has good ideas, but the structure can be clearer. Try this simple argumentative structure:

Introduction: introduce the topic and thesis
Body paragraph 1: convenience and access in emergencies
Body paragraph 2: time-saving and flexible banking hours
Body paragraph 3: safety and reduced need to carry cash
Body paragraph 4: acknowledge counterargument and respond
Conclusion: restate your view and summarize main points

This would help your essay feel more planned and persuasive.

Use a more formal academic tone

Some phrases are too personal or conversational for an academic essay, for example:
“I really appreciate my ATM card”
“I do not know what I would do without my card”

These are understandable, but in academic writing it is usually better to sound less emotional and more analytical.

For example:
“In such situations, ATM cards are especially useful.”
“For these reasons, ATMs have become an essential part of modern life.”

Grammar and word choice

There are several language issues, many of which may come from German-English differences. Here are important ones:

Articles and compounds
“Bank-cash machines” is not idiomatic English. Use “bank machines,” “cash machines,” or “automated teller machines (ATMs).”
“ATM machines” is repetitive because M already means machine. Write “ATMs” or “ATM services.”

Word choice
“without this convenience at access to money” → better: “without this convenient access to money”
“assigned banking hours” → better: “regular banking hours”
“appear in all areas of the World” → better: “can be found throughout the world”
“This entices robbers and can promote danger.” → better: “Keeping large amounts of cash may attract thieves and increase the risk of robbery.”

Spelling and punctuation
“can not” → usually “cannot”
“every day life” → “everyday life” as an adjective
Be careful with extra spaces after periods.
“1900's” is better written as “1900s”

Develop your ideas more fully

Some points are introduced but not explained enough. For example, you mention job loss in banks, but then you immediately move on. Try to explain why the benefits still outweigh the disadvantages.

Example:
“Although some people argue that ATMs reduce employment opportunities in banks, they also allow banks to offer faster service and make basic financial transactions available outside normal business hours. As a result, the overall benefit to society is substantial.”

This makes your argument more balanced and convincing.

Make connections between sentences smoother

Use linking expressions to guide the reader:
First, In addition, For example, Furthermore, However, Therefore, In conclusion

These connectors will improve flow and coherence.

Suggested revision of one sentence

Original:
“The development of computerized technology has enhanced our environment providing convenience and time saving activities which reduce the stress of our every day life.”

Possible revision:
“Overall, computerized technology has improved daily life by making routine tasks more convenient and less time-consuming, thereby reducing everyday stress.”

This version is more concise and natural.

Final advice

Your essay already has a solid central idea and useful examples. For revision, focus on:
writing a sharper thesis,
organizing the essay into clearer paragraphs,
using more formal academic language,
correcting word choice and grammar,
developing each reason more fully.

You have good argumentative instincts. With clearer structure and more precise English, this could become a strong essay.

Your essay has a clear topic, a recognizable opinion, and a lively personal voice. Paragraph 2: benefits (convenience, emergencies, travel)

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear topic, a formal tone, and a consistent focus on the debate over teaching evolution and creation together. You also show strong engagement with sources and attempt to present both sides before defending one position. That is a good foundation. For revision, the main areas to improve are clarity of argument, use of evidence, organization, and language accuracy.

Main argument and thesis

Your position is visible, but the thesis is too long and not precise enough. In the introduction, you ask several rhetorical questions and only later state your claim. Try to make your thesis direct and specific.

For example, instead of:
“Those who support a two-model approach… present a strong, effective argument…”

you could write:
“This essay argues that supporters of the two-model approach present persuasive arguments about critical thinking, fairness, and student engagement; however, their case is weakened by legal and scientific concerns.”

This kind of thesis is clearer because it tells the reader exactly what the essay will do.

Organization and paragraph structure

At the moment, many paragraphs are long and include several ideas at once. This makes the essay harder to follow. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Argument 1: why supporters favor teaching both  
Argument 2: questioning accepted ideas / critical thinking  
Evidence and studies  
Counterarguments: legal, scientific, and educational objections  
Evaluation: why your side is stronger or where it is limited  
Conclusion

Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence. For example:
“Supporters of the two-model approach argue that studying both theories encourages critical thinking.”

Also, some transitions are confusing. For instance, “The opposition (two-model approach) claim…” is unclear because earlier the two-model supporters are not the opposition. Be careful to keep terms consistent:
supporters of the two-model approach
opponents of the two-model approach
evolutionists
creationists

Use of evidence

This is your biggest issue. Many quotations are missing and replaced by “”. Because of this, some claims seem unsupported. In an academic essay, every major claim needs evidence.

Also, some evidence is presented uncritically. For example, the reference to Darwin, Newton, and Einstein sounds impressive, but it does not automatically prove that creationism should be taught in science class. Ask yourself:
Does this example directly support my argument?
Is the source reliable?
Does the evidence prove the point, or only suggest it?

The Bliss study is potentially useful, but you should explain it more carefully:
Who conducted it?
When?
How many students were involved?
What exactly was measured?
Was the study unbiased?

Since you mention “Bergman’s book,” you should cite it properly and evaluate whether it is an objective academic source.

Critical thinking and balance

You clearly want to defend the two-model approach, but the essay sometimes sounds too one-sided. Phrases such as “excellent reasoning,” “incredible claims,” or “real evidence” are too evaluative. In academic writing, it is better to sound analytical rather than enthusiastic.

Instead of:
“their argument becomes increasingly weak due to excellent reasoning”

try:
“their counterarguments are less convincing because supporters of the two-model approach emphasize critical inquiry and exposure to multiple perspectives.”

Also, be careful with logical assumptions. For example, questioning accepted theories is important in science, but that does not mean all alternative explanations belong in science curricula. This is exactly the point your opponents would raise, so you should address it directly.

Style and academic tone

Your style is generally formal, but rhetorical questions are overused in the introduction. In academic argumentation, statements are usually stronger than questions.

For example:
“isn’t the classroom the most effective place…?”
could become
“The classroom is often presented as the most effective place to examine competing explanations of human origins.”

Try to avoid emotional or exaggerated language such as:
“infamous equation”
“surprise”
“incredible claims”

These expressions sound informal or biased.

Grammar and language points

Your writing is understandable, but there are repeated grammar and style problems. Here are important ones to revise:

Word choice / collocation
“pondered … this” → pondered this question
“where as” → whereas
“a two model approach” → a two-model approach
“fro ma” → from a
“labelled the genuine places of inquiry” → awkward; better: seen as places of inquiry

Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and should be divided. For example:
“Those advocating a two model approach use different methods of reasoning to support the argument.”
This is fine, but later sentences become overloaded with clauses. Shorter sentences will improve clarity.

Agreement and pronouns
“many of the world’s population are students” → many people in the world are students
“the student… she/he” → students… they

Capitalization / punctuation
“What about attempts made by teachers to indoctrinate students? the opposition asks.”
→ “What about attempts made by teachers to indoctrinate students?” the opposition asks.

What to do next

For your revision, I recommend these priorities:

Add the missing quotations and full citations.
Rewrite the thesis so it is direct and specific.
Improve paragraph structure with one main idea per paragraph.
Use a more neutral academic tone.
Check grammar, especially sentence length, word choice, and consistency of terms.

You have a solid starting point and a clear interest in the topic. With stronger evidence, clearer structure, and more careful academic style, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Essay about Euthanasia

Your essay raises an important and difficult ethical issue, and you clearly try to engage with different viewpoints before stating your own position. That is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. You also show awareness that euthanasia involves medical, legal, moral, and religious dimensions, which gives your topic depth.

Main strength: engagement with the issue
A positive feature of your essay is that you do not treat euthanasia as a simple “yes/no” question. You discuss:
personal choice,
moral beliefs,
legal uncertainty,
religious values,
public opinion.

This shows thoughtful engagement. Your personal voice is also clear, especially in the beginning and end.

Main problem: your thesis is unclear and inconsistent
Your biggest revision priority is to make your position clear and consistent.

At several points, you seem to support euthanasia:
“an individual should be allowed the freedom…”
“that should be the patient’s personal decision”

But elsewhere, you seem to oppose it:
“to allow mercy killing to be a phenomenon would be outrageous”
“It is not society’s choice to decide when he or she has had enough of life”

Because of this, the reader cannot tell exactly what you are arguing.

What to do
Write one precise thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

If you support limited euthanasia:  
  *Although euthanasia raises serious moral concerns, competent adults who are suffering from incurable and unbearable pain should have the right to choose it under strict legal and medical regulation.*

If you oppose euthanasia:  
  *Although euthanasia may seem compassionate, it should not be legalized because it conflicts with moral principles and creates dangerous social and medical consequences.*

Everything in the essay should then support that thesis.

Organization and paragraph structure
Right now, the essay feels repetitive and sometimes unfocused. Several paragraphs discuss “values” in a general way without developing one clear point.

Try this structure
Introduction  
   Define euthanasia, explain the controversy, present your thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: Arguments in favor  
   Personal autonomy, relief from suffering, dignity.

Body Paragraph 2: Arguments against  
   Moral/religious objections, risk of abuse, role of doctors.

Body Paragraph 3: Legal/social example  
   Dr. Kevorkian or another case, and explain how it supports your position.

Conclusion  
   Restate your argument clearly and explain why it matters.

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and focus on one main idea.

Use of evidence
You mention articles and cases, but often the references are vague:
“In many of my articles…”
“In my article ‘The Last Goodbye’…”
“In my article ‘Prolifers Say Cruzan Death…’...”

The reader needs more explanation. What exactly did the article argue? How does that evidence support your claim?

Also, there are several missing parts marked with , which interrupt meaning. These must be completed or removed.

Style and academic tone
Some expressions are too informal or imprecise for academic writing:
“spark up the issue”
“useless to society”
“making this a habit”

Try more formal wording:
“raise the issue”
“is sometimes perceived as lacking social contribution”
“normalize the practice”

Also avoid overusing phrases like “I feel” and “I think.” In academic argument, stronger phrasing is often better:
instead of *I think euthanasia should be restricted*, write  
  *Euthanasia should be strictly regulated.*

Grammar and language issues
Because your first language is German, some sentence patterns may transfer directly into English. Focus especially on:

a) Articles
“an complex issue” → a complex issue
“with ethic and human rights” → with ethics and human rights

b) Word choice
“Opposers” → Opponents
“tested competent” → found competent
“death process” → means of ending life / end-of-life procedure

c) Possessives
“someone else life” → someone else’s life
“patients personal decision” → patient’s personal decision

d) Sentence form
“I, also think” → I also think
“than that should be” → then that should be

e) Capitalization and spelling
“Euthanasia” does not need a capital letter in the middle of a sentence.
Check spelling: Kevorkian, ethical, substitute, sympathetic, pro-life.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these three priorities:
Decide exactly what your argument is.
Reorganize the essay so each paragraph has one clear purpose.
Improve grammar and remove incomplete sentences.

You have a meaningful topic and several promising ideas. With a clearer thesis, stronger structure, and more precise language, your essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the case against capital punishment is stronger than the case in favor of it. This gives the essay a strong overall direction. You also show awareness of counterarguments, which is important in argumentative writing. Below are the main areas to improve.

Strengths

Clear thesis: You state early that the anti-death-penalty argument is “stronger and more sensible.”
Good structure at paragraph level: You move from arguments in favor of capital punishment to arguments against it.
Engagement with opposing views: This strengthens your credibility because you do not ignore the other side.
Strong vocabulary: Words such as *deterrence, sanctity, premeditated,* and *advocates* suit the topic well.

Most important revision priority: use evidence

The biggest problem is that many quotations are missing: “” appears several times. At the moment, this makes your argument incomplete. If you refer to Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, and Reiman, you must include:

the exact quotation or a clear paraphrase
proper citation
explanation of how the source supports your point

Do not just insert a quotation and move on. Use the pattern:

Claim → Evidence → Explanation

Example:
Claim: The death penalty is not clearly a better deterrent than life imprisonment.
Evidence: A study/source argues that murder rates do not consistently fall in places that use execution.
Explanation: This weakens the deterrence argument because if executions were uniquely effective, we would expect clearer statistical results.

Your essay currently relies too much on assertion (“it is a known fact,” “it is clear,” “simply impossible”) without enough proof. In academic writing, avoid presenting debatable claims as facts unless you can support them.

Make your argument more academic and less emotional

You use strong emotional language, for example:

“heinous act”
“barbaric”
“society simply craves violence”
“people need to wake up”

This language shows passion, but in academic argumentation it can make the essay sound biased. Try to use a more measured tone. For example:

Instead of “society simply craves violence”, write “support for capital punishment may partly reflect a desire for retribution.”
Instead of “people need to wake up”, write “this suggests that imprisonment may achieve the same practical goal without taking a life.”

A calmer tone will make your argument more persuasive.

Strengthen logic and avoid overgeneralization

Some claims are too broad:

“Anyone who is placed in their shoes would wish for the death penalty…”
“for many violent criminals there simply is no fear”
“society and the government have no right to take the life of another man”

These are absolute statements. In academic writing, absolute language is risky unless proven. Use more careful phrasing:

many family members may support
some violent offenders may not be strongly deterred
critics argue that the state should not have the authority

This is especially important because strong claims need strong evidence.

Improve paragraph unity

Some paragraphs begin with one point and then shift into another. For example, the paragraph on the sanctity of life also discusses rage, revenge, equality of lives, and the role of government. Try to give each paragraph one main focus.

A possible structure:

Introduction and thesis  
Retribution argument in favor  
Deterrence argument in favor  
Sanctity of life argument against  
Premeditation / moral contradiction  
Rebuttal of deterrence using evidence  
Conclusion

This will make the essay easier to follow.

Style and grammar notes

Your English is generally strong, but there are a few patterns to revise:

Word choice:  
  - “highest sentence” → harshest penalty or most severe punishment
Prepositions:  
  - “Opponents to capital punishment” → Opponents of capital punishment
Pronoun agreement / clarity:  
  - “When society takes the life of a criminal, they…”  
    Better: when society takes the life of a criminal, it…
Repetition:  
  You repeat “value of human life” many times. That phrase is important, but vary it sometimes: human dignity, sanctity of life, worth of life.
Sentence clarity:  
  “The highest sentence that can be enforced…” sounds unnatural. More natural:  
  The most severe punishment in the American judicial system is the death penalty.

Conclusion

You have a solid foundation and a clear stance, but to become a strong academic essay, you need to:
add real evidence and complete citations,
reduce emotional and absolute language,
make your reasoning more precise,
improve paragraph focus.

Your main idea is clear and potentially persuasive. With stronger evidence and a more formal tone, this essay could become much more convincing.

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that affirmative action is still necessary, and you try to strengthen that claim by considering opposing views. This is a good basis for an argumentative essay. You also show awareness that the topic is complex and that definitions matter. Below are the main areas to revise.

Strengthen the thesis and overall focus

“I feel that legislation… is necessary…”
“affirmative action is still necessary”

Try one clear thesis sentence instead, for example:
“Although affirmative action is controversial, it remains necessary because racial discrimination continues to shape access to education and employment, and legal protections are still needed to counter that inequality.”

Also, the essay shifts between two purposes:
arguing that affirmative action is necessary
explaining how advocates could improve their arguments

Both are interesting, but together they weaken the focus. Choose one as your main purpose, or clearly separate them.

Improve organization and paragraph structure
Many paragraphs begin with a useful topic, but the internal logic is sometimes unclear. Revise so that each paragraph has:
one main idea
evidence or example
explanation of how it supports your thesis

For instance, the paragraph beginning “Opponents argue that blacks will never experience true equality…” includes opponents’ claims, your response, and social commentary. Consider splitting it into:
one paragraph summarizing the objection
one paragraph rebutting it

Your transitions can also be more formal. “Now that we have established…” sounds conversational and assumes agreement. Instead use:

Use evidence more effectively
A major problem is that several quotations are incomplete, shown as . In a final essay, this is not acceptable. Every quotation must be fully included, introduced, and cited. Also, your argument often makes broad claims without concrete support.

For revision:
add complete quotations or paraphrases from sources
explain each source after using it
include specific examples, legal cases, or statistics if available

For example, instead of saying opponents claim reverse discrimination, you could briefly refer to a court case or a specific public debate. Evidence will make your argument much more persuasive.

You sometimes make claims that are too broad or absolute. For example:
“the only way to guarantee they get into the system is through affirmative action programs”
“without laws to insure a certain number of minorities be hired, many would never be hired”

These are strong claims and need proof. Also, saying “the only way” is risky unless you can fully defend it.

Similarly, when you summarize opponents, be careful not to oversimplify them. The sentence about blacks needing to “get out of jail, stope using and selling drugs…” sounds like a very extreme version of the opposing side. If you include such claims, you must show where they come from and explain them fairly.

Examples:
“nearly infallible” is too strong
“flames and rage” is dramatic for an academic opening
“how should we do it then?” is too conversational

Aim for a more formal tone:
“If racism persists, the policy question becomes which measures are most effective in reducing inequality.”

Examples:
“1960's” → 1960s
“the tents of affirmative action” → the tenets of affirmative action
“achie ved” → achieved
“in regards to” → with regard to
“to insure” → to ensure
“writer's need” → writers need
“stope” → stop
“Beside the fact” → Besides the fact
“raise their morals” probably should be improve their lives or raise their educational and social prospects
“expose both blacks and whites to one another's culture” → better: promote greater mutual understanding between Black and white communities

Also check subject-verb agreement and sentence structure. For example:
“what affirmative action and whether…” → what affirmative action is and whether…

Black Americans
white people
minority groups, where appropriate

Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear point.
Edit for formal tone and grammar.

You have a strong topic and a clear commitment to your position. With a sharper thesis, better evidence, and more careful organization, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a consistent purpose: you argue that the case against teaching New Age beliefs is stronger than the case for it. Your essay also shows engagement with sources and an attempt to compare arguments using criteria such as values, consequences, credibility, and evidence. That is a strong starting point. For revision, the main issues are focus, academic tone, organization, and use of evidence.

Clarify your central argument

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph, but it is too long and tries to do too many things at once. It also mixes your conclusion with a list of reasons before the essay has explained them.

Current thesis idea:
the case against teaching New Age beliefs is stronger
because it uses values, consequences, firsthand accounts, mainstream sources, and facts

This can be made much clearer. For example:
“The argument against teaching New Age beliefs is more persuasive than the argument in favor because it relies on more credible sources, stronger evidence, and clearer appeals to values and consequences.”

Also, define exactly what you mean by “teaching New Age beliefs.” Do you mean in public schools? universities? society in general? religious education? This is important because your essay sometimes sounds like it is about schools, but sometimes about public opinion more generally.

Improve organization

Your essay needs a more logical structure. Right now, some paragraphs move suddenly from one point to another.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction
   - introduce topic
   - define “New Age”
   - present thesis

Problems with arguments in favor
   - weak definition
   - lack of evidence
   - questionable claims / credibility issues

Strengths of arguments against
   - use of consequences
   - use of testimonies
   - broader circulation of sources

Evaluation of evidence
   - compare source quality
   - explain why some evidence is stronger than others

Conclusion
   - restate claim
   - summarize key reasons

Make sure each body paragraph has one main point. Begin with a topic sentence, then explain it, then use evidence.

Use a more academic tone

Some expressions are conversational and weaken your authority as a writer. Examples:

“Yoda, that little green guy…”
“I honestly ran out of my room…”
“stupendous job”
“for all I know”

These sound informal and personal. In academic writing, use a more neutral tone. For instance:

Instead of:
“I honestly ran out of my room…”
write:
“This claim appeared doubtful and led me to question the author’s credibility.”

Also avoid exaggeration and emotional language such as “very true” or “fairytales.” Academic argument works better when it sounds measured and precise.

Be careful with evidence and bias

This is the most important issue. You often judge sources by whether they agree with Christianity rather than by whether they are reliable, balanced, and well supported.

For example, you argue that anti–New Age sources are stronger because they refer to the Bible and appear in Christian magazines. But in an academic essay, you need to explain why those sources are credible for your audience. Simply saying the Bible is true or that a Christian magazine is popular is not enough as proof.

Similarly, saying that a source is weak because one statement seems false is not enough unless you verify that it is false with evidence.

To strengthen your essay:
evaluate sources by authority, evidence, logic, and accuracy
distinguish between religious belief and academic proof
include more direct analysis of the sources’ arguments, not only your reaction to them

Develop quotations properly

Several places in the essay show “”, which suggests missing quotations. This creates major gaps in the argument. Every quotation should be:
introduced
cited
explained afterward

Do not assume the quotation speaks for itself. After each source, explain:
what the source claims
how it supports your point
why it is convincing or not convincing

Sentence-level language issues

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are grammar and style problems that should be revised.

Articles and capitalization
“new age” / “New Age” → choose one form and use it consistently
“the teaching of New Age ideas” → repeated too often; vary wording

Word choice
“the te aching” → typo
“one authors opinion” → one author’s opinion
“can not” → usually cannot
“many time made reference” → many times made reference

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and hard to follow, for example:
“However, the United States being the big melting pot it has claimed to be, everyone must be pleased at the same time.”
This needs rephrasing for clarity.

Paragraph coherence
Use transitions more carefully. Sometimes “however,” “moreover,” or “on the other hand” do not match the logic of the sentence.

Main revision priorities

Narrow the topic: define what “teaching New Age beliefs” means.
Rewrite the thesis so it is shorter and clearer.
Remove informal language and personal anecdotes unless directly relevant.
Use complete, documented quotations instead of placeholders.
Judge sources academically, not only according to personal belief.
Improve paragraph structure with one clear point per paragraph.

Final comment

You have the basis of an argument, and your position is clear. The next step is to turn this from a personal response into a stronger academic essay by improving structure, tone, and source analysis. If you revise with those goals in mind, your paper will become much more persuasive.

Your essay has a clear overall position: you argue that proponents of nuclear power are more persuasive because they use evidence, while opponents rely more on fear and public values. This is a strong argumentative basis. The essay also has a recognizable structure: introduction, discussion of opponents, discussion of proponents, and conclusion. That said, to revise effectively, you should focus on thesis clarity, paragraph organization, evidence use, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Clear topic and stance: The reader understands that the essay is about arguments for and against nuclear power.
Logical broad structure: You move from introduction to opposing view to supporting view.
Awareness of rhetoric: You notice an important distinction between emotional appeals and factual appeals.
Some effective comparisons: For example, comparing nuclear risks with risks from coal mining helps develop your argument.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction is dramatic, but the thesis is too long and somewhat unclear. Try to state exactly what your essay will prove.

Current idea:
Opponents appeal to values and beliefs.
Proponents use facts and figures more effectively.

A stronger thesis could be:
“Although opponents of nuclear power appeal effectively to public fears about safety, proponents present the stronger argument because they support their claims with evidence about cost, safety, environmental impact, and waste management.”

This version is clearer, more concise, and gives the essay a roadmap.

B. Improve paragraph unity
Several body paragraphs become too broad and include many different ideas. Each paragraph should have:
a topic sentence,
evidence/examples,
explanation of how that evidence supports your thesis.

For example, your paragraph on safety also discusses the media at length. Ask yourself: *Is this paragraph about safety, or about media influence?* If both, split it into two paragraphs or remove the less relevant part.

C. Use evidence more carefully
You mention percentages and claims such as nuclear power being cheaper or safer, but you do not cite any sources. In academic writing, unsupported statistics weaken your credibility.

For revision:
If this is based on a source text, refer to it clearly.
If you cannot verify a number, either remove it or present the idea more generally.
Avoid claims like “there is nothing to worry about,” which sound too absolute.

Also, be careful not to overstate. For example, saying nuclear plants run “completely environmentally safe” is too strong. A more academic phrasing would be:
“Supporters argue that nuclear plants produce far less air pollution during operation than fossil-fuel plants.”

D. Use a more objective tone
Some phrases sound emotional or biased:
“closed minded”
“beat the nuclear waste issue to death”
“flat out”
“blowing issues out of proportion”

In academic argumentation, use more neutral language:
“skeptical”
“emphasize heavily”
“strongly”
“sometimes exaggerating risks”

This will make your essay sound more credible and scholarly.

Language and grammar issues

Because your first language is German, some sentence structures suggest direct translation or patterns that are less natural in English. Focus especially on these areas:

A. Word choice
“do to” → “due to”
“analyzation” → “analysis”
“counter act” → “counteract”
“then” (comparison) → “than”
“took” → “taken”
“radio active” → “radioactive”
“rail road” → “railroad”
“meggawatts” → “megawatts”

B. Sentence boundaries / comma splices
Example:
“Thus, a large group of opponents to nuclear power are formed, they fear this new entity...”
This joins two independent clauses incorrectly.

Better:
“Thus, a large group of opponents to nuclear power is formed. They fear this new entity...”

Also note:
“a large group … is formed” not “are formed” because *group* is singular.

C. Articles and phrasing
“has strength to” → “has the शक्ति to” / “has enough force to”
“the fact around nuclear power” → “the facts about nuclear power”
“the amount lives saved” → “the number of lives saved”
“today’ world” → “today’s world”

D. Awkward or unclear sentences
One sentence in the waste paragraph is incomplete:
“Though simple, easy-to-understand facts such as:  the average American can easily relate to the amount of waste being produced.”
This needs to be rewritten completely. It currently looks like a draft note remained in the essay.

Content and logic
A few claims need more nuance:
Nuclear power is not really “new”; your wording suggests a much earlier historical context.
The link to the Persian Gulf War feels underdeveloped. Either explain it more clearly or remove it.
Be careful when discussing nuclear bombs in an essay about nuclear energy. The opening may attract attention, but it also confuses the distinction between weapons and civilian energy.

You might begin instead with the public controversy around nuclear energy itself.

Suggested revision plan
Rewrite the introduction with a focused thesis.
Give each body paragraph one clear main idea: cost, safety, environmental impact, waste.
Remove emotional or exaggerated wording.
Check every statistic and example.
Edit for grammar, especially comma splices, word choice, and comparison forms (*than* vs. *then*).

Overall, your essay shows good argumentative potential and a clear sense of debate. With a more precise thesis, stronger paragraph control, and careful language editing, it can become much more persuasive and academic.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows several strengths: you address a complex topic, consider both benefits and drawbacks of school integration, and include both research-based points and personal experience. This gives your argument depth. For revision, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, using evidence more precisely, and revising language for academic tone and correctness.

Main argument and thesis
Your introduction gives historical background, but your main claim is still too vague. The last sentence says the debate involves “value and consequence,” but it does not clearly state your position.

What to revise:
End the introduction with a clear argumentative thesis.
Make sure each body paragraph supports that thesis.

Possible thesis:
“Although school integration can improve academic opportunities and interracial understanding, its success depends on early implementation, supportive school environments, and strong community commitment.”

This would fit the points you make later.

Organization and paragraph focus
The essay is generally structured logically:
history/background  
academic benefits  
possible psychological/educational harms  
4–5. personal experience with race relations  
community influence  

However, some paragraphs become too broad. For example, the paragraph beginning “Once people started to get to the point…” contains many different ideas: friendship, age differences, discipline, self-segregation, and racial tension. This should be split into two paragraphs.

Suggestion:
One paragraph on how integration initially improved contact.
Another on how tensions increased when more students entered at older ages.

Also, use stronger topic sentences so the reader immediately understands the paragraph’s main point.

Use of evidence
You refer to studies, books, and a thesis, which is good, but some evidence is incomplete because of the marked gaps . Also, some sources are mentioned too generally:
“a study done by two researchers from New York”
“a writer for the New York Times Magazine”

In academic writing, you should identify sources more specifically if possible and explain how the evidence supports your point.

What to revise:
Fill in the missing quotations or references.
Introduce sources clearly.
After each source, explain its significance.

For example, after mentioning that black students performed better in formerly all-white schools, add one sentence explaining why this supports integration: better resources, expectations, or learning conditions.

Balance and fairness in argument
You do well to present both sides. However, some parts of the essay risk sounding too generalizing, especially in the discussion of black students’ behavior. For example:

“many of them were rebellious”
“Many black students took advantage...”
“This led to abuse of the rules...”

These statements are very broad and may weaken your credibility because they seem based on personal observation rather than careful evidence. In an academic argument, avoid making claims about large groups unless you have strong support.

How to improve:
Use more cautious language: “some students,” “in my experience,” “certain conflicts emerged.”
Separate your observations from broader conclusions.
Make clear that community and institutional conditions, not race itself, shaped these tensions.

This will make your argument more precise and fair.

Academic tone
Your personal experience is valuable, especially in the section on Chapter 220, but the essay sometimes shifts from formal academic style to conversational or emotional language.

Examples:
“infested with crime and drugs”
“in a frenzy”
“I can verify”
“220 kids”

These phrases sound informal or overly loaded.

Better alternatives:
“affected by crime and drug activity”
“strongly opposed” or “deeply concerned”
“my experience suggests”
“students in the Chapter 220 program”

Grammar and style
Because you are a German speaker, some sentence structures are a bit long and heavy, with limited punctuation. English academic writing usually prefers shorter, clearer sentences. Several sentences are run-ons or need commas.

Examples to revise
“As of 1970 forty-four percent...”  
  → Add comma: “As of 1970, forty-four percent...”
“This is because the children had more time to adapt to the environment.”  
  Good clear sentence—use this style more often.
“thus, these transfer students...”  
  The punctuation is awkward. Consider splitting into two sentences.
“As can seen...”  
  → “As can be seen...”
“hinderance”  
  → “hindrance”
“conductive to learning”  
  → better: “conducive to learning”
“more suitable and conductive”  
  → “more suitable and conducive”
“implemented into the program”  
  → “added to the program” or “entered the program”

Also, be careful with repetition of “black students” and “white students.” Sometimes you can vary with “students,” “transfer students,” “participants,” or “children.”

Strong final conclusion
Your conclusion makes an important point: integration needs support beyond schools. This is persuasive, but the ending could be stronger if it directly restates your thesis and summarizes your main reasons.

Possible revision strategy:
Restate that integration is not automatically successful or harmful.
Emphasize the conditions required: early integration, supportive teachers, and community backing.
End with a clear judgment.

Final priorities for revision
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.  
Split overly long paragraphs and improve topic sentences.  
Replace broad generalizations with precise, careful wording.  
Make the tone more formal and academic.  
Edit for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.  
Strengthen source integration and conclusion.

You have a strong foundation and an interesting perspective. With revision, this essay can become much clearer, more balanced, and more academically convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear argumentative position: racism on predominantly white campuses is real, and you argue that separation into race-specific organizations can worsen division rather than solve it. This gives your essay a strong sense of purpose. You also try to address counterarguments, which is an important feature of academic argumentation.

Main strengths
Clear central claim: You consistently argue for integration over segregation.
Serious engagement with the topic: The essay shows moral commitment and awareness of campus racism.
Counterargument structure: You include “One might argue…” paragraphs, which helps make the essay more persuasive.

Biggest revision priorities

A. Complete and support your evidence
Several places contain placeholders: “”. These make the argument feel unfinished. Before revising style, first:
add the missing quotations, examples, or statistics,
explain each source clearly,
connect each example directly to your thesis.

For example, when you mention Pennsylvania State University or Isaiah Thomas, the reader needs:
what exactly happened,
where the information comes from,
why it proves your point.

Without this, some claims sound too general.

B. Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis now appears across several sentences in the introduction. Try to state it in one strong sentence at the end of the introduction. For example:

> Although Black students on predominantly white campuses face real racism and exclusion, creating separate organizations does not solve the problem; greater integration and shared institutional responsibility offer a stronger response.

This is clearer and more academic.

C. Avoid overgeneralization
At times, your essay makes very broad claims, for example:
“the white majority… have held themselves in higher respect”
“the white population is not going to be the one to step forward”
“it is the actions of the white community that have forced the black community…”

These statements may weaken your credibility because they sound absolute. In academic writing, qualify your claims:
many, some, often, historically, in many institutions
This will make the argument more precise and fair.

Organization and paragraphing
Your essay has a recognizable structure, but some paragraphs are too long and contain multiple ideas. Try this pattern for body paragraphs:

Topic sentence
Evidence/example
Explanation
Link back to thesis

For instance, the paragraph beginning *“Seemingly a perfectly logical thing to do…”* discusses several different points:
white students ignoring racism,
uneasiness caused by Black organizations,
administrators’ role,
“politics of difference.”

This would be stronger as two separate paragraphs.

Also, your conclusion is too incomplete because of the placeholder. A conclusion should:
restate the main argument,
briefly summarize why your reasons matter,
end with a strong final thought.

Style and academic tone
Your writing is often passionate, but sometimes the tone becomes too dramatic or informal for an academic essay. Examples:
“race war”
“a horrible reminder of the past”
“out of sight, out of mind”

A more formal style would sound stronger. For example:
“racial conflict” instead of “race war”
“may reproduce segregation” instead of “is segregation”

Also avoid phrases like “it is quite obvious” unless the evidence truly makes it obvious. In academic writing, show rather than announce.

Grammar and language issues to revise

A. Article and possessive forms
Common examples:
“attend a University” → “attend a university”
“in ones life” → “in one’s life”
“the rights of a race” → “the rights of their race”

B. Word choice
Some phrases sound unnatural in English:
“freedoms abound” → “new freedoms”
“a felling of inferiority” → “a feeling of inferiority”
“an unreality” → “not imaginary” or “a real problem”

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or ungrammatical. Example:
“what is would be like to be of another color”
Better:
“what it would be like to be of another race”

Another example:
“Only in integrating the black students with the white students can the problems can the problems begin to be solved.”
Better:
“Only through integration can these problems begin to be addressed.”

D. Commas
You often place commas where they are not needed:
“Leaving home for that first time to attend a University, is for many, a major step…”
Better:
“Leaving home for the first time to attend university is, for many, a major step…”

This may reflect German punctuation habits. In English, commas are usually used more sparingly.

Ideas to deepen your argument
Right now, the essay sometimes suggests that Black student organizations mainly increase division. This is a sensitive claim, so you should acknowledge more fully why such organizations exist:
community and safety,
cultural support,
response to exclusion,
leadership opportunities.

Then explain carefully why you still believe integration is preferable. This will make your position more balanced and thoughtful.

Final advice
When revising, work in this order:
Fill in all missing evidence.
Rewrite the thesis clearly.
Separate large paragraphs into one idea each.
Remove overgeneralizations.
Edit grammar and punctuation sentence by sentence.

You have the basis of a serious argumentative essay. The next step is to make it more precise, balanced, and academically supported.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that surrogacy is unethical because it creates emotional harm for the surrogate mother, the infertile couple, and possibly the child. This gives your paper a strong central claim. You also try to engage with opposing views, which is important in argumentative writing. For revision, focus mainly on clarity, organization, evidence, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear stance: Your opinion is easy to identify.
Relevant ethical focus: You consistently discuss emotional and moral consequences.
Attempt to include counterarguments: You mention views supporting surrogacy before rejecting them.

Most important revision priorities

A. Complete missing information
Several places contain “”, which makes the argument incomplete. You need to replace these with:
definitions or explanations,
source information,
quotations or paraphrases,
and transitions between ideas.

At the moment, these gaps interrupt the logic of the essay. Before anything else, fill them in.

B. Improve your thesis
Your introduction should define surrogacy and end with one precise thesis statement. Right now, the first sentence is incomplete, and the thesis is somewhat repetitive.

A stronger thesis could be:
“Surrogacy is ethically problematic because it can reduce women to reproductive instruments, create emotional suffering for the surrogate mother, and produce conflicts about parenthood and the child’s status.”

This is more specific and gives your body paragraphs a structure.

C. Organize body paragraphs more clearly
At present, some paragraphs move between several ideas at once. Try this structure:

Paragraph 1: Surrogacy can objectify the surrogate mother.
Paragraph 2: Surrogacy creates emotional harm and unequal power between the surrogate and the intended parents.
Paragraph 3: Surrogacy causes legal and moral confusion about parenthood and the child.
Paragraph 4: Counterargument and refutation.

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence, then give evidence, and then explain how that evidence supports your thesis.

Use stronger evidence and analysis
Your essay makes many strong claims, but often without enough support. For example:
“They do not see the surrogate mother as a person who has feelings…”
“Many times the mother may feel left out.”
“There can be an overwhelming feeling of emptiness.”

These points may be true in some cases, but you need evidence:
facts from sources,
examples,
legal cases,
expert opinions,
or direct quotation/paraphrase from the text you were assigned.

Also be careful not to overgeneralize. Instead of saying “They do not see…”, say:
“Some intended parents may begin to see the surrogate primarily in terms of her reproductive role.”

This sounds more academic and fair.

Address counterarguments more effectively
You mention supporters of surrogacy, but the opposing side is not fully developed. A good argumentative essay should present the strongest version of the other side before responding to it.

For example:
Supporters argue that surrogacy respects women’s bodily autonomy.
They may also argue that it helps infertile couples create a family.

Then respond:
autonomy may be limited by financial pressure,
emotional consequences cannot always be predicted,
and contracts may not solve moral problems.

This will make your argument more persuasive.

Language and style issues

A. Grammar
There are several recurring grammar problems:

Subject-verb agreement:  
  “Health services, however, argues” → “Health services, however, argue”
Plural and possessive forms:  
  “the infertile couples lives” → “the infertile couple’s lives”
Pronouns:  
  “the child is biologically only the fathers” → “the child is biologically only the father’s”
Agreement:  
  “Others feels” → “Others feel”

B. Word choice
Some phrases sound unnatural or too absolute in academic English:
“walking womb” is emotionally strong; use it only if quoting a source or if you explain it carefully.
“reproductive structure” sounds awkward. Better: “a mere reproductive instrument” or “an object of reproduction.”

C. Spelling and form
Watch for:
“mother hood” → “motherhood”
“bore” → in this context better “borne”
“substantial” not “substancial”
“whose” not “who’s” in “people whose lives...”

Tone and academic style
Your essay is passionate, which is good, but some sentences sound too emotional or absolute. Academic argument is stronger when it is precise and balanced.

For example:
“No human being should be looked upon as a reproductive structure who has no feeling.”  
Better: “Treating a surrogate mother primarily as a means of reproduction ignores her emotional and moral agency.”

This sounds more formal and analytical.

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points, but it could be more effective if it:
briefly restates the thesis,
summarizes your strongest reasons,
ends with a final ethical judgment.

Final advice
For revision, I suggest this order:
Fill in all missing parts.
Rewrite the introduction with a clear definition and thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by one main idea each.
Add evidence and examples.
Strengthen the counterargument section.
Edit grammar, spelling, and word choice.

You already have a clear argument and a strong sense of purpose. If you improve structure and support your claims with clearer evidence, this essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you argue that the federal government should create a drug price review board to control excessive pharmaceutical prices. You also show strong engagement with the topic, and your concern for patients gives the paper emotional force. The essay already has a clear position; revision should now focus on organization, evidence, accuracy, and language control.

Strengths
Clear thesis: You argue that federal intervention is necessary.
Relevant examples: Canada, Europe, Depo-Provera, hospital packaging, and Clozapril all aim to support your claim.
Strong sense of audience: You try to persuade readers by connecting drug pricing to ordinary Americans and to your future work as a nurse.

Main revision priorities

A. Strengthen the structure
Right now, some paragraphs feel disconnected, and a few points are repeated. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Brief background on medical advances
   - Problem: drug prices have risen too fast
   - Thesis: the U.S. should establish a federal drug price review board

Body Paragraph 1: Why drug prices are a public problem
   - Explain who is harmed: elderly people, low-income patients, hospitals
   - Use one or two reliable statistics

Body Paragraph 2: International comparison
   - Explain how Canada/Europe regulate prices
   - Show how this results in lower prices

Body Paragraph 3: Specific examples of overpricing
   - Use 1–2 strong examples only
   - Explain clearly how each example proves the need for regulation

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument and response
   - Present the industry’s claim that regulation reduces innovation or competition
   - Refute it with evidence

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Emphasize consequences for patients and public health

B. Use more precise and reliable evidence
Some claims are strong, but they need clearer support. A few examples also seem factually uncertain or incomplete. For instance:
“This happens partly because ” is unfinished.
“which ” is also unfinished.
Some drug descriptions may be inaccurate, so you should verify them carefully before revising.

In academic argument, credibility is very important. Check:
drug names
what each drug is used for
whether the statistics are correct
dates and sources

Also, explain the statistics more carefully. For example, if you mention total health expenditures, make clear how this proves overpricing. At the moment, some numbers are interesting but not fully connected to your argument.

C. Improve paragraph development
Several paragraphs begin with a good point but do not fully explain it. Use this pattern:

Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation of the evidence
Connection to thesis

Example:
Instead of only saying prices are lower in Canada, explain why this matters:
“Canada’s price review board shows that regulation does not necessarily destroy the pharmaceutical market; instead, it can keep prices lower while maintaining access to important medicines.”

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, which is good, but some phrases are too informal or emotional for an academic essay. Examples:
“mind boggling”
“nice picture”
“price blackmail”
“crunch in the pocketbooks”

Try to replace these with more formal academic language:
“striking”
“misleading impression”
“coercive pricing practice”
“financial burden”

Also avoid rhetorical questions in formal argument unless used very sparingly. For example, “When does drug overpricing stop?” would be stronger as a statement.

Language issues to revise

A. Word choice and grammar
Some sentences are awkward or repetitive. Examples:
“have made the quality of life for several people increase”  
  → “have improved the quality of life for many people”
“who otherwise wouldn't have lived otherwise”  
  → “who otherwise would not have survived”
“This is why federal intervention is necessary with the establishment...”  
  → “Therefore, federal intervention in the form of a price regulation review board is necessary.”

B. Articles, prepositions, and plurals
As a German speaker, watch especially for:
articles: “the pharmaceutical industry,” “a review board”
plural/singular agreement: “board is,” not “board are”
prepositions: “in the 1980s,” “at a cheaper rate,” “dependent on”

C. Spelling and capitalization
There are many small errors that reduce credibility. Examples:
“agrues” → “argues”
“recieving” → “receiving”
“cant” → “can’t”
“i European subsidiaries” → “in European subsidiaries”
“Dupont-merck” → “DuPont Merck”
“Federal” should usually not be capitalized in the middle of a sentence unless part of a proper name

Final advice
For revision, focus first on content and organization, then on sentence-level editing. Your argument has potential, but it will be more persuasive if you:
verify your facts,
remove incomplete sentences,
use fewer but stronger examples,
explain clearly how each example supports federal regulation,
edit carefully for grammar and academic tone.

You have a strong position and clear commitment to the issue. With better organization and more precise evidence, this could become a convincing argumentative essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position, a recognizable argumentative structure, and a strong sense of purpose. You state your thesis early and organize the body around three main claims: ineffectiveness, constitutional problems, and ethics. This is a solid foundation. For revision, the main priorities are completeness of evidence, precision of language, and stronger academic style.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your introduction clearly argues that the death penalty should be abolished.
Logical organization: Each body section addresses a distinct reason.
Awareness of counterarguments: You mention the supporters’ position before arguing against it, which is important in argumentative writing.
Consistent focus: The essay stays on topic and returns to the main claim in the conclusion.

Most Important Problem: Missing Evidence

The biggest weakness is the repeated use of “”, which leaves quotations, statistics, and explanations incomplete. Because of these gaps, many paragraphs do not fully support your argument.

For example:
“A study conducted by Dane Archer, Rosemary Gartner, and Marc Beittel showed that the murder rates of fourteen nations, .”
“They believe, .”
“Time magazine reported that, .”

These missing parts seriously reduce the credibility of the essay. In revision:

Complete every quotation or paraphrase.
Give exact information: what the study found, when, and why it matters.
Cite sources consistently in the style required by your course.

Without full evidence, readers cannot evaluate your argument.

Argument Development

Sometimes you make a strong claim but do not explain it enough. Try to follow this pattern in each body paragraph:

claim → evidence → explanation → link to thesis

Example:
Claim: The death penalty does not deter crime.
Evidence: A study found no reduction in homicide rates after abolition.
Explanation: This suggests fear of execution does not prevent murder.
Link: Therefore, capital punishment fails in one of its main justifications.

At the moment, some paragraphs list studies without enough analysis. Do not only present statistics—interpret them for the reader.

Use of Sources and Academic Style

Your essay refers to studies, magazines, and journals, which is good, but the source use needs to become more academic.

Suggestions:
Prefer scholarly and recent sources over magazines when possible.
Introduce sources more precisely:
  - Instead of: “Time magazine reported that…”
  - Better: “According to a report in *Time*, …”
Avoid overgeneralized phrases such as:
  - “Most people are opposed…”
  - “Many people believe…”
  
These are too broad unless you provide evidence. Academic writing values precision.

Language and Style

Your English is generally understandable, but some sentences are informal or imprecise.

Examples:
“fair is fair” → too informal for an academic essay. Use: “the retributive argument” or “the principle of proportional punishment.”
“put a stop to it all together” → should be “altogether” if you mean “completely.”
“Simply putting the murderer to death will not bring back his victim.” → understandable, but more formal: “Executing the offender does not restore the victim’s life.”

Also avoid rhetorical questions too often:
“If one life is taken, is it right to take another?”
These can be effective occasionally, but in academic essays, direct statements are often stronger.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few language issues appear throughout the essay:

Capitalization: “both sides” should be “Both sides.”
Word choice: “nu merous” should be “numerous.”
Consistency: switch carefully between death penalty and capital punishment; both are fine, but use them consistently.
Pronouns: “his victim” assumes the murderer is male. A more neutral academic option is “the victim.”
Sentence clarity: Some long sentences would be easier to read if divided into two shorter ones.

Paragraphing and Balance

The first body paragraph is quite long and contains many studies. Consider dividing it or reducing the number of examples. It is better to discuss fewer examples in more depth than many examples briefly.

Also, the counterargument section is relatively short. Since this is an argumentative essay, strengthen it by presenting one or two main arguments for the death penalty more fully before rebutting them.

Revision Priorities

Fill in all missing quotations and evidence.
Add explanation after each source so the reader sees how it supports your claim.
Use more formal academic language.
Check grammar, capitalization, and word choice.
Strengthen the counterargument and rebuttal.

Final Comment

This essay already has a strong basic structure and a clear viewpoint. Your main task now is to turn it from a solid draft into a convincing academic argument by making the evidence complete, precise, and well explained. If you revise carefully, this could become a strong argumentative essay.
