Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative position: Prozac should continue to be prescribed because it has medical and social benefits. This is a strong starting point. You also try to address counterarguments, which is an important feature of argumentative writing. Below are the main areas to revise.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your first paragraph states your claim clearly.
Logical overall structure: introduction, benefits, objections, conclusion.
Use of counterarguments: You mention criticism about suicide reports and side effects.
Topic focus: The essay stays on Prozac and does not go off-topic.

Most important revision priorities

A. Replace missing quotations and incomplete evidence
Several places contain “”, so the essay currently feels unfinished. Because you rely heavily on expert opinion, these missing quotations weaken your credibility.

Also, many examples are anecdotal:
Sam
Tess
the rabbi

These can support your argument, but they are not enough by themselves. Add stronger evidence such as:
clinical studies
approval by regulatory agencies
rates of effectiveness
frequency of side effects compared with other antidepressants

B. Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is understandable, but the wording is awkward:

> “because of the positive effect it already has made in medicine and society”

A clearer version:
> Prozac should continue to be prescribed for depression because it has been shown to relieve symptoms effectively, has fewer side effects than many older antidepressants, and helps many patients function better socially.

This version tells the reader exactly which three arguments the essay will develop.

C. Improve argument quality and avoid overstatement
Sometimes the essay makes claims that are too broad or not careful enough. For example:
“The drug has also been tested and found safe by all major pharmaceutical agencies.”
“only a few confirmed Prozac deaths”
“should be easily tolerable”

These statements need evidence and more cautious wording. In academic argument, avoid sounding absolute unless you can prove it. Try phrases like:
has generally been considered safe
research suggests
for many patients
in some cases

This is especially important in medical topics.

Organization and paragraph development

Your body paragraphs mostly have one main idea, which is good. To make them stronger:

Start each paragraph with a clear topic sentence.
Follow with specific evidence.
Then explain how the evidence supports your thesis.

For example, the paragraph about medical safety could follow this pattern:
Topic sentence: Prozac has been considered safer than many older antidepressants.
Evidence: fewer side effects, lower risk of dependency, large number of prescriptions.
Explanation: therefore doctors may prefer it for some patients.

At the moment, some paragraphs list points without enough explanation.

Style and academic tone

Your style is sometimes too conversational or emotional for an academic essay. Examples:
“what is wrong with making yourself better?”
“simply ask a physician ‘for something’”
“street drugs”
“happily return to their past social lives”

Try to sound more neutral and analytical. Instead of asking rhetorical questions, state your point directly.

For example:
> “If the drug has proven to be reliable and safe, then we should let the sick make themselves well again.”

could become:
> If a medication is shown to be effective and reasonably safe, physicians should remain free to prescribe it when appropriate.

Language issues to revise

Grammar and word choice
A few sentences show non-native phrasing. Examples:

“the positive effect it already has made”  
  → the positive effect it has already had

“an architect name Sam”  
  → an architect named Sam

“being prescribed with Prozac”  
  → being prescribed Prozac / being given Prozac

“regain their confidence in society”  
  → regain their confidence in social situations

“pas social lives”  
  → probably past social lives or better normal social lives

“call for its banishment”  
  → this is possible, but sounds dramatic; call for it to be banned is more natural.

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are difficult to understand:
“The drug causes the serotonin to slow in spaces between the nerve cells”
“where a chemical called serotonin is”

You could revise this more accurately:
> Prozac affects serotonin levels in the brain by slowing the reabsorption of serotonin between nerve cells, which may improve communication between them.

Mechanics
Watch for:
split words from line breaks: banish ment, com plain
hyphenation consistency: anti-depressant / antidepressant
quotation punctuation

Content caution
Because this is a medical topic, be careful with ethical and factual complexity. For example, the suicide issue should not be dismissed too quickly. A stronger approach would be:
acknowledge the concern seriously
explain that causation is difficult to establish
present available evidence fairly
conclude that the issue requires monitoring, not necessarily a ban

This makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these steps in order:

Fill in all missing quotations or remove them.
Strengthen evidence with reliable sources, not only case studies.
Rewrite the thesis more precisely.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Edit grammar and awkward phrasing carefully.

You already have a solid basic structure. With stronger evidence, a more cautious tone, and language revision, this could become a much more convincing argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you compare two articles and argue that Pattullo’s position is more convincing than Wall’s. The reader can follow your overall direction, and you return to your main claim throughout the essay. That is a strong starting point. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis and organization, use of evidence, logic, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear argumentative position: You do not remain neutral; you clearly state which author you support.
Topic focus: Most paragraphs stay connected to the issue of gays in the military.
Comparative structure: You try to contrast Pattullo and Wall, which is appropriate for this assignment.

Most important revision issue: evidence
The biggest problem is that your essay often refers to quotations with “” instead of actually presenting the evidence. As a result, the reader cannot evaluate whether Pattullo or Wall really says what you claim. In an argumentative essay, your opinion must be supported by specific textual evidence.

What to do
Insert the actual quotation or paraphrase the idea accurately.
After each quotation, explain how it supports your claim.
Avoid summarizing vaguely.

Example
Instead of:
> “Pattullo merely addresses the very real concern of sexual feelings that may arise…”

Write something like:
> Pattullo argues that shared barracks may create discomfort because soldiers may feel sexually observed. He uses this point to suggest that privacy and trust are essential for military cohesion.

Then explain whether this reasoning is persuasive and why.

Logic and critical thinking
At the moment, the essay mostly accepts Pattullo’s claims without questioning them. A stronger university essay should not just repeat one author’s opinion. It should evaluate the argument critically.

For example, you say that allowing homosexuals to serve would “destroy” relationships and lead to the “disintegration of camaraderie.” These are very strong conclusions, but you do not prove them. Ask yourself:
Does Pattullo provide evidence, or only assumptions?
Does discomfort automatically justify exclusion?
Could the same argument be used unfairly against other groups?

Your essay will become much stronger if you acknowledge counterarguments and respond to them.

Thesis and introduction
Your introduction is energetic, but the thesis is too long and grammatically confusing. Also, the historical examples at the beginning are interesting, but they take up space before your main point is clear.

Try to make the thesis more direct:
> Although Wall presents military service as a civil rights issue, Pattullo’s article appears more persuasive because it emphasizes concerns about privacy, morale, and unit cohesion.

This is clearer and easier to support.

Organization
Each body paragraph should have:
a topic sentence
evidence from the text
analysis
a link back to your thesis

Right now, some paragraphs repeat similar ideas about discomfort, barracks, and sexuality. Try to give each paragraph one distinct focus:
Paragraph 1: privacy/barracks
Paragraph 2: unit cohesion
Paragraph 3: consequences/policy
Paragraph 4: weaknesses in Wall’s argument

Style and tone
Some phrases sound too emotional or absolute for academic writing, for example:
“the obvious answer”
“destroy the traditional close-knit relationships”
“virtually accepted”
“does not see the point”

Try to use a more formal, analytical tone:
“Wall frames the issue primarily as one of civil rights”
“Pattullo suggests that…”
“This claim is persuasive/unpersuasive because…”

Also avoid phrases such as “the writer of this essay.” Simply write “I” if first person is allowed, or stay objective.

Grammar and language corrections
You communicate your ideas well, but several sentences need correction. Here are some important examples:

“the right for homosexuals” → the right of homosexuals
“there should be a ban gays serving” → there should be a ban on gays serving
“will make on change their mind” → will make one change one’s mind
“Wall’s articles does” → Wall’s article does
“share a barrack” → share a barracks / live in the same barracks
“for whomever” → for whoever
“there will not be any unity” → correct, but there will be less unity sounds more natural
“there was not reason” → there was no reason
“claims has had an affect” → claims have had an effect
“trust amongst our troops” → trust among our troops

Also watch article use (a/the), plural forms, and subject-verb agreement. As a German speaker, pay special attention to English word order in long sentences; some of your sentences become difficult to follow because they are too extended.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities in order:
Add real quotations or precise paraphrases
Clarify your thesis
Strengthen analysis instead of repeating claims
Use a more formal academic tone
Edit grammar and sentence structure

Overall
You have a clear position and a workable comparative structure, but the essay needs stronger textual support and more critical analysis to be convincing at university level. Right now, it reads more like agreement with Pattullo than a fully developed argument. With better evidence and clearer reasoning, it could become much stronger.

Your essay has a clear position from the beginning: mandatory drug testing for athletes is ethical and necessary. That is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. What works well

Use of examples  
   The Ben Johnson example is effective because it gives a real case of doping in sport.

Introduce the source clearly and explain how it supports your point.
Do not let quotations stand alone; comment on them.

Example:
Instead of:  
*Dr. Gary Wadler agrees, .*  

Try:

Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation
Link back to thesis

This will make your argument more convincing.

Articles and possessives

*athletes health and safety* → athletes’ health and safety

Word choice
*lesson* → lessen
*meathod* → method
*strickter* → stricter
*On example* → One example
*would go too far* is fine, but later you should avoid informal phrasing like *swoop down* unless it is part of a quotation.

  → Mandatory drug testing would lessen and possibly prevent the use of performance-enhancing drugs.

Feedback Report

Main strength: clear argumentative structure

It evaluates the strengths and weaknesses of different articles.
It ends with your own position rather than only summarizing sources.

This is exactly what an argumentative essay should do. The main problem is not your ideas, but how clearly and accurately they are expressed.

Most important revision goal: use real evidence from the sources
Many places contain “”, which means key quotations or evidence are missing. This is the biggest weakness in the draft. You often say that an argument is strong or weak, but without actual quotations or paraphrased evidence, the reader cannot judge your evaluation.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. Your essay consistently argues that the death penalty should be abolished, and you give three main reasons: it does not deter crime, it is immoral, and it is applied unfairly. That gives your essay a solid foundation. For revision, focus especially on clarity, evidence, grammar, and academic tone.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your main claim appears at the end of the introduction and guides the essay well.
Logical organization: Each body paragraph has a separate main idea.
Engagement with the topic: You clearly care about the issue, which gives the essay energy.
Use of counterarguments: You mention what supporters of the death penalty believe, which is important in argumentative writing.

Most important revision priorities

A. Replace missing quotations and incomplete evidence
Several places contain , so the essay currently feels unfinished. You refer to Forst, Solomon, Jim Gittings, and supporters of the death penalty, but the reader cannot evaluate these sources because the quotations or facts are missing.

What to do:
Either add the exact quotation with proper citation, or
Paraphrase the source clearly in your own words.

Also, explain why each source supports your argument. Do not just insert names.

B. Make your argument more academic
Your voice is passionate, but sometimes the wording is too conversational.

Examples:
“Well, I believe...”
“Well it has been proven...”
“What does a serious murder case do besides... give people something to talk about.”

These phrases weaken the academic tone.

Better style:
“This essay argues that...”
“Several studies suggest that...”
“Such cases often provoke public and legal debate rather than providing closure.”

Try to avoid rhetorical questions unless they are used very carefully.

C. Improve the logic and precision of claims
Some claims are too broad or unsupported:
“the crime rate has continued to increase”
“many politicians, especially Liberal Democrats...”
“there have been too many cases in which someone has been falsely accused and put to death”

These may be true or partly true, but in academic writing they need evidence and precise wording. Be careful not to overgeneralize.

For example:
Instead of “It has been proven,” write “Some studies have found...”
Instead of “the only reason we are debating...,” write “One major reason for the debate is...”

Language and grammar issues

Because you are a German speaker, some errors may come from direct translation or sentence structure. Here are recurring issues:

A. Word choice
“increases crime” → increased crime / the increase in crime
“over it necessity” → over its necessity
“full-proof” → foolproof
“so ciety” → society

B. Articles and prepositions
“there is no need for a death penalty” → better: there is no need for the death penalty / for a death penalty system
“inform the public that we need to abolish the death penalty” is fine, but keep article use consistent.

C. Comma problems
You often place commas where they are not needed:
“In fact, capital punishment is so highly debated that .”
“Well, I believe that no matter what the circumstances, there is no need...”
“Then, when criminals do get sentenced to death...”

Read sentences aloud and remove unnecessary pauses. English academic prose usually uses fewer commas than German.

D. Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and combine too many ideas. Break them into two sentences when necessary.

Example:
> “The sad part of this issue is that the only reason we are debating over the ethics of capital punishment is because of the increases crime we face in our world.”

Possible revision:
> “One reason this issue remains controversial is the rise in serious crime. As murder rates increase, some people view capital punishment as a necessary response.”

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction
Good topic, but it is too long and repetitive. Move more quickly to the thesis. Also, avoid informal examples in parentheses such as “(For example, the Jeffrey Dahmer case).” Integrate examples into full sentences.

Body paragraph 1
This is your strongest paragraph because it presents a clear claim: the death penalty does not deter murder. Strengthen it by:
giving complete source information,
explaining the studies more precisely,
avoiding “scientist” if the source is a criminologist, researcher, or scholar.

Body paragraph 2
The moral argument is important, but it needs more careful development. The sentence “then is it moral for the government to turn right around and murder them” is emotionally strong, but in academic writing “execute” is more precise than “murder,” unless you explicitly want to argue that execution is morally equivalent to murder.

Body paragraph 3
This paragraph raises an excellent point: unequal access to legal defense and wrongful convictions. However, you need stronger evidence and clearer explanation. This could become your best paragraph if you add concrete examples and statistics.

Conclusion
The conclusion restates your position effectively, but the final sentence is again too informal (“huge mistake”). Murder is not usually described as a “mistake” in formal writing. Use “crime” or “act.”

Final revision strategy

When revising, do these steps in order:
Fill in all missing quotations/evidence.
Reduce informal language.
Correct grammar and word choice.
Make claims more precise and supported.
Proofread for spelling, commas, and sentence length.

You already have a strong basic argument. With better evidence and a more formal style, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically effective.

The conclusion also returns clearly to your thesis and offers an alternative solution, which strengthens the argument.

What works well

Clear stance
Your thesis is clear: prayer should not be reintroduced into public school classrooms, especially not as a solution to social problems.

Logical organization

Good critical thinking

Main revision priorities

Complete and integrate your sources
The biggest problem is that the essay contains many placeholders such as “”. In its current form, the argument cannot fully persuade the reader because key evidence is missing.

When revising:
insert the exact quotations or paraphrases,
identify the source clearly,
explain how each quotation supports your point.

For example, instead of:

write:

topic sentence,
evidence from source,
explanation,
link back to thesis.

Why does a majority opinion not automatically make a policy just?
How does this relate to constitutional rights?

That would deepen the analysis.

At times the wording is too absolute or informal for academic argument.

Examples:
*“It is obvious”* → better: *“The evidence suggests”* or *“This indicates”*
*“quite weak”* → better: *“unconvincing”* or *“insufficiently supported”*

Academic writing is usually stronger when it sounds precise rather than emotional.

Grammar and usage
first Amendment → First Amendment
many writes → many writers
wide-spread → widespread
religious intrest → religious interest
oppotunity → opportunity
at the sake of → at the expense of
more of Americans favor → better: 75% or more of Americans favor

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: government-sponsored prayer in public schools should not be instituted. This is a strong argumentative topic, and your conclusion returns clearly to your main claim. You also show awareness of counterarguments, which is an important strength in academic writing.

What is working well

Clear thesis
   Your thesis appears early and is understandable: prayer in public schools should not be instituted because it imposes religion and would not lead to meaningful prayer.

Consistent focus
   Most paragraphs relate to your central argument. You stay on topic and do not drift too far away from the main issue.

Use of counterargument
   You include an opposing view (“An opponent of my argument could claim…”). This improves the essay because it shows critical thinking.

Formal tone
   In general, your tone is appropriate for an academic argument.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen organization and paragraph structure
Several body paragraphs contain multiple ideas at once. Try to give one main point per paragraph, introduced by a clear topic sentence.

For example, your essay seems to make these main arguments:
parents and communities, not schools, should teach religion
school prayer would not be meaningful
school prayer does not solve crime or moral decline
after-school religious activity is a better alternative
government-written prayer threatens religious freedom

These could become more clearly separated. At the moment, some paragraphs repeat similar ideas, especially about prayer lacking meaning.

Clarify your logic
Some claims are too broad or not fully supported. For instance:
“students will not pray since there will be no reason to take this mental action”
“the majority of communities would, indeed, find agreement”
“there is some connection between the rise in crimes and the lack of prayer”

Be careful with statements that sound absolute unless you can prove them. In academic writing, it is better to qualify:
“many students may not take the prayer seriously”
“it is difficult to prove that school prayer affects crime”
“some communities might support after-school religious groups”

Improve use of evidence
You refer to many sources, but the quotations are missing (“”), so your support feels incomplete. Also, sometimes you mention a source without explaining it enough. In argumentative writing, use this pattern:

Introduce source → present evidence → explain how it supports your point

For example:
introduce the author and article
summarize or quote the relevant idea
explain why this idea helps your argument about religion, freedom, or education

Do not assume the quotation speaks for itself.

Avoid repetition
Your essay repeats these ideas many times:
prayer should be meaningful and personal
schools are not the right place for prayer
government should not impose religion

These are good points, but you should develop each one differently rather than restating them. Try to combine overlapping paragraphs.

Language and style corrections

Because your first language is German, some sentences sound slightly translated or unnatural in English. Focus especially on articles, word choice, and sentence structure.

Examples

“promote moral values i children” → “promote moral values in children”
“if they are of a religion” → “if they belong to a religion”
“This means that…” not “Does this means…”
“become angered” → “become angry”
“the students would be able to freely express their religion” → better: “their religious beliefs” or “their faith”
“miss out from more of their academic time” → “lose more academic time”
“degrade the already decadent public school system”  
  “decadent” is the wrong word here. Better: “weaken the already troubled public school system”

Grammar points to watch
Subject-verb agreement
   - “Does this means” → “Does this mean”
Pronoun agreement
   - “the student’s ability to freely express their faith”  
     Better: “students’ ability to express their faith”
Word form
   - “instituted” / “established” / “implemented” are not always interchangeable
Sentence length
   Some sentences are too long and become unclear. Break them into two sentences.

Suggestions for revision

Rewrite the introduction
   Make the background shorter and state your thesis more directly.

Create a clearer structure
   Possible outline:
   - Introduction + thesis
   - Paragraph 1: religion should be taught by families and communities
   - Paragraph 2: school prayer would be forced and not meaningful
   - Paragraph 3: school prayer is not a solution to moral decline/crime
   - Paragraph 4: after-school religious groups are a better alternative
   - Paragraph 5: government-written prayer violates religious freedom
   - Conclusion

Use more cautious academic language
   Avoid “obviously” unless something is truly obvious. In argument essays, nuanced language is stronger.

Revise for concision
   Remove repeated statements and focus on the strongest evidence.

Final comment

This essay has a solid central argument and good potential. Your main revision goals are clearer organization, more precise support, and cleaner language. If you revise with those priorities, your argument will become much more persuasive and more academic.

   - Give brief background.
   - End with a clear thesis.

Body paragraph 1: Why supporters defend animal testing
   - Medical benefits
   - Human safety
   - Example of life-saving research

Body paragraph 2: Opposing view
   - Animal suffering
   - Ethical concerns
   - Example such as the Draize test

Body paragraph 3: Your response
   - Some testing is necessary, but not all testing is justified
   - Distinguish between life-saving medical research and unnecessary or cruel experiments

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Emphasize balanced ethical position

This structure will help your essay sound more academic and controlled.

Thesis and argument quality
Your central claim is present, but it should be stated more precisely. Right now, some sentences sound too absolute, for example:
“Animals were put on earth for humans to do with as they please”
“A human’s life is more important than an animal’s life”

These are strong claims, but in academic writing they need careful support. Also, because your audience may not share your religious beliefs, religious authority alone is not enough evidence. If you mention religion, combine it with ethical or practical reasoning.

Possible revised thesis
> Although animal testing causes suffering and should be strictly limited, it remains ethically justifiable in medical research when it is necessary to save human lives and when no alternative exists.

This thesis is more nuanced and therefore more persuasive.

Evidence and credibility
You use examples, but several points need stronger evidence or clearer source use.

Some quotations are missing and shown as . These must be completed or removed.
The “Timmy” example is vivid, but it sounds anecdotal. Explain how it supports a broader pattern in medicine.
The thalidomide paragraph is confusing and factually risky. Be careful: thalidomide is known for causing severe birth defects, so your explanation seems inaccurate.
Statistics like “seventeen to twenty-two million animals” should be cited.

In revision, make sure each factual claim has a reliable source.

Tone and academic style
Some wording is too emotional or informal for academic argument:
“Thank God for little Timmy”
“who’s life is more sacred”
“do with as they please”

Try to use a more neutral tone. Strong arguments are usually more effective when they sound measured rather than emotional.

Also avoid rhetorical questions too often. Instead of asking many questions, directly state your point.

Grammar and language issues
Your writing is understandable, but there are recurring grammar and word-choice problems. A few may be influenced by German.

Common issues
a. Sentence fragments
“Some of them easily justified, and some are not.”
  - Correct: “Some of them are easily justified, and some are not.”

b. Possessive forms
“which friends side”
  - Correct: “which friend’s side”
“if human’s want”
  - Correct: “if humans want”
“who's life”
  - Correct: “whose life”

c. Prepositions
“base this argument of experience”
  - Correct: “base this argument on experience”

d. Word choice
“pain being implemented on the animals”
  - Better: “pain being inflicted on animals”
“a medicine aides in the healing off birth defects”
  - Better: “a medicine that aids in treating...”  
  But again, this example should be checked for accuracy.

e. Plural/singular agreement
“Animal researches also argue”
  - Correct: “Animal researchers also argue”

f. Spelling
“insure” → usually “ensure” in this context
“contro versy” → “controversy”
“pain less” → “painless”
“my not” → “may not”

Develop a more balanced argument
One of your strongest ideas appears near the end: some animal experiments could be eliminated, while necessary medical research should continue. This is a sophisticated position. Develop it earlier and more fully. It will make your essay more convincing than a simple “all testing is justified” claim.

Revision priorities
Create a clear thesis with a balanced position.
Reorganize paragraphs logically.
Remove repetition.
Replace missing quotations and verify factual claims.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Edit grammar, especially articles, possessives, and sentence structure.

Overall, you have the basis for a solid argumentative essay. Your next step is to make the argument more precise, better organized, and more academically supported.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and strong commitment to the topic. The opening is vivid and dramatic, and it immediately makes the reader care about the issue. You also show good awareness of counterarguments, which is important in argumentative writing. The main revision needs are: evidence, organization, and language accuracy/style.

Strengths

Engaging introduction: The imagined championship ending is memorable and emotionally effective.
Clear thesis: Your argument is easy to identify: women’s college basketball deserves equal respect, rights, and opportunities.
Strong sense of purpose: The essay consistently returns to inequality in treatment.
Use of counterarguments: You address objections about money, interest, and pressure, which strengthens the argument.

Most important revision: add specific evidence

The biggest weakness is that many paragraphs contain placeholders () or general claims without proof. In an academic argument, readers need facts, examples, statistics, quotations, or named cases.

For example, instead of:
“Men's teams have better equipment, nicer and newer uniforms, and upgraded facilities,”

add evidence such as:
budget differences,
scholarship numbers,
examples from a specific university,
media coverage statistics,
a quotation from Title IX policy or a coach/player.

Also, some claims are too broad:
“They don't see women as athletes but as sex objects”
“Money is being wasted on men's teams”

These may contain truth, but in academic writing they need careful support and more precise wording. Try to show who says this, where, and what evidence proves it.

Organization and development

Your essay has a good overall direction, but some paragraphs feel repetitive or underdeveloped. A clearer structure would help.

You might organize it like this:

Introduction + thesis
Background: what Title IX is and what it was intended to do
Argument 1: unequal media coverage
Argument 2: unequal funding, facilities, scholarships
Argument 3: stereotypes about female athletes
Counterarguments + response: money, audience size, pressure
Conclusion

Within each body paragraph, use a clear pattern:
topic sentence,
evidence,
explanation,
link back to thesis.

Example topic sentence:
“One major form of discrimination is the unequal media coverage given to women’s basketball.”

Tone and style

Your voice is passionate, which is a strength, but some expressions sound too informal for an academic essay. Examples:

“at least we don't belong in the kitchen anymore”
“men screwed things up first”
“lower end of the totem pole”

These phrases are conversational and emotional, but they reduce academic credibility. Replace them with more formal language:
“Women are still judged by gender stereotypes.”
“Male athletic programs have historically faced these problems.”
“Women’s teams are often placed at a disadvantage.”

Also avoid overstatement unless you can prove it. For example, “for nothing more than their love for the game” is powerful, but probably too absolute.

Language corrections

There are frequent grammar and word-choice errors, many of which are typical for German-speaking learners. Pay special attention to:

Articles and possessives
“as a women” → as women
“their lucky” → they’re lucky
“women rights advocates” → women’s rights advocates

Word choice
“supposeively” → supposedly
“otherhand” → other hand
“keep update equipment” → keep equipment up to date
“take competition as serious as men” → take competition as seriously as men

Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long or loosely connected. Example:
“But so far the only improvement has been in the players themselves, so , and a gained sense of respect which all women athletes deserve.”

This sentence is incomplete and unclear. Make sure each sentence has a clear subject and verb.

Agreement and formality
“Many coordinators fell” → Many coordinators feel
“women's teams should not be discriminated against and be given” → better: should not be discriminated against; they should be given

Suggestions for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

Replace every placeholder with real evidence.
Explain Title IX more accurately—equal opportunity does not always mean identical treatment, so be precise.
Use one main idea per paragraph.
Revise informal or emotional phrases into academic language.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence completeness.

Final comment

This essay has a strong foundation: a clear argument, energy, and genuine engagement with an important issue. Right now, it reads more like a passionate opinion piece than a fully developed academic argument. If you add concrete evidence, improve paragraph structure, and revise the language for formality and accuracy, this could become a much stronger essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. You introduce the issue, present opposing views, and try to refute them one by one. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. Your main claim is also clear: schools should distribute condoms to help prevent AIDS among sexually active teenagers. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Clear thesis: The reader understands your argument from the first paragraph.
Logical organization: Each body paragraph focuses on one objection and your response to it.
Appropriate topic for argumentation: You engage with public opinion, policy, evidence, and counterarguments.
Serious tone: Your essay sounds committed and persuasive.

Most important problem: missing evidence

The biggest weakness is that many of your supporting points contain placeholders such as “” instead of actual facts, quotations, or statistics. Because of this, the argument often feels incomplete.

For revision:
Replace every “” with a real quotation, statistic, or paraphrased source.
Introduce the source clearly.
Explain how the evidence supports your claim.

For example, instead of:
“According to Time, .”

write:
“According to *Time*, many teenagers remain sexually active despite abstinence-based instruction, which suggests that prevention strategies must address real behavior, not only ideals.”

Also, be careful not to rely too heavily on vague references like “studies have been done.” Name the study, who conducted it, and what it found.

Development and analysis

Sometimes you present evidence, but the explanation is too brief. Try to follow this pattern:

Claim -> Evidence -> Explanation

Example:
Claim: Condom distribution does not encourage promiscuity.
Evidence: A study in Baltimore found that students with access to condoms and AIDS education delayed sexual activity.
Explanation: This suggests that access to protection can increase caution and awareness rather than encourage reckless behavior.

At the moment, some paragraphs jump too quickly from one point to another. Slow down and explain the connection.

Style and tone

Your tone is persuasive, but at times it becomes too emotional or absolute. Phrases like “this horrible epidemic” or “the idea of abstinence is a mere fantasy” may sound overstated. In academic argument, it is usually stronger to sound measured.

For example:
“this horrible epidemic” -> “this serious public health crisis”
“abstinence is a mere fantasy” -> “abstinence alone may not be a realistic policy for all students”

This makes your writing sound more credible.

Grammar and word choice

There are several language issues that are typical and fixable. Here are some important examples:

“grounds to pre-marital sex” -> “grounds of opposition to premarital sex”
“steer away form” -> “steer away from”
“John Hopkins Medical School” -> likely “Johns Hopkins Medical School”
“more apt to practice safer sex” -> better “more likely to practice safer sex”
“than anyone who engages” -> “then”/“than” problem: here it should be “then” only if it means “in that case,” but your sentence actually needs “then” rephrased: “people who engage… should have easy access”
“the rate in which AIDS is spreading” -> “the rate at which AIDS is spreading”
“refutals” -> “refutations”

Also check article use and plural forms:
“adolescent male” -> probably “adolescent males”
“staff volunteers hand out condoms” -> acceptable, but “staff volunteers would hand out condoms” is smoother in context

Coherence and paragraphing

Your paragraphs are organized by objection, which works well. To improve coherence:
Begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.
Use transitions more precisely: “Furthermore,” “However,” “In contrast,” “More importantly.”
Make sure each paragraph ends by linking back to your thesis.

For example, after discussing condom reliability, end with a sentence such as:
“Therefore, while condoms are not perfect, their proven effectiveness makes school access a reasonable public health measure.”

Introduction and conclusion

The introduction is clear, but it would be stronger if you gave brief context without sounding too dramatic. The conclusion restates the argument, but it needs stronger final synthesis. Right now, it mainly repeats earlier points.

In revision, your conclusion should:
Restate the thesis,
Summarize the strongest reasons,
End with a broader policy significance.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities in order:

Insert all missing evidence
Explain evidence more fully
Use a more academic, less emotional tone
Correct grammar and wording errors
Strengthen transitions and conclusions

Overall, this essay has a solid argumentative framework, but it currently reads like a draft because key evidence is missing. Once you add complete source material and polish the language, your argument will become much more convincing.

Consistent focus: Most paragraphs support the same overall claim.

For example, you write:

“the ban, according to Republican state senator Mike Gunn, amounts to .”

These gaps weaken your credibility. Add the exact claim, quotation, or legal reference, and cite it properly.

At times, the essay relies on strong language instead of careful proof. For example:

“This view holds no validity”

Better approach:

Also, some claims need more support:
You say prayer does not reduce gang violence or social problems. That may be true, but you need evidence or more careful wording.
You mention segregation, lynchings, and the oppression of women. This is an interesting counterpoint, but explain the logic more clearly: past school prayer did not guarantee a morally just society.

state the point clearly,
give evidence,
explain how the evidence supports your thesis.

For example, paragraph 2 begins:  
“An easy claim to make…”  
This is too general. Start more directly by naming the claim.

Possible revision:  
“Supporters of school prayer often argue that prayer would reduce social problems among young people.”

This makes the paragraph easier to follow.

Also, two of your strongest ideas are:
public prayer pressures nonreligious or minority-religion students
private prayer is already allowed, so banning official prayer does not ban religion

These points should receive the most development because they are your most persuasive arguments.

Language and style

Your vocabulary is ambitious, which is good, but sometimes word choice is unnatural or inaccurate. A few examples:

“rendering their argument much more convincing” → better: “which makes their argument more convincing”
“prayer lead to moral virtue” → “prayer leads to moral virtue”
“moral tarnishes” → better: “moral failings”
“The concept of God is not discourage” → “The concept of God is not discouraged”
“determent” → better: “obstruction” or “interference”
“discouraging religion diversity” → “discouraging religious diversity”

Be careful with very dramatic expressions:
“students would face apostasy in the classroom” is probably too strong and not quite the right word.
You likely mean pressure to betray or suppress their beliefs.

Grammar and sentence-level corrections

A few patterns to check:

Articles and prepositions
“in public school” → usually “in public schools”
“allow for its inclusion into the public school” → “allow its inclusion in public schools”

Sentence structure

  Better: “Trying to get a troubled youth out of a gang, for example, through a school prayer is like trying…”

Subject-verb agreement
“prayer lead” → “prayer leads”

Word forms
“discourage” → “discouraged”
“religion diversity” → “religious diversity”

Suggested revision strategy

Fill in all missing quotations/claims.
Revise the thesis so it sounds more academic and less emotional.
Strengthen each body paragraph with one clear claim and one piece of evidence.
Reduce absolute or aggressive language.
Proofread for grammar, word choice, and sentence clarity.

Final thought

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you strongly oppose discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS in employment and insurance. Your position is clear from the introduction, and your moral commitment gives the essay energy and urgency. You also try to use examples and legal support, which is important in argument writing.

What is working well

Clear thesis and stance
   - The main claim is easy to identify: discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS is morally and legally wrong.
   - This gives the essay a strong sense of direction.

Passionate voice
   - Your writing shows conviction, which helps persuade readers emotionally.
   - Phrases like “living and breathing human being” show empathy and human concern.

Use of examples
   - You include several cases (Macy’s, the nurse, the pharmacist), which helps move beyond general opinion.
   - This is good argumentative strategy.

Main areas to revise

Replace emotional language with more formal academic language
At times, the essay sounds too dramatic or absolute for an academic argument. For example:
“martyr to the AIDS community”
“ousted by corporate America”
“bury someone with AIDS alive”

This kind of language can weaken your credibility because it sounds rhetorical rather than analytical. Try to sound firm but controlled.

Example revision:
Instead of: “So there stood Mark Woodley, a martyr to the AIDS community...”
Write: “Woodley’s case illustrates how fear and stigma could shape employment decisions.”

Be careful with factual accuracy
Some claims are too broad or factually questionable:
“Aids is the most common disease of our world today.”
“when someone becomes infected with HIV, it is going to develop into AIDS, and they are going to die.”
“A person with AIDS has a disability much like someone who is paralyzed has a disability. This is not true...”

These statements are either inaccurate, outdated, or oversimplified. In an academic essay, factual precision is essential. Also, HIV and AIDS should be distinguished carefully.

Suggestion: use more cautious wording:
“HIV/AIDS has been one of the most feared global health crises of the late twentieth century.”
“Without treatment, HIV can progress to AIDS.”

Improve organization and paragraph development
Your essay has a strong beginning, but some paragraphs become repetitive or unclear. Often, you introduce a case but do not fully analyze it. Also, the placeholders  make parts of the argument incomplete. If direct quotations are missing, either add the source properly or paraphrase the information.

A stronger structure would be:
Introduction with thesis
Explanation of how HIV is and is not transmitted
Examples of workplace discrimination
Why the employers’ argument is flawed
Legal perspective (ADA, anti-discrimination law)
Conclusion

Use evidence more carefully
You often mention examples, but the essay needs more analysis of the evidence. After each case, explain:
What exactly happened?
Why was it discriminatory?
How does it support your thesis?

Also, avoid claims like “This is more than enough proof” unless you have presented strong evidence from reliable sources.

Revise grammar and style
There are many language issues, some of which may be influenced by German word order or punctuation habits.

Articles/capitalization
“Aids” → AIDS
“Americans With Disabilities Act” → Americans with Disabilities Act

Verb agreement
“The instances... has been reduced” → have been reduced
“None of these involve” is correct, but check similar structures throughout.

Word choice
“horrible illness” → more formal: serious illness
“hospice car” → probably hospice care
“spokes person” → spokesperson
“full-blown AIDS” is understandable, but more neutral wording is better.

Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and overloaded with commas. For example:
“It is morally and legally wrong for people with HIV, or AIDS to be discriminated against...”
You often place commas where English would not normally use them. This may come from German punctuation habits.

Tip: In English, do not automatically place commas before “and” or “or” unless needed for clarity.

Avoid overgeneralization
Sentences such as:
“They have nothing to stand on.”
“The opposition offers a weak argument...”
sound too absolute. Academic argument is usually stronger when it acknowledges the other side fairly and then refutes it logically.

Better approach:

Final advice for revision

For your next draft, focus on these priorities:

Correct factual inaccuracies, especially about HIV/AIDS.
Remove exaggerated emotional language and use a more academic tone.
Complete or replace all missing quotations ().
Shorten long sentences and reduce unnecessary commas.
Strengthen analysis after each example instead of only describing cases.
Distinguish clearly between HIV and AIDS.

You have a strong central argument and good persuasive instincts. With more precise language, better evidence integration, and a more formal tone, this could become a much more convincing academic essay.

Current idea:
orphanages should be re-instituted

Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation
Link back to thesis

For example, your paragraph on foster care could begin:

Then add evidence, explain it, and connect it to your argument.

“the vast majority were a pleasant place for orphans to grow up”

These need strong evidence. Without evidence, they sound exaggerated. Use more cautious academic language:
“some orphanages appear to have provided stability and educational opportunity”

Also, try to distinguish between:
orphans
children removed from abusive homes
children in poverty
children in foster care

These groups are not identical. Your essay sometimes treats them as if they were the same.

Word choice / collocation
“publics attention” → public’s attention
“no where near” → nowhere near
“re instituted” → reinstituted
“refute it mainly because of two reasons” → oppose it for two main reasons
“difficult to content with” → difficult to contend with
“provide and alternative” → provide an alternative
“disparity” → probably isolation or abandonment
“with he use of” → with the use of

Grammar
“Opposition to the whole idea of orphanages refute it”  
  → Opponents of orphanages reject the idea
“many such as Republican Newt Gingrich feel”  
  → many people, such as Republican Newt Gingrich, feel
“payments should be stopped or greatly reduced to mothers continuing to have children on welfare”  
  → This is unclear. Try: payments to mothers who continue having children while remaining dependent on welfare should be reduced
“This anti-orphanage view was also reinforced by historian writers”  
  → This anti-orphanage view was also reinforced by historians
“children in orphanages were not taken from their families, but voluntarily placed there by parents who couldn't adequately care for them”

Rewrite the thesis to be more specific and balanced.
Give each body paragraph one clear main idea.

This is a strong argumentative topic, and you show good awareness that business affects consumers, employees, and society. The main revision work now is to make your argument more precise, better supported, and more academically organized.

Main strengths

Relevant topic: Business ethics is an important issue, and you connect it to real business practices such as advertising, consumer relations, and corporate behavior.
Ambition: You try to use outside sources and examples, which is good for an argumentative essay.

Most important problems to revise

A. Missing evidence and incomplete citations
Several places contain “”, which means the essay currently lacks key evidence. This is the biggest problem. Since your argument depends on examples and sources, you must:
insert the missing quotations or paraphrases,
identify the source clearly,
explain how each source supports your claim.

For example, instead of:
> “According to Fortune, .”

write something like:
> “According to *Fortune*, companies that maintain customer trust are more likely to retain long-term buyers. This supports my argument that ethical behavior is not separate from business success.”

B. Thesis needs to be sharper
Your introduction gives definitions, but the main claim could be more direct. At the end of the introduction, state your argument in one sentence.

Possible revision:
> “Although many people believe business is only about profit, ethical relationships with consumers, employees, and society are essential for long-term business success.”

This gives the essay a clearer direction.

C. Paragraph focus and organization
Many paragraphs contain repeated ideas rather than one clear point. Try this structure for each body paragraph:
Topic sentence: one main idea
Evidence/example
Explanation
Link back to thesis

For example, your paragraph on advertising should focus only on advertising ethics, not move back generally to all business relationships.

A possible structure:
Paragraph 1: Business depends on trust between producers and consumers.
Paragraph 2: Ethical rules matter because deception damages business and society.
Paragraph 3: Advertising can become unethical when it misleads consumers.
Paragraph 4: A positive example of ethical business leadership.
Paragraph 5: Counterargument and response.

D. Counterargument is underdeveloped
You introduce a contrasting view, but the paragraph actually supports your own claim. A real counterargument should present the opposing side fairly, for example:
> “Some argue that the main responsibility of a business is to maximize profit, and that ethical concerns are secondary.”

Then respond:
> “However, this view ignores the fact that dishonest practices destroy consumer trust and harm long-term profitability.”

Style and academic tone

Avoid informal or emotional wording
Some phrases sound too conversational or judgmental:
“This is what irritates business”
“they are just greedy”
“get their money”

Try more academic alternatives:
“This creates problems within the business environment.”
“Such behavior reflects a narrow focus on short-term gain.”
“maximize profit”

Avoid repetition
You repeat “relationships,” “profit,” and “money” very often. Repetition weakens the style. Use synonyms where appropriate:
trust
long-term cooperation
stakeholder relations
financial gain
commercial success

Grammar and language issues to correct

Because your first language is German, some sentence patterns may be influenced by German structure. Here are key issues:

A. Subject-verb agreement
“Ethics are the goals…” → better: “Ethics is concerned with the goals…”
“business and ethics don’t mix which is why business has…” → needs punctuation and clearer structure.

B. Word choice
“suppose to follow” → supposed to follow
“low cost contact” → probably low-cost contract
“health of happiness” → health or happiness

C. Sentence boundaries and punctuation
Many sentences are too long or joined without proper punctuation.

Example:
> “It is often said that business and ethics don't mix which is why business has its own rules, objectives, ethical standards, and judgments to follow.”

Better:
> “It is often said that business and ethics do not mix. However, business still requires ethical standards, because it is built on human relationships.”

D. Articles and plurals
Watch for small errors such as:
“the consumers control over their end” → “consumers have control over their part”
“causing harm to the consumers” → “causing harm to consumers”

Suggestions for revision
When you rewrite, do these steps:
Add all missing source material.
Write a clear one-sentence thesis at the end of the introduction.
Give each paragraph one main point.
Use evidence + explanation in every body paragraph.
Revise for formal tone.
Proofread grammar and punctuation carefully.

Final comment
Your essay has a solid moral argument and a clear sense of purpose. The main issue is not lack of ideas, but lack of precise structure and complete support. If you add the missing evidence, sharpen the thesis, and revise for academic style, this essay will become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0026.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear overall position: you argue that Shriver’s article is more persuasive than Methvin’s. You also show a good understanding of key concepts in argument analysis, such as claim, evidence, opposition, and fallacy. This gives your essay a solid foundation. To revise successfully, focus on clarity, accuracy, organization, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths

Clear thesis: Your final sentence of the introduction clearly states your judgment: Shriver’s argument is stronger.
Good comparative structure: You discuss Methvin first and then Shriver, which helps the reader follow your evaluation.
Use of analytical terms: You correctly try to evaluate the arguments by discussing claims, support, and fallacies.
Strong conclusion: The conclusion returns to your main point and summarizes your evaluation effectively.

Most important revision issue: missing quotations

The biggest problem is that the essay contains many placeholders: “”. Because of this, your analysis often cannot be verified. If you say a claim is weak or a quote is powerful, the reader must see the actual passage.

What to do:
Replace every “” with the real quotation.
Introduce each quotation clearly.
After each quotation, explain how it supports your point.

A useful pattern is:

Introduce quote → present quote → explain significance

For example:
*Methvin argues that “….” This statement is weak because …*
*Shriver writes, “….” This is effective because …*

Improve your analysis

Sometimes your essay makes a judgment but does not fully prove it. For example, you say Methvin uses “other people’s opinions” and therefore his argument is weak. That may be true, but you need to explain more carefully why the source is unqualified or why the evidence is insufficient.

Also be careful with the word “facts.” In literary/rhetorical analysis, it is often better to say:
evidence
examples
historical support
reasoning

Shriver may not always use “facts”; he may use religious reasoning, historical examples, or moral argument.

Suggestions:
Explain exactly why Shriver’s evidence is more convincing.
Distinguish between evidence, opinion, and appeal to authority more precisely.
If you identify a fallacy, prove it carefully.

Organization and paragraph development

Your essay has a logical order, but some paragraphs are too general or repetitive. Try to make each body paragraph focus on one clear point.

Possible revised structure:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Methvin’s unclear claim
Methvin’s weak support and limited response to opposition
Shriver’s clear claim
Shriver’s strong evidence and engagement with opposing views
Conclusion

Within each paragraph, start with a clear topic sentence. For example:
*Methvin’s argument is weakened by an unclear central claim.*
*Shriver strengthens his position by addressing opposing viewpoints directly.*

Style and tone

Your tone is generally appropriate, but some sentences sound too absolute or informal. For instance:
“how is the reader to know…” → better as: *the reader cannot easily determine whether this source is qualified.*
“This is hard to disagree with” → better as: *this point is likely to appeal to many readers.*

Try to use a more academic tone by avoiding conversational phrasing.

Grammar and language corrections

Because you are a German speaker, some of your errors are typical and easy to fix. Pay special attention to:

Articles
“continues to be influential factor” → “continues to be an influential factor”
“foundation of society of the past” → “foundation of past societies”

Verb forms
“people and learned” → “people learned”
“This ruling ha become” → “This ruling has become”
“Methvin’s claim in unclear” → “Methvin’s claim is unclear”

Word choice
“A research for his argument” → “In researching his argument” or “For his argument, he conducted a survey”
“attributes to poor argumentation” → “contributes to poor argumentation”
“confiments” → probably “confines” or better: “requirements” / “restrictions”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and become grammatically unstable. For example:

Original:
“But even though it has been instrumental in the development of societies and states, people and learned through war and consequence, that church and state do not mix.”

Better:
*Although religion has been instrumental in the development of societies and states, people have learned through war and its consequences that church and state do not mix.*

Capitalization/punctuation
“modern day society” → “modern-day society”
“public schools. although” → “public schools. Although”
“any where” → “anywhere”
“well thought out” → “well-thought-out”

Final advice

For revision, focus on these priorities:

Insert all missing quotations
Explain your analysis more specifically
Revise grammar and sentence structure
Use a more precise academic style

You already have a strong basic argument. With clearer evidence and cleaner language, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: the media should be allowed to witness and report on executions, but access may be limited for safety reasons. This is a strong, debatable claim, and your use of the Robert Alton Harris case gives the paper a concrete focus. You also do a good job of acknowledging the opposing view before rejecting it. That is an important feature of argumentative writing.

Main strengths

Clear thesis: Your central argument is understandable from the introduction.
Relevant example: The Harris case helps ground the discussion in a real legal dispute.
Balanced position: You do not argue for unlimited access; instead, you distinguish between reporting and filming. This makes your argument more nuanced and persuasive.
Awareness of counterargument: You mention the “high ratings” criticism and respond to it.

Most important revision priorities

A. Replace missing evidence
Several places contain “”, where quotations or information are missing. This is the biggest problem in the essay. At the moment, some claims are unsupported because the reader cannot see the evidence.

For revision:
Insert the exact quotation or paraphrase.
Cite the source consistently.
Make sure each quotation is introduced and then explained.

For example, instead of:
“Vasquez reasoned that .”
write:
“Vasquez argued that media access would create security risks for prison staff and witnesses. This claim is important because …”

B. Strengthen the thesis
Your first sentence is too broad: “the public has a right to be informed about anything and everything.” This is an absolute claim and is easy to challenge. It may weaken your argument.

A more precise thesis might be:
“The media should be allowed to witness and report on executions because the public has a legitimate interest in how the state carries out the death penalty, although prisons may restrict cameras and other equipment for security reasons.”

This version is more academic and easier to defend.

C. Improve organization
The essay generally follows the case, but some paragraphs feel like a list of facts rather than steps in an argument. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Background on Harris case
Legal dispute over media access
Counterargument: sensationalism / ratings
Response: public interest and First Amendment
Why witnessing is acceptable but filming may be restricted
Conclusion

At the start of each paragraph, add a topic sentence that shows the paragraph’s purpose.

Use stronger analysis

At times, the essay summarizes events but does not fully explain how they support your claim. After each example, ask yourself:

What does this show?
Why does it matter?
How does it support my thesis?

For instance, after mentioning Judge Schnacke’s decision, explain that the ruling suggests restrictions must be justified by real evidence, not just general fears.

Language and grammar issues

There are several grammar problems, some of which may be influenced by German word order or confusion between similar English forms.

Common corrections
“there access” → “their access”
“One such case which receive” → “One such case that received”
“had made to effort” → “had made no effort”
“inmate are” → “inmates are”
“are not reason to exclude” → “are not reasons to exclude”

Sentence-level advice
Be careful with:
subject-verb agreement: “case … received,” not “receive”
articles: “the death penalty,” “a documentary”
plural forms: “reasons,” “inmates,” “reporters”
possessives and pronouns: “their,” not “there”

Style
Some expressions sound too conversational for academic writing:
“If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off”
This is vivid, but a more formal conclusion would be stronger:
“Those who object to such coverage are free not to watch it, but their discomfort alone is not sufficient grounds for restricting press access.”

Clarity and precision

A few ideas need more careful wording. For example:

“the media has a clear right”  
  This may be too strong legally unless you can prove it. You could say:
  - “the media has a strong First Amendment argument for access”

“anything and everything”  
  This is too absolute. Academic arguments are usually more precise and qualified.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these steps in order:

Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Rewrite the thesis to make it more precise.
Add analysis after each major example.
Improve paragraph topic sentences and transitions.
Edit grammar carefully, especially verb forms, articles, and pronouns.
Make the tone more formal in the introduction and conclusion.

Overall, your essay has a solid argumentative core and a useful case study. With better evidence, clearer analysis, and careful language editing, it could become a convincing academic argument.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0028.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and an important purpose: you want to argue that young people need guidance about sex, relationships, and consequences. The personal story at the beginning is engaging and gives your argument emotional force. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, supporting claims with evidence, and adjusting tone and language.

Main strengths
Strong personal engagement: The opening anecdote is memorable and shows why the topic matters to you.
Clear concern for young people: Your essay consistently emphasizes responsibility, safety, and guidance.
Sense of urgency: You make the reader feel that this issue has real consequences.

Thesis and argument
Your final sentence of the first paragraph seems to be the main thesis:

> “The lack of parental guidance regarding sexual relationships for youths emphasizes the need for schools to teach children morals and values of sexual relationships.”

This is a workable thesis, but it needs to be more precise. Right now, the essay argues several things at once:
parents do not teach enough,
schools should teach sex education,
schools should teach morals and values,
casual sex is wrong,
marriage should be the goal.

These are not exactly the same claim. Decide what your central argument is. For example:

Option 1: Schools should provide comprehensive sex education because many parents do not discuss sex openly and teenagers need accurate information and guidance.
Option 2: Schools should teach both sexual health and relationship responsibility because young people are influenced by peers and media.

Be careful: if you argue that schools should teach morals, readers may object that morals differ across families and religions. You may be more persuasive if you emphasize responsibility, respect, consent, health, and consequences rather than one specific moral code.

Organization
Your essay would be stronger with a clearer structure.

A possible structure:
Introduction: personal anecdote + thesis.
Body paragraph 1: why parental guidance is often missing.
Body paragraph 2: why this lack of guidance creates risks for teens.
Body paragraph 3: why schools are a practical place for sex education.
Body paragraph 4: respond to objections (for example, “schools should not teach morality”).
Conclusion: return to your personal experience and explain what should change.

At the moment, some paragraphs repeat the same idea, especially that parents and churches are failing and that teens need education. Try to make each paragraph focus on one main point.

Use of evidence
This is the biggest area for improvement. Several places include placeholders like “,” which means the evidence is incomplete. Without reliable evidence, your argument sounds mostly based on personal opinion.

You need:
statistics from credible sources,
studies about sex education,
evidence about teen pregnancy or STI prevention,
perhaps evidence comparing abstinence-only education with comprehensive sex education.

When you use sources, introduce them clearly. For example:
“According to the CDC…”
“A 2023 study found that…”
“Researchers argue that…”

Also explain how the evidence supports your claim. Do not just insert a statistic; interpret it.

Tone and audience
Some wording is too judgmental for an academic argument. For example:
“Casual sex is unacceptable, it’s degrading, animalistic…”
“Parents are hiding behind their bedroom doors…”
“What these parents are failing to understand…”

These statements may alienate readers who might otherwise agree with you. In academic writing, a calmer tone is usually more persuasive. Instead of attacking people, describe the problem neutrally:
“Many parents feel uncomfortable discussing sex.”
“Media often presents sex without showing emotional or health consequences.”

Language and grammar
You communicate your ideas well, but there are recurring grammar and style issues. Common patterns:

Capitalization
Do not capitalize common nouns unnecessarily:
“Father” → “father”
“Him” → “him”

Apostrophes / plurals
“bird’s and the bee’s” → “birds and the bees”
“its degrading” → “it’s degrading”
“class mate” → “classmate”

Word choice
“peer pressures” → “peer pressure”
“morals and values of sexual relationships” → “morals and values related to sexual relationships”
“Education of sexual activity for youth” → “Sex education for young people”

Sentence structure / punctuation
Some sentences are run-ons or comma splices:
“We were wrong, after a few difficult years of marriage we divorced.”
Better:
“We were wrong. After a few difficult years of marriage, we divorced.”

More natural phrasing
“He has no intention to have sex with some one.”  
  Better: “He has no intention of having sex with someone.”

Content suggestions
Consider broadening your discussion. Healthy sex education today often includes:
consent,
respect,
communication,
contraception,
STI prevention,
emotional readiness,
relationship health.

Adding these would make your essay more balanced and more persuasive for a broad audience.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clearer thesis.
Complete and cite evidence instead of using placeholders.
Improve paragraph focus and logical flow.
Use a more academic, less judgmental tone.
Edit for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

You have a strong foundation because your essay is sincere and personally meaningful. With clearer structure and stronger evidence, it could become a much more convincing academic argument.

   You acknowledge different viewpoints: some people want every possible treatment, while others do not want prolonged suffering. This balance helps the essay feel fair.

Strong personal conclusion
   The paragraph beginning “I personally consider…” is one of the strongest parts because it becomes concrete and shows what your position means in practice.

---

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis and structure
At the moment, the essay sometimes feels reflective rather than fully argumentative. You discuss many ideas, but the line of argument is not always clear.

Revision goal:
Make each paragraph clearly support your main claim.

A possible structure:

Paragraph 1: why individuals should decide for themselves.
Paragraph 2: how definitions of life and death affect the decision.
Paragraph 3: opposing views and why personal choice still matters.
Paragraph 4: the role of doctors as advisers, not decision-makers.
Conclusion: restate why autonomy is essential.

Your thesis could be made more direct, for example:
“Because ideas of life, dignity, and suffering differ from person to person, the decision to refuse life support should remain an individual right.”

---

Develop your arguments with clearer reasoning
Some paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but they remain general. For example, you say that memories, personality, and life experiences shape how we value life. That is a good point, but you should explain how this supports your argument.

Try this pattern:
make a claim
explain it
connect it to the thesis

Example:
“People value life differently because they have different experiences, beliefs, and expectations. For this reason, no universal rule can determine when life support should continue. The decision must therefore belong to the individual.”

---

Add evidence or examples
The essay relies mainly on personal reflection. That gives it authenticity, but an argumentative essay is stronger when it includes examples, ethical principles, or real-life situations.

You could briefly refer to:
patient autonomy
living wills / advance directives
cases in which families and doctors disagreed
the emotional and financial burden of prolonged treatment

Even one or two specific examples would make the essay more persuasive.

---

Sometimes the essay moves too quickly from one idea to another. Add transition words and make connections explicit.

Examples of useful transitions:
Furthermore
However
For example
In contrast
Therefore
As a result

For instance, “If we have defined life, then we must define death” is a good transition. Try to do this more often within paragraphs too.

---

Language and style

Avoid repetitive wording
You repeat words such as life, death, define, and decision very often. Some repetition is natural in this topic, but too much makes the writing sound less polished.

Try varying your vocabulary:
life support → medical intervention / treatment
decision → choice / determination
life → existence / quality of life
death → end of life

---

Watch grammar and sentence form
There are several sentences with awkward phrasing or small grammatical errors. Some may reflect German sentence habits. Focus especially on:
article use
word order
pronoun agreement
sentence completeness

Examples:

“These are to be considered when deciding what makes our life important to use.”
  → likely meant: “These factors should be considered when deciding what makes life important to us.”

“I've heard of people o want to be put on life support”
  → “I’ve heard of people who want to be put on life support”

“Would we want someone else to make this decision without regarding our wish(es).”
  → “Would we want someone else to make this decision without considering our wishes?”

“Since it is our life, our death we are considering here”
  → “Since it is our life and death that we are considering here”

---

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are slightly conversational:
“cheat death”
“there’s no turning back”
“this is our life, our death that we are talking about”

These are expressive, but in an academic essay, more formal phrasing would be stronger:
“delay death”
“the decision is irreversible”
“the issue concerns the individual’s own life and death”

---

Final advice
Your essay has a sincere voice and a meaningful argument. To improve it, focus on turning your reflections into a more sharply organized argument. Explain each point more fully, add at least a little evidence, and revise for grammar and style.

Revision priorities
Write a more precise thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one clearly supports that thesis.
Expand general ideas with explanation or examples.
Edit for grammar, wording, and repetition.

Your essay has a clear position, strong energy, and a consistent focus on one central claim: welfare reform will fail unless it strengthens families and helps people move into work. That is a solid argumentative foundation. You also do two things effectively: you connect policy to human consequences, and you include a personal example to make the issue feel real.

Strengths

Clear thesis and purpose
Your main argument is understandable throughout the essay.
The reader can easily see that you oppose punitive welfare cuts without support programs.

Engaged voice
The opening is lively and immediately introduces the political context.
Your personal experience adds credibility and emotional force.

Some useful policy ideas
In the later paragraphs, you move from criticism to solutions: job training, childcare, housing, counseling, and transitional support. This improves the essay because it becomes constructive, not only negative.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the argument more balanced and evidence-based
At the moment, many claims are presented as facts without enough proof. For example:
welfare has caused the decline of the “American Family”
people on welfare have “forgotten all about pride”
family decline has led to violence and crime

These are very broad claims and may sound judgmental. In academic argument, you need evidence, not only strong opinions. Try to:
add statistics, historical sources, or research
distinguish between some recipients and all recipients
avoid moral generalizations about poor people

For example, instead of:
> “They have forgotten all about pride and self worth…”

write:
> “Long-term dependence on welfare may reduce economic independence in some families, especially when jobs, childcare, and education are limited.”

This sounds more precise and fair.

B. Reconsider historical accuracy
Your historical discussion needs checking. The U.S. welfare state is more strongly associated with Franklin D. Roosevelt’s New Deal than with Herbert Hoover. If you keep the Hoover paragraph, make sure your facts and quotations are correct and complete. Right now, the essay contains missing quotations marked by , which must be fixed.

C. Clarify the thesis earlier
Your real thesis appears gradually. State it more directly at the end of the introduction. For example:
> “Effective welfare reform must not focus only on cutting benefits; it must strengthen family stability and provide practical support such as job training, childcare, counseling, and transitional assistance.”

That will guide the whole essay more clearly.

Organization

The essay has a logical general direction, but some paragraphs are too long and contain too many ideas. Improve structure by grouping points more clearly:

Introduction + thesis
Background/history of welfare
Problems in the current system
Why punitive reform proposals are insufficient
Your proposed reforms
Conclusion

Also, the numbered list is useful, but the tone becomes very political and emotional there. Try to explain each policy more neutrally and support your criticism with evidence.

Style and tone

Because this is an argumentative essay, aim for a more formal academic tone.

Avoid overly emotional or sweeping phrases such as:
“the greatest Country in the World”
“obvious that Mr Gingrich does not understand”
“fill them with the children”

These expressions can weaken your credibility. A more academic version would be:
> “These proposals appear unlikely to address the structural causes of welfare dependence.”

Also, be careful with terms like “indolent” or “chemical abusers.” They sound harsh and reduce complexity.

Language corrections

You communicate well, but several grammar and usage issues should be revised.

Common errors in the essay:

its / it’s
it’s = it is
its = possessive  
Examples:
“It’s purpose” → Its purpose
“the family and it’s values” → the family and its values
“lose it’s position” → lose its position

Capitalization
Do not capitalize common nouns unnecessarily:
  - “Reform” → reform
  - “Welfare System” sometimes can be welfare system
  - “Country” → country

Punctuation
Avoid comma errors:
  - “My reply, was” → My reply was
Hyphens/dashes should be used carefully and consistently.

Word choice
“air programs” may be a typo; perhaps you mean aid programs
“income vs. my bills” is too informal; write income in relation to my expenses
“rear them” is understandable, but raise them is more natural in modern English

Parallel structure
When listing ideas, keep the grammar consistent:

Final advice for revision

   - Make your exact argument clear in one sentence early in the essay.

   - Replace emotional or political language with precise analysis.
