Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You clearly take a position against euthanasia and try to support it with three main reasons. Your essay has a recognizable structure, and your introduction gives readers a roadmap. That is a good start. For revision, the main priorities are: (1) accuracy and use of evidence, (2) strength of argument, (3) organization and clarity, and (4) grammar/style.

Argument and thesis

Your thesis is understandable: euthanasia should not be allowed as freely as it is in the United States. However, the essay sometimes argues against all euthanasia, sometimes against “complete legalization,” and sometimes only against current laws. You need one precise claim and then keep it consistent.

For example, decide whether you are arguing:
euthanasia should be illegal, or
euthanasia should be more strictly regulated, or
physician-assisted dying laws in the U.S. are too vague.

This distinction is important because your conclusion mainly argues for stricter safeguards, not a total ban.

Evidence and factual accuracy

This is the biggest issue. Some claims are too broad or factually uncertain.

Important accuracy problem:
You write that euthanasia is “practiced throughout the U.S.” This is not accurate. In the U.S., active euthanasia is generally illegal; some states permit physician-assisted dying/assisted suicide under strict conditions. These are not the same thing. You should define your terms early:
euthanasia = someone directly ends another person’s life to relieve suffering
physician-assisted dying = a doctor provides medication that the patient takes

If you mix these terms, the argument becomes weak.

Source use:
Several references are unclear or incomplete:
You include placeholders like “”, which means key quotations/evidence are missing.
Some organizations or articles are introduced without enough context.
You make strong legal claims without citing exact laws or court cases accurately.

Before revising, check every factual statement. A stronger essay would use:
a clear legal source,
one or two scholarly or credible ethical sources,
and perhaps one case study.

Strength of reasoning

Some arguments need stronger logic.

First body paragraph:
You argue that because Americans protested animal euthanasia, they are not ready for human euthanasia. This is a weak comparison. Public reaction to animals does not necessarily prove a valid policy position about humans. Also, “America is not ready” is not enough by itself; public discomfort does not automatically show something should be illegal.

Second body paragraph:
The abortion comparison is your most developed paragraph, but it still has problems. Abortion and euthanasia do involve ethical questions about life, autonomy, and law, but they are not identical. You should explain more carefully why one predicts the other. Also, fear of protest or violence is not a strong main reason to restrict a medical practice. Otherwise, law would be shaped by extremists.

Third body paragraph:
This is potentially your strongest point: the law may be too vague, competence is difficult to assess, and safeguards may be inadequate. Develop this more. Explain:
What law are you discussing exactly?
Which safeguards are missing?
Why is competence assessment difficult?
What changes would improve the system?

This paragraph could become the center of your essay.

Organization

Your essay has a basic structure, but some paragraphs are too long and repetitive. Try this revision plan:

Introduction
   - define euthanasia / physician-assisted dying
   - give brief background
   - present a precise thesis

Body Paragraph 1
   - concern about patient competence and emotional stability

Body Paragraph 2
   - legal vagueness and insufficient safeguards

Body Paragraph 3
   - social and ethical consequences (if you want to keep this point)

Conclusion
   - summarize argument
   - propose a solution (for example, stricter regulation rather than broad permission)

Style and language

Your meaning is often clear, but there are many grammar and word-choice problems. As a German speaker, pay special attention to articles, plurals, word forms, and idiomatic phrasing.

Examples of corrections:
“all American possess” → all Americans possess
“there own lives” → their own lives
“there is no black or white answers” → there are no black-and-white answers
“too much gray areas” → too many gray areas
“can not” → cannot
“a live is worth living” → a life is worth living
“an animals life” → an animal’s life
“not accepted my America” → not accepted by America
“full of wholes” → full of holes
“only one question need be asked” → only one question needs to be asked

Also avoid phrases that sound awkward:
“snuffed out” is too emotional/informal for an academic essay.
“God-given life” introduces a religious assumption; use it only if your argument is explicitly religious.

Final advice

For your next draft, focus on precision:
define key terms,
correct factual errors,
use complete and credible evidence,
and build your argument around your strongest point: the difficulty of ensuring informed, competent, and well-regulated end-of-life decisions.

You have a clear opinion and strong engagement with the topic. With more accurate research and a tighter thesis, this could become a much more persuasive essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the case for televising executions is weaker than the case against it. You also try to analyze the issue in terms of types of argument (“value” vs. “consequence”), which shows strong engagement with rhetorical analysis. The opening anecdote about Amy is vivid and helps create interest. For revision, focus on three main areas: use of evidence, clarity of argument, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths
Clear stance: Your conclusion is consistent throughout the essay.
Engaging introduction: The Amy scenario effectively introduces the emotional stakes.
Awareness of argument structure: You attempt to classify claims and compare the quality of support on both sides.

Most important revision priority: missing and weak evidence
The biggest problem is that your essay refers to sources but does not actually present the quotations or details. The repeated “” makes key parts of your argument incomplete. Because of this, readers cannot judge whether your interpretation is fair or persuasive.

What to do
Insert the missing quotations or paraphrases.
After each quotation, explain how it supports your claim.
Be specific about the sources: who is speaking, what exactly they argue, and why it matters.

Example
Instead of:
> “As KQED states, .”

Write something like:
> “KQED argues that banning cameras from the execution chamber restricts the press’s ability to report fully on a matter of public concern. However, this claim remains weak because KQED does not explain why live television access is necessary for press freedom when written and filmed reporting outside the chamber is still possible.”

That structure—source, summary/quote, analysis—will make your essay much stronger.

Clarify your central claim
Sometimes your thesis is hard to follow because the wording is too long and repetitive. For example, your first paragraph ends with a very long sentence that could be made more precise.

Suggested revision of the thesis
> “Although KQED argues that televising executions is protected by the First Amendment, this claim is less convincing than the opposition’s argument because the opponents provide clearer evidence about the harmful social consequences of broadcasting executions.”

This version is shorter, clearer, and easier to defend.

Improve paragraph focus and logic
Several body paragraphs repeat the same point: that KQED’s side is weak and the opponents’ side is stronger. Try to give each paragraph one clear purpose.

A possible structure
Introduction + thesis
Paragraph on KQED’s argument  
   Explain their First Amendment claim and why its support is limited.
Paragraph on the opposing argument  
   Present historical evidence and claims about social consequences.
Paragraph comparing the two sides  
   Show why consequences are better supported than rights-based claims in this case.
Conclusion

This will reduce repetition and make the essay easier to follow.

Be careful with analysis of “value” and “consequence”
Your essay uses these terms, but sometimes not consistently. For instance, you say the issue “is established as an issue of consequence,” then later say KQED sees it as “a matter of value.” This may confuse readers.

Make sure you define the categories clearly:
Argument of value: asks whether something is right, wrong, good, bad, just, unjust.
Argument of consequence/policy: focuses on results or effects.

You can then say:
KQED mainly uses a rights/value argument.
Opponents mainly use a consequence argument.
In your opinion, the consequence argument is stronger because it is better supported.

Tone and precision
Sometimes your wording sounds too absolute or speculative:
“the only concern that they have at this time is how televising this execution will effect their ratings”

This is a strong claim, but your essay does not prove it. Avoid assuming motives unless a source explicitly states them.

Better:
> “The essay does not show that KQED addresses the possible social effects of televised executions; instead, it focuses primarily on press rights.”

This is more academic and objective.

Language corrections
Because you are a German-speaking writer, some of your errors are typical and fixable. Pay special attention to:

Spelling/word form
  - “Six-year old” → Six-year-old
  - “routing” → routine
  - “effect” (verb) → affect
  - “their first amendment right” → their First Amendment rights
Capitalization
  - “their argument is more thoroughly defined” → Their argument
  - “pat Clark” → Pat Clark
Sentence structure
  - Some sentences are too long and should be divided.
Word choice
  - “is not a valid argument” is repeated often. Try alternatives like:
    - is insufficiently supported
    - lacks evidence
    - remains underdeveloped

Final advice
For your revision, I recommend this order:
Add the missing source material.
Rewrite the thesis clearly.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one main idea.
Reduce repetition.
Edit grammar and style.

You have a solid foundation and a clear opinion. Once you support your points with complete evidence and sharpen the organization, the essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you take a strong pro-life position and try to support it through moral, biological, and medical arguments. The paper shows confidence, a formal tone, and an effort to use sources. These are good foundations. To revise successfully, focus on thesis clarity, argument logic, use of evidence, tone, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths

Clear position: Your reader always knows where you stand.
Structured progression: You move from defining the debate, to biological claims, to the role of doctors, and finally to the broader social conflict.
Use of sources: Referring to named authors and medical figures gives the essay a more academic feel.
Engaged style: The topic is emotionally and ethically important to you, and that energy comes through.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis complete and precise
Your introduction builds toward a thesis, but the sentence before the second paragraph ends with “however,” and seems incomplete. Then the next paragraph begins with rhetorical questions. Because of this, the central claim is not presented as clearly as it could be.

What to do: End the introduction with one strong thesis sentence, for example:
*Abortion is morally wrong because it ends a human life, violates the rights of a separate individual, and conflicts with the physician’s duty to preserve life.*

This gives your essay a clear roadmap.

B. Reduce rhetorical questions and emotional wording
You use many rhetorical questions such as “Can one really justify…?” and highly charged language such as “senseless killing” or “turn their backs.” This weakens academic credibility because it sounds more persuasive than analytical.

What to do: Replace questions with claims and evidence.
Instead of:
*Can one really justify an innocent child being denied a chance at life as moral?*
Write:
*From a pro-life perspective, abortion cannot be considered moral because it ends what supporters regard as innocent human life.*

This makes the tone more scholarly.

C. Strengthen logic and acknowledge counterarguments fairly
At several points, the essay moves too quickly from “the fetus is alive” to “abortion is murder.” In an argumentative essay, that conclusion needs more careful reasoning, because opponents do not necessarily agree that biological life automatically equals legal or moral personhood.

What to do: Show the steps in your reasoning:
the fetus is biologically alive,
it is human,
it is a distinct organism,
therefore it deserves moral protection.

Also, present the opposing view more fairly. Right now, “pro-choice” is simplified. A stronger essay would briefly explain that supporters of abortion rights often focus on bodily autonomy, legal privacy, and difficult social circumstances.

A fair summary of the other side makes your own position more convincing.

D. Integrate evidence properly
Many quotations are missing and marked with , so currently the paper feels unfinished. Also, some source use is dropped into the paragraph without much explanation.

What to do:
Add the full quotations or paraphrases.
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain how each piece of evidence supports your argument.
Be consistent with spelling of names: you write both Calahan and Callahan.

A useful pattern is:
Introduce source → present quote/paraphrase → analyze significance.

Organization

Your overall order is logical, but paragraph development can improve.

Suggested structure
Introduction: background, opposing sides, thesis.
Paragraph 1: biological argument that fetal life is human life.
Paragraph 2: argument that the fetus is a distinct entity.
Paragraph 3: moral implications of those claims.
Paragraph 4: doctors and the Hippocratic Oath.
Counterargument paragraph: explain pro-choice reasoning and respond to it.
Conclusion: restate thesis and end with a reasoned final claim.

Your current conclusion introduces new questions instead of fully closing the argument. Try to finish with a direct restatement of your position.

Language and style corrections

As a German speaker, you sometimes produce word order or phrasing that sounds slightly unnatural in English. Examples:

“Or ‘endure’ the right word?”  
  Better: *But is “endure” the right word?*

“for more ninety-eight percent”  
  Better: *for more than ninety-eight percent*

“the woman in which it resides”  
  Better: *the woman in whom it resides* or more naturally *the woman carrying it*

“deny her child of life”  
  Better: *deny her child the right to life*

“play a hand in”  
  Better: *play a role in*

“taken form the oath”  
  Better: *taken from the oath*

“remain” split as “re main”  
  Fix formatting issues carefully.

Also watch for:
article use (*the abortion debate*, *a moral decision*)
prepositions
repetition of phrases like *in essence*, *serves as strong evidence*, *without a doubt*

Final advice

For revision, focus less on emotional force and more on clear reasoning. Your argument will be stronger if you:
present a precise thesis,
complete and document your sources,
explain evidence more carefully,
acknowledge the opposing side fairly,
and use a more academic tone.

You already have a strong base and a clear commitment to your topic. With these revisions, the essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises a provocative question about medicine, resources, and the value of life. You clearly aim to challenge assumptions, and the opening paragraph is strong in tone and ambition. For revision, the main tasks are to clarify your thesis, strengthen evidence, improve logical fairness, and correct language issues.

Main argument: make it precise
At the moment, your essay seems to argue several things at once:

modern medicine focuses too much on prolonging life
symptom treatment is less valuable than prevention or cure
some medical care weakens the human gene pool
genetic counseling should discourage some people from having children
health-care costs are too high

These are related, but not identical. Because of this, the essay feels scattered. Try to state one central claim early and make every paragraph support it. For example:

> Modern medicine should place greater emphasis on prevention, causes of disease, and quality of life rather than automatically extending life at any cost.

This would let you discuss costs and treatment without making the essay depend on controversial claims about “weak” people reproducing.

Logic and ethics: avoid overgeneralization
Your strongest problem is not grammar but reasoning. Several claims are too sweeping or ethically problematic:

“The medical community derives its existence solely on the preservation of life.”  
  This is too absolute. Medicine also aims to reduce suffering, improve quality of life, prevent disease, and support patient autonomy.

The argument that people with genetic diseases or weaker immune systems should die earlier so they do not reproduce is a form of biological determinism and moves close to eugenic reasoning. In an academic essay, this will likely alienate readers unless you address the ethical dangers directly and very carefully.

You often assume that extending life automatically harms society. That needs evidence, not assertion.

A stronger approach would be to ask:
When does treatment become disproportionate?
How should societies balance life extension, suffering, cost, and quality of life?
What are the limits of medical intervention?

These are still challenging questions, but they are more persuasive and academically responsible.

Evidence: develop and document it
Many paragraphs rely on statements that need support. Also, the placeholders  leave important gaps. You must fill these.

Examples needing evidence or correction:
PKD statistics and claims about inheritance
Darwin/evolution argument in modern human society
what genetic counselors actually do
antibiotics and resistance
AZT and AIDS treatment
Hantavirus example
health-care spending claims

Be careful with scientific accuracy. For instance:
A viral infection in a finger is unlikely in the example; cuts more commonly lead to bacterial infection.
Bacteria do not “recognize” antibiotics; instead, resistant bacteria survive and reproduce.
“AIDS” should usually be written AIDS, and “cancer” is not capitalized unless at the start of a sentence.
Not all traits like asthma or poor eyesight can be discussed as simply “defects” in the way you suggest.

Use reliable academic or medical sources and cite them consistently.

Organization: improve paragraph focus
Several body paragraphs mix topics. For example, the antibiotics paragraph is clearer than others because it stays on one idea. Try this structure:

Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Paragraph 1: medicine’s goals and the problem of “life at any cost”  
Paragraph 2: prevention vs symptom treatment  
Paragraph 3: costs and allocation of resources  
Paragraph 4: ethical limits of genetic screening/counseling  
Counterargument: why life-saving treatment still matters  
Conclusion: restate balanced claim

Including a counterargument will make the essay more mature and persuasive.

Tone: reduce judgmental language
Some phrasing sounds harsh or emotional rather than analytical:
“This is disgraceful”
“selfish act”
“uniformed people” (also probably meant uninformed)

In argumentative academic writing, a calmer tone is more effective. Instead of attacking people, analyze systems, incentives, and ethical dilemmas.

Language and style corrections
You have many good sentences, but there are recurring issues:

Word choice / grammar
“fallibility to disease” → vulnerability to disease
“a common cliche” → a common cliché
“runs the gamut” is fine, but the sentence is long; simplify.
“Cancer, Aids” → cancer, AIDS
“highly advance defense mechanism” → highly advanced defense mechanism
“over thousands years” → over thousands of years
“t decide” → to decide
“particularly anomaly” → a particular anomaly
“uniformed people” → uninformed people
“Doctors’ orders” → usually doctors’ orders
“non 20/20 vision” → less-than-20/20 vision or impaired vision

Sentence length
Many sentences are too long and carry several ideas. Shorter sentences will improve clarity.

Capitalization
Do not capitalize common nouns like doctors, cancer, or science unless required.

Best revision strategy
When you revise, do this in order:
Write a new one-sentence thesis.
Remove or soften claims that imply some lives are less worth saving.
Fill all missing quotations and evidence.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one main point.
Edit for tone and scientific accuracy.
Proofread grammar and word choice.

Final comment
You are clearly willing to engage difficult issues, which is a strength. The next step is to make the essay more focused, better supported, and more ethically nuanced. If you do that, your argument will become much more convincing.

Your essay shows clear engagement with a difficult ethical issue, and you do a good job identifying that both supporters and opponents of assisted suicide have strengths and weaknesses. This balanced approach is one of the essay’s biggest strengths. You also try to evaluate arguments rather than simply summarize them, which is appropriate for argumentative academic writing.

Main strengths

Balanced structure:  
You consistently compare both sides of the debate. This gives the essay a thoughtful tone.

Clear sense of importance:  
The introduction shows why the issue matters socially, morally, and medically. This helps create relevance for the reader.

Analytical intention:  
You do not just state opinions; you often explain *why* an argument is strong or weak. For example, you discuss the “possible consequences” argument and evaluate its persuasive effect.

Main areas to revise

A. Make your thesis more precise
At the moment, your introduction discusses many questions, but your central claim is not fully clear. Your thesis seems to be: both sides have valid strengths and weaknesses, and society must examine them carefully before accepting or rejecting assisted suicide.  
State this more directly in one sentence at the end of the introduction.

Possible revision:  
*Although supporters of assisted suicide emphasize compassion and relief from suffering, opponents raise serious concerns about abuse, medical ethics, and public trust; therefore, both sides must be critically examined before society accepts such practices.*

B. Improve paragraph focus

For example:
Paragraph 1: slippery-slope / abuse of the practice
Paragraph 2: need for better palliative and hospice care
Paragraph 3: trust in doctors and medical ethics
Paragraph 4: supporters’ argument about mercy and compassion

Begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence and then support it with evidence and explanation.

C. Use evidence properly
Many quotations are marked as , so the argument currently feels unfinished. In revision, make sure every quotation is:
introduced,
quoted accurately,
explained afterward.

Do not let quotations stand alone. After each one, explain how it supports your point.

D. Develop the supporters’ side more fairly
Most of the essay gives detailed attention to the opponents’ arguments, while the supporters’ side is discussed much more briefly. This creates some imbalance. If your goal is to compare both sides, give supporters equal treatment.

For instance, you could add:
the idea of patient autonomy,
the right to avoid unnecessary suffering,
the distinction between killing and allowing to die,
cases where pain relief is no longer sufficient.

Language and style issues

A. Formal academic tone
Some wording is too emotional or informal for academic writing.

Examples:
“play God” → acceptable only if attributed to opponents
“easy way out” → too judgmental
“mercy killings” → very loaded term unless quoted from a source

*physician-assisted dying*
*end-of-life decision-making*
*terminal suffering*
*medical ethics*

There are many useful ideas, but some sentences are too long or awkward. Shorter sentences will improve clarity.

Examples:
“Supporters' arguments have great strengths in their reliance of the belief...”  
  Better: *Supporters’ arguments are strong because they rely on the belief that Kevorkian is relieving suffering.*
“the neglection” → *the failure* or *the neglect*
“a doctors stands for” → *a doctor stands for*
“This makes it seems” → *This makes it seem*
“the maybe” → *then maybe*

C. Articles, prepositions, and word choice

articles: *a doctor*, *the medical profession*
prepositions: *reliance on* (not *reliance of*)
word forms: *suffering* (not *sufferings* in most cases), *acceptance*, *opposition*

D. Pronouns and consistency

*one*, *people*, *we*, *he/she*

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim: the *opponents* of corporal punishment make an ineffective argument because they use unclear definitions and take an inconsistent position. This is a strong argumentative focus, and your introduction is vivid and engaging. The opening scene immediately draws the reader in and shows the emotional stakes of the topic. You also organize the essay around two main points, which gives the paper a recognizable structure.

That said, the essay needs revision in evidence, precision, and style to become more convincing.

Clarify your thesis and purpose
Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction: the opponents’ argument is ineffective because of “ambiguous terminology and a wavering position.” This is good, but you should make the wording more direct and academic.

For example:
Current idea: “The opponents' argument is ineffective because of ambiguous terminology and a wavering position.”
Possible revision: “Arguments against corporal punishment in schools are weakened by inconsistent definitions of the term and by contradictory claims about its effects.”

This version is more formal and precise.

Strengthen the use of evidence
The biggest weakness is that your body paragraphs often make claims without enough concrete proof. You mention several authors, but the essay contains placeholders (“”) instead of actual quotations or paraphrases. Without those, the reader cannot judge whether your interpretation is fair.

To revise:
Include the exact definitions given by Jambor, Johns, and MacNaughton.
Show specifically how the definitions differ.
Quote or paraphrase the statements by Diamantes that you believe are contradictory.
Explain *why* these differences matter for the debate.

In other words, each body paragraph should follow this pattern:
Topic sentence  
Evidence from a source  
Analysis of that evidence  
Link back to the thesis  

Right now, the essay often stops after step 2.

Develop your analysis more fully
You often make good points, but the reasoning is sometimes too brief. For example, you ask: “How can one make an effective argument without establishing what, exactly, one is fighting against?” This is a useful question, but you should answer it more fully. Explain what kinds of confusion result from unclear definitions. Does one author mean spanking with a paddle, while another includes all physical discipline? If so, say that directly.

Similarly, when you argue that the opponents “vacillate,” explain whether this means:
they disagree with one another,
they acknowledge exceptions,
or they contradict themselves within the same source.

These are different problems, and distinguishing them would make your analysis sharper.

Improve paragraph unity and transitions
Your essay has a logical overall structure, but some paragraphs feel underdeveloped or disconnected. For instance, the paragraph beginning “The opponents also vacillate…” is very short and general. It would be stronger if combined with the previous paragraph or expanded with specific examples.

Also, some transitions can be smoother. Instead of repeating “The opponents,” vary your language:
“Critics of corporal punishment”
“These authors”
“Anti-corporal-punishment writers”

This will make the prose less repetitive.

Use a more formal academic tone
The narrative opening is effective, but parts of the essay become too conversational for academic argument. Examples include:
“Were you a tommy?”
“How is it, then, that anyone would choose to believe…?”

These rhetorical questions create engagement, but too many of them weaken the scholarly tone. Keep one or two if you wish, but rely more on direct analysis.

Also avoid emotionally loaded or vague wording such as:
“bad”
“fight will never be won”
“not be soon coming”

Aim for neutral, precise language.

Sentence-level corrections
There are a number of grammar and word-choice issues, some likely influenced by German. Here are important examples:

“took he seat” → “took his seat”
“with his head hung low” → “with his head hanging low”
“Even if definition” → “Even if the definition”
“opponents to corporal punishment” → better: “opponents of corporal punishment”
“unteeteringly” → probably “unwaveringly”
“subjected the paddle” → “subjected to the paddle”

Also watch for:
article use (“the,” “a”)
prepositions
spelling consistency (“utilized” / “used”)
comma placement

Consider the essay’s fairness
One final point: your argument criticizes the *opponents’* case, but you do not address whether corporal punishment itself is justified. That is acceptable if your task is rhetorical analysis, but then make that purpose explicit. Otherwise, a reader may feel that the essay avoids the larger ethical issue.

Revision priorities
Replace all placeholders with real quotations or paraphrases.
Make the thesis more formal and precise.
Expand analysis after each example.
Reduce conversational questions and repetition.
Correct grammar and phrasing errors.

You already have a strong basic structure and an engaging opening. With better evidence and more precise language, this could become a much more persuasive academic essay.

Children are impressionable (study / psychological evidence)
Violent programs encourage aggression
Sexual content shapes attitudes and behavior
Counterargument: parental responsibility
Why parental control alone is insufficient
Government / networks / standards
Conclusion

At the moment, phrases like “the advocates for censorship” appear in almost every paragraph, and sometimes the paper becomes a summary of what advocates think rather than *your own argument*. Write more directly.

Instead of:  
“The advocates for censorship realize…”  
write:  
“This suggests…” or “These examples show…”

Language issues to correct

A. Word choice
Several words are used incorrectly or awkwardly:
“effects” → usually “affects” as a verb  
  “what they view affects their behavior”
“censorships” → “censorship” is usually uncountable
“peak” → “pique” interest
“suppose” → “supposed”
“caniving” → likely “conniving”
“effecting America’s youth” → probably “affecting”

B. Grammar and sentence structure
Many sentences are too long and contain comma splices or missing punctuation.

Example:  
“Children are very impressionable, the information that is instilled into them…”  
This should be split:
“Children are very impressionable. Information instilled in them, whether good or bad, becomes part of their development.”

Also check:
article use: “a child,” “the government,” “the networks”
plural/singular agreement
verb tense consistency
possessives: “today’s children,” “society as a whole”

C. Prepositions and idiomatic English
As a German speaker, you may transfer sentence patterns directly. Some phrases need adjustment:
“standards upon something institution” → “standards imposed on an institution”
“for they know can’t win” → “because they know they cannot win”
“in protection with the children” → “to protect children”

Academic style
Your tone is often emotional and persuasive, but sometimes too informal or exaggerated for academic writing.

Examples to revise:
“birds and the bees” → too informal
“all they do is use karate punches and kicks” → acceptable in analysis, but phrase more neutrally
“Together they shout that we need censorship” → too dramatic

Aim for a more formal tone:
“Taken together, these examples support the argument for stricter television standards.”

Also avoid unsupported claims such as:
“programs in 1970 were censored ten times more than the shows of today”  
If you cannot prove this, remove or qualify it.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many examples, but some need better explanation:
After each example, explain how it proves your point.
Distinguish between violent content, sexual content, and social desensitization.
Some examples from juries and art controversies feel only loosely connected to television. Make the connection clearer or cut them.

A useful paragraph pattern:
Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation
Link back to thesis

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points but should be more concise and confident. Avoid writing about “the advocates” again. State what *you* have shown.

For example:  
“In conclusion, stricter television censorship is justified because children are highly influenced by what they watch, repeated exposure to sex and violence can normalize harmful behavior, and parental supervision alone cannot solve the problem. Public television should therefore be held to stronger standards.”

Final advice
For your next draft:
Write a shorter, clearer thesis.
Organize paragraphs by one main point each.
Replace repeated phrases like “the advocates for censorship realize” with direct analysis.
Correct grammar, punctuation, and word choice carefully.
Use a more formal academic tone.

You have strong ideas and plenty of material. The next step is to make your argument more focused, precise, and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear engagement with a difficult ethical topic, and you already have several strengths: a serious tone, relevant examples, and an awareness that strong argumentation requires addressing counterarguments. You also organize the paper in a way that moves from background, to counterarguments, to consequences. That is a solid foundation.

Main issue: clarify your thesis and purpose

At the moment, the essay is not fully an argument about genetic research itself. Instead, it often becomes an argument about how someone could write an argument against genetic research. For example, you repeatedly write “one should present,” “a writer could show,” or “this would be effective in an argument.” This makes the essay sound like a plan for an essay rather than the essay itself.

Revise by making your own position direct and explicit.  
For example, instead of:

“One possible way of presenting the most effective argument opposing genetic research…”

write:

“Genetic research should be pursued only under strict ethical limits because it risks privacy violations, discrimination, and the misuse of gene manipulation.”

This will make the essay more persuasive and academically confident.

Use a stronger argumentative structure

A clearer structure would help:

Introduction
Brief background
Clear thesis

Body paragraph 1: historical warning
eugenics/Nazi misuse
explain relevance carefully

Body paragraph 2: privacy and discrimination
employers, insurance, military
why this matters

Body paragraph 3: limits of “knowledge for its own sake”
scientific knowledge often leads to application
risks of misuse

Body paragraph 4: gene manipulation/designer children
ethical line between therapy and enhancement

Counterargument paragraph
acknowledge medical benefits
explain why regulation is still necessary

Conclusion
restate thesis
emphasize cautious, ethical progress

Right now, some paragraphs repeat similar ideas, and the line of reasoning is sometimes difficult to follow.

Develop analysis more carefully

You raise important points, but they are sometimes stated too generally. For example:

“genetic research has been harmful in the past”
“this consequence could lead people to be skeptical”
“it should not be done”

These claims need more explanation. Ask yourself:
How exactly?
Why?
Under what conditions?

Also, be careful with comparisons to the Nazis. This can be powerful, but it must be handled precisely. Genetic research itself is not identical to Nazi eugenics; rather, the point is that scientific knowledge can be misused by political systems. That distinction makes your argument more credible.

Integrate sources properly

A major problem is the use of placeholders such as . These make the argument incomplete. You need to insert the actual quotations or paraphrases and explain them clearly.

When using sources:
Introduce the source
Present the quotation/paraphrase
Analyze it

For example:
“George Annas argues that genetic information could become a tool of discrimination. This is important because employers or insurers might use such data to exclude people with inherited risks.”

Do not assume the quotation explains itself.

Style and academic tone

Your vocabulary is ambitious, but some phrases are awkward or imprecise. A few examples:

“do three doings” → do three things
“from the layman” → from ordinary citizens / the general public
“malevolent genes” → harmful genes
“if the governments of the world cannot control the research than it should not be done” → if governments cannot regulate the research, it should not proceed unchecked

Try to prefer simple, precise academic wording over dramatic phrasing.

Grammar and language points

Because your first language is German, here are some patterns to watch:

Articles and singular/plural
“One consequences” → One consequence
“the field of genetic research has swelled to an astounding size” is okay stylistically, but has grown enormously is more natural.

Word choice
“layman” is outdated in formal writing.
“than” / “then”:  
  - than = comparison  
  - then = time/order  
  You confuse them in a few places.

Possessive and contractions
“ones argument” → one’s argument

Verb forms
“if genetic information is known what will stop…”  
  Better: If genetic information becomes available, what will stop…

Missing verbs
“The question is if genetic information be kept private”  
  Better: The question is whether genetic information can be kept private.

Accuracy and mechanics

A few details need correction:
“Gregorian monk named Mendal” should be Gregor Mendel, and he was an Augustinian monk, not “Gregorian.”
“National Institute of Health” is usually National Institutes of Health (NIH).

These corrections matter because factual accuracy strengthens credibility.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

State your own thesis clearly
Remove “one should / a writer could” language
Insert and explain all missing source material
Support each claim with analysis
Edit for grammar, precision, and factual accuracy

You have a strong topic and good instincts about ethical complexity. With a clearer thesis and more direct argumentation, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: the same topic can be presented differently depending on audience, purpose, context, and attitude. That is a strong basis for an argumentative comparison, and you consistently return to it. You also organize the paper into recognizable sections, which helps the reader follow your discussion.

What is working well

Clear comparative focus  
   You compare *Newsweek* and *JAMA* throughout the essay instead of discussing them separately for too long. This is good for an argumentative analysis.

Awareness of rhetoric  
   You identify important rhetorical concepts such as audience, physical context, social context, and tone/attitude. This shows strong analytical ambition.

Effective opening  
   The opening scene about Saturday night is vivid and engages the reader. It also leads naturally into the topic of binge drinking.

Main revision priorities

Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is interesting, but it is somewhat long and slightly unclear. For example, you suggest that *Newsweek* may “exploit” the topic, but later you mostly argue that it adapts its writing to its audience. Those are not exactly the same claim.

A stronger thesis would state:
what you are comparing,
which rhetorical features you will analyze,
and what overall conclusion you draw.

For example:  
“Although both articles address college binge drinking, JAMA presents the issue through neutral statistics for a professional audience, while Newsweek uses emotional language and selective examples to engage a general audience.”

This is more direct and easier to prove.

Use more specific evidence
Often you make strong claims, but the evidence remains general. For example, you say *Newsweek* uses shocking facts and emotional stories, but you do not quote enough concrete language. In several places, there are placeholders like , which makes the analysis incomplete.

For revision:
add short quotations from both articles,
analyze particular words or phrases,
explain how these choices affect the audience.

Try to follow this pattern:  
claim → evidence → explanation.

Example:  
“Newsweek describes drunken incidents in dramatic terms, which creates alarm for general readers. By contrast, JAMA uses categories such as ‘methods’ and ‘results,’ which emphasizes objectivity.”

Avoid overgeneralizations
Some statements are too broad, for example:
“People are for some reason drawn to look at the dark side of issues.”
“Making those assumptions is what makes a good writer.”
“When readers see detailed charts they know that this is an article that is well written.”

These are difficult to prove and may weaken your credibility. Try using more careful academic wording:
“Many general-interest magazines emphasize dramatic aspects of social issues.”
“Writers often make assumptions about what will interest their audience.”
“Charts and tables can suggest scientific credibility to readers.”

Improve paragraph focus
Several body paragraphs are long and discuss multiple ideas at once. For example, the paragraph on audience also includes rhetoric in general and a judgment about what makes a good writer.

Try giving each paragraph one main point:
Paragraph 1: audience
Paragraph 2: physical presentation
Paragraph 3: social context/content selection
Paragraph 4: tone and attitude

Then begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence.

Language and style corrections

Because your first language is German, some issues seem related to direct translation or article/preposition use. Here are important corrections:

“any give time” → “any given time”
“todays college campuses” → “today’s college campuses”
“56% or men” → “56% of men”
“has an affect on” → “has an effect on”  
  (“affect” is usually a verb; “effect” is the noun)
“take in to account” → “take into account”
“This make for differences” → “This makes for differences”
“hook it audience” → “hook its audience”
“readers attention” → “reader’s attention” or “readers’ attention”
“premade assumptions” → better “preconceived assumptions” or simply “assumptions”
“serve a negative effect” → “have a negative effect”
“rhetorical site” → probably “rhetorical situation”

Also, try to avoid using “they” for magazines or articles when the reference is unclear. Sometimes write “the Newsweek article” or “the JAMA authors” instead.

Tone and academic style

Your voice is confident, which is good, but some phrases sound too conversational or subjective for an academic essay:
“as any college student can tell you”
“for some reason”
“all the more interesting”

Replace these with more formal phrasing:
“From a student perspective…”
“This suggests that…”
“This increases the article’s impact…”

Final advice

For your revision, focus on three goals:

Clarify the thesis
Add specific quotations and closer analysis
Edit for grammar, word choice, and formal tone

You already have a solid comparative structure and a relevant argument. With more precise evidence and tighter language, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically strong.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position: you argue that comprehensive sex education is more credible and effective than abstinence-only education, especially because some abstinence-only arguments rely on misleading claims. Your essay shows good critical thinking and an effort to evaluate evidence rather than simply summarize both sides. That is a strong foundation.

Main strengths
Clear argumentative purpose: your conclusion supports comprehensive sex education consistently.
Awareness of counterarguments: you do not ignore the other side; instead, you try to challenge it.
Use of examples and studies: this gives your essay an academic tone and helps support your claims.

Most important revision priorities

A. Make your thesis more precise
Your introduction discusses the controversy, but the main claim is not expressed sharply enough. Try ending the introduction with a direct thesis, for example:

> Although abstinence should be included in school sex education, comprehensive sex education is more effective because it provides accurate information, prepares teenagers to make informed decisions, and avoids the misleading claims often used in abstinence-only programs.

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

B. Improve organization and paragraph focus
Several body paragraphs contain many ideas at once. Try using one main point per paragraph. For example:

Abstinence-only arguments often rely on misleading information
Some studies used by abstinence-only supporters are weak or misrepresented
Fear-based teaching can create unhealthy attitudes and poor decision-making
Comprehensive education better addresses teenagers’ real needs

Begin each paragraph with a topic sentence that states the paragraph’s main point.

C. Avoid repetition of “proponents” and “opponents”
These words appear very often, which makes the style repetitive and sometimes confusing. You can vary them:
supporters of abstinence-only education
critics of abstinence-only programs
advocates of comprehensive sex education
defenders of abstinence-based instruction

Also be careful: sometimes it is unclear whose side “proponents” refers to.

Use of evidence
You rely heavily on quotations and reported claims, but some are incomplete because of the placeholders. In revision:

Introduce each source clearly.
Explain the quotation after presenting it.
Show how the evidence supports your argument.

For example, after giving a statistic, do not move on immediately. Add 1–2 sentences of analysis:
Why is this statistic unreliable?
What does it show about abstinence-only education?
Why does this make comprehensive education more persuasive?

At the moment, some paragraphs list examples without fully analyzing them.

Style and clarity
Your wording is sometimes too complicated, which makes the meaning less clear. Aim for simpler, more direct sentences. For instance:

Original:
> In following with "abstinence-only", the rates of sexually transmitted diseases, AIDS, and pregnancy cases among teenagers would decrease.

Clearer:
> Supporters of abstinence-only education argue that it would reduce rates of STDs, AIDS, and teenage pregnancy.

Also avoid phrases that sound awkward in English:
“more than recent” → not new
“cut down their opponents” → criticize their opponents
“focusing in on” → focusing on
“facile reasoning” is possible, but oversimplified reasoning may sound more natural.

Grammar and language issues to revise

A. Articles and word forms
As a German speaker, you may sometimes omit or misuse articles. Check:
“their opponents misinformation” → their opponents’ misinformation
“the proponents case” → the proponents’ case
“birthcontrol pills” → birth control pills

B. Verb forms
“teens will not being informed” → teens will not be informed
“studies have beared this scrutiny” → studies have borne/stood up to this scrutiny
“which she consider a joke” → which she considers a joke

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are run-ons or awkwardly connected. Example:

> Accordingly, opponents of "abstinence-only" argue that most teens know neither of those ideas is true and since, why should teens even listen...

This should be divided and clarified:
> Opponents of abstinence-only education argue that most teenagers know these claims are false. As a result, they may stop listening to the message altogether.

D. Word choice/spelling
“prevelant” → prevalent
“pregnancy cases” → better: pregnancy rates or teen pregnancies
“lend any type of education” → better: provide effective education

Academic tone
Be careful with informal or overly emotional wording such as:
“have become enraged”
“consider a joke”
“made up” (unless directly quoted)

A more academic tone would be:
strongly criticized
dismissed as simplistic
inaccurate or unsupported

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on this order:

Write a strong thesis
Reorganize paragraphs around single clear points
Clarify who is speaking in each paragraph
Add analysis after each example
Simplify sentence structure and correct grammar

Overall, your essay shows strong argumentative potential and a thoughtful position. The biggest improvements will come from clearer structure, more precise language, and deeper explanation of your evidence.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong engagement with a difficult ethical issue, and you do several things well: you summarize both sides, you notice weaknesses in the arguments, and you try to evaluate evidence rather than simply repeat it. This analytical approach is a real strength. For revision, I would focus on clarity of thesis, organization, precision of language, and sentence-level correctness.

Main argument: make your position clearer
Your introduction ends with: “there is still no answer as to who should have rights over a child.” This is understandable, but for an argumentative essay, the thesis should be more precise. At the moment, your essay mostly argues that both sides use weak reasoning and that each case should be judged individually. That is a stronger and clearer claim than “there is no answer.”

A possible revised thesis:
> Although both biological and adoptive parents make emotionally powerful claims, media coverage and extreme examples weaken the debate; therefore, custody disputes should be decided case by case with greater attention to evidence, the child’s welfare, and legal clarity.

This gives the reader a clear line to follow.

Organization: reduce repetition and group ideas more logically
Several body paragraphs repeat the same point: both sides rely on extreme cases, hypotheticals, or weak evidence. Instead of discussing this again and again, try grouping your essay into clear categories:

How biological parents’ supporters argue  
How adoptive parents’ supporters argue  
Why both sides are weakened by media framing and extreme cases  
Why case-by-case judgment is necessary

This would make your essay feel more controlled. Right now, some paragraphs move back and forth between summary and evaluation in a way that can confuse the reader.

Use more precise analysis
Often you say an argument is “weak,” “reasonable,” or “strong,” but you do not always explain fully why. Try to name the problem more directly:
Is it an appeal to emotion?
Is it overgeneralization?
Is it a contradiction?
Is it insufficient evidence?
Is it a false dilemma?

For example, instead of:
> “This is a somewhat strong argument...”

you could write:
> “This example is emotionally persuasive, but as a single unusual case it does not justify a general rule.”

That sounds more academic and analytical.

Be careful with tone
Some phrases are too informal or dramatic for academic writing:
“Blood is thicker than water”
“saints”
“evildoers”
“scare tactics”
“shock value”

These can be used if you are quoting or analyzing rhetoric, but in your own voice, a more formal style would be better. For example:
“supporters privilege biological ties”
“the media idealized adoptive parents”
“the example relies heavily on emotional impact”

Missing citations / incomplete evidence
There are several places marked with , which interrupt the essay and weaken credibility. If these are missing quotations or statistics, they must be completed. Also, when you mention cases, articles, psychologists, or the National Council for Adoption, make sure the source is clearly introduced and accurately used.

At times, your essay criticizes others for lacking evidence, but your own argument also needs stronger support. If possible, add:
one or two reliable legal facts,
one source about child psychology,
one source about media framing or adoption law.

Language issues to revise
Because you are a German-speaking writer, some sentences show patterns common for German learners: awkward word choice, article use, and plural/singular agreement. A few examples:

“proponents for biological parents” → better: “supporters of biological parents”
“opponents to biological parents” → “opponents of biological parents”
“each cases” → “each case”
“could possible happen” → “could possibly happen”
“on could argue” → “one could argue”
“isn’t fair to both sides” → better: “does not do justice to either side”

Also watch pronouns. You often use “him” or “his/her” for “the child.” In formal modern English, “them/their” is smoother:
> “raise and love them”  
> “the child may view the move as their fault”

Sentence style
Some sentences are long and overloaded with ideas. Try to shorten them and make one point at a time. For example:

Original:
> “Because the media chose to pick out extreme cases of adoption battles, they created a false dilemma.”

Possible revision:
> “By focusing on extreme custody battles, the media created a false dilemma. The public was encouraged to see the issue as a simple choice between biological and adoptive rights.”

This improves readability and emphasis.

Conclusion
Your conclusion has good emotional force, but it becomes speculative at the end. Try to return more directly to your thesis. Emphasize your main insight: the debate remains unresolved because both sides oversimplify complex individual cases.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so your claim is explicit.  
Reorganize paragraphs by theme.  
Replace repeated “weak/strong” judgments with precise analytical terms.  
Fix incomplete quotations and add stronger evidence.  
Edit for grammar and more formal academic style.

Overall, this is a thoughtful essay with a strong critical instinct. Your next step is to make the structure tighter and the analysis more precise.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and an appropriate argumentative structure: introduction, body paragraphs with different solutions, and a conclusion. Your essay shows engagement with the topic and attempts to compare alternatives rather than simply describe them. That is a strong basis for revision.

Main strengths

Clear topic: The essay consistently focuses on cheating in higher education.
Argumentative intent: You do not only summarize sources; you evaluate solutions and state your own view.
Use of examples: The references to the University of Virginia and the “XF” grade help make the discussion more concrete.
Personal stance: Your conclusion clearly states which solution you support.

Most important revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction suggests that two methods are strongest, but later you argue that honor codes are the best method. This creates some inconsistency.

For example, in paragraph 1 you write that the authors show that revamping the academic code and establishing strict honor codes are the two strongest methods, but in the conclusion you clearly prefer honor codes.

Revise the thesis so it matches the whole essay. For example:
*Although both honor codes and stricter academic penalties can reduce cheating, a strong honor code is the most effective long-term solution because it shapes student values as well as behavior.*

B. Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs begin with one point and then move to another without a clear transition. Try to give each paragraph one main claim.

For example:
Paragraph 2 is mainly about honor codes, but it also includes your personal experience and then suddenly mentions Harvard and peer pressure.
Paragraph 4 starts with teacher involvement, but it is not clearly connected to your main comparison between honor codes and academic penalties.

Suggestion: Organize the essay like this:
Introduction + thesis  
Honor codes: strengths  
Honor codes: weaknesses + response  
Stricter academic penalties: strengths  
Penalties: weaknesses  
Why honor codes are better overall  
Conclusion

C. Explain your evidence more fully
You often mention examples, but sometimes you do not explain why they support your claim.

Example:
*“The ‘XF’ grade has had proven successes…”*  
This is useful evidence, but explain what it shows: Does it deter students because it affects future employment? Does it create a visible and lasting consequence?

Try the pattern:
claim → evidence → explanation.

Language and style

A. Word choice and collocations
Some phrases sound unnatural in English, which is common for German-speaking writers. Watch for literal translations or awkward combinations.

Examples:
*“major question of value”* → better: major moral issue
*“are unsuccessful”* → better: have not been effective
*“taken in account”* → taken into account
*“just stands”* → firm positions or clear stands
*“prevailing issue”* → serious issue or growing problem
*“to eliminated”* → to eliminate
*“on a first hand basis”* → firsthand
*“ensure and honest academic environment”* → ensure an honest academic environment

B. Grammar issues
Pay attention especially to:
Articles: *an honest environment*, *the honor code*
Prepositions: *obligation to myself*, *presented with the school’s honor code*
Subject-verb agreement: *honor codes require… and encourage…*
Word forms: *to eliminate*, *less likely to cheat* rather than *less reluctant to cheat*

Example:
*“When teachers, as role models, do not take just stands then students tend to be less reluctant to cheat.”*  
This sentence is unclear. Better:
*When teachers fail to take a firm stand against cheating, students may feel more willing to cheat as well.*

C. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too vague because key information is missing.

Examples:
*“Mr Levine of Harvard believes that .”*
*“Trying to curtail cheaters through the court system is weak because of the .”*

You need to complete these ideas fully. If evidence or quotations are missing, either add them or remove the incomplete references.

Academic writing concerns

A. Avoid overusing “the authors”
You repeatedly say *“the authors show…”* or *“authors that advocate…”*. If possible, name the source or summarize more directly. This will sound more academic and specific.

B. Integrate personal opinion carefully
Your personal experience can be useful, but in an academic argument it should support—not replace—evidence from sources. For example, the sentence *“As a student, I believe…”* is fine, but it should be followed by stronger reasoning or research-based support.

C. Handle counterarguments more clearly
You do mention weaknesses, which is good. Now make the rebuttal explicit:
What is the main weakness of honor codes? Peer pressure.
Why are honor codes still better despite this? Because they build a culture of honesty and prevention, not only punishment.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these three steps:
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly argues that honor codes are the best solution.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one has one clear purpose.
Edit sentence-level language for natural English, especially articles, prepositions, and word choice.

Overall, this essay has a solid argumentative foundation. With a clearer thesis, better paragraph structure, and more precise language, it can become a strong academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows a clear understanding of the controversy around the Fourth Amendment and the Simpson case. You identify both sides of the debate and explain the rhetorical strategies they use. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative analysis. For revision, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and polishing language.

Thesis and purpose

At the moment, your essay sometimes sounds like a summary of both sides, and sometimes like an argument about which side is stronger. The final paragraph says that “whether one side’s reasons seem more credible… is not the issue,” but earlier you evaluate strengths and weaknesses. This creates some confusion.

Revise by deciding exactly what your paper argues. For example:

Are you arguing that the prosecution’s justification was more persuasive?
Are you arguing that the defense more effectively raised doubt?
Or are you arguing that both sides used rhetorical strategies effectively?

If your goal is rhetorical analysis, state that clearly in the introduction. A stronger thesis might be:

> In the debate over the admissibility of evidence from Simpson’s home, the prosecution relies mainly on emotional appeals and judicial authority, while the defense focuses on police credibility and procedural inconsistency; although both strategies are effective, the defense raises deeper doubts about the reliability of the evidence.

This would give the essay a clearer direction.

Organization

Your overall structure is logical: introduction, background, prosecution/proposition, opposition, conclusion. However, some paragraphs are too broad and mix several ideas.

Suggestions:
Keep one main point per paragraph.
Begin each body paragraph with a topic sentence that clearly states the paragraph’s claim.
Make sure each paragraph connects back to your thesis.

For example, your paragraph about the prosecution includes:
emotional appeal,
hypothetical danger,
sympathy for police,
respect for authority.

These could be divided into two paragraphs: one on emotional appeal, one on appeal to authority.

Use of evidence

A major weakness is that some quotations are missing and replaced by “”. This makes parts of the essay incomplete. Since you refer to specific articles, the reader needs the actual quotation or a more precise paraphrase.

Also, some claims need stronger support. For example:
“They deserve to have their decisions supported by the court” sounds more like your opinion than analysis.
“This is a definite strength in the opposition’s argument” is fine as evaluation, but you should explain why it is persuasive.

Try to do more analysis after evidence:
What does the quote show?
Why would this persuade readers?
How does it support your thesis?

A useful pattern is: claim → evidence → explanation.

Style and academic tone

Your tone is often energetic and engaged, which is good, but some phrases are too informal or imprecise for academic writing.

Examples:
“pull sympathy out of the readers” → evoke sympathy from readers
“scratching to find anything” → struggling to find grounds for objection
“did a little homework” → investigated Fuhrman’s background
“picking on the L.A.P.D.” → targeting the LAPD’s credibility

Also, avoid overstated claims unless supported by evidence. For instance, “There is no reason, then…” is too absolute.

Grammar and wording

Your meaning is usually clear, but there are several language issues to correct. As a German speaker, you may want to watch especially for prepositions, word choice, capitalization, and sentence structure.

Important corrections:
except for in → except in
Los Angelas → Los Angeles
Ronald Goldwin → likely Ronald Goldman
question the validity → questions the validity / question the validity of
one way → One way
revolved from one attempting to prove → evolved from one attempting to prove
the detective's pasts → the detectives’ pasts or the detective’s past
lured them into the house is awkward; better: prompted them to enter the house
4th Amendment: in formal academic writing, spell it as Fourth Amendment

Sentence-level issue:
Some sentences are long and crowded with ideas. Break them into shorter sentences when needed. This will improve clarity.

Conclusion

Your conclusion should do more than repeat that both sides argued effectively. It should return to your main thesis and show what your analysis demonstrates.

For example, instead of ending only with a practical comment about search warrants, you could say:

> Ultimately, the case shows that disputes over the Fourth Amendment are shaped not only by legal interpretation but also by how persuasively each side frames police conduct, emergency, and credibility.

This gives the essay a stronger final insight.

Revision priorities

Write a clearer thesis in the introduction.
Replace missing quotations and cite evidence more precisely.
Separate body paragraphs by main idea.
Add more analysis after examples.
Revise for academic tone and grammar.

You have a strong topic and good instincts about rhetoric. With a sharper thesis and cleaner structure, this could become a much more persuasive and polished essay.
