Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting and important claim: many arguments against suicide may sound persuasive, but they are often not supported with enough evidence. This is a clear argumentative direction, and throughout the essay you consistently return to that main idea. That consistency is a strength.

Main strengths
Clear focus: You stay on one central argument: opponents of suicide often fail to provide evidence.
Organized by themes: The essay is divided into sections on selfishness, family, religion, and AIDS/assisted suicide. This gives the paper a visible structure.
Critical thinking: You do not simply repeat sources; you question their logic and ask whether they actually prove their claims.

Main areas to revise

A. Clarify your thesis
Your introduction circles around the topic, but the thesis should be sharper and more precise. At the moment, some sentences sound too broad or too judgmental, for example:

“Suicide means the end of all problems”
“Though those problems are usually solved easily.”

These statements are too absolute and may weaken your credibility. Also, they do not fit well with the more analytical tone of the rest of the essay.

Suggestion: End the introduction with a thesis such as:
> This essay argues that common objections to suicide—especially claims about selfishness, harm to family, religion, and assisted suicide—are often presented without sufficient evidence.

B. Improve use of sources
A major problem is that several quotations are missing and replaced by . Because of this, the reader cannot judge whether your interpretation is accurate. If you analyze a source, you must include the exact quotation or summarize it precisely.

Also, you often say a source gives “no evidence,” but sometimes your essay does not explain what kind of evidence would count: statistics? case studies? expert testimony? interviews? Clarify this.

Suggestion: After each source, briefly explain:
what the author claims,
what evidence they give,
why that evidence is strong or weak.

C. Strengthen logical development
Sometimes your criticism is reasonable, but the explanation is not fully developed. For example, in the paragraph on “selfishness,” you argue that a dying cancer patient may think of family members when choosing suicide. This is a good counterexample. However, it would be stronger if you explicitly connected it to your claim:
> This example shows that suicide cannot automatically be called selfish, because some people may believe they are reducing the suffering of others.

Likewise, some sentences rely on assumptions:
“I assumed that Parks and Weiss are knowledgeable…”
This sounds uncertain. In academic writing, try to verify credentials rather than guess.

D. Tone and sensitivity
This topic requires a careful academic tone. Some phrases sound too casual, harsh, or overly certain. For example:
“the person really must be selfish to receive attention”
“There is no escape from involvement with suicide”

These statements are too sweeping. Try to use more cautious language:
“some acts may be interpreted as appeals for attention or help”
“many people may encounter suicide indirectly in their lives”

E. Grammar and style
Your writing shows strong vocabulary, but there are recurring grammar/style issues:

Word choice
“the opponents of suicide often neglect to prove their statements”  
  Better: fail to support their claims
“good statements”  
  Better: plausible claims
“view this argument as a good one”  
  Better: find this argument persuasive

Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or ungrammatical:
“He should ask what does the person attempting suicide thinking?”  
  Correct: He should ask what the person attempting suicide is thinking.
“When there little safety from diseases such as these…”  
  Correct: When there is little protection from diseases such as these…
“solution” is split as “so lution.”

Articles and plurals
As a German speaker, you may want to watch article use and noun forms:
“the effects of a suicide” → the effects of suicide or the effects of a suicide on others
“different sessions with different people” → therapy sessions with different patients

Repetition
You repeat phrases such as “this argument is a good one” or “there is no evidence” many times. Try varying your wording:
unsupported
insufficiently documented
lacks empirical support
rests on assertion rather than proof

Organization revision plan
For your next draft, try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the debate
   - Present a precise thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Selfishness
   - Present source
   - Explain claim
   - Evaluate evidence
   - Offer counterexample

Body Paragraph 2: Harm to family
   - Same pattern

Body Paragraph 3: Religion
   - Same pattern
   - Note limits of religious arguments in a pluralistic society

Body Paragraph 4: Assisted suicide / slippery slope
   - Define the fallacy clearly
   - Apply it to the source

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Emphasize need for evidence and careful analysis

Final advice
Your essay has a strong critical purpose, but it needs revision in precision, evidence, tone, and grammar. The biggest priorities are:
replace missing quotations,
sharpen the thesis,
use more formal and cautious language,
correct sentence-level grammar issues.

With those improvements, your argument will become much more persuasive and academically credible.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0034.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important topic and shows real effort to use sociological theory to explain adolescent suicide. You also include statistics and references, which helps give the paper an academic direction. For revision, focus on three main areas: argument, organization, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Important topic: You clearly see adolescent suicide as a serious social issue.
Use of theory: Bringing in Durkheim and Marx/conflict theory gives the essay a strong academic basis.
Attempt to use evidence: You include data and sources, which is good for an argumentative essay.

Biggest issue: unclear thesis and argument
At the moment, the paper reads partly like an informative report and partly like an argument. Your central claim needs to be stated more clearly.

Current problem
In the introduction, you argue that adolescent suicide is “the worst problem” facing young people, but later the essay mainly explains it through theory. These are not exactly the same goal.

What to do
Choose one clear thesis, for example:
Argumentative thesis: “Adolescent suicide deserves more public attention than it currently receives because it is widespread and can be better understood through sociological theories such as Durkheim’s and conflict theory.”
Or: “Sociological theory helps explain adolescent suicide by showing how social isolation, social change, and family conditions shape adolescent depression.”

This will help the reader understand what the essay is trying to prove.

Organization and paragraph structure
Your essay has a recognizable structure, but some paragraphs are too broad or repetitive.

Suggestions
Introduction: Present the problem, give brief background, and end with a precise thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Why adolescent suicide is a major social problem (statistics, public attention, significance).
Body paragraph 2: Durkheim/structural functionalism.
Body paragraph 3: Egoistic suicide and adolescents.
Body paragraph 4: Anomic suicide and adolescents.
Body paragraph 5: Conflict theory and Marx.
Body paragraph 6: Depression as a shared factor.
Conclusion: Do not introduce a new idea; instead, summarize your main argument.

Transitions
Some transitions are weak or repetitive, especially “being that,” “thus,” and “this goes right along with.” Use more formal academic transitions:
“Furthermore”
“In contrast”
“For example”
“This suggests that…”
“Therefore”

Development of ideas
Some points are promising but underdeveloped.

Durkheim
This is the strongest part of the essay, but explain more clearly why egoistic and anomic suicide are relevant to adolescents. For example:
egoistic suicide → social isolation, lack of belonging
anomic suicide → sudden change, instability, family disruption

Conflict theory
This section is less convincing. The connection between Marx and adolescent suicide needs more explanation. Why does social conflict or inequality increase suicide risk? Are family stress, economic pressure, or social instability involved? Make this clearer.

Depression paragraph
Be careful here. The sentence “There are no theories… that argue that adolescent suicide isn't a direct result of depression” is too absolute and probably inaccurate. Suicide is usually understood as multicausal. You could revise to:
“Depression is one of the most common factors associated with adolescent suicide, although social isolation, family instability, and other pressures also play important roles.”

Use of evidence
You use many statistics, but some are old and some quotations are incomplete. Also, the placeholders `<R>` and  must be replaced with proper citations.

For revision
Check whether your sources are current and reliable.
Integrate evidence more smoothly.
Explain each statistic: why is it important?
Use a consistent citation style (MLA, APA, Chicago, etc, depending on your class).

Language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word-choice errors. Since you are a German speaker, pay special attention to word order, possessives, articles, and verb agreement.

Common issues in your essay
a. Wrong word choice
“phenomenal” usually has a positive meaning. Better: “alarming” or “striking.”
“bondness” → “bonding” or “sense of belonging”
“contraversal” → “controversial”
“publically” → “publicly”
“programed” → “programmed”

b. Grammar
“the worst problem concerning are young people” → “the worst problem concerning young people”
“its” / “it’s”:  
  - its = possessive  
  - it’s = it is
“These works and studies has looked” → “have looked”
“adolescents who has moved” → “adolescents who have moved”

c. Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter, clearer units.

For example:
Original: “Thus, relating the social problem of adolescent suicide to theoretical perspectives and social theorists that best relate to this issue of adolescent suicide giving a better social understanding of the reasons why such a phenomena exists.”
Better: “Examining adolescent suicide through sociological theory can provide a better understanding of why this phenomenon exists.”

Final advice
For your next draft:
Write a clear thesis.
Make each paragraph support that thesis.
Explain theory in simpler, more direct language.
Use evidence carefully and cite it properly.
Edit for grammar, especially verb forms, punctuation, and it’s/its.

You have a solid topic and a thoughtful theoretical approach. With a clearer argument and more careful editing, this could become a much stronger academic essay.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0035.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important topic and makes a clear effort to connect environmental issues with sociological theory. You also include concrete examples and statistics, which helps show the seriousness of water pollution. For revision, the main areas to improve are: clarity of thesis, stronger use of theory, organization, sentence style, and grammar/mechanics.

Main strengths
Relevant topic: Water pollution is socially important, and your argument has a clear purpose.
Use of examples: The opening examples (oil leaks, fertilizer, waste from boats) are vivid and effective.
Attempt to apply theory: You do more than just describe pollution; you try to interpret it through Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory.
Sense of urgency: Your conclusion shows strong engagement with the issue.

Most important revision priority: sharpen the thesis
Right now, the paper says it will discuss water pollution and its relationship to the two theories, but it does not clearly state what your argument is. A stronger thesis should explain how each theory helps us understand water pollution.

For example, you could write something like:

> This paper argues that water pollution can be understood through Rational Choice Theory because individuals and companies act according to perceived benefits and available knowledge, and through Exchange Theory because behavior changes when costs, rewards, and consequences become personal.

This gives the essay a clearer direction.

Organization and development
At the moment, some paragraphs read more like a list of facts than a step-by-step argument. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Explain the problem of water pollution
   - Introduce the two theories
   - Present your thesis

Background on water pollution
   - Define water pollution
   - Briefly explain types/sources
   - Keep this section shorter unless it directly supports your argument

Rational Choice Theory
   - Define the theory clearly
   - Explain how it applies to individuals
   - Explain how it applies to companies
   - Use 1–2 strong examples

Exchange Theory
   - Define the theory clearly
   - Explain costs/rewards in pollution behavior
   - Show how laws, education, and personal consequences can change behavior

Conclusion
   - Summarize the argument
   - End with a strong final statement

Use the theories more precisely
This is the biggest conceptual issue. Sometimes the essay mentions the theories, but the explanation stays vague.

Rational Choice Theory
You suggest that people and companies will make better decisions if they know the consequences. That is a good starting point. But Rational Choice Theory usually means that actors weigh costs and benefits and choose what seems most beneficial to them. So ask:
Why would a company pollute?
What are the short-term benefits?
What would make clean behavior seem more rational?
How do laws, fines, education, and public pressure change the calculation?

Exchange Theory
You discuss rewards, costs, and reinforcement, which fits better. However, some examples are not fully convincing. For example, saying people stop drinking bad-tasting water is more about reaction to poor quality than about the social exchange process. You need to connect more directly:
Polluting may bring rewards: low disposal cost, convenience, profit
Preventing pollution must also have rewards: avoiding fines, better reputation, healthier communities

This would make your application of theory stronger and more academic.

Style and academic tone
Try to avoid repeating phrases such as “society needs,” “people need,” or “therefore” too often. Also, some sentences are very long and hard to follow. Use shorter, more direct sentences.

For example:

Original:
> Therefore, studies need to be done or presented to these individuals so that they can, in turn, see, know and understand water pollution.

Revised:
> Therefore, individuals and companies need access to clear evidence about the causes and consequences of water pollution.

This sounds more formal and concise.

Grammar and language corrections
Because you are a German speaker, some of your sentence structure and word choice may be influenced by German. Below are common issues in the essay:

Possessive forms
its' → its
ones' behavior → one’s behavior

Word choice / form
divide then into → divide them into
human race → the human race or better human beings
war pollution threat → probably water pollution threat
insure the cooperation → ensure cooperation
resign themselves from interacting with it → stop interacting with it

Grammar
The main source of these agents are → The main source of these agents is
human excrement which are untreated → untreated human excrement, which enters the water cycle
there are two- non-point and point source water pollution → There are two types of water pollution: non-point source and point source pollution.

Punctuation / mechanics
Avoid unnecessary hyphens: this-water, two- non-point
Remove double words: to to
Add commas carefully, but do not overuse them.

Missing material
Your draft contains several placeholders like  <R>. These interrupt the essay and make parts of the argument incomplete. Be sure to replace all of them before submitting the final version.

Final advice
For revision, focus on:
Writing a precise thesis
Defining each theory clearly
Explaining exactly how each theory applies to pollution behavior
Reducing repetition
Correcting grammar and missing sections

You have a solid topic and useful material. With clearer argumentation and more precise theory application, this can become a much stronger academic essay.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0036.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

You present a clear pro-legalization position and include several different arguments: medical use, prison overcrowding, industrial uses, crime, and tax revenue. Your essay shows ambition and strong engagement with the topic. For revision, focus mainly on organization, academic tone, accuracy, and language control.

Main strengths
Clear argumentative purpose: Your position is unmistakable.
Range of supporting points: You do not rely on only one reason; this makes the essay more persuasive.
Use of sociological theories: Bringing in Structural Functionalism, Exchange Theory, and Conflict Theory gives the essay an academic framework.
Historical context: The legal history helps readers understand the debate.

Most important revision priorities

A. Create a sharper thesis
Your introduction contains a lot of background, but the main argument comes late and is too broad. A strong thesis should state your claim and the main reasons.

Current problem:  
“This paper will focus on…” is repetitive and sounds like a plan, not an argument.

Better approach:  
“Marijuana should be legalized because it has significant medical value, criminalization worsens social problems such as prison overcrowding and unequal justice, and legalization would create economic benefits through taxation and industrial production.”

This is more direct and academic.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs are very long and mix evidence, opinion, and theory. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
History/legal background (shorter)
Medical marijuana
Criminalization and prisons
Social inequality and justice system
Industrial/economic benefits
Crime and black market
Conclusion

At the moment, the sociological theories sometimes seem added afterward. Instead, make each paragraph follow this pattern:
topic sentence
evidence/example
explanation
connection to theory
mini-conclusion

C. Use a more academic tone
Some vocabulary is too informal or emotional for an argumentative essay.

Examples:
“pot,” “weed,” “getting high” → better: marijuana, cannabis, recreational use, intoxication
“simply amazing” → too emotional; better: notable
rhetorical questions like “Would it not make sense…?” → better as statements

Also avoid exaggerated claims unless you can prove them, for example:
“not a single death…”  
“the black market would also very soon disappear”

These statements need very strong evidence.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many facts, but several points need better source handling and more caution.

Problems:
Some claims seem outdated or unsupported.
Placeholder citations like <R> and  are not acceptable in a final draft.
Some comparisons may be inaccurate, for example classifying drugs or discussing medical effects without balanced evidence.

Advice:
Use a consistent citation style required by your course.
Add recent and reliable sources.
Distinguish between fact, expert opinion, and your interpretation.
Acknowledge counterarguments fairly. For example, discuss concerns about dependency, impaired driving, or youth use before responding to them.

This will make your argument stronger, not weaker.

Develop analysis, not only opinion
Sometimes the essay jumps from evidence to conclusion too quickly. For example, when discussing prison overcrowding, explain how legalization would reduce burden on courts and prisons, and whether this depends on full legalization or only decriminalization.

Also, be careful with theory:
Conflict Theory: good fit for unequal enforcement across classes.
Structural Functionalism: useful for discussing law, medicine, and economic institutions.
Exchange Theory: can work, but “rewards outweigh costs” becomes repetitive. Use it more precisely.

Language corrections
Because your first language is German, some errors are typical and fixable. Watch for:

A. Spelling/word choice
cannavis sativa → *Cannabis sativa*
heroine → *heroin*
foe → *for*
mar on drugs → *war on drugs*
ideal → probably *illegal*
breakins → *break-ins*

B. Capitalization
In English, capitalization is more limited than in German.
not Few, Not, United states
use United States
theory names are usually capitalized only if your instructor requires it consistently.

C. Sentence punctuation
Some sentences are run-ons or have comma splices. Example:

“Even with all of this being stated the DEA will still not legalize marijuana for medical purposes.”

Better:
“Despite this evidence, the DEA did not legalize marijuana for medical purposes.”

D. Articles and plurals
industrial fabrics may not be the best phrase; perhaps industrial products, textiles, or hemp fiber products
overcrowded prisons is → *overcrowded prisons are*
doctors supervision → *doctors’ supervision* or *a doctor’s supervision*

E. Hyphenation and formatting
Be consistent:
first-time offenders
mandatory minimum sentences
Schedule I / Schedule II

Conclusion advice
Your conclusion restates your position, but it should summarize the strongest reasons more clearly and avoid introducing dramatic new claims. End with a concise final judgment about why legalization is the better policy.

Final recommendation
For the next draft, focus on:
rewriting the thesis
reorganizing paragraphs by topic
replacing informal language
adding proper citations
correcting grammar and spelling
using theory more precisely

You have a strong base and a clear viewpoint. With better structure and more careful academic style, this could become a convincing university-level argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0037.2.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Essay

Your essay shows real strengths: you take homelessness seriously as a social problem, you include historical background, and you try to apply two sociological theories rather than only giving opinions. That gives your paper ambition and intellectual range. For revision, the main goal is to make your argument clearer, more focused, and better supported by evidence.

Main argument and thesis

At the moment, your essay discusses:
the history of homelessness,
causes of homelessness,
conflict theory,
symbolic interactionism,
and a final claim that the micro-level view is “better.”

These parts do not always connect into one strong argument. Your thesis should clearly answer: What exactly are you arguing?

For example, a stronger thesis could be:
*Although homelessness has many structural causes, symbolic interactionism is the most useful theory for understanding how stigma and labeling keep homeless people trapped in poverty.*
or
*Homelessness is best understood through both conflict theory and symbolic interactionism, but conflict theory better explains its structural causes.*

Then each paragraph should support that exact claim.

Organization

The essay is very long and often repeats similar ideas. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - define the issue
   - give brief context
   - present your thesis

Background on homelessness
   - short historical overview only if it supports your argument

Causes of homelessness
   - housing costs
   - unemployment
   - deinstitutionalization
   - inequality/discrimination

Conflict theory
   - explain the theory accurately
   - apply it to homelessness with examples

Symbolic interactionism
   - explain the theory accurately
   - apply it to homelessness with examples

Comparison
   - explain which theory is more useful and why

Conclusion
   - restate your argument
   - show why it matters

This will help your reader follow your reasoning.

Use of evidence

A major weakness is that many claims are broad but not supported. For example:
“The powerful people in society are immensely greedy…”
“The government… tagged the homeless as lazy…”
“Drug use has increased dramatically in the homeless community.”

These may reflect real debates, but in academic writing you need evidence, not general statements. Add:
statistics,
scholarly sources,
historical examples,
quotations properly introduced and cited.

Also, there is one incomplete citation: *“which states that .”* This must be completed or removed.

Be careful not to make claims that sound too absolute. Instead of “The government only sees…,” write:
*Some political leaders portray homelessness primarily as a public-order issue rather than a housing issue.*

That sounds more academic and precise.

Analysis of theory

You show a good attempt to use sociological theory, but some explanations are oversimplified or inaccurate.

Conflict theory
Good idea: linking homelessness to inequality and power.

Needs revision:
Avoid emotional wording like “immensely greedy.”
Explain more precisely how unequal access to housing, jobs, and political influence creates homelessness.
Distinguish between structural inequality and individual attitudes.

Symbolic interactionism
Good idea: discussing labels and stigma.

Needs revision:
“Homelessness is only a social problem because people define it as such” needs careful phrasing. Otherwise, it sounds as if homelessness is not materially real.
Explain labeling theory more carefully: labels affect identity, treatment, and opportunities.

Your final paragraph says the micro-level view is better, but much of your paper strongly emphasizes structural causes. So your conclusion partly contradicts earlier sections. Revise so your ending matches the evidence you presented.

Style and tone

Your tone is passionate, which is good, but academic writing needs more neutrality and precision. Avoid phrases like:
“mindlessly follow”
“atrocious things”
“immense time and effort”
“constant thorn in the sides of all Americans”

Choose calmer academic wording.

Grammar and language

You communicate many ideas clearly, but there are recurring grammar and word-choice problems. Common issues:

Subject-verb agreement
“People living without shelter has been a problem” → “People living without shelter have been…”
“the number of homeless that are mentally ill are” → “the number… is”

Word choice
Some phrases sound unnatural in English:
“lobs” → “jobs”
“live threat” → “real threat”
“job permanence” → “job security”
“abundant amount” → “large number”
“mayor cause” → “major cause”
“effects” → “affects”

Articles and prepositions
“in order the ‘clean our cities’” → “in order to ‘clean up our cities’”
“used educate people” → “used to educate people”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward. Example:
“There is an agreement between all opposing viewpoints in government that something must be done that to combat homelessness.”
Better:
*Across the political spectrum, there is general agreement that homelessness requires government action.*

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis
Reduce repetition
Use evidence for claims
Make theory explanations more precise
Adopt a more academic tone
Edit carefully for grammar and wording

You have a strong foundation and an important topic. With a clearer argument and better support, this could become a much stronger academic essay.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0038.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear ambition: you do not simply describe welfare policy, but compare two theoretical frameworks (Spencer and Merton) and use them to evaluate reform versus abolition. This gives the paper intellectual depth, and your conclusion shows that you are trying to arrive at a balanced position rather than a simplistic one. That is a real strength.

Main strengths
Strong topic and scope: You address history, policy, sociology, and ethics.
Clear contrast structure: Spencer vs. Merton is an effective organizing idea.
Good use of evidence: You include statistics, programs, and examples.
Independent voice: Your conclusion makes your own position visible.

Most important revision priority: sharpen the thesis
At the beginning, the essay asks, “Why does welfare exist?” but the final position is really: welfare should not be abolished, but reformed in realistic ways that address structural barriers while also limiting abuse. State this clearly in the introduction.

For example, your thesis could be:
> Although critics argue that welfare encourages dependency, the welfare system should be reformed rather than abolished because many recipients face structural disadvantages in education, employment, and family support; at the same time, reform must address long-term dependency and misuse.

This will help unify the essay.

Organization and focus
Right now, the essay is thoughtful but too long and sometimes repetitive. Consider this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Brief history of welfare
Argument for abolition: Spencer / conservative critique
Argument for reform: Merton / structural inequality
Your evaluation: reform is better, but must be realistic
Conclusion

Specific issue:
Some paragraphs only list statistics or anecdotes without explaining how they support your argument. After each example, add 1–2 sentences of analysis:
What does this example prove?
Does it support Spencer, Merton, or your own position?

Use of evidence
You use many facts, but sometimes the evidence feels selective or emotionally loaded. Be careful with examples that seem designed to portray recipients negatively. A university argument is stronger when it acknowledges complexity.

For example:
One anecdote about a welfare recipient does not prove a general pattern.
Statistics need interpretation and context.
If you mention missing quotations () or references (`<R>`), those must be completed or removed.

Right now, the placeholders seriously weaken credibility. Before submitting, make sure:
all quotations are complete,
all sources are cited consistently,
all placeholder marks are deleted.

Academic tone and word choice
Some language is too judgmental for academic writing. Try to describe arguments without adopting biased wording yourself.

Problematic expressions:
“too lazy to work”
“illegitimate births”
“their laziness”
“allow their extinction”

If you are presenting a conservative or Spencerian view, signal this clearly:
> Conservatives argue that…
> From Spencer’s perspective…
> Critics claim that…

This avoids sounding as if you personally endorse extreme claims.

Style and clarity
Your sentences are often sophisticated, but sometimes awkward or imprecise. As a German speaker, you may naturally produce longer sentences with abstract nouns. In English academic writing, shorter and more direct sentences are often stronger.

Examples of corrections:
“Me United States” → the United States
“exploded in to” → exploded into
“deferent men” → different men
“work force” → workforce
“abolishment” → abolition
“on the basis that” is better than “based this abolition on the basis that”
“This writer refuses to believe…” → better: I argue that or This essay argues that
“he no useful place” → has no useful place
“Consider, for example. the father…” → punctuation error; should be Consider, for example, the father…
“<R>..” → remove extra punctuation

Grammar patterns to watch
A few patterns are especially worth revising:

Articles
German and English use articles differently, so check:
the welfare system
in the labor market
support from the government

Commas
Do not put commas between subject and verb:
Incorrect: “The best explanation for this idea is Robert Merton’s Matthew Effect (1968) which…”
Better: “The best explanation for this idea is Robert Merton’s Matthew Effect (1968), which…”

Parallel structure
“how to write a resume, fill out job applications, and talk to a receptionist”
not “how to write…, how to fill out…, and how to talk…” unless you want full repetition

Wordiness
“the process whereby advantages in one area can be translated into advantages in another” is fine, but many other sentences could be trimmed.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these steps in order:
Complete or remove all placeholders and references.
Write a sharper thesis in the introduction.
Cut repetition and shorten the historical background.
Add analysis after evidence.
Use more neutral academic language.
Proofread for grammar, punctuation, and spelling.

Overall, this is an intelligent and ambitious essay with strong potential. Your best revision strategy is not to add more material, but to make the argument clearer, more focused, and more academically controlled.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0039.2.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Essay

Your essay shows strong effort and clear knowledge of sociological theories. A major strength is organization by theory: symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory. This gives the essay an academic structure and shows that you understand the topic from several perspectives. You also use examples and refer to research, which makes the essay sound informed.

To improve the paper, focus on argument, clarity, and language accuracy.

Main issue: your essay is more explanatory than argumentative
You call this an argumentative essay, but much of it describes theories rather than making and defending one central claim. Your implied argument seems to be: divorce has risen because social changes have weakened marriage, and better education/preparation could reduce divorce. Make that your thesis clearly in the introduction.

For example, your last sentence of the introduction could be stronger:
Current version: “Several different theories can be used to analyze why divorce exists and why the divorce rate has increased.”
Better argumentative version: “Although divorce has many causes, the rise in divorce is best explained by changing expectations of marriage, shifting gender and economic roles, and weaker social pressures to remain married.”

Then, in each body section, do not only explain the theory; show how it supports your main claim.

Improve paragraph focus
Some paragraphs are clear, but others combine too many ideas. Try this structure:

Topic sentence: one clear claim
Evidence/example
Explanation
Link back to thesis

For example, the paragraph on changing sex roles could better connect to the thesis:
changing expectations → conflict/uncertainty → marital dissatisfaction → higher divorce risk

Be careful with logic and tone
Some claims sound too absolute or outdated. For instance:
“Marriage is a way men maintain their control…”  
“Other causes include… homosexuality.”

These statements need more careful wording. In academic writing, avoid presenting complex social issues as simple facts unless your source clearly supports them. You could write:
“Conflict theorists argue that marriage can reflect broader gender inequalities in society.”
If discussing sexuality, be very cautious and contextualize it historically or theoretically.

Also, avoid moralizing language such as “people should not get a divorce just because it is easier.” That sounds judgmental. A more academic tone would be:
“Easier access to divorce may reduce legal barriers, but it does not address the underlying causes of marital dissatisfaction.”

Use sources more effectively
You include many citation markers (“<R>”), which is good, but often the sources are just inserted rather than discussed. After citing, explain:
Why is this source relevant?
How does it support your point?
Does it show a trend, a theory, or a consequence?

Also, be careful with statistics such as “two-thirds of all first marriages will end in divorce.” If this is an older source, say so, because such predictions may not be accurate today.

Language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word-choice problems. As a German speaker, watch especially for article use, word order, prepositions, and false friends.

Common patterns to revise:
“has stabilized for the time” → “has stabilized for now” / “for the time being”
“coning” → “coming”
“take for granted of what to expect” → “take for granted what to expect”
“then own job” → “their own job”
“it was once a symbol” → capitalize: “It”
“other pasts of the social system” → “other parts”
“because of a fob” → “because of a job”
“must decided” → “must decide”
“moves forward divorce” → “moves toward divorce”
“satisfactions securities” → “satisfactions, securities,”
“Children many times” → “Children often”
“Society wilt always” → “Society will always”
“With me changing sex roles” → “With the changing sex roles”
“easier then working” → “easier than working”
“Piety needs to make” → probably “Society needs to make”
“has involved through history” → “has evolved through history”
“may possible lower” → “may possibly lower”
“there will stronger marriages” → “there will be stronger marriages”

Style and sentence fluency
Many sentences are short and repetitive: “We have seen… We have also looked…” This weakens the conclusion. Try more formal academic phrasing.

For example:
Current: “We have seen, through several different theories, how the changes in the family have caused the divorce rate to rise.”
Better: “Taken together, these theories suggest that rising divorce rates are closely connected to broader changes in family structure, gender roles, and social expectations.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points but does not fully synthesize them. A strong conclusion should:
Restate the thesis,
Summarize the most important causes,
End with a thoughtful final insight.

Revision priorities
Write a clearer thesis.
Make each paragraph support that thesis.
Reduce summary and increase analysis.
Correct grammar and word choice.
Use a more neutral academic tone.

Overall, this is a thoughtful and well-informed draft with a solid sociological foundation. With clearer argumentation and careful editing, it can become a much stronger academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that coaches accused of “running up the score” are often acting rationally because rankings, money, awards, and job security reward large wins. This is a strong argumentative idea, and you give concrete football examples to support it. You also show awareness of the opposing view, which is important in persuasive writing.

What is working well

Clear topic and opinion  
   Your main claim is understandable: coaches should not be blamed so easily for running up the score.

Relevant examples  
   The discussion of Steve Spurrier, rankings, bowl games, the Heisman Trophy, and fan support gives your essay substance.

Logical overall structure  
   Each body paragraph presents a different reason:
   - rankings
   - second/third-string players
   - play-calling
   - individual awards
   - money/job security
   - fan expectations

This is a good basis for revision.

---

Main areas to improve

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your essay suggests several related claims, but the main argument could be stated more directly in the introduction.

For example, instead of ending the introduction with a question, write a thesis such as:

“Although the NCAA criticizes coaches for running up the score, these coaches are often responding to a system that rewards large margins of victory, individual statistics, and financial success.”

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

---

Improve paragraph unity and topic sentences
Some paragraphs begin vaguely or drift between ideas. Each paragraph should start with one clear controlling idea.

For example:

Current: “I don't believe that in most cases, these coaches run the score up purposely.”
Better: “In many cases, coaches are not deliberately humiliating opponents; instead, substitute players continue to perform at a high level.”

This makes the argument more academic and focused.

---

Use a more formal academic tone
The essay often sounds conversational, which weakens your credibility. Avoid expressions such as:

“getting their butts beat bad”
“nice guy”
“jerk”
“No one knows”
“I have been sitting here talking”

Replace them with more formal language:

“losing heavily”
“acted cautiously”
“appears unsportsmanlike”
“This contradiction suggests…”
simply remove self-referential comments

Also avoid rhetorical questions too often. They can be effective once, but in an academic argument, direct statements are usually stronger.

---

Strengthen grammar and sentence structure
There are many run-on sentences, comma splices, and punctuation problems. This is the most important language issue to revise.

Example:
Original:  
“It’s fourth down and just two yards to go with a forty-nine to seven lead, will they go for it?”

Better:  
“It is fourth down with only two yards to go, and the team already leads 49–7. Will the coach still go for it?”

Another example:
Original:  
“Florida was clearly the better team, so why did the NCAA, who discourages running the score up, give the better ranking to a team that did just that.”

Better:  
“Florida was clearly the better team, so why did the NCAA, which discourages running up the score, reward another team for doing exactly that?”

Important grammar points:
Use its for possession, not it’s  
  - “its ranking” not “it’s ranking”
Use too instead of to when you mean “also”  
  - “smaller teams do it too”
Use insurmountable instead of unsurmountable
Be careful with subject-verb structure:
  - “part of the reason coaches run up the score is…”
Avoid extra commas:
  - “what can a coach do but pass?”

---

Be careful with wording and accuracy
Some phrases are unclear or imprecise:

“call everything on the ground” → better: “call only running plays”
“this little pay-cut” → this is the wrong expression; use “financial benefit” or “revenue”
“it all tables up” → use “it all adds up”
“secured a position” → better: “secure their positions”

Because you are writing in English as a non-native speaker, checking idiomatic expressions will help a lot.

---

Add more balance and acknowledge objections
Your argument would be stronger if you briefly admitted that critics have a valid concern. For example:

“Critics argue that continuing to score against much weaker opponents is unsportsmanlike. However, in a system that rewards margin of victory and individual statistics, coaches face strong incentives to keep scoring.”

This makes your essay sound more mature and persuasive.

---

Revise the conclusion
Your conclusion restates the point, but it could be stronger if it goes beyond naming the coaches again. Try to end with a broader insight:

“Therefore, blaming individual coaches misses the larger problem: college football rewards dominant victories while publicly condemning them. Until that system changes, coaches will continue to feel pressure to win by as much as possible.”

That gives the essay a more powerful ending.

---

Final revision priorities

Write a clearer thesis in the introduction.
Fix run-on sentences and punctuation.
Replace informal expressions with formal academic language.
Strengthen topic sentences in each paragraph.
Add one short counterargument and response.
Rewrite the conclusion to emphasize the larger system, not only individual coaches.

You have a solid argument and good supporting ideas. With clearer structure and more formal language, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and engages seriously with a controversial issue. You also do something effective in argumentative writing: you acknowledge the opposing view before presenting your own. This gives your essay a stronger sense of balance. In addition, your personal connection to the topic makes the argument sound committed and sincere.

That said, your essay needs revision in three main areas: thesis and consistency, evidence and logic, and style/language.

Thesis and overall argument

Your introduction ends with a clear claim: the Confederate battle flag should not fly over the South Carolina State Capitol. This is a strong starting point.

However, later your argument changes. In the middle and at the end, you argue that the “real” Confederate flag should fly instead of the battle flag. This creates a major contradiction. If your thesis is that the battle flag should come down because it represents racism, that is one argument. If your thesis is that another Confederate flag should replace it because it represents heritage, that is a different and more controversial argument.

What to revise:
Decide on one central thesis and keep it consistent throughout the essay.
Ask yourself:  
  - Do you want to argue only that the battle flag should be removed?  
  - Or do you want to argue that it should be removed and replaced with another symbol?
If you keep the second idea, you must justify it much more carefully, because many readers will still connect any Confederate flag with slavery and racial oppression.

At the moment, your strongest argument is actually the simpler one: the battle flag should not fly at a state capitol because it is divisive and tied to racism, especially in its modern political use.

Evidence and reasoning

You include an important historical point: the flag was raised in 1964, during the civil rights era. This is potentially your best piece of evidence because it connects the flag to resistance against civil rights rather than only to “heritage.”

But the evidence needs to be more precise. For example:
“This was the year that the civil rights movement started” is historically inaccurate. The civil rights movement began much earlier.
“Actually, the flag was set up the day after the civil rights movement started” is also not correct as written.

Because this is an argumentative essay, factual accuracy matters a lot. If a historical claim is weak or incorrect, it weakens your credibility.

What to revise:
Replace broad claims with specific, accurate historical facts.
Explain why the 1964 decision matters. For example: if the flag was raised during the civil rights era, the timing suggests a political message tied to segregation and resistance to racial equality.
Add more evidence, not only personal opinion. You could include:
  - historical context,
  - public reactions,
  - the symbolic role of a state capitol,
  - why government buildings should represent all citizens.

A useful line of reasoning would be: a state capitol is a public institution, so it should not display a symbol that many citizens experience as exclusionary or racist.

Tone and audience awareness

Your essay sometimes uses very absolute language:
“The battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred.”
“true southerners”

These phrases may alienate readers, especially those who do not fully agree with you. In academic argument, it is usually more effective to avoid sweeping statements and instead use precise language.

For example, instead of “nothing but racism and hatred,” you could write:
“For many people, especially African Americans, the battle flag is inseparable from the history of slavery, segregation, and racial violence.”

This sounds more careful, more persuasive, and more academic.

Also, the phrase “true southerners” is problematic because it suggests that some people are more authentic than others. That can weaken your credibility.

Organization

Your essay generally follows a logical structure, but some paragraphs could be developed more clearly.

Suggested structure:
Introduction with clear thesis
Acknowledge that some see the flag as heritage
Explain why the battle flag is different in meaning and public effect
Present historical evidence about its placement in 1964
Explain why a state capitol should represent all citizens
Conclusion

If you keep the discussion of the “real Confederate flag,” make sure it directly supports your thesis. Right now it distracts from your main claim.

Language and style

Your English is understandable, but several sentences sound repetitive or slightly unnatural.

Examples:
“It holds many different beliefs for different groups of people.”  
  Better: “It has different meanings for different groups of people.”
“reflects on my history”  
  Better: “reflects my history” or “is connected to my history.”
“I was brought up to respect Southern tradition and way.”  
  Better: “I was brought up to respect Southern traditions and values.”
“succeeded from the union”  
  Correct word: “seceded from the Union.”

Also watch capitalization:
“Civil War”
“Union”
“South”
“Southern”

Final advice

Your essay has a strong topic, a clear personal voice, and the beginnings of a real argument. For revision, focus first on the big issue: make your thesis consistent. Then strengthen it with accurate historical evidence and a more measured academic tone. If you do that, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position and strong conviction. You also attempt to support your claims with sources, statistics, and historical context, which is a good feature of argumentative writing. For revision, the main areas to improve are: thesis precision, logical development, fairness of argument, paragraph organization, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear stance: The reader always knows what you believe.
Use of evidence: You include quotations, statistics, and references to outside texts.
Range of points: You discuss social, physical, emotional, and cultural consequences, which gives the essay scope.

Main argument and thesis
Your thesis appears in the introduction: premarital sex is destructive because it leads to disrespect for women, illegitimate families, and sexually transmitted diseases. This is a usable thesis, but it should be more precise and academically phrased.

For example, instead of:
“the foundation of this nation… has crumbled greatly”
“America headed down the toilet”

use a more formal version such as:
“This essay argues that the normalization of premarital sex has contributed to changes in family structure, attitudes toward gender, and public health.”

This does not mean you must change your opinion. It means the claim should sound reasoned rather than emotional.

Organization and paragraph structure
At the moment, many paragraphs are long and contain several ideas at once. Try this structure for each body paragraph:
Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation of how the evidence supports your point
Link back to thesis

Example:
Paragraph 1: historical change after the sexual revolution
Paragraph 2: sexually transmitted diseases
Paragraph 3: pregnancy/family structure
Paragraph 4: gender respect and objectification
Conclusion: restate argument and implications

Also, some transitions are abrupt. Use clearer linking phrases such as:
“Another consequence is…”
“A related concern involves…”
“However, this evidence does not necessarily prove…”

Use of evidence and logic
This is the most important area for revision. You often present statistics, but sometimes the connection between the statistic and the conclusion is weak or too absolute.

For example:
A high number of sexual partners may correlate with disease risk, but it does not prove that all premarital sex is destructive.
The claim that non-traditional families produce more crime and lower educational outcomes needs much stronger and more careful evidence.
Some statements rely on stereotypes rather than argument, especially in the paragraph about men and women.

Try to distinguish between:
evidence
interpretation
personal belief

You should also avoid overgeneralization:
“women are becoming sexual objects”
“men are becoming animalistic sex hunters”
These are too broad and weaken academic credibility.

A stronger method is to acknowledge complexity:
“Some media representations may encourage objectification.”
“Certain social attitudes surrounding casual sex may contribute to depersonalization.”

Tone and academic style
Your tone is often highly emotional and judgmental. In academic argumentation, strong opinions are more persuasive when expressed calmly.

Avoid:
insults or mocking language
exaggerated phrases
rhetorical statements that sound moralistic rather than analytical

Examples to revise:
“headed down the toilet”
“ritual parties to get free stuff”
“immoral slaughter of babies”
“piece of ass”

If you quote offensive language as evidence, introduce it carefully and explain why it matters.

Language issues to correct
Because you are a German-speaking writer, some errors may come from direct translation or confusion with similar English words. Here are common problems from your essay:

Word choice / form
“affects” → effects (noun)
“lost of respect” → loss of respect
“have sex” → having sex
“there bad side affects” → their bad side effects
“far to many” → far too many
“life long” → lifelong
“contacted through blood transfusion” → contracted through blood transfusion
“planed” → planned
“at lest” → at least
“all evolved” → all involved
“bare the brunt” → bear the brunt
“has lead” → has led

Grammar
“One the most potent solvents” → One of the most potent solvents
“It is without any doubt that” → better: There is little doubt, in the author’s view, that…
“The major change… coming” → sentence fragment; make it a full sentence.
Check subject-verb agreement and article use throughout.

Punctuation
There are many comma splices and run-on sentences. Read each sentence and check whether two complete ideas are joined incorrectly with only a comma.

Fairness and counterargument
Your essay would become much stronger if you briefly addressed opposing views. For example:
Some argue that premarital sex can occur in committed, respectful relationships.
Others argue that the real issue is not premarital sex itself, but lack of responsibility, consent, education, or contraception.

Then explain why you still disagree. This makes your essay more persuasive because it shows critical thinking.

Final revision advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Rewrite the thesis in a formal academic way.
Shorten and reorganize body paragraphs around one main claim each.
Remove overgeneralizations and emotionally loaded phrases.
Explain evidence more carefully.
Proofread for grammar, spelling, and word choice.

You have a strong sense of argument and commitment to your position. With a more formal tone and tighter reasoning, this essay could become much more effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that women should serve in the military, but not in combat roles. The paper is engaged and forceful, and your personal voice is strong. For revision, the main areas to improve are argument quality, organization, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengthen the argument with evidence
At the moment, your essay relies mostly on assertion and personal belief (“I think,” “I believe,” “it seems”). In an argumentative essay, readers expect reasons supported by evidence.

For example, you claim:
military standards are lowered for women,
women are physically weaker,
women are emotionally different in ways that affect combat,
allowing women in combat could harm national defense.

These are major claims, but they are not supported with facts, examples, studies, or military policy evidence. To revise, add:
statistics or studies on physical requirements in combat roles,
evidence from countries that allow women in combat,
military experts’ views,
specific examples of standards, training, or outcomes.

Also, be careful not to generalize from “women” and “men” as fixed groups. A stronger argument would distinguish between average differences and individual capability.

Improve fairness and academic tone
Some wording may sound biased or overly absolute, which can weaken your credibility. Examples include:
“the weaker sex”
“A woman’s body … cannot function in every capacity as a man’s does”
“The emotional makeup of a women is quite different from a man’s”
“feminists … are obsessed”

In academic writing, avoid language that sounds dismissive or stereotypical. Try more neutral phrasing:
“average physical differences between men and women”
“some combat roles may require physical capacities that fewer women meet”
“some argue that psychological differences may affect performance, though this claim requires evidence”

This will make your essay sound more reasoned and persuasive.

Clarify your thesis
Your main idea appears in the first paragraph, but it could be more precise. A clearer thesis might be:

> Although women can serve successfully in many military roles, combat positions should be assigned according to strict physical and psychological requirements, and these requirements should not be lowered in the name of equality.

This version is more focused and easier to defend because it shifts from “women should not be in combat” to “combat roles require standards that must remain constant.”

Reorganize for clearer structure
The essay currently repeats similar ideas several times, especially about “differences” between men and women. Try a simpler structure:

Introduction
Introduce the debate
Present your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Physical standards
Explain why combat roles require specific physical abilities
Provide evidence
Explain why lowering standards could be harmful

Body Paragraph 2: Psychological/emotional demands
Discuss this carefully and with evidence
Avoid broad stereotypes

Body Paragraph 3: Alternative roles
Explain how women contribute significantly in non-combat and leadership/intelligence roles
Show that your position is not anti-woman but based on role requirements

Counterargument paragraph
Acknowledge that some women do meet very high standards
Respond to this point fairly

Conclusion
Restate your argument in a concise way

Fix repetition and coherence
There is one obvious repeated sentence in paragraph 2 (“For example, when a female enters the military…”). Delete the duplicated part.

Also, some sentences are very long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter units. For example:

Original:
> “A woman’s body is not the same as a man’s body and cannot function in every capacity as a man’s does and when one assumes it can, many problems could arise.”

Possible revision:
> “A woman’s body is different from a man’s body in some important physical respects. If military planners ignore those differences, problems may arise in roles with extreme physical demands.”

Language corrections
A few recurring grammar issues:

male / female as nouns: In formal writing, “men” and “women” often sound more natural than “a male” or “a female.”
agreement: “a male’s” not “a males”
plural/singular: “a woman” / “women”; “a man” / “men”
word form: “women” not “a women”
sentence connection: use commas and conjunctions carefully to avoid run-on sentences

Examples:
“The emotional makeup of a women” → “The emotional makeup of a woman”
“the feminists that support this equality is” → “the feminists who support this equality are”
“a women without changing” → “a woman without changing”

Be careful with cultural/religious assumptions
You write that men and women were “created different for a reason.” This may reflect a personal belief, but in an academic argument, such claims need to be framed carefully unless the assignment specifically invites religious reasoning. Otherwise, your audience may not accept this as evidence.

Final advice
Your essay has passion and a clear stance, which is a strong starting point. For the next draft, focus on:
a more precise thesis,
evidence-based reasoning,
neutral academic language,
clearer paragraph structure,
grammar and sentence clarity.

With these changes, your argument will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important topic and shows clear engagement with psychological, sociological, and economic effects of gender stereotypes. You also try to use outside sources, which is a strong academic move. The main area for revision is argument clarity: at the beginning, your thesis says culture *should maintain gender identities, but only to a certain extent*, yet your conclusion argues that people should be free from traditional definitions. These two positions do not fully match.

Main strengths
Relevant topic and clear concern: You stay focused on gender stereotypes in American culture.
Use of multiple perspectives: You discuss effects on both men and women.
Attempt to support claims with sources: Farrell, Friedan, and Pollitt help give your essay authority.
Logical categories: Psychological, sociological, and economic reasons are a good structure.

Most important revision: clarify your thesis
Your thesis currently seems mixed:
early claim: society should maintain gender identities “to a certain extent”
later claim: people should be free to choose who they are

These ideas can work together, but only if you explain them carefully. Ask yourself:
Do you believe some gender roles are useful as optional role models?
Or do you believe traditional stereotypes are mostly harmful and should not define people?

A clearer thesis might be:
> Although traditional gender roles may provide some people with a sense of identity, American culture should not strictly maintain them because they create psychological pressure, limit social freedom, and weaken women’s and men’s economic security.

This would better match your body paragraphs and conclusion.

Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs mix advantages and disadvantages without a clear controlling point. Try this structure:

Introduction: define gender stereotypes and present a clear thesis  
Men: psychological/social harm  
Women: psychological/social harm  
Economic effects on families and women’s independence  
Counterargument: why some people think traditional roles provide stability  
Rebuttal and conclusion

This will make your essay easier to follow.

Develop analysis, not just examples
Sometimes you mention an example, but do not explain how it proves your point. For instance, when you discuss men being pressured to succeed, explain more directly:
how social expectations shape male identity,
why this damages family relationships,
and how this supports your thesis.

Try using a pattern like:
claim → evidence → explanation → link to thesis

Example:
> Boys are often taught that masculinity means emotional control. As a result, they may hide vulnerability rather than express it openly. This can damage mental health and relationships, showing that rigid gender expectations harm both individuals and families.

Use sources more precisely
You refer to authors, but some references are incomplete or unclear, especially where you wrote “” and “<R>.” Before submitting a final draft, you need to:
complete all missing quotations or paraphrases,
give accurate names and titles,
cite sources in the style required by your course.

Also, be careful not to rely on sources without explaining them. After each source, add your interpretation.

Style and academic tone
Some sentences are strong, but others are too informal or unclear. A few examples:

“afterall, big boys don’t cry”  
  Better: *because boys are often taught that showing emotion is unmanly.*

“damaging to the soul”  
  Better: *psychologically harmful*

“shocking tale”  
  Better: *example* or *case study*  
  (“Shocking” sounds emotional rather than academic.)

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as:
“The very few women who went to college...”
“Psychologists have also conceded...”

These need either evidence or more cautious wording:
*Some women who attended college...*
*Some psychologists argue...*

Grammar and language issues to correct
Because your first language is German, a few patterns are noticeable. Focus especially on:

word choice
  - “genre identities” → gender identities
  - “femine” → feminine
  - “mascularity” → masculinity
  - “enstranged” → estranged
  - “abnomal” → abnormal
  - “defination” → definition

apostrophes/possessives
  - “womans' role” → woman’s role or women’s roles, depending on meaning

spacing and compounds
  - “alot” → a lot
  - “school age boys” → school-age boys
  - “life saving drug” → life-saving drug

sentence fragments/run-ons
  Example:
  > “On the other hand, a woman would be femine. Meaning gentle...”
  Better:
  > “On the other hand, a woman would be expected to be feminine, meaning gentle, emotional, sensitive, and loving.”

plural/singular agreement
  - “The negative psychological effects on women results” → result

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Rewrite the thesis so it matches the conclusion
Reorganize paragraphs by clear topic
Finish and correctly cite all source material
Edit for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity

You have a strong foundation and a meaningful argument. With a clearer thesis and more careful sentence-level editing, this could become a persuasive and thoughtful academic essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear opinion and strong personal voice. The essay consistently argues that residence hall rules can prevent students from developing independence, and you support this with specific examples such as visitation policies, roommate contracts, and quiet hours. Your topic is relevant, and your passion makes the argument engaging. For revision, focus on organization, tone, sentence correctness, and stronger evidence.

Main strength: clear position
Your thesis is understandable: residence hall rules are too restrictive and can interfere with students’ maturity and freedom. This is a good argumentative starting point. You also try to address fairness, safety, and different student situations, which shows you are thinking beyond your own experience.

Improve the thesis and overall structure
At the moment, the introduction is broad and repetitive. Try to end the introduction with one clear thesis statement. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although residence hall policies are intended to promote safety and order, overly strict rules on visitation and quiet hours can limit students’ independence and should be revised to give students more responsibility.*

Then organize the essay into clearer body paragraphs:
Visitation rules limit independence
Roommate agreements are a better solution than blanket restrictions
Some policies may be unfair or inconsistent
Quiet hours should be more flexible, especially on weekends

This will make your argument easier to follow.

Use a more academic tone
Your essay sometimes sounds conversational or emotional, which weakens the argument in an academic setting. Examples:
“That’s ridiculous!”
“mom and dad from downstairs”
“Heaven forbid…”

These phrases express feeling, but in formal writing you should sound more measured. Instead of “That’s ridiculous,” write something like:
*Such a policy seems unnecessarily restrictive.*
*This rule may create practical and safety concerns.*

Also avoid directly addressing the reader too often (“I know you think…”). Academic argument is usually stronger when it focuses on claims and evidence rather than imagined conversation with the audience.

Be careful with logic and evidence
Many of your points are interesting, but they often rely only on personal opinion. Try to explain why a policy is harmful and what alternative would work better.

For example, instead of only saying visitation rules are unfair, explain:
how they limit adult decision-making,
how roommate agreements could protect shared spaces,
how universities could balance freedom and safety.

Also, some claims need more care, especially in the paragraph about gender, sexuality, and rape. This section raises an important issue of fairness, but the wording can sound insensitive or overgeneralized. For example:
“Not every male is a rapist” is too blunt and may distract readers.
“This gives gays, lesbians, and bisexuals an advantage” oversimplifies a complex issue.

A better approach would be:
*Gender-based visitation rules may be inconsistent because they assume risk only in opposite-sex visits. A more equitable policy would apply similar procedures to all overnight or late-night guests, regardless of gender.*

This keeps your fairness argument but makes it more precise and respectful.

Paragraph development
Some paragraphs are too long and include several ideas at once. Try this structure for each body paragraph:
topic sentence
explanation
example
analysis
concluding sentence

For instance, your quiet-hours paragraph could begin:
*Quiet-hour policies are necessary, but the current schedule may be too inflexible, especially on weekends.*

Then explain why, give the USC example, and suggest a revised policy.

Grammar and language issues
There are frequent grammar and word-choice errors. Some may be influenced by German, especially article use, sentence structure, and word forms. Common issues:

Word choice / form
“maturing station” → *place for personal growth* / *stage of maturation*
“parenting rolls” → *parenting roles*
“resident halls” → usually *residence halls*
“guest” / “guests” → check plural forms
“rapist” → if used, be careful; often *sexual assault* or *sexual violence* is more appropriate in academic writing
“atleast” → *at least*

Grammar
“it has makes” → *it has made*
“policy… state” → *policy… states*
“they wanted to” → *they want to*
“there opinions” → *their opinions*
“Another rule in resident halls are” → *Another rule in residence halls is*
“almost as worse” → *almost as bad*
“you are suppose to” → *you are supposed to*
“What maybe loud to you” → *What may be loud to you*

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are run-on or awkward. Example:
“To restrict a person on what time a visitor can see them is like you are trying to violate their rights.”
Better:
*Restricting the hours during which students may receive visitors can feel like an unnecessary limitation on personal freedom.*

Final revision advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis
Organize body paragraphs by one main idea each
Replace emotional phrases with formal academic language
Support claims with reasoning and, if possible, evidence
Edit grammar carefully, especially verb forms, plurals, and word choice

You have a strong foundation: a clear opinion, relevant examples, and a willingness to address difficult issues. With better structure and a more academic tone, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: college football should adopt a playoff system. You also do several things well. First, your argument is easy to identify from the introduction. Second, you use concrete historical examples from multiple seasons, which gives your paper substance. Third, your conclusion returns clearly to the main claim. These are strong foundations.

Main strength: strong evidence and clear purpose
Your essay is persuasive because you do not speak only in general terms; you discuss the 1992–93, 1993–94, and 1994 seasons in detail. This helps readers see the problem with the ranking system. Your thesis is also clear:

> “A playoff system will generate more revenue, create more excitement for the fans … and most importantly, it will determine a true champion…”

That is a solid argumentative thesis because it makes a claim and previews reasons.

Main area to revise: organization and focus
Right now, the essay is much longer on background examples than on analysis. In several body paragraphs, you summarize entire seasons, but sometimes the connection to your argument becomes weak. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Problem with polls and bowls  
Example 1: disputed season
Example 2: disputed season
Example 3: disputed season
Benefits of playoffs: fairness, fan interest, revenue
Counterargument: tradition/season length
Conclusion

After each example, add 2–3 sentences explaining why that season proves your point. For instance: *This case shows that undefeated or head-to-head results were not enough to settle the championship fairly; therefore, only a playoff could decide the winner on the field.*

Argumentation: develop counterarguments more
You mention objections, such as bowl tradition and a longer season, but you do not fully answer them. A stronger argumentative essay should refute those points more directly. For example:

Objection: playoffs would make the season too long.  
  Response: limit the playoff to 8 or 16 teams and use bowl games as playoff sites.
Objection: bowls would lose importance.  
  Response: bowls could remain part of the playoff structure and still earn revenue.

This will make your essay sound more balanced and academic.

Style: avoid repetition and informal phrasing
You repeat phrases such as “national champion,” “number 1,” and “playoff system” very often. Repetition weakens style. Try synonyms like:

*top-ranked team*
*title winner*
*postseason tournament*
*championship format*

Also, some phrases are too informal for an academic essay, for example:
“absolutely ridiculous”
“whipped”
“dream match-ups”

A more formal tone would improve the essay. For example:
“highly problematic”
“defeated decisively”
“highly anticipated matchups”

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are frequent grammar errors, especially with articles, verb forms, word choice, and sentence structure. As a German speaker, some of these may come from direct translation or different word order.

Common patterns to revise:
Subject-verb / verb form
  - “there is always some debated” → “there is always some debate”
  - “they had prove” → “they had proven” or “they had proved”
Word choice
  - “implied this rule” → “applied this rule”
  - “their was” → “there was”
Articles and prepositions
  - “in a bowl game” / “go to the Rose Bowl” / “a shot at the national title”
Sentence fragments or awkward sentences
  - “So Alabama ended up being the nation's only unbeaten team and the national championship.”  
    → “So Alabama ended up as the nation’s only unbeaten team and won the national championship.”
Punctuation
  - You often need commas after introductory phrases and around quotations.
Quotation format
  - Your quotation marks and citation style are inconsistent. Follow one academic style (MLA, APA, etc) and format quotations carefully.

Example revisions
Here are two sample sentence improvements:

Original: “But how can you take a team's chance of winning the national title just because of the conference they're in and the teams they played on their schedule.”
Revised: “However, it is unfair to deny a team a chance at the national title solely because of its conference or schedule.”

Original: “The bowl games pay out a good amount of money to the colleges that attend their bowls, but a playoff system would generate more revenue because there would be more games played and the fan excitement would be unbelievable.”
Revised: “Although bowl games already generate significant revenue for participating schools, a playoff system would likely produce even more income because it would add meaningful games and increase national interest.”

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Shorten season summaries and increase analysis.
Improve paragraph structure with clear topic sentences.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Correct grammar and quotation formatting carefully.
Strengthen the counterargument section.

Overall, this essay has a persuasive core and good historical evidence. With clearer organization, more formal language, and careful editing, it could become a much stronger academic argument.
