Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you want to persuade readers to follow go-cart safety rules. The strongest part of the paper is your personal experience. Because you work at the amusement park, you sound credible, and your examples of accidents make the argument feel real. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Strong topic and clear position
   - From the introduction, your main claim is easy to understand: people should follow the rules while riding go-carts.

Good use of real-life evidence
   - Your experience as an employee gives the essay authority.
   - Specific examples—people not using brakes, workers being hit, children being injured—help support your argument.

Logical organization
   - The essay moves through several rules one by one: seatbelts, brakes, no bumping, watching workers, entering the pit slowly, and staying seated.  
   - This structure helps the reader follow your points.

Main areas to revise

Make the essay more formally argumentative
Right now, the essay sounds partly like a spoken warning or safety speech. For an academic argumentative essay, use a more formal tone and a clearer thesis.

Your introduction could be more direct. Instead of beginning with “I don't know how to express…,” state your argument confidently.

Possible revised thesis:
> Everyone who rides go-carts at an amusement park should follow the safety rules because these rules prevent injuries, protect workers and children, and reduce damage to the cars.

This thesis is specific and previews your main points.

Improve paragraph unity
Some body paragraphs are quite long and include several ideas at once. Try to give each paragraph:
one main point,
a topic sentence,
supporting examples,
a concluding sentence.

For example, the long paragraph about bumping could be split into:
why bumping is dangerous for riders,
why it is especially dangerous for children and workers,
why it damages equipment and causes conflict.

Use more precise and formal language
Some expressions are informal:
“a good bit of people”
“real bad”
“you got to remember”
“doesn't feel good”

Replace them with more academic wording:
“many people”
“severely”
“it is important to remember”
“it is painful” or “it can cause serious injury”

Also avoid directly addressing the reader too often (“you”) unless your instructor allows a personal style. In academic writing, it is often better to refer to “riders” or “customers.”

Work on grammar and word choice
There are many grammar issues, but several are repeated patterns, so revising those will improve the essay a lot.

Common issues:

a. Subject-verb agreement
“The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off” → “The seatbelts should not be taken off”
“The only reason the bumpers are on the cars in the first place are” → “The only reason the bumpers are on the cars in the first place is”

b. Word form / wrong word
“taking off” → “taken off”
“their” → “there”
“When ever” → “Whenever”
“annoucements” → “announcements”

c. Countable/uncountable nouns
“I have seen a good bit of people get hurt” → “I have seen many people get hurt”

d. Sentence structure / comma splices
You often join two complete ideas with only a comma.  
Example:
“I was even lucky that when I got hit, I wasn't seriously hurt, I just bruised my arm real bad.”
Better:
“Luckily, when I got hit, I was not seriously injured; I only bruised my arm badly.”

Punctuation
You use too many commas, sometimes where they are not needed:
“people, who don't use their brakes”
“amount of people, who don't use their brakes”

In restrictive clauses, no comma is needed:
“people who do not use their brakes”

Read each sentence and ask: does the comma separate ideas clearly, or does it interrupt the sentence unnecessarily?

Style note for a German speaker
German writing often allows long sentences with many linked ideas. In English academic writing, shorter and more direct sentences are usually stronger. If a sentence feels long, divide it into two.

Suggested revision plan

Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Divide long paragraphs into smaller ones.
Use a more formal tone.
Correct repeated grammar problems:
   - subject-verb agreement
   - word choice
   - comma splices
   - spelling
Proofread carefully for punctuation and sentence clarity.

Final comment

This essay has a strong basis because you clearly know the subject and care about it. Your personal knowledge makes the argument persuasive. The next step is to shape that experience into a more formal academic essay with clearer sentences, stronger paragraph structure, and more careful grammar. With revision, this could become a convincing and effective argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Susan Smith should not receive the death penalty. This gives the paper a strong argumentative focus. You also show commitment to your ideas, and your writing communicates emotion and moral seriousness. For revision, the main areas to improve are: thesis and organization, use of evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear opinion: The reader always knows what you believe.
Relevant background: You include details about Susan Smith’s childhood, mental health, and relationships to explain your reasoning.
Strong engagement: Your voice is confident and your argument feels personally meaningful.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
In the introduction, you say you want to express your opinion on the “justness” of the sentence, but your exact argument is not fully stated. A stronger thesis should explain why life imprisonment is more just than the death penalty.

For example:
> *Although Susan Smith committed a terrible crime, the jury was right not to sentence her to death because her history of trauma and mental illness reduced her moral responsibility, and life imprisonment is a more meaningful punishment than execution.*

This gives the essay a roadmap.

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from biography, to mental illness, to punishment, to religion. The ideas are interesting, but the structure feels uneven. Try organizing by reasons rather than by emotional flow.

Possible structure:
Introduction + thesis
Background and mitigating factors  
   (childhood trauma, depression, irrational decision-making)
Why life imprisonment is a stronger punishment than death
Moral/religious argument against taking life
Conclusion

Also, each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that states the main point.

For example:
> *One reason the jury’s decision was justified is that Susan Smith’s past trauma and depression affected her mental state.*

C. Use evidence more carefully
Some claims are presented as facts but need support or more cautious wording. For example:
“She obviously cared deeply for this boyfriend...”
“She will always live with a hideous picture etched in the back of her mind...”
“Every morning she will wake up and see herself as a cold-hearted murderer...”

These are powerful statements, but they are speculative. In academic argument, it is better to distinguish between fact, interpretation, and opinion.

You can revise by using phrases like:
*It is likely that...*
*The defense argued that...*
*According to the psychiatrist’s testimony...*

Also, you mention “the Supreme Court,” but in this case it was the jury, not the U.S. Supreme Court, that decided the sentence. Be careful with legal terminology.

D. Consider audience and tone
The essay is persuasive, but some language is very emotional and judgmental:
“cold-hearted murderer”
“same sick woman”
“scandalous life”

This kind of wording can weaken an academic argument because it sounds more like preaching than analysis. Since you are making an argument about justice, use a more formal and balanced tone.

For example, instead of:
> *She will be the same sick woman...*

You could write:
> *Without psychological and spiritual change, she may remain trapped in the same destructive patterns that led to the crime.*

E. Integrate the religious argument more effectively
Your Christian perspective is important to your argument, but it should be connected more clearly to the main claim. Right now, the Bible references are incomplete (“”), which interrupts the essay. If you include religious support, either quote accurately or paraphrase.

For example:
> *From a Christian perspective, the commandment “Thou shalt not kill” suggests that human beings should not take life unnecessarily, even in response to a terrible crime.*

However, if this is for a general academic audience, make sure religion is one reason among several, not the only basis for the argument.

Language corrections
You have several grammar and spelling problems, many of which are manageable in revision.

Common issues:
Spelling:  
  *recieve* → *receive*  
  *prision* → *prison*
Article/preposition use:  
  *at the age of fifteen years old* → *at the age of fifteen*  
  *endowed with logical mental coherence* sounds unnatural
Sentence structure / run-ons:  
  “The news media and many individuals have stated facts as well as opinions, I would like...”  
  → This should be split into two sentences or joined with a semicolon.
Word choice:  
  *slept with her* is more natural than *continued to sleep with her* in this context, though the subject matter should be handled carefully and formally.

Example revision
Original:
> *These past behavior patterns are a few reasons why the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty.*

Revised:
> *These patterns of trauma, depression, and impaired judgment help explain why the jury chose life imprisonment instead of the death penalty.*

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Writing a clear thesis
Grouping ideas into logical paragraphs
Replacing emotional speculation with supported reasoning
Using a more formal academic tone
Correcting sentence-level grammar and spelling

You have a strong foundation and a clear viewpoint. With better structure and a more academic style, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. The introduction states the claim directly, and the body paragraphs are organized around three main reasons: crime, responsibility, and health. This is a strong foundation. You also try to use sources to support your ideas, which is important in academic argument.

What is working well
Clear thesis: You consistently argue that lowering the drinking age to eighteen would have social benefits.
Logical paragraphing: Each body paragraph focuses on one main reason.
Sense of purpose: The essay stays on topic and keeps returning to the central claim.

Main areas to improve

Strengthen logic and evidence
At the moment, many claims are asserted rather than proved. For example, you often say that lowering the drinking age would reduce crime, binge drinking, or alcoholism, but you do not fully explain why, and some points seem speculative.

Examples:
“Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly.”
“The common problem of binge drinking would be cured...”

These are very strong claims. A reader will ask: *What evidence proves this?* In revision, avoid absolute statements unless you can support them with strong research. Use more cautious academic language:
“may reduce”
“could contribute to”
“is likely to”
“some researchers argue that...”

Also, some arguments need better causal explanation. For instance, if underage drinking becomes legal, that may reduce one kind of lawbreaking, but it does not automatically reduce alcohol-related violence. Make sure each reason is carefully reasoned.

Use sources more academically
Your source integration needs major revision. Some citations are incomplete, informal, or not credible in their current form, for example:
email addresses
website links in the sentence
“Netscape. Young Drinkers Homepage”
inconsistent citation style

In an academic essay, use a consistent citation system (MLA, APA, etc) and introduce sources properly:
Weak: “on most college campuses, alcohol is involved...”
Better: “According to a study by X, alcohol is involved in many incidents of campus violence.”

Also, evaluate source quality. Government reports and peer-reviewed studies are stronger than personal pages or unclear websites.

Improve grammar and word choice
There are frequent grammar and usage problems. As a German speaker, you may want to watch especially for article use, plural forms, apostrophes, and verb forms.

Common corrections
“in high school and college environment” → “in high school and college environments”
“encourage a more responsible approach” is fine, but later:
  - “would be encourage” → “would be encouraged”
“effect the United States” → “affect the United States”
“amoung” → “among”
“eighteen-year old's” → “eighteen-year-olds”  
  Apostrophes are not used for plurals.
“youths” is possible, but “young people” or “youth” sounds more natural in academic English.
“cut back on reform programs” is unclear; perhaps you mean “treatment programs” or “prevention programs.”

Also check capitalization:
“on most college campuses” should begin with a capital: “On most college campuses...”

Make wording more precise
Some expressions are vague or unnatural:
“fight alcoholism” → “struggle with alcohol abuse”
“giving back drinking rights” → “lowering the legal drinking age”
“get a grip on alcohol” → too informal for academic writing
“new and fun environment” → unclear; perhaps “new and exciting activity”

Try to choose formal and precise academic vocabulary.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side. Here, an obvious counterargument is that lowering the drinking age might increase drunk driving, alcohol poisoning, or alcohol access for younger teenagers. If you address such concerns and respond to them with evidence, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Organization suggestions
Your overall structure is good, but some paragraphs repeat the same idea. For revision, try this pattern for each body paragraph:

Topic sentence  
Evidence from a credible source  
Explanation of how the evidence supports your claim  
Consideration of a possible objection  
Concluding sentence linking back to thesis

This will make the argument more analytical and less repetitive.

Example of revision
Original:
> “Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly.”

Revised:
> “Supporters of a lower drinking age argue that legal access to alcohol at eighteen could reduce the attraction of forbidden behavior and encourage more supervised, moderate drinking.”

This version sounds more academic and avoids making an unsupported certainty claim.

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Using stronger, more reliable evidence
Explaining your reasoning more carefully
Correcting grammar and citation problems
Using a more formal academic style
Adding a counterargument paragraph

You already have a clear thesis and a solid basic structure. With better evidence, more precise language, and careful editing, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose and a strong public-safety argument: schools should require swimming instruction in P.E. This is a persuasive topic, and your opening creates urgency effectively. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Strong sense of importance: You make the reader feel that drowning is a serious and preventable problem.
Clear position: Your thesis is easy to identify: swimming should be part of school physical education.
Practical focus: You do not only argue for the idea; you also suggest how schools could implement it.

Main issues to revise

A. Thesis and argument structure
Right now, the essay has a clear opinion but a somewhat loose structure. Try to make your thesis more precise and let each paragraph support it in a clear way.

A possible revised thesis:
“Swimming instruction should be required in middle-school physical education because it can save lives, teaches water safety, and can often be implemented with existing community resources.”

Then organize body paragraphs around 2–3 main reasons:
swimming prevents drowning,
students learn rescue and safety skills,
schools can realistically provide the program.

This will make the essay feel more academic and easier to follow.

B. Tone and audience
Your introduction uses a dramatic second-person story (“you jump in the water…”). It is vivid, but for an argumentative essay, it sounds more emotional than academic. You can keep the example, but shorten it and connect it quickly to your argument.

Also, avoid speaking directly to the reader too often (“How often have you heard…”). Academic argument is usually stronger when it relies on reasoning and evidence rather than rhetorical questions.

C. Need for evidence
This is the most important improvement. You make strong claims, but you do not support them with sources or data. For example:
Is drowning really common among young people?
How many people do not know how to swim?
Can most beginners learn basic swimming in four weeks?
Do many schools actually have under-used pools?

To strengthen your argument, add facts from reliable sources such as public health agencies, education departments, or swimming organizations. Even 2–4 well-chosen sources would make a big difference.

D. Counterargument
A strong argumentative essay usually addresses objections. For example:
swimming programs may be expensive,
some schools do not have pools,
some students may fear water or have medical limitations.

Then respond to those concerns:
schools can partner with local pools,
the course can focus on basic safety, not advanced swimming,
exemptions or alternative arrangements can be offered when necessary.

This will make your essay more balanced and persuasive.

Organization and paragraphing

Some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas at once. Try this structure:

Introduction: problem + thesis
Body paragraph 1: drowning is preventable; swimming is a life-saving skill
Body paragraph 2: students also learn water safety and how to respond in emergencies
Body paragraph 3: implementation in schools is practical
Body paragraph 4: address costs/objections
Conclusion: restate argument and emphasize social benefit

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence.

Language and style

Your meaning is generally clear, but there are many grammar, punctuation, capitalization, and spelling errors. These weaken the credibility of the essay.

Common issues:
Capitalization:  
  “your only problem” → Your only problem  
  “one class” → One class  
  “these organizations” → These organizations

Spelling:  
  “panicing” → panicking  
  “drownding” → drowning  
  “cericulum” → curriculum  
  “evryone” → everyone  
  “instuctor” → instructor  
  “wuold” → would  
  “instuctors” → instructors  
  “thaech” → teach  
  “employent” → employment  
  “simlpe” → simple  
  “remidied” → remedied

Sentence boundaries / comma splices:  
  Example: “This is not uncommon, most of your friends do not know how to swim.”  
  This should be split into two sentences or joined correctly.  
  Better: “This is not uncommon; most of your friends do not know how to swim either.”

Word choice:  
  “floods your head” sounds unnatural. Better: “comes to mind” or “fills your mind.”

Formality:  
  “the News” should be “the news.”  
  “young children” is inconsistent if you are discussing middle school students; be precise.

Example of a more academic style

Original:
“How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?”

More academic:
“Unlike many traditional P.E. activities, swimming provides a practical life-saving skill.”

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on this order:
Create a clear thesis
Reorganize paragraphs by main reasons
Add evidence from sources
Address a counterargument
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation

You have a solid central idea and a genuine persuasive purpose. With better structure, stronger evidence, and more careful language, this could become a convincing argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: society should move beyond rigid gender identities because they can be harmful. The argument is relevant and your overall structure is logical: introduction, explanation of the issue, examples about women, examples about men, application to single parents, and conclusion. This gives your essay a strong foundation. For revision, focus on clarity, evidence, precision, and language control.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: You consistently argue that strict gender identities should be abolished.
Logical organization: Each paragraph develops one part of your argument.
Good use of examples: The paragraphs on women, men, and single parents help make the issue concrete.
Formal tone: Your style is mostly appropriate for an argumentative essay.

Most important revision priorities

A. Clarify your key terms
Your essay depends on the phrase “distinct gender identities,” but the term is unusual and not fully clear. You try to define it, which is good, but your explanation should be sharper.

Instead of repeating “distinct gender identity/identities,” consider using:
rigid gender roles
traditional gender stereotypes
fixed ideas about masculinity and femininity

These terms are more natural in academic English and easier for readers to understand.

B. Strengthen the thesis
Your thesis is understandable, but it is too broad. You could make it more specific by naming your main reasons.

For example:
> Society should reject rigid gender roles because they create psychological pressure, limit personal fulfillment, and no longer reflect the realities of modern family and work life.

This gives the reader a roadmap for the essay.

C. Improve evidence and integration of sources
Several quotations or references are incomplete (, `<R>`), so the essay currently feels unfinished. In revision:
include the full quotation or paraphrase,
explain how the source supports your claim,
cite consistently.

Also, avoid relying too heavily on authority alone (“noted psychologist,” “noted researchers”). What matters is what they found, not only that they are respected.

D. Avoid overgeneralization
At times your essay makes absolute claims, for example:
“these are strictly male gender identity characteristics”
“these are distinct female gender identity characteristics”

These statements can sound too rigid, especially in an essay arguing against rigid categories. A better approach is:
“these traits have traditionally been associated with men”
“these qualities have often been labeled feminine”

This wording is more accurate and academically careful.

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction
Good topic and clear stance, but the introduction is repetitive. You mention stereotypical behavior several times before fully developing the thesis. Try to condense it.

Body paragraphs
Most body paragraphs follow a good pattern: topic sentence → example/source → explanation. However, some paragraphs need stronger analysis. After presenting evidence, ask:
What does this example prove?
Why does it support abolishing rigid gender roles?

For example, in the paragraph about women’s dissatisfaction, explain more directly how restrictive expectations can damage identity and mental well-being.

Conclusion
The conclusion summarizes well, but it mostly repeats earlier points. Try to end with a stronger final thought about social change, equality, or individual freedom.

Language issues to revise

A. Word choice
Some vocabulary choices are unnatural:
“emanate these qualities” → display or show these qualities
“radiate these qualities” → demonstrate these qualities
“provide for a more healthy male gender” → lead to healthier lives for men
“on order to succeed” → in order to succeed

B. Grammar
Watch for:
capitalization: “if these traits…” and “it would be ridiculous…” should begin with capital letters.
adjective forms: “more healthy” → healthier
parallel structure: “to be a wife and then a mother” is fine, but in other places lists should match grammatically.
prepositions: “looked upon to be” is awkward; use seen as or considered.

C. Sentence style
Many sentences are grammatically correct but repetitive because they follow the same pattern. Vary sentence openings and combine shorter sentences where appropriate.

For example:
Original:
> There are women who are having to play the role of mother and father. At the same time, there are also men who are having to play the role of mother and father.

Revised:
> As divorce rates rise, both women and men increasingly find themselves raising children alone and taking on responsibilities traditionally associated with both parents.

Likely L1 (German) influence
A few phrases sound slightly translated or overly formal, which is common for German speakers writing in English. English academic style usually prefers:
more direct phrasing
fewer abstract nouns
less repetition of the same key phrase

So instead of repeating “distinct gender identities” many times, use pronouns or synonyms where appropriate.

Suggested next steps
Replace “distinct gender identities” with a clearer term.
Rewrite the thesis to include your three main reasons.
Complete all missing quotations/citations.
Revise overgeneralized claims.
Edit for grammar, capitalization, and more natural word choice.
Read the essay aloud to identify repetition and awkward phrasing.

Overall, this is a thoughtful essay with a strong central argument. With clearer terminology, fuller evidence, and more precise language, it could become much more persuasive and academically polished.

Main strengths

Most important revision priorities

   - End with a precise thesis, for example:

   - Explain why these standards are unattainable.

   - Discuss anorexia, bulimia, risky cosmetic procedures, and harmful products.

Body Paragraph 3: Social and economic consequences
   - Discuss discrimination against larger women and effects on self-esteem.

Conclusion
   - Restate the argument and propose a healthier understanding of beauty.

This will help your reader follow your reasoning more easily.

B. Use more formal academic tone
Your essay is persuasive, but some expressions are too conversational for academic writing:
“yeah right”
“top notch”
“I mean”
repeated rhetorical questions

These make the essay sound more like spoken language. In academic writing, use a more formal style. For example:
Instead of *“yeah right”*, write *“this promise is unrealistic.”*
Instead of *“top notch models”*, write *“high-profile models.”*

C. Be careful with facts and evidence
You include many statistics and medical claims, but they are not cited. Some claims also seem too strong or possibly inaccurate, for example:

implants are “a leading cause of Cancer”
beauty chemicals “break down skin and bone”

In an argumentative essay, unsupported or exaggerated claims weaken credibility. Revise by:
adding sources if required,
checking facts carefully,
using cautious language such as *may contribute to*, *can increase risk*, or *has been associated with*.

Language issues to correct

A. Grammar
Pay special attention to:
subject-verb agreement:  
  *“society place”* → *“society places”*
possessives/plurals:  
  *“1990’s”* → *“1990s”*  
  *“women everywhere include themselves in disorders”* → *“women everywhere develop eating disorders”*
pronouns:  
  *“there job”* → *“their job”*

B. Word choice
Some words are not quite natural in English:
*“altering of a real woman’s appearance”* → *“distortion of women’s real appearance”*
*“beautification processes”* → *“cosmetic procedures”*
*“state of mind”* is understandable, but *“mental health”* is more precise.

C. Sentence style
Many sentences are long and contain too many ideas. Break them into shorter sentences for clarity.

Example:
> “Plastic surgery is on an increase these days, it seems that everywhere you look, girls as young as fourteen are having breast implants…”

This is a run-on sentence. Better:
> “Plastic surgery has become more common. In some cases, even very young girls seek breast implants or facial surgery.”

Frequent spelling/capitalization corrections
Please check these:
Naomi Cambell → Naomi Campbell
adolecense → adolescence
Anorexia / Bulimia → usually lowercase: anorexia, bulimia
tooth enwnel → tooth enamel
Cancer → cancer (unless at the beginning of a sentence)
Christie Brinkley is correct, but be consistent with names.

Punctuation
You often join independent clauses with commas. This creates comma splices.

Example:
*“Of course this problem is not the model’s fault, they are doing their job…”*

Possible correction:
*“Of course, this problem is not the models’ fault; they are simply doing their job.”*

Also, add commas after introductory phrases:
*“Of course, …”*
*“Above all, …”*
*“In the long run, …”*

A note for a German-speaking writer
A likely influence from German is writing very long sentences with several linked clauses. In English academic writing, shorter and more direct sentences are often stronger. Also, English usually avoids too much capitalization; nouns such as *women, society, beauty,* and *cancer* are not capitalized.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation because it shows conviction and addresses a meaningful issue. For the next draft, focus on:
clearer paragraph structure,
more formal tone,
accurate and supported evidence,
grammar and punctuation editing.

If you revise these areas, your argument will become much more convincing and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and interesting topic, and you show that you understand both sides of the debate about talk shows. That is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. You also include a concrete example near the end, which helps make the discussion more vivid. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis and argument focus, paragraph organization, sentence control, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Relevant topic: The subject is engaging and appropriate for argument.
Balanced perspective: You recognize both entertainment value and possible harm.
Concrete example: The murder case gives your essay urgency and seriousness.
Strong voice: Your writing often sounds confident and expressive.

Main issue: unclear thesis
At the moment, your essay discusses many questions, but your main position is not fully clear. Do you argue that talk shows are mostly harmless, partly beneficial, or dangerous but ultimately the guests’ responsibility?

Your conclusion suggests this main claim:
People should be free to appear on talk shows, but they must accept responsibility for the consequences.

If that is your argument, state it clearly at the end of the introduction. For example:

> Although many people criticize trash talk shows as vulgar and harmful, these programs can provide entertainment and sometimes support; therefore, adults who choose to appear on them must take primary responsibility for the risks involved.

This would give the essay a sharper direction.

Organization and paragraph development
Your paragraphs contain good ideas, but some are too long and mix different points together. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce talk shows and the public debate
   - End with a clear thesis

Body paragraph 1: Why people watch / possible benefits
   - entertainment
   - public discussion
   - information on health, family, finances, social issues

Body paragraph 2: Criticism of talk shows
   - sensationalism
   - conflict encouraged for ratings
   - humiliation and aggression

Body paragraph 3: Example of serious consequences
   - explain the murder case clearly and carefully
   - show how it supports your argument

Body paragraph 4: Responsibility
   - discuss host responsibility
   - discuss guest responsibility
   - explain which is greater and why

Conclusion
   - restate thesis
   - summarize key points

This structure will help the reader follow your logic more easily.

Use stronger topic sentences
Each paragraph should begin with one sentence that clearly states its main idea. For example:

Many viewers defend talk shows because they offer both entertainment and practical advice.
However, critics argue that these programs often create conflict for commercial purposes.
A tragic real-life incident shows that appearances on such shows can have serious consequences.
For this reason, guests must carefully consider the risks before participating.

Style and tone
Your language is energetic, but sometimes it becomes too emotional or dramatic for an academic essay. Phrases like “deep, dark secrets,” “dark skeletons,” or “on the edge of their seats day in and day out” sound more like journalism than academic argument.

Try to use a more formal tone:
instead of “trashy” → sensational
instead of “lash out angrily” → argue aggressively
instead of “flying fist” → physical violence
instead of “television debut” → public appearance on television

Grammar, spelling, and word choice
There are many language errors, but most are fixable with careful proofreading. Common patterns:

a. Spelling
Examples:
audeinces → audiences
agressively → aggressively
thier → their
heath → health
fueding → feuding
dies deep → digs deep
thay → they
nieghbor → neighbor

b. Singular/plural agreement
guest panelist → guest panelists
These differences in opinions leads → These differences in opinion lead
The guest are willing → The guests are willing

c. Sentence punctuation
You often use periods where commas are needed, or create sentence fragments:
“Many faithful viewers. feel …”
“labeled trash t.v.. is vulgar.”

Read each sentence aloud and check whether it is complete. Avoid extra punctuation.

d. Word choice
Some words do not fit the meaning:
solitude probably should be comfort or support
main course is too informal; use main issue
put their, character on the line → put their reputation on the line

Clarify the example
The murder case is important, but explain it more clearly and objectively. Avoid confusing repetition such as “neighbor wanted to reveal to his neighbor.” Also, be careful not to overgeneralize from one example. You can say that it illustrates the potential risks, not that all talk shows create deadly outcomes.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis
Separate ideas into well-organized paragraphs
Use formal academic tone
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation
Make sure every paragraph supports your main argument

You have the material for a strong essay. With clearer structure and more careful editing, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the legal drinking age in the United States should be lowered to 18. This is a good start for an argumentative essay because your thesis is direct and easy to identify. You also use several reasons to support your opinion, such as military service, personal responsibility, graduation, and crime reduction. These ideas give you useful material for revision.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: “Eighteen should be the legal age to consume alcohol in the United States.”
Relevant topic sentences: Each body paragraph focuses on a separate reason.
Strong voice: Your opinion is consistent and confident.

Main issues to revise

A. Logic and evidence
Your essay makes several strong claims, but many are not supported with evidence. In an academic argument, opinions alone are not enough. You need facts, statistics, examples, or reliable sources.

For example:
“Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving.”  
“There would be less crime in the United States among teenagers, if eighteen year old was allowed to drink.”  
“Maybe a higher percentage of teenagers will graduate…”

These statements may or may not be true, but in the essay they sound like guesses. Add evidence or change the wording to make it more careful.

Suggestion:  
Instead of “There would be less crime…,” write something like:  
“Supporters of a lower drinking age argue that legal access at 18 could reduce secretive drinking, which may lower some alcohol-related offenses.”

This is more academic because it avoids presenting an unsupported idea as a fact.

B. Organization
Your essay has an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion, which is good. However, some paragraphs need clearer development.

A stronger structure would be:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: military/adulthood argument  
Body paragraph 2: responsibility and independence at 18  
Body paragraph 3: possible effects on hidden drinking/crime  
Conclusion: restate thesis and explain why it matters

Also, paragraph 3 introduces the idea of linking drinking rights to a high school diploma. This point feels unusual and not fully connected to your main argument. It may be better to remove it unless you can explain and support it much more clearly.

C. Tone and style
Your essay should sound more formal. Some sentences are very personal or emotional, which weakens the academic tone.

Examples:
“Kids are dieing for our country…”
“If they pass that law, I would feel more like an adult…”

Instead of focusing on your personal feelings, focus on the broader social argument.

Use “young adults” instead of “kids” when talking about 18- to 20-year-olds.

Grammar and language issues

Because your first language is German, some of your sentence patterns may transfer directly into English. Pay special attention to the following:

A. Spelling
There are many spelling mistakes:
“Every since” → Ever since
“Independance” → Independence
“can not” → cannot
“dieing” → dying
“too young” not “two young”
“affects” → effects or better: “experience the effects”
“gonvernment” → government

B. Verb forms and agreement
“eighteen year old was allowed” → eighteen-year-olds were allowed
“People under the age of twenty one are considered to be minors, when it comes to drinking…”  
  Better: People under the age of twenty-one are considered minors when it comes to drinking…

C. Sentence completeness
One sentence is incomplete:
“How can you think you are a man and fight in combat, when the government says that you are two young to do what all of the other adults.”

This needs correction:
How can the government consider someone old enough to fight in combat but too young to do what other adults can do?

D. Word choice
Some expressions are unnatural in English:
“condemned to go to war” is too strong unless you mean forced military service.
“containing alcohol in the car” should be possessing alcohol in a car.
“better environment to live in” is vague. Be more specific.

What to do in revision
Keep your thesis, but rewrite the introduction more clearly.
Remove weak historical references unless they are relevant and accurate.
Develop 2–3 strong arguments instead of many weak ones.
Add evidence for each main point.
Use a more formal tone and avoid “I would feel more like an adult.”
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and sentence structure.

Possible revised thesis
“The legal drinking age in the United States should be lowered to 18 because people at that age already have major adult responsibilities, including military service, and a lower drinking age may encourage more responsible alcohol use.”

Overall, your essay has a clear opinion and a workable structure, but it needs stronger evidence, more formal language, and careful proofreading to become a convincing academic argument.

Feedback Report

You have a clear argumentative purpose: you want to show that children receive harmful messages about gender and that society should revise those messages so people can develop healthier identities. Your essay also uses several sources, which shows ambition and awareness of academic argument. The main work now is to make your thesis clearer, your reasoning more consistent, and your language more precise.

Main strengths
Strong topic: The essay addresses an important social issue.
Use of sources: You bring together Pollitt, Friedan, Ferrell, and Gray to support your discussion.
Big-picture thinking: You try to connect childhood socialization with adult dissatisfaction, which gives the paper direction.

Thesis and argument
Your central claim is not fully stable. At some points, you argue that traditional gender stereotypes are harmful. At other points, you argue that men and women are naturally different and should learn those differences early. These ideas can coexist, but in your draft they sometimes seem to conflict.

For example:
You criticize stereotypes.
But you also say children should learn that there is “a core of behavior” that makes them boys or girls.

This raises a question: Are gender roles socially imposed, naturally different, or both? You need to define your position more carefully.

Revision advice
Try to write a thesis that clearly states:
what is harmful,
what should replace it,
why this matters.

For example:
> Children should be taught that gender does not limit their abilities or life choices; although some differences in communication or self-expression may exist, rigid stereotypes harm both boys and girls and contribute to unhappy adult lives.

This would make your argument more balanced and clearer.

Organization
Your essay has a recognizable structure, but some paragraphs do not develop one main point clearly.

What to improve
Introduction: Good opening, but the thesis should be more direct and specific.
Body paragraph 1: Pollitt is introduced well, but the paragraph needs a stronger topic sentence and more explanation of how TV shapes identity.
Body paragraph 2: The move from childhood to adulthood is logical, but the paragraph becomes too broad and mixes several ideas.
Body paragraph 3: The discussion of Gray feels less critical than the earlier paragraphs. Explain more clearly how his work supports your claim.
Conclusion: It restates the main idea, but it should avoid introducing new claims or oversimplified statements.

Suggestion
Give each paragraph one job:
Paragraph 1: media teaches stereotypes in childhood.
Paragraph 2: these stereotypes damage both women and men in adulthood.
Paragraph 3: society should promote more flexible gender expectations.

Use of sources
You mention your sources often, but sometimes the essay summarizes them without enough analysis. Academic argument needs more than “X says this.” You should explain how the evidence supports your claim.

Also, some source references and names are inaccurate:
Katha Pollitt (not Pollit/Pollitt inconsistently)
Betty Friedan (not Frieden/Fredian/Friedon)
Warren Farrell (not Ferrell, if this is the intended author)
John Gray (not Grey)
Men Are from Mars, Women Are from Venus (not Venue)

Be careful with quotations and citations. For example, “Pollit contends:that…” has punctuation problems, and “she cites. many painful examples” should be corrected.

Style and tone
Your tone is engaged, but some wording is too informal or emotionally loaded for academic writing:
“life’s roller coaster ride”
“scarry confession”
“wonderful start” (used ironically, but unclear)
“parents are not interested in justice, they just want quiet”

Such phrases weaken the academic tone. Try to sound analytical rather than sarcastic or exaggerated.

Also, avoid broad generalizations such as:
“Men and women today need to understand and respect the fact that they are different.”
“Children need to know men and women walk, talk, drive, and grow in different ways.”

These are sweeping claims and need qualification or evidence.

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are frequent errors in spelling, agreement, punctuation, and word choice. Because you are a German speaker, one useful focus is article use, apostrophes, and sentence punctuation.

Examples
“well adjusted” → well-adjusted
“adulthood society” → adult society
“society must ammend the method and the message we are giving our children”  
  → society must amend the methods and messages we give our children
“behavior that is open to change”  
  → behaviors that are open to change
“boys lives” → boys’ lives
“their’s” → theirs
“its easy” → it’s easy
“massed produced” → mass-produced

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward. For example:
> “Children should be taught that there is a core of behavior that makes them boys or girls but, there are other types of behavior that is open to change.”

This could become:
> Children may notice gendered behaviors around them, but they should also learn that their interests and abilities are not fixed by sex.

Final priorities for revision
Clarify your thesis so your position on gender roles is consistent.
Improve paragraph focus with one main idea per paragraph.
Analyze sources more deeply instead of only summarizing them.
Correct names, titles, spelling, and citations.
Edit for grammar and academic tone.

You have the basis for an interesting essay. With a clearer thesis and careful editing, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that legalizing some drugs, especially marijuana, could benefit society. You also raise several relevant points, such as regulation, prison overcrowding, tax revenue, and public health. These are strong starting ideas for an argumentative essay. To improve the paper, focus on thesis, organization, reasoning, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: The reader understands that you support legalization, especially of marijuana.
Range of arguments: You discuss health, crime, law enforcement, economics, youth access, and personal freedom.
Engaged voice: Your writing shows conviction, which is useful in argumentation.

Most important revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your current thesis is broad: *“The legalization of some drugs like marijuana would improve many different aspects of society.”*  
This is understandable, but it should be more specific and consistent. Sometimes the essay focuses only on marijuana; at other times it discusses “marijuana and other drugs” or even all drugs. Choose one scope.

A stronger thesis could be:
“Legalizing and regulating marijuana would improve public health, reduce drug-related crime, and allow governments to use tax revenue for social programs.”

This gives you a clear structure for the body paragraphs.

B. Organize the essay by argument
At the moment, some paragraphs mix too many ideas. For example, one paragraph moves from age limits to fake IDs to smoking laws. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Public health and regulation
Crime and prison overcrowding
Economic benefits / tax revenue
Counterargument and response
Conclusion

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence. Example:
“First, legalization would make drug use safer by allowing government regulation of quality and distribution.”

C. Improve logic and support
Some claims are interesting but need more explanation or evidence. For example:
*“Hopefully, the number of drug users would decrease.”* Why would legalization decrease use?
*“They could develop some types of drugs that would get people high, but not cause as much deadly harm.”* This is a very large claim and needs evidence.
*“If somebody does not want to live on this earth, why make them?”* This idea is too extreme and weakens your credibility.

Try to avoid unsupported statements or emotional claims. Instead, explain how and why legalization might lead to a better outcome.

D. Address counterarguments fairly
You mention health concerns, but you do not fully respond to them. A good argumentative essay shows awareness of the other side. For example:
Opponents worry legalization increases use.
They argue drugs can damage health and families.
You can respond by saying regulation, education, age controls, and treatment are more effective than prohibition.

This will make your essay sound more academic and persuasive.

Language issues to revise

A. Grammar
There are many sentence-level errors. Some common ones:

Subject-verb agreement
  - *“The side effects of marijuana is”* → “The side effects of marijuana are”
  - *“People are going to use drugs regardless of what anyone say”* → “...what anyone says”

Articles
  - *“at young ages”* is fine, but
  - *“can not get a hold of some alcohol beverages”* → “cannot get ahold of alcoholic beverages” or “cannot get alcohol”

Word form
  - *“pureness”* → “purity”
  - *“stimulate from”* → probably “stem from”
  - *“lease harmful”* → “least harmful”

B. Sentence fragments and run-ons
Example:
*“Issues such as permanent damage to the brain and other parts of the anatomy.”*  
This is a fragment. It could become:
“Opponents of legalization often mention issues such as permanent brain damage and damage to other parts of the body.”

C. Word choice
Some vocabulary sounds informal or unclear:
*“get high off of”* → “become intoxicated through” or simply “use to get high”
*“harm their body”* → “harm their bodies”
*“the government claim”* → “the government claims”

Because your first language is German, watch especially for plural forms, verb endings, and article use, since these often differ between German and English.

Tone and academic style
Avoid very conversational questions such as:
*“what is the big difference?”*
*“why stop them?”*

Instead, use a more formal style:
“The difference between alcohol and marijuana regulation therefore deserves closer examination.”

Also, avoid absolute statements like:
*“they are only hurting themselves”*  
Drug use can also affect families, public safety, and healthcare systems. Acknowledging this makes your argument more credible.

Quotation and citation
You include one quotation, but it is not integrated smoothly and has errors:
Introduce the source clearly.
Explain why the quotation matters.
Check punctuation and citation format.

For example:
As Wibotsky argues, excessive anti-drug policies may threaten civil liberties more broadly (17). This supports the view that prohibition can create harms beyond drug use itself.

Final advice
When revising, do these three things first:
Choose one focus: marijuana only, or a limited category of drugs.
Rewrite the thesis and topic sentences so the essay has a clear structure.
Edit carefully for grammar and formal style.

You have strong raw material here. With better organization and more precise language, this could become a much more convincing argumentative essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position and a strong sense of urgency. You argue consistently that professors at USC should be able to speak English well enough to teach effectively, and your opening anecdote gives the paper an immediate human focus. This is a strong foundation. To revise successfully, focus on organization, fairness of argument, sentence-level clarity, and grammar/punctuation.

Main strengths

Clear thesis: Your position is easy to identify.
Relevant example: The story about your friend helps readers understand why the issue matters.
Strong sense of purpose: You stay focused on the problem and propose a solution.
Good awareness of audience: You write in a persuasive way, especially when discussing tuition and student expectations.

Most important revision: strengthen structure

Right now, the essay sometimes feels repetitive. Several body paragraphs make similar points: students pay tuition, professors should teach clearly, and language is necessary for teaching. Try to make each paragraph do one distinct job.

A possible structure:

Introduction
   - Brief anecdote
   - Context of the issue
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Why communication matters in teaching
   - Teaching requires explanation, answers to questions, feedback

Body Paragraph 2: Effects on students
   - Dropping classes, delayed graduation, confusion, unfair cost

Body Paragraph 3: University responsibility
   - USC already tests teaching assistants, so professors should meet a similar standard

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument + response
   - For example: research ability is valuable, but teaching positions also require communication

Conclusion
   - Restate claim and proposed solution

This will make your argument seem more logical and mature.

Improve the thesis

Your current thesis is understandable, but it can be more precise and formal. For example:

Possible revision:  
“Before professors are assigned to teach at USC, they should demonstrate sufficient English proficiency, because effective instruction depends on clear communication, students pay for education as well as research, and inadequate language skills can delay academic progress.”

This version is more specific and previews your main reasons.

Make the argument fairer and more academic

At times, your wording sounds too absolute, such as “the only solution” or “professors can’t teach them.” In academic argument, readers trust you more when you acknowledge complexity.

For example:
Instead of “professors can’t teach them”, write “professors may struggle to teach effectively.”
Instead of “the only solution”, write “one practical solution is…”

Also, be careful not to sound as if being from another country is the problem. The issue is language proficiency in the classroom, not nationality. This distinction is important.

Develop evidence more fully

The personal story is effective, but one anecdote is not enough for a strong academic argument. If this were a revised college essay, you could add:
university policy examples,
evidence about classroom comprehension,
data on course withdrawal or student complaints,
comparison with requirements for teaching assistants.

Even if you do not add outside sources, you can still explain your reasoning more deeply. For instance, why is office-hour communication essential? Why does this problem matter more in advanced courses?

Sentence clarity and style

Some sentences are too long or awkward, which makes your meaning less clear. Try to:
use shorter sentences,
remove repetition,
choose direct wording.

For example:

Original:  
“The task of professors is to teach and share their expertise with others.”

Revision:  
“A professor’s primary responsibility is to communicate knowledge clearly to students.”

Another example:

Original:  
“When students pay tuition, they should also receive what they're paying for-a teacher who can teach them.”

Revision:  
“When students pay tuition, they reasonably expect effective instruction in return.”

This sounds more formal and persuasive.

Grammar and editing issues

There are many small language errors that distract from your ideas. Pay special attention to:

Punctuation and spacing
Do not put random periods in the middle of sentences.
Avoid extra spaces.
Example: “It s” should be “It’s.”

Sentence boundaries
Some sentences are fragments or run-ons. Example:

Original:  
“She really needed the course for her major her only alternatives were…”

Corrected:  
“She really needed the course for her major, and her only alternatives were…”

Word choice
A few expressions are unnatural:
“stumble upon difficulties frequently” → “frequently encounter difficulties”
“squander through” → probably “struggle through”
“invoke the student to ponder” → “encourage students to consider”

Pronouns and agreement
You often use “he” or “his” for all professors or students. In modern academic English, this sounds outdated. Use:
“professors… they”
“students… they”

Apostrophes and plurals
teacher's assistants → usually teaching assistants or teachers’ assistants
Ph.D.s is acceptable, but be consistent.

Final advice

When revising, focus first on big issues:
clearer paragraph structure,
less repetition,
more balanced and precise argument.

Then do a second revision for grammar and proofreading.

You have a strong central idea and a persuasive instinct. With better organization and more careful editing, this essay could become much more convincing and academically polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Art Modell’s decision to move the Cleveland Browns was driven by greed and betrayed the fans and the city. The strongest part of the essay is your passion. The reader can clearly feel your anger and sympathy for Cleveland. You also use specific examples and figures (stadium costs, tax money, attendance numbers), which helps make the argument more concrete.

To revise successfully, focus on organization, tone, grammar, and evidence.

Argument and organization

Your main claim is strong, but the essay sometimes reads more like an emotional reaction than a carefully structured argument. Try to make the progression of ideas clearer.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the Browns move and present your thesis.
Background: describe the wider trend of NFL team relocations.
Evidence that Cleveland supported its teams: city investments, fan loyalty, stadium plans.
Evidence against Modell: his promises, Baltimore’s offer, lack of negotiation with Cleveland.
Conclusion: explain why this move was unethical, not just “business.”

Your current introduction has energy, but the thesis could be more direct. For example:

> Although team owners often defend relocation as a business decision, Art Modell’s move of the Cleveland Browns was unjustified because it ignored fan loyalty, broke promises to the city, and placed profit above civic responsibility.

This would help the reader understand your argument immediately.

Tone and academic style

Your essay is persuasive, but some phrases are too informal or too emotional for an academic argumentative essay. Examples:
“dump city”
“suck it up and take it like a man”
“gave Cleveland the one finger salute”
“Arthur B. Modell is a liar”

Such phrases make the essay sound more like a speech or editorial. In academic writing, it is stronger to show through evidence rather than insult directly. Instead of “Arthur B. Modell is a liar,” you could write:

> Modell contradicted his earlier public statements that he would keep the team in Cleveland.

That sounds more formal and persuasive.

Use of evidence

You include many useful details, but sometimes they need explanation. Don’t just list numbers; explain why they matter.

For example, when you mention Cleveland’s spending on the Gateway Project and the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame, connect it more clearly to your argument:
What do these investments show?
How do they prove that Cleveland was rebuilding itself?
Why does that make the team’s departure especially damaging?

Also, be careful about factual accuracy and consistency. A reader may question your credibility if names or facts seem uncertain. For example:
“Tennessee Cherokees” appears questionable.
“Los Angeles Rains” should be “Rams.”
“late march of 1983” should be capitalized as “March.”

Paragraph development

Some paragraphs include too many ideas at once. Try to use one main idea per paragraph. For instance, the long paragraph beginning “He never even gave Cleveland a chance…” includes:
the Gateway project
stadium renovation
the sin tax
Modell’s promise
ticket sales
fan culture
the NFL vote
the team name and colors

This should be divided into two or three paragraphs. That will make your reasoning much easier to follow.

Grammar and sentence-level issues

There are frequent grammar and mechanics problems. The most important ones to correct are:

Possessives and contractions
“the cities most beloved team” → the city’s most beloved team
“it's tax subsidized teams” → its tax-subsidized teams

Capitalization
“march” → March
“science museum” may need capitalization if it is the official name.
“the dawg pound” → the Dawg Pound

Word choice / spelling
“inwhich” → in which
“to fast” → too fast
“sell outs” → sellouts
“nation wide” → nationwide
“rapid” → probably rabid
“profoundly refused” → firmly refused

Punctuation
You have several comma splices and punctuation errors:
“tradition. and loyalty” → tradition, and loyalty
Add spaces after periods and commas.
Quotations need correct punctuation:
  - Bob Costas of NBC Sports called it “the renaissance of Cleveland.”
  - He said, “I will never take the team out of Cleveland.” Now he says, “Business is business.”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are difficult to understand. For example:

> “They were sold out for dollars and cents inwhich they were not even given time to make a counter offer.”

Possible revision:

> Cleveland’s fans were betrayed for financial gain, and the city was not even given time to make a counteroffer.

Strongest revision priority

If you only have time for a few changes, focus on these:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Divide long paragraphs into shorter, focused ones.
Replace emotional attacks with formal claims supported by evidence.
Correct major grammar and punctuation errors.

Final comment

This essay has a strong core argument and a real sense of urgency. Your passion is an advantage, but in revision you should channel that passion into a more organized and formal argument. If you improve structure and tone, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central concern: people often let social expectations determine their life choices, and this can lead to regret. You also make good use of examples about both women and men, which helps broaden the discussion beyond one gender. The essay shows strong engagement with the topic and an interest in moral and personal questions. For revision, focus on three main areas: argument structure, use of evidence, and language accuracy/style.

Argument and structure

Your main claim is interesting, but it needs to be stated more precisely. At the moment, the essay moves between several related ideas:

people should not follow others’ advice blindly
women have been pressured first toward domesticity, then toward careers
men are trapped by breadwinner expectations
a “middle road” is best

These ideas fit together, but the line of argument is sometimes unclear. Try revising your thesis so that it makes one strong claim, for example:

Social expectations often pressure both men and women into life choices that do not match their own values; therefore, individuals should make career and family decisions according to their personal priorities rather than social approval.

Then make sure each paragraph clearly supports that thesis.

Suggested structure
Introduction: introduce the problem and state your thesis clearly.
Paragraph on women’s expectations: explain Friedan and the pressure on women.
Paragraph on men’s expectations: explain Ralph and pressure on men.
Paragraph comparing the two: show that both genders face social traps.
Paragraph on alternatives / counterargument: discuss whether there are realistic alternatives and acknowledge difficulties.
Conclusion: return to your thesis and end with a strong final statement.

At present, some paragraphs are too long and include multiple points. Break them into shorter units with one topic each.

Use of evidence

You refer to Betty Friedan and Warren Farrell, which is a good start, but your evidence needs to be handled more carefully.

You include placeholders such as “ wrote Friedan” and “Ralph confessed ”. These must be replaced with actual quotations or removed.
If you quote texts, integrate the quotations and explain their significance. Do not assume the example speaks for itself.
Be careful about generalisations such as “many women ended up dissatisfied” or “most men helped around the house.” These are broad claims and need support, qualification, or more cautious wording.

For example, instead of:
“But many women ended up dissatisfied with their career, their marriage, and a good part of their whole life.”

you could write:
“For some women, the attempt to combine professional success with full domestic responsibility created deep dissatisfaction, because the demands of both roles were difficult to reconcile.”

This sounds more academic and less absolute.

Tone and academic style

Your essay sometimes uses rhetorical questions effectively, but there are too many, and they can make the essay sound conversational rather than academic. For instance:

“But what if it’s wrong?”
“Is it a harder life to forgo motherhood?”
“What’s not to like…?”

Try turning some of these into statements. Academic argument is usually stronger when it states a position directly.

Also, avoid informal or value-loaded phrases such as:
“follow the herd”
“worn to a frazzle”
“corporate cog-in-the-wheel”

These expressions are vivid, but they reduce the formal tone. Use more neutral wording.

Language and grammar

There are a number of grammar, word-choice, and punctuation problems. Some may come from German interference, especially word order and article use. Here are some important examples:

“being ,pressured” → “being pressured”
“what they want to do the rest of their lives” → “what they want to do for the rest of their lives”
“erected of them” → probably “expected of them”
“reouired” → “required”
“actuality prefer” → “actually prefer”
“tine-in” → “tie-in” or better “internet connection”
“Men and women, then as now” → better “Men and women, both then and now”
“Ralph … are likely to find” → subject agreement problem; if singular, use “is likely”

Watch punctuation too:
Avoid extra spaces before commas or periods.
Use quotation marks consistently.
Do not overuse commas between subject and verb.

Clarity and precision

Sometimes the sentence meaning is hard to follow because too many ideas are packed together. For example:

“The alternative, if a woman chooses not to follow the herd, is to suffer a harder life by eliminating a part of the whole…”

This could be simplified:
“If a woman rejects conventional expectations, she may feel forced to sacrifice either career or motherhood, and each choice brings difficulties.”

Shorter sentences will often make your argument stronger.

Final advice

For your next draft:
Write a clear one-sentence thesis.
Organise each paragraph around one main idea.
Replace placeholders with real quotations or remove them.
Reduce generalisations and explain evidence more carefully.
Edit for grammar, spelling, and formal academic tone.

You have a thoughtful topic and a strong sense of engagement. With clearer structure and more precise language, this could become a persuasive and mature argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

You have a clear, energetic voice, and your essay takes a strong position: men and women are different, and society should not treat those differences as shameful. That gives your paper a definite argument, which is a good starting point for an argumentative essay.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: Your position is easy to identify from the introduction.
Confidence and engagement: The essay sounds passionate and personal.
Use of examples: You try to support your ideas with examples from sports, entertainment, advertising, and restaurants.

Biggest issue: focus and argument quality
Right now, the essay’s central claim is too broad and unclear. Sometimes you argue that men and women are biologically different; sometimes that society should “enjoy” these differences; sometimes that businesses like Hooters or strip clubs are acceptable. These are not exactly the same argument.

Revision advice
Choose one precise thesis. For example:
Society should recognize sex differences while still protecting equal legal rights.
Equal treatment under the law does not require identical treatment in every social setting.
Attraction between men and women should not automatically be seen as sexist.

Then make sure every body paragraph directly supports that thesis.

Problems with evidence and logic
Many examples rely on personal opinion or weak comparisons. For instance:
The football/volleyball example assumes revenue alone should decide funding, but it does not address fairness, educational purpose, or Title IX.
Calling split funding “communism” is too extreme and weakens your credibility.
The strip-club and Hooters examples are controversial, but you do not seriously address objections such as exploitation, workplace discrimination, or exclusion.
Comparing Charles Barkley and Madonna is interesting, but the claim that one represents violence and the other love is too simplified.

Revision advice
For each paragraph, ask:
What exactly am I claiming?
What evidence supports it?
What might an intelligent opponent say?
How can I answer that objection fairly?

A stronger essay would use:
facts, laws, or statistics
more balanced reasoning
acknowledgment of counterarguments

Organization
The essay currently moves from topic to topic without a strong structure. This makes the argument feel scattered.

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Define your key idea clearly.
   - State a focused thesis.
Body paragraph 1: legal equality vs. social difference
Body paragraph 2: sports as an example
Body paragraph 3: media/advertising
Body paragraph 4: counterargument and response
Conclusion
   - Restate the thesis in a more careful way.

Try to begin each paragraph with a topic sentence that connects directly to the thesis.

Tone and audience
At times, the essay sounds provocative rather than analytical. Phrases like “Let us lust after the opposite sex” or “open one called buttocks for women” may distract readers from your argument. In academic writing, you should sound thoughtful and precise, even when discussing controversial topics.

Revision advice
Use a more formal style:
replace emotional or exaggerated claims
avoid slang such as “trashy place”
explain ideas instead of shocking the reader

Language and grammar
As a German speaker, some of your errors are very understandable, but they still need correction. The main patterns are:

Word choice / wrong word
underlining → *underlying*
resent → *recent*
faithful → *supporters/fans*
scene → *seen*
sexes → *sexist*
sells job → *sales job*
trask → *trash*

Common homophone errors
there → *their* / *there* / *they’re*
were → *where* / *were*
vise versa → *vice versa*
tearn → *team*

Sentence problems
Some sentences are run-on sentences or missing words. Example:

Agreement and plural forms
“There has been several lawsuits” → *There have been several lawsuits*
“a man and a women” → *a man and a woman*

Although men and women must have equal legal rights, recognizing biological and social differences between the sexes does not necessarily lead to unfairness; in some areas of culture and social life, these differences are part of normal human attraction and interaction.

Final advice

Delete weak or distracting examples and keep only the strongest two or three.
Proofread carefully for spelling, word choice, and sentence structure.

You have strong energy and a willingness to argue boldly. The next step is to make the essay more focused, more logical, and more academic in tone.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that welfare reform should combine ideas from both Democrats and Republicans. This is a strong starting point because your thesis is specific and gives the reader a roadmap: time limits, required training, reduced funding for some programs, and ending extra payments for additional children. You also use sources throughout, which shows effort to support your claims with evidence.

What is working well

Clear argumentative purpose  
You do not only describe the debate; you take a position. That is important in an argumentative essay.

Logical overall structure  
The essay moves through major policy areas one by one: training, time limits, spending cuts, and AFDC. This helps the reader follow your argument.

Use of evidence  
You include quotations and examples such as Project Match and Princess Rowell. These concrete examples make the essay more persuasive.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen your thesis and make it more precise
Your thesis is understandable, but it is a bit broad and sounds more like a list than a fully developed claim. Try to explain why combining the two parties’ ideas would be more effective and fair.

For example:  
Revised thesis: “The U.S. welfare system should combine time limits and mandatory job training with targeted spending cuts because this approach would encourage employment, reduce long-term dependency, and lower government costs.”

This version states both your position and your reasons.

Develop analysis, not only summary
At times, the essay reports what sources say without fully explaining how the evidence proves your point. After each quotation or example, add 1–2 sentences of analysis.

Example:  
After the Project Match example, explain why it matters:  
Does it show that training programs can work better than simple benefit cuts?  
Does it suggest that support and accountability should happen together?

This kind of explanation will make your argument stronger.

Address counterarguments
A good argumentative essay should consider objections. For example:
What happens to people who cannot find work within two years?
Could cutting benefits harm children?
Are all welfare recipients choosing dependency, or are some facing structural barriers such as low wages or lack of childcare?

You do not need to agree with these objections, but acknowledging them will make your essay sound more balanced and academically credible.

Be careful with generalizations and tone
Some sentences sound too absolute or judgmental, for example the claim that women on welfare are having more babies “at the government’s expense.” This can make the argument sound emotional rather than carefully reasoned.

Instead, use more formal and precise language:
“Some critics argue that certain policies may create financial incentives that unintentionally encourage larger family size.”
This sounds more academic and avoids overgeneralization.

Language and style issues

Because you are writing in academic English, focus on grammar, punctuation, capitalization, and word choice.

Common problems in this essay

Quotation formatting and punctuation
There are many punctuation errors with quotations. Examples:
“Everyone agrees that the welfare system should be changed, but 'how, is the big question”
missing quotation marks, commas in the wrong place, double punctuation

Make sure quotations are copied exactly and integrated grammatically into your sentence.

Capitalization
Examples:
“fourteen million people…” → “Fourteen million people…”
“highschool” → “high school”
“whitman” → “Whitman”

Spelling
Examples:
“Sliverstein” should be Silverstein
“life time” should be lifetime
“get on welfare” is better than “get on welfare” if quoted exactly; otherwise paraphrase carefully
“systems” should be system

Sentence grammar
Example:
“If these time limits were enforced there would no chance…”  
  → “If these time limits were enforced, there would be no chance…”

Word choice
“less people” → fewer people
“cut off recipients, benefits” → cut off recipients’ benefits
“more people being able to provide for and support their own family”  
  → more people able to provide for their own families

Organization suggestions

Consider revising by using this paragraph structure:

Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Body paragraph 1: mandatory training programs  
Body paragraph 2: time limits  
Body paragraph 3: spending cuts and reform of AFDC  
Body paragraph 4: counterargument and response  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why compromise is better than partisanship

Also, use clearer topic sentences. For example:
“Mandatory job training should be central to welfare reform because it helps recipients gain skills and become financially independent.”

Final advice
Your essay has a solid foundation: a clear claim, relevant evidence, and a recognizable structure. The next step is to make it more academic by improving grammar and citation style, adding deeper analysis, and avoiding broad or emotional statements. If you revise carefully, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that dorm curfews and restrictions on overnight visitation are unfair and should be changed. The strongest part of the essay is that you use personal experience to make the issue feel real. Main strengths
Clear stance: From the beginning, the reader understands that you oppose the curfew.
Relevant examples: You include several examples from campus life, which helps support your claim.
Sense of audience: You write as a student speaking to a university community, which suits the topic.

Main revision priorities

A. Make your thesis more precise
Right now, your argument moves between several related claims:
single-sex dorms should allow overnight visitation,
curfews are unfair,
curfews are unsafe,
freshmen should be treated like adults.

These ideas fit together, but your essay needs one clear central thesis. For example:

> Colleges should remove 2 a.m. dorm curfews because they treat students like children, create unnecessary safety risks, and make it difficult for students to host guests responsibly.

A thesis like this gives your essay structure.

B. Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads more like a stream of thoughts than a carefully planned argument. Try using a clearer paragraph structure:

Introduction  
   Introduce the issue and state your thesis.

Body paragraph 1: Fairness and adulthood  
   Explain why college students should be trusted to make decisions.

Body paragraph 2: Practical problems caused by the curfew  
   Use the homework example and explain how the rule makes study/social life harder.

Body paragraph 3: Safety concerns  
   Explain why forcing people to leave at 2 a.m. can be dangerous.

Body paragraph 4: Family/guest visits  
   Use your cousin example to show the rule can be unreasonable.

Conclusion  
   Restate your claim and suggest a reasonable policy change.

Try to give each paragraph one main idea.

C. Use stronger formal style
Your voice is engaging, but some phrases are too informal for an academic essay. For example:
“chill out” → “spend time”
“we all know” → “it is widely understood”
“things don’t always go our way” → more formal wording

Also, avoid emotional overstatement unless you support it carefully. For example, listing “mugged, raped, kidnapped, or beat up” is powerful, but it would be even stronger if connected to a broader claim about campus safety rather than sounding dramatic.

Because your first language is German, pay special attention to word choice, sentence structure, and pronoun clarity.

Common problems in this draft:
Typing/word errors:  
  “he treated” → “be treated”  
  “A could” → “it could” or rewrite  
  “sigu.her mate guest” → likely “sign her male guest in”  
  “bad to go” → “had to go”

Pronoun/reference problems:  
  “If a person chooses to do whatever in their room...”  
  This idea is understandable, but rewrite more clearly:  
  > What a student chooses to do in their room should be a matter between that student and their roommate.

Grammar:  
  “A friend of mine that’s a Bates House resident...”  
  Better:  
  > A friend of mine who lives in Bates House...

Word form:  
  “We are adult enough” → “We are old enough” or “We are mature enough”

Capitalization/punctuation:  
  You have several unnecessary capital letters (“In,” “Into,” “Is,” “Influenced”).  
  Also, avoid double spaces and comma splices.

Sentence-level advice
Many sentences are either too short and abrupt or too loosely connected. Try combining related ideas logically. For example:

Original:

This shows critical thinking.

Final revision advice

Reorganize the essay into topic-based paragraphs.
Replace informal expressions with academic language.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation carefully.

You have a strong topic and genuine commitment to your position. With clearer structure and more polished language, this essay could become much more convincing.
