Your essay has a clear and heartfelt message: teachers deserve more recognition, appreciation, and pay. The personal conviction in your writing is a real strength. Personal voice

Relevant examples

Main areas to improve

Right now, the essay repeats the idea that teachers should be rewarded, but the main argument could be more precise. Try to state exactly why they deserve recognition and who should provide it.

For example, your thesis could be:

“Teachers, especially high school teachers, should receive greater recognition from students, parents, and schools because they shape students’ lives, work under difficult conditions, and are often underappreciated both emotionally and financially.”

This gives your essay a stronger structure.

Improve organization
The essay has many good ideas, but they are sometimes repeated or placed in a loose order. Group similar ideas together.

A possible structure:

Body paragraph 1: teachers change students’ lives  
Body paragraph 2: high school teachers face special challenges  
Body paragraph 3: teachers deserve emotional recognition  
Body paragraph 4: teachers also deserve better pay  
Body paragraph 5: students, parents, and schools all share responsibility  
Conclusion: restate the importance of recognizing teachers

This will make your essay feel more focused and persuasive.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases sound conversational rather than argumentative. For example:

“go an extra mile” → go the extra mile
“I guess they have to be that way” → They may need to be especially dedicated
“put up with a lot” → deal with many challenges

Also avoid direct emotional phrasing that may sound vague in academic writing, such as “They too deserve their flowers while they live.” This is expressive, but in an academic essay it would be clearer to say:

“They deserve appreciation during their careers, not only after retirement.”

Develop reasons with clearer evidence
Many claims are valid, but they need fuller explanation. For example:

“High school teachers deserve the most recognition because students in high school seem to be the hardest to deal with.”  
  This is an interesting point, but explain *why*: teenage students may be less motivated, more independent, and under greater academic and social pressure.

“Teachers also deserve more pay.”  
  Good point, but add support: the workload includes lesson planning, grading, emotional support, and classroom management.

Try to move from claim → explanation → example in each paragraph.

Correct grammar and sentence structure
There are several language errors that you should revise carefully.

Articles and quantity
“a limited amount of teachers” → a limited number of teachers  
  (“amount” is for uncountable nouns; “teachers” is countable.)

Possessives
“teacher's limitations” → teachers’ limitations
“a teachers name” → a teacher’s name

Word choice
“more heart touching” → more touching / more heartfelt
“Everyday” → Every day  
  (“Everyday” is an adjective; “every day” is an adverbial phrase.)

Sentence fragments
“A material gift or a kind word of expression.”  
  This is a fragment. You could write:  
  “The reward may be a material gift or a kind expression of gratitude.”

Awkward phrasing
“it teachers that have helped made these figures” →  
  “it is teachers who have helped shape these figures”

Punctuation
There are extra spaces and punctuation problems:
“care and.try” → care and try
“The students. should” → The students should
Remove the stray quotation mark/apostrophe after compliment teachers.
Avoid comma splices and overlong sentences.

Some claims are too broad, for example:

“Teachers seem to have a mother instinct”
“many answer with the name of a teacher”
“students in high school seem as if they don't care”

These statements may not apply to everyone. In academic writing, use softer and more precise language:

Many teachers show a strong nurturing instinct
Some students name a teacher as a role model

This makes your argument sound more credible.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis
Reorganize the essay into distinct argument paragraphs
Reduce repetition
Replace informal language with more academic wording
Correct grammar, punctuation, and sentence fragments

You have a strong foundation because your essay is sincere and full of relevant experience. With clearer structure and more polished language, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important topic and shows that you have done some research by referring to sources such as Hepburn, Stern, and Jensen. You also try to consider more than one perspective, which is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise successfully, focus on three main areas: clear thesis and argument, organization, and language accuracy/style.

Thesis and argument

At the moment, your position is not fully clear. In the introduction, you suggest that television violence is “a serious concern,” but in the conclusion you say violence is meant “to add texture” and that children will have to deal with it anyway. These ideas point in different directions.

Ask yourself: What exactly am I arguing? For example, you could argue one of these:
Television violence can negatively affect children, so stricter controls are necessary.
Television violence is common, but censorship is not the best solution.
Parents, not the government, should be mainly responsible for regulating children’s viewing.

Choose one main claim and make it explicit in the introduction. Then make sure every paragraph supports that claim.

Example of a stronger thesis:
> Although violence on television is widespread and may influence children, government censorship is less effective than parental supervision and informed viewing choices.

This would help unify your discussion of the V-chip, government proposals, and parents’ responsibility.

Organization

Your essay currently reads more like a list of points than a fully developed argument. The paragraphs often introduce facts, but they do not always explain how those facts support your position.

A clearer structure could be:

Introduction
   - Introduce the topic
   - Explain why it matters
   - State your thesis clearly

Body Paragraph 1: Extent of TV violence
   - Use your evidence about viewing time and violent programming
   - Explain why this matters for children

Body Paragraph 2: Proposed solutions
   - Discuss the V-chip and congressional proposals
   - Evaluate whether they are effective

Body Paragraph 3: Counterargument
   - Explain why some people defend violent content (realism, adult audience, artistic freedom)
   - Respond to this point

Body Paragraph 4: Your preferred solution
   - Parental supervision, ratings, media literacy, etc

Conclusion
   - Restate your main argument
   - End with a clear final thought

Try to include a topic sentence at the start of each paragraph. For example:
> One reason television violence remains controversial is that policymakers disagree about how to regulate it.

Use of evidence

You include sources, which is good, but the essay often quotes or summarizes facts without analysis. After each piece of evidence, explain:
What does this evidence show?
Why is it important?
How does it support my thesis?

For example, after mentioning the violent cartoons, you should explain whether this supports the idea that children are especially exposed to violence, or whether the concern may be exaggerated.

Also, be careful about unsupported statements such as:
> “the murder must happen”
This sounds too absolute. A more academic phrasing would be:
> “Some writers argue that violent scenes are necessary for certain dramatic plots.”

Style and academic tone

Some sentences are too informal or subjective for academic writing. Examples:
“the action thrills them”
“the infamous V-chip”
“well that is strictly up to the viewer to decide”

Try to use a more neutral tone. For example:
“Children may be attracted to fast-paced and violent content.”
“The V-chip has been criticized as ineffective.”
“However, definitions of excessive violence may vary among viewers.”

Also avoid rhetorical questions unless used very carefully. Instead of:
> “but how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?”
write:
> “In practice, many parents may not consistently supervise what their teenagers watch.”

Grammar and language issues

Because your first language is German, pay special attention to word choice, capitalization, and sentence structure.

Specific corrections:
“violent tv shows” → “violent TV shows”
“Some of the proposals to antiviolence” → “Some proposed anti-violence measures”
“Some television shows show violence” → repetitive; better: “Some television programs include violence”
“made for television movies” → “made-for-television movies”
“1995-1995 season” → probably “1994–1995” or “1995–1996” (check source)
“sinister combat violence'” → punctuation error; check the quotation carefully
“whomever is writing” → “whoever is writing”
“The cause for television violence” → better: “The purpose of violence in television programs”

Also watch for sentence variety. Many of your sentences begin with “Some” or “Many.” Try to vary your structure.

Most important revision priorities

Decide on one clear thesis.
Reorganize the essay so each paragraph supports that thesis.
Add analysis after evidence.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Proofread for grammar, punctuation, and source accuracy.

You have a strong topic and useful source material. With a clearer argument and more careful organization, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a wide range of supporting ideas. You argue consistently that government should support the arts, and you discuss historical, social, economic, educational, and therapeutic benefits. This breadth is a real strength. The tone is also appropriately formal in many places, and your paragraphs generally stay focused on one main idea.

What is working well

Clear argumentative stance  
   From the introduction onward, your position is easy to understand: arts funding should be maintained or increased.

Variety of arguments  
   You do not rely on only one reason. You include culture, communication, community, tourism, creativity, therapy, and crime prevention. This gives the essay richness.

Ambitious vocabulary and style  
   You attempt sophisticated phrasing and abstract concepts, which is impressive. Your essay sounds thoughtful and engaged.

Main areas to improve

Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction presents the topic, but the thesis could be more direct and specific. Right now, the essay moves into many different claims without clearly previewing them.

Current idea: government should support the arts.  
Stronger thesis example:  
*Government should continue funding the arts because the arts preserve cultural identity, strengthen communities, contribute to local economies, and promote education and mental well-being.*

This gives your essay a clearer structure.

Improve paragraph organization
Some paragraphs begin well but become too general or drift into unrelated ideas. Try this structure for each body paragraph:

topic sentence
explanation
concrete example
link back to your argument

For example, in the paragraph about economic benefits, you could focus only on tourism, jobs, and related industries, instead of mixing several loosely connected points.

Use more specific evidence
Many claims are interesting but broad: “psychologists have told us for years,” “researchers have found,” “continual research has been done.” These phrases sound persuasive, but they need evidence. In an academic essay, readers expect examples, data, or named studies.

For revision, either:
add specific evidence, or
make the claim more cautious.

For instance:

Weak: *As researchers have found, arts such as drawing and painting help develop clearer thinking.*
Better: *Several educational studies suggest that participation in the arts can improve concentration, problem-solving, and creative thinking.*

Avoid overgeneralization
A few statements are too absolute. For example:
*art is something which produces a positive, constructive analysis of aspects of human existence*
*some works are not meant be valued as art*

These claims are philosophically controversial and may weaken your argument because they distract from funding. Also, the paragraph on pornography feels somewhat separate from your main claim. Unless your assignment specifically asks about censorship or definitions of art, you might cut or shorten that section.

Strengthen sentence-level grammar and style
Your writing is advanced, but there are recurring issues with article use, word choice, and sentence construction. Here are some examples:

“which should keep” → “which should be maintained” or “which should continue to receive support”
“man has seen a need” → “human beings have felt a need”  
  (“man” sounds outdated and too general.)
“it is interpretable” → “they are understandable”
“which directly can influence” → “which can directly influence”
“pschosanalysts” → “psychoanalysts”
“Besides from being productive” → “Besides being productive” or “In addition to being productive”
“not meant be valued” → “not meant to be valued”
“in a ever-more complex” → “in an ever more complex” or “in an increasingly complex”

Watch for German-influenced phrasing
Some expressions are understandable but not natural in English. This is common for German speakers. Examples:

“look in retrospect” → “look back”
“find means of relieving stress” → acceptable, but “find ways to relieve stress” is more natural
“develop the criminal into a more successful, reformed individual” → “help rehabilitate offenders”
“the very essence of early human thought” is dramatic; use carefully unless you can support it

In general, prefer simpler, more direct wording over very abstract phrasing.

Suggested revision priorities

Rewrite the introduction with a sharper thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around 3–4 main reasons.
Add specific examples or evidence.
Remove or reduce the paragraph about pornography unless it clearly serves your thesis.
Edit for grammar, concision, and more natural English phrasing.

Possible revised structure

Introduction: thesis
Cultural value: arts preserve history and express shared values
Social value: arts build community and improve communication
Economic value: tourism, jobs, local development
Educational/therapeutic value: creativity, mental health, rehabilitation
Conclusion: restate why public funding is justified

Final comment

This essay shows strong ideas and genuine intellectual engagement. Your biggest challenge is not lack of content, but control: clearer structure, more precise claims, and more natural English will make your argument much more convincing. You already have the material for a strong essay; revision should focus on shaping it into a more focused academic argument.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position from the beginning: you strongly support a salary cap for professional football players. This is a strength, because your reader always knows what you are trying to argue. You also organize the essay into several reasons, which gives it a recognizable structure. For revision, the main areas to improve are argument quality, organization, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear thesis: You answer the question directly and take a strong stand.
Strong engagement with the topic: Your voice is confident and passionate.
Paragraph development: Most body paragraphs focus on one main reason, such as fairness, greed, facilities, and player salaries.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction lists three reasons, but later the essay discusses more than those three. Try to make your thesis more focused and consistent.

Current idea:  
“There are many reasons why there should be a salary cap.”

Stronger version:  
“A salary cap in professional football should be introduced because it would reduce financial inequality between teams, limit excessive player salaries, and encourage teams to invest more in fans and public good.”

This version is more formal and previews your main points clearly.

B. Improve logic and evidence
Many claims are very broad or unsupported. For example:
“A professional football player makes more money in one year than a doctor does in a lifetime.”
“The whole reason why OJ Simpson was let off was because he was a popular football player.”
“Money and power didn't get involved until the 1950's.”

These statements need evidence, and some are factually questionable or too absolute. In academic argument, avoid claims you cannot prove.

What to do instead:
Use more cautious language: “often,” “in some cases,” “may,” “can.”
Include facts, examples, or statistics if possible.
Distinguish between opinion and evidence.

For example:
Instead of saying football players “do nothing but entertain,” you could argue that “although athletes provide entertainment, their salaries seem disproportionate when compared with socially essential professions such as medicine or nursing.”

C. Use a more academic tone
Some phrases sound emotional or insulting:
“greedy football players”
“not smart enough to pass the second grade”
“do nothing but entertain”

These weaken your credibility because they sound unfair and exaggerated. A stronger argument is usually more balanced.

Try replacing emotional judgments with neutral phrasing:
“highly paid players”
“athletes may be rewarded more for market value than for educational achievement”
“sports are a form of entertainment rather than an essential public service”

D. Strengthen paragraph structure
Several paragraphs contain many ideas at once. Try this structure for each body paragraph:
Topic sentence
Explanation
Example or evidence
Link back to the thesis

For instance, your paragraph about doctors and football players could focus only on social value vs. financial reward. The paragraph about facilities should focus only on how teams should spend revenue.

Language and Style Issues

A. Word choice
A few expressions are incorrect or outdated:
“wee” → we
“affective” → effective
“use to be” → used to be
“ant other team” → any other team
“mentally retarded” is outdated and offensive in modern English. Use people with intellectual disabilities.

B. Grammar
Watch for:
Subject-verb agreement: “A doctor, or a nurse work” → “A doctor or a nurse works”
Pronouns: “other peoples lives” → “other people’s lives”
Sentence boundaries: “So. why don't we…” should be one sentence.
Comparatives: “not as good as they should be” is okay, but some comparisons need tightening.

C. Formality
Avoid rhetorical questions one after another. A few can be effective, but too many make the essay sound conversational rather than academic. Instead of asking many questions, turn them into statements.

Example:
“Is this really fair?” → “This situation appears unfair.”

Content Suggestions
You have interesting ideas, but some need better development:
Fairness between professions: good topic, but explain *why* society rewards entertainment more than essential work.
Facilities for fans: this is a promising practical argument. It would be stronger with broader evidence than one personal experience.
Competitive balance between teams: this is probably your strongest argument. Expand it more, because salary caps are often defended for this reason.

Suggested Revision Strategy
Rewrite the introduction with a clear, formal thesis.
Choose 3 main arguments only.
Remove exaggerated or offensive statements.
Add examples that are more factual and less emotional.
Revise for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Final Comment
You have a strong opinion and a clear desire to persuade the reader, which is an excellent basis for an argumentative essay. The next step is to make your argument more credible, balanced, and academic. If you revise the tone, tighten the organization, and support your claims more carefully, your essay will become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that stronger gun control is constitutional and necessary to reduce violence. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. You also use sources throughout, which shows good research effort. For revision, focus especially on organization, clarity, grammar, and source integration.

Main strengths

Clear argumentative stance: Your thesis is consistent throughout the essay.
Relevant support: You include constitutional, historical, and statistical points.
Serious tone: The essay sounds formal and appropriate for academic writing.
Use of evidence: You attempt to support claims with quotations and references.

Main areas to improve

A. Improve the thesis and overall structure
At the moment, the first paragraph already gives your argument, but the essay later repeats similar ideas. Try to create a single clear introduction with:
background,
thesis,
preview of main points.

For example, your thesis could be:

“Stronger federal gun control laws, including registration, background checks, and waiting periods, are constitutional and would reduce violent crime and prevent avoidable deaths.”

Then organize the body into clear sections:
Constitutional argument
Public safety and crime statistics
Why current laws are insufficient
What reforms are needed
Counterargument and response

This will reduce repetition.

B. Avoid repetition
Several ideas appear multiple times:
gun control is constitutional
waiting periods and background checks help
guns cause many deaths
federal legislation is necessary

Instead of repeating them, develop each point once in a fuller way.

C. Strengthen paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain too many ideas. Each paragraph should have:
one topic sentence,
explanation,
evidence,
analysis,
a concluding sentence or transition.

For example, the paragraph beginning “In 1981, Jim Brady…” could focus only on how that event changed the political debate on gun control.

Language and grammar issues

Because your first language is German, some of your English shows common L2 problems, especially with capitalization, word choice, article use, and sentence correctness.

A. Capitalization
In English, do not capitalize common nouns unless they begin a sentence.

Incorrect:
“Gun Control”
“Constitutional Rights”
“Federal Legislation”

Correct:
“gun control”
“constitutional rights”
“federal legislation”

B. Word form and spelling
There are several spelling mistakes and incorrect word forms. Examples:

“sawed of shotguns” → sawed-off shotguns
“hand gun” → handgun
“machineguns” → machine guns or machine guns
“ant-gun control” → anti-gun-control
“fire arms” → firearms
“definatly” → definitely
“riffles” → rifles
“licencing” → licensing
“can not” → cannot

Also check source names carefully:
“Cosec” / “Cozic”
“CUF” / “The Close Up Foundation”

Be consistent.

C. Sentence-level correctness
Some sentences are awkward or incorrect. For example:

“Registering guns and requiring a waiting period to check prospective buyers backgrounds…”  
  → “Registering guns and requiring a waiting period to check prospective buyers’ backgrounds…”

“The NRA debates…”  
  → “The NRA argues…”

“the 2nd Amendment guarantees a state's right…not an individuals right…”  
  → “…not an individual’s right…”

“Federal law requires gun buyers to prevent identification…”  
  → “Federal law requires gun buyers to present identification…”

D. Serious formatting problem
This sentence must be fixed immediately:

“lnl981,JimBrady,W'hiteHousePresssecretarywasshot…”

It looks like a copying or typing error. It should be something like:

“In 1981, Jim Brady, the White House press secretary, was shot during the attempted assassination of President Ronald Reagan.”

Errors like this seriously damage readability.

Use of sources and evidence

You include many citations, which is good, but you need to integrate sources more smoothly. Do not simply place facts and quotations one after another. Explain why each source matters.

For example:

Current version:
> Firearms are used to murder nearly 12,000 people annually…

Better:
> Statistics also support the need for stronger regulation. According to Cozic, firearms are used in nearly 12,000 murders annually, while thousands more deaths result from accidents and suicide (197). These figures suggest that gun violence is not limited to criminal attacks; it is also a broader public health issue.

Also be careful with claims such as “Most courts” and “The courts agree.” These are broad claims and should be precisely supported.

Argumentation

Your argument would be stronger if you addressed counterarguments more directly. For example:
Some people argue that guns are necessary for self-defense.
Others argue that criminals will get guns anyway.
Some argue that regulation punishes law-abiding citizens.

You mention some of these points, but you should answer them in a fuller and more balanced way. This will make your essay sound more persuasive and academic.

Revision priorities

Create a clearer introduction with one strong thesis
Reorganize body paragraphs by topic
Remove repetition
Correct grammar, spelling, and capitalization
Fix quotation/citation formatting
Add more analysis after evidence
Address counterarguments more fully

Final comment

This essay shows good ideas and a clear sense of purpose. Your biggest challenge is not the argument itself, but the presentation of the argument. With better organization, cleaner language, and more precise source use, this could become a much stronger academic essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You clearly have a strong opinion about abortion, and your essay shows commitment, personal engagement, and a willingness to address exceptions such as rape and danger to the mother’s life. These are good foundations for an argumentative essay. To revise successfully, focus on organization, tone, grammar, and the strength of your reasoning.

Main strengths
Clear position: Your reader understands that you generally oppose abortion but allow some exceptions.
Personal involvement: The story about your cousin adds emotional force and shows why the topic matters to you.
Attempt at complexity: You do not present the issue as entirely simple; you acknowledge difficult cases.

Main problems to revise

A. Thesis and consistency
Your position is understandable, but it is not always stated in a precise and consistent way. At first, you argue for “strict rules,” but later you say some women should have the option of abortion. Then you argue that women who have abortions after consensual sex should go to jail for murder. These are very strong claims, and they need to fit together logically.

Suggestion: Write a thesis that states your exact position more carefully. For example:  
“Although abortion should not be treated as a casual solution to unintended pregnancy, it should remain legally available in cases of rape, coercion, and serious risk to the mother’s life.”

This kind of thesis is clearer and easier to defend.

B. Tone and word choice
Your essay often uses highly emotional or judgmental expressions:
“play the game”
“horseplay”
“kill it”
“wild maniac”
“vicious sperm”
“should be put into jail for murder”

Such language can weaken an academic argument because it sounds more like anger than analysis. In argumentative writing, a respectful and precise tone is more persuasive.

Better approach: Use neutral academic wording:
“consensual sexual activity”
“terminate a pregnancy”
“victim of rape”
“criminal penalties” instead of “jail for murder”

C. Evidence and reasoning
Many claims are presented as facts, but they are not supported with sources. For example:
effectiveness rates of contraceptives
government aid solving financial problems
abortion causing infertility in your cousin’s case
claims about what “all” women should do

In academic writing, personal belief is not enough. You need reliable evidence from studies, laws, or medical sources.

Also, some reasoning is too simplified. For example, saying adoption is always an easy alternative ignores the physical and emotional burden of pregnancy. Saying welfare solves financial difficulties is also too broad.

Suggestion: Add evidence and recognize complexity. A stronger argument often admits limits and responds to opposing views fairly.

D. Organization
The essay has paragraphs, but the structure can be improved. Some paragraphs are too long, and ideas sometimes repeat or move suddenly from one point to another.

Suggested structure:
Introduction – Present the issue and your thesis.
Body paragraph 1 – Responsibility and contraception.
Body paragraph 2 – Moral argument against abortion after consensual sex.
Body paragraph 3 – Exceptions: rape.
Body paragraph 4 – Exceptions: danger to the mother’s life.
Body paragraph 5 – Personal example of your cousin, connected clearly to your argument.
Conclusion – Restate your position in a balanced way.

Use topic sentences to guide the reader.

Language issues to correct

Because you are a German-speaking learner, some grammar problems may come from article use, agreement, and sentence structure. Here are important examples:

“a sensible, well-minded women” → “a sensible, clear-minded woman”
“is said to ninety-five percent effective” → “is said to be ninety-five percent effective”
“a method that should be practiced by all single persons should practice” → remove repetition
“that not very fair” → “that is not very fair”
“can not” → usually “cannot”
“persons how are financially unable” → “persons who are financially unable”
“is she was raped” → “if she was raped”
“case and point” → “case in point”
“I does think” → “I do think”
“govermnent” → “government”
“should be prevent” → “should be prevented”

Also watch:
subject-verb agreement
singular/plural nouns
comma splices (two full sentences joined only by a comma)
run-on sentences

For example, this sentence is too long and should be divided:
“I know if I was impregnated by this fiend… it would always be there.”  
Break it into 2–3 shorter sentences.

Specific revision advice
Replace emotional wording with formal language.
Reduce repetition of “I feel” and “I believe.”
Support factual claims with evidence.
Clarify your legal position: regulation, restriction, or criminalization?
Make sure exceptions are logically explained.
Shorten very long sentences.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

Final comment
You have passion and a clear desire to persuade, which is valuable. Your next step is to make the essay more academic, balanced, and logically structured. If you revise the tone, clarify the thesis, and support your claims with evidence, your argument will become much stronger and more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a strong sense of voice. The reader always knows what you believe: that sex is overused in the media and that this can influence teenagers negatively. You also organize the essay around different media forms (television, radio, magazines), which gives the paper a recognizable structure. These are good foundations for an argumentative essay.

Main strength: clear position
Your thesis is easy to identify: sex is “overrated” in the media and this has harmful effects. You also try to move from examples to consequences, especially in the later paragraphs about teenagers, pregnancy, and sexually transmitted diseases. That movement from description to argument is important in academic writing.

Main area to improve: make the argument more precise
At the moment, the essay often repeats the same idea (“sex is everywhere”) instead of developing it more deeply. Try to make your central claim more specific. For example:

Do you mean that media contains too much sexual content?
Or that media shows sex without responsibility?
Or that media links sex to consumerism and entertainment?

These are related, but not identical. Your strongest point seems to be: media often presents sex irresponsibly, without showing consequences or protection, and this can shape teenagers’ attitudes. If you revise around this claim, the essay will feel more focused and persuasive.

Use stronger evidence and analysis
You include many examples, but often they are based on personal impression: “every time I turn on the radio” or “almost every magazine.” In academic argumentation, such statements can sound exaggerated. Instead:

Use more careful wording: “many,” “often,” “frequently,” rather than “every” or “always.”
Explain why each example matters.
If possible, add facts, studies, or statistics.

For instance, instead of only naming TV shows and songs, explain how they normalize casual sex, omit contraception, or connect attractiveness with product marketing.

Improve paragraph development
Some paragraphs list examples but do not fully connect them back to the thesis. A stronger paragraph should do three things:

Topic sentence: state the main point of the paragraph.
Evidence/examples: provide specific support.
Analysis: explain how the evidence proves your argument.

Example structure:
*Television often presents sex as casual and consequence-free.*
Then give one or two examples.
Then explain: *Because these shows rarely show discussions of consent, contraception, or emotional consequences, they can create unrealistic expectations for young viewers.*

Tone and academic style
Your voice is passionate, which is good, but some phrases sound too conversational for an academic essay, for example:
“Now radio is almost as bad as television.”
“Now if I’m not mistaken…”
“That’s not reality.”

Try a more formal tone:
“Radio presents similar patterns.”
“This advertising strategy is questionable because…”
“Such portrayals do not reflect the realities of sexual responsibility.”

Also avoid directly addressing the reader too often with “you.” In academic essays, it is usually better to write “viewers,” “audiences,” or “teenagers.”

Grammar and language issues
Because you are a German-speaking writer, pay special attention to word choice, verb forms, punctuation, and possessives. Here are some recurring issues:

Capitalization
“Sex Is used to do everything” → Sex is used to sell everything

Verb form / grammar
“bluntly saids” → bluntly says
“their only conception of sex is through the media” (remove stray apostrophe)
“The only things else that needs to be said is” → The only thing left to say is

Word choice
“overrated” may not always be the best word. You may mean:
  - overused
  - overemphasized
  - overexposed
“promoting sex to sell something” is understandable, but using sexual imagery to sell products sounds more academic.

Possessives / homophones
“your selling” → you’re selling
“Its funny” → It’s funny

Sentence structure
Some sentences are long or repetitive. Shorten and combine carefully for clarity.

For example:
“With all this sex in the media, I wonder why teenagers think the way they do about sex.”
Better:
Given the amount of sexual content in the media, it is not surprising that many teenagers develop unrealistic ideas about sex.

Conclusion
Your conclusion currently repeats earlier points. A stronger conclusion should:
restate the thesis in new words,
summarize the main reasons,
and end with a final thought or recommendation.

For example, you could conclude that media should not avoid sexuality completely, but should portray it more responsibly, including communication, contraception, and consequences.

Revision priorities
When you revise, focus on these steps in order:

Rewrite the thesis so it is more precise.
Reduce repetition and make each paragraph serve a clear purpose.
Add analysis after each example.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Edit grammar and sentence clarity carefully.

Final comment
This essay has a strong core argument and clear commitment, which are valuable. Your next step is to turn that strong opinion into a more focused, evidence-based academic argument. If you do that, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents an interesting topic: the psychological and social influence of clothing. You use sources throughout, and you clearly try to show that fashion affects identity, social acceptance, age groups, and ideas of modesty. This gives your paper breadth. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis/focus, organization, use of evidence, tone, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths
Strong topic choice: The connection between clothing and psychology is engaging and relevant.
Use of research: You include multiple sources and page references, which shows academic effort.
Range of ideas: You discuss self-image, adolescence, consumer behavior, older adults, and modesty.

Thesis and argument
Right now, the essay feels more like a report of ideas from sources than a clear argument. Your introduction suggests that clothing influences how we judge others and how people see themselves, but the essay does not develop one precise claim consistently.

What to revise
Create a clearer thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

> Clothing shapes both personal identity and social judgment, because it influences self-perception, group belonging, and the way others interpret character and status.

This gives you a central argument that the body paragraphs can support.

Organization
At the moment, paragraphs often read as separate source summaries. Try to organize them by argument points, not by whichever source comes next.

Possible structure
Introduction: topic + thesis  
Clothing and first impressions  
Clothing and self-image/identity  
Peer pressure and adolescence  
Consumer behavior and fashion trends  
Age and clothing expectations  
Modesty and cultural values  
Conclusion

Also, use transitions to show how ideas connect:
“In addition to shaping first impressions…”
“This influence is especially strong among adolescents…”
“Consumer behavior further shows…”

Use of evidence
You include many quotations and paraphrases, but often they are not fully explained. In academic writing, each source should support your point.

Example
You quote Herrick’s poem at length, but then do not explain clearly how it supports your argument. Ask:
What does the poem show about clothing?
Does it suggest that disorder in dress creates attraction?
How does that connect to psychology or social perception?

After using evidence, add 1–3 sentences of analysis. A useful pattern is:
claim → evidence → explanation.

Also, avoid unsupported generalizations such as:
“If she wears a mini-skirt, she probably feel sexy.”
“One may judge them to be a slut, a pimp…”

These statements sound too absolute and can seem offensive or biased. Academic tone should be more careful:
> “People may make assumptions about personality or sexuality based on clothing, although such judgments are often unfair.”

Tone and style
Your essay sometimes uses informal, subjective, or judgmental wording. For an argumentative essay, aim for a more neutral academic tone.

Examples to revise
“this is where fashion designers feed” → “this is where fashion designers profit”
“slut” / “pimp” → avoid these terms in academic writing unless directly analyzing stereotypes
“Funny, strict, friendly, or sloppy…” → “Observers may infer traits such as friendliness, seriousness, or carelessness…”

Also, avoid switching between one / he / she / they / I. Choose a consistent formal style. Usually, “people,” “individuals,” or “they” works best.

Sentence-level grammar and word choice
Because you are a German speaker, some errors may come from direct translation or article/preposition use. Watch especially for:

Subject-verb agreement
“that person feel” → “that person feels”
“she probably feel sexy” → “she probably feels sexy”

Word form
“has strong affect on adolescents” → “has a strong effect on adolescents”
“The needs for clothing are…” → “The needs related to clothing are…”

Capitalization
“one doesn’t know…” → “One doesn’t know…”
“older men and women…” → “Older men and women…”

Prepositions
“indicator on how” → “indicator of how”
“aspects from fashion” → “aspects of fashion”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and hard to follow. Break them into shorter units.

For example:
> “There are so many psychological aspects from fashion that influence the way people dress the way they do.”

Could become:
> “Fashion involves many psychological factors. These factors influence why people dress as they do.”

Citations and integration
You cite page numbers, which is good, but the source information is incomplete in the essay itself. Make sure your final paper includes a proper Works Cited/References page in the required style (MLA, APA, etc).

Also, introduce sources more smoothly:
“According to Mary Shaw Ryan…”
“Flugel argues that…”
“Herrick’s poem suggests that…”

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points but could be stronger if it returns to the thesis. Instead of ending with general comments about enjoying clothes, emphasize the significance of your argument:
> Clothing matters not only as decoration but also as a social and psychological signal that shapes identity, belonging, and judgment.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs by main argument.
Replace informal or biased statements with careful academic language.
Add analysis after quotations.
Edit for grammar, agreement, and sentence clarity.

You have a solid base of ideas and research. With clearer argumentation and more formal language, this could become a much stronger academic essay.

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that abortion should remain the woman’s decision. This gives the paper a strong sense of purpose. You also try to engage with opposing arguments, which is important in argumentative writing. For revision, focus on three main areas: organization and clarity of argument, development of evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths
Clear thesis/stance: Your main claim is understandable: abortion should be left to the woman.
Awareness of counterarguments: You mention anti-abortion arguments and try to refute them.
Relevant moral focus: You recognize that this topic involves ethical complexity, which suits an argumentative essay.

Main revision priorities

A. Make your thesis more precise
Right now, your thesis appears in the second paragraph: “the decision to have an abortion or not should be left entirely up to the woman.” That is clear, but you should also state why in a more structured way.

For example, your introduction could end with a thesis like:
“Abortion should remain a legal choice because pregnancy affects the woman’s body and future directly, because special cases such as rape make universal bans unjust, and because forcing unwanted births can create serious social and psychological harm.”

This gives your essay a roadmap.

B. Improve paragraph structure
Some paragraphs begin well but then become unclear or incomplete. Each body paragraph should have:
a topic sentence
an explanation
an example or evidence
a link back to the thesis

For example:
Paragraph 1: abortion as a matter of bodily autonomy
Paragraph 2: the weakness of absolute anti-abortion arguments in cases such as rape
Paragraph 3: why the genocide/Nazi comparison is inaccurate
Conclusion: restate claim and summarize reasons

C. Develop your arguments with evidence
At the moment, many claims are asserted but not supported. In academic argumentation, you need reasons plus evidence, not only opinion.

Examples of claims that need support:
“she is the only one who knows what consequences will be taken on”
“there would be higher death rates from self-induced abortions”
“If a child is born unwanted, he is automatically psychologically damaged for life”

Be careful with words like “automatically” because they sound too absolute and may be factually questionable. Instead, use more cautious academic language:
“may face”
“can lead to”
“is associated with”

If possible, include sources, statistics, or studies.

Problems in logic and wording
Some parts are hard to understand because of incomplete or incorrect sentences. For example:

“The pro-choicers, as well as proffers, have argued various points...”
  - “proffers” is probably the wrong word. You likely mean “pro-lifers.”

“Although if you analyze many of these arguments they are not very substantial.”
  - This is a sentence fragment. Better:
  - “However, many of these arguments are not very substantial when analyzed closely.”

“For example some anti-abortionist, think that even woman women who have abortions.”
  - This sentence is incomplete and needs to be rewritten completely. Perhaps you mean:
  - “For example, some anti-abortion activists believe that even women who become pregnant through rape should not be allowed to have abortions.”

“The anti-abortionist's neglect of these topics shows there tendencies...”
  - Use “anti-abortionists’” and “their,” not “there.”

A. Articles, plurals, and word forms
As a German speaker, watch for singular/plural and article use:
“this categories” → “these categories”
“some anti-abortionist” → “some anti-abortionists”
“woman women” → “women”
“its hard” → “it’s hard”

B. Spacing and punctuation
There are many punctuation problems:
no space after commas or periods
unnecessary commas
comma splices

Examples:
“pro-choiceor” → “pro-choice or”
“true,there” → “true, there”
“done nothing, but enlarge” → “done nothing but grow”

C. Word choice
Some expressions sound unnatural in English:
“enlarge throughout the years” → “grown over the years”
“obtains enough knowledge” → “has enough knowledge”
“make their points” → “support their views”

Suggested revision plan

Correct grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: television exposes children to sexual content, violence, and profanity too early, and adults and media companies should do more to protect them. This gives your paper a strong argumentative purpose. You also use examples and personal experience, which help show why the issue matters to you. For revision, focus on three main areas: organization, evidence, and language accuracy/style.

Main strengths
Clear claim: You consistently argue that children are negatively affected by adult television content.
Strong sense of concern: Your voice is passionate and engaged, which is useful in argumentative writing.
Use of examples: The examples about talk shows, cartoons, and your daughter’s reactions make the topic concrete.

Main revision priorities

A. Strengthen the thesis
At the moment, your essay begins with a broad situation, but your exact argument could be sharper. Try stating your thesis earlier and more precisely.

For example:
> Television should provide more child-appropriate programming after school because adult content can confuse young children, encourage imitation, and expose them to ideas they are not ready to understand.

This helps the reader see both your position and your reasons.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has many ideas, but they sometimes repeat or appear in a loose order. A clearer structure would make the argument more persuasive.

Possible structure:
Introduction: present the problem and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: children often watch TV unsupervised and have access to adult programs.
Body paragraph 2: children may imitate behavior they see.
Body paragraph 3: children misunderstand sexual content because they lack maturity.
Body paragraph 4: parents and TV networks share responsibility.
Conclusion: restate argument and propose a solution.

Try to give each paragraph one main idea and begin with a topic sentence.

C. Use stronger evidence
Some claims are too general or unsupported, for example:
“Approximately ninety eight percent of television programs are not for children.”
“That only leaves a small two percent...”
“about ninety percent of the rated ‘R’ movies... you can watch on your television”

These statistics seem doubtful unless you cite a source. In academic argument, unsupported numbers weaken credibility. If you do not have evidence, either:
remove the statistics, or
replace them with more careful wording, such as:
> Many television programs shown after school are aimed at adults rather than children.

Also, some cause-and-effect claims are too direct, for example the suggestion that seeing TV content will make a child think early pregnancy is acceptable. This may be part of your argument, but it needs more careful phrasing:
> Such programs may normalize behavior that young children do not yet understand.

Style and tone
Your essay sometimes uses informal or emotional language, such as “Big people shows” and “that’s about two programs a day.” In an academic essay, use a more formal tone.

Examples:
“dad” / “mom” → parents
“Big people shows” → adult television programs
“it’s all right” → it is acceptable
“gotten bad” → become more explicit

Also avoid overgeneralizations like “every child” or “the majority.” Use cautious language:
many children
some children
may
can

This makes your argument sound more thoughtful and credible.

Grammar and language issues
Because you are a German speaker, some sentence structures may be transferring directly from German or from spoken English. Focus especially on:

A. Word choice / fixed expressions
“make the children to want” → make children want
“for an example” → for example
“suppose to explain” → supposed to explain
“some where” → somewhere
“in the bed” → in bed

B. Articles and plurals
“a women” → a woman
“man and women kiss” → man and woman kiss
“televisions stations” → television stations

C. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are very long and include several ideas. Break them into shorter sentences.

Original:
> With these television programs on every day the child starts to watch his or her favorite show and once interested then wants to experiment.

Revision:
> When children watch these programs every day, they may become attached to them. Once they are interested, they may want to imitate what they see.

D. Punctuation and formatting
There are many small punctuation problems:
missing commas
extra commas
quotation marks typed incorrectly
spacing problems

Example:
> “The Simpsons”, because...
should be
> *The Simpsons* because...

Content note
Be careful with claims about homosexuality. In your current wording, it may sound as if homosexuality itself is harmful. If your real point is that very young children may struggle to understand complex sexual topics of any kind, say that more clearly and neutrally.

Final advice
When revising, focus on:
writing a precise thesis,
organizing paragraphs around one idea each,
replacing unsupported statistics with reliable evidence or cautious wording,
using a more formal academic tone,
correcting grammar and sentence clarity.

You have a strong topic and clear concern for children’s well-being. With better structure and more careful evidence, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you want to persuade readers that recycling is necessary and practical. You also include many specific examples (plastic, glass, batteries, oil, printer cartridges), which gives the essay substance. This is a good foundation. For revision, focus especially on argument, organization, source use, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Strong commitment to the topic: your position is clear from the beginning.
Wide range of examples: you show that recycling applies to many materials, not only paper and cans.
Use of evidence: quotations and facts help support your claims.
Persuasive ending: the conclusion returns to the main idea and calls for action.

Main revision priorities

A. Clarify your thesis
At the moment, the introduction is broad and slightly unclear. Your main argument seems to be: recycling should become a normal part of daily life because it reduces landfill waste, saves resources, and protects human health and the environment.

Try to express that in one precise sentence at the end of the introduction.

Possible revision:
> Recycling should become a routine practice because it conserves natural resources, reduces pollution, and prevents dangerous waste from harming people and the environment.

This gives the essay a stronger focus.

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay often reads like a list of recyclable items. To make it more argumentative, group paragraphs by larger reasons, not only by material.

For example:
Recycling conserves natural resources  
   (paper, plastic, glass, steel)
Recycling reduces pollution and saves energy  
   (glass bottle energy, plastic vs. paper discussion, motor oil)
Recycling protects public health  
   (batteries, refrigerators, household toxics)
Recycling is practical and should be part of daily life  
   (printer cartridges, take-back programs, conclusion)

This structure would make the essay feel less like an informational report and more like an argument.

C. Develop analysis, not just facts
You often present a fact and then move immediately to the next example. Add 1–2 sentences of explanation after important evidence. Explain why the fact matters and how it supports your thesis.

Example:
> “One quart of motor oil can kill fish…”  
Then add:  
> This shows that careless disposal affects not only landfill space but also water systems and wildlife. Recycling hazardous materials is therefore essential for environmental protection.

D. Use sources more carefully
You rely heavily on quoted material. Too many quotations can weaken your own voice. Try to paraphrase more and quote only especially striking phrases or statistics.

Also, your citation style is inconsistent:
quotation marks are sometimes missing or incorrect
source names are abbreviated differently (“Recycler’s,” “Recycler’s Bill of Rights”)
page numbers are unclear

Ask yourself:
Who is the author or organization?
Is the source reliable and current?
Can I cite it consistently?

If your teacher expects MLA or another format, revise all in-text citations accordingly.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, which suits an argumentative essay. However, some sentences are too emotional or informal for academic writing.

For example:
“please make the effort”
“just tossing out the trash”
“the few minutes of lost time”

Try to sound firm but academic:
> Consumers should make a consistent effort to recycle paper when its use cannot be avoided.

Also, avoid overstatements unless you can prove them:
“Plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags” is too absolute.  
Better:
> In some cases, plastic bags may require fewer natural resources to produce than virgin paper bags.

This is more precise and credible.

Language issues to correct
Because you are a German-speaking writer, some issues may come from direct translation or sentence structure. Focus on these:

A. Grammar
Subject-verb agreement:  
  “the human population needs to educate themselves” →  
  “people need to educate themselves” or “the population needs to educate itself”
Article use:  
  “in the landfills” / “in landfills” — choose depending on meaning
Word form:  
  “categorized in” → “categorized as”
Prepositions:  
  “disposed of properly” is correct; keep checking fixed verb phrases

B. Punctuation
You often have errors with:
semicolons instead of commas  
  “includes but is not limited to;” → no semicolon
spacing and quotation marks
sentence fragments

C. Spelling/typing errors
Examples:
“hangars” → hangers
“fight bulb” → light bulb
“m the normal trash” → in the normal trash
“fun” → full
“win suffer” → will suffer
strange symbol in “earth�s” → encoding problem; fix to earth’s

D. Awkward phrasing
Some sentences sound unnatural in English:
“the world to be one that our children and grandchildren can benefit from”
“give the [earth] countless years to keep turning”

Use simpler, more direct phrasing:
> Recycling helps preserve the planet for future generations.

Final advice for revision
When you rewrite, aim for:
A clear thesis
Body paragraphs organized by reasons
More explanation after evidence
Fewer quotations, more paraphrasing
Careful proofreading for grammar and spelling

You already have many useful ideas and examples. The next step is to shape them into a more focused academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a strong central claim: society associates pink with femininity and blue with masculinity, especially in advertising and baby products. You also use several concrete examples, which is a good strength in an argumentative essay. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

What is working well

Clear focus
   - The essay stays on one topic throughout.
   - Your reader always knows what argument you are making.

Relevant examples
   - You use examples from commercials, diapers, deodorant, and baby clothes.
   - These examples help support your point about gender stereotypes.

Logical general structure
   - You move from general ideas about pink, to commercials, to babies, and then to boys/blue.
   - This shows that you are trying to organize your ideas by topic.

Main areas to improve

Make your thesis clearer

Right now, your argument is understandable, but the main thesis should be stated more directly in the introduction.

For example, instead of beginning only with questions, you could end the first paragraph with a sentence like:

“In modern society, advertising and consumer products reinforce the stereotype that pink belongs to girls and women, while blue belongs to boys and men.”

This gives the essay a clear academic argument.

Improve paragraph structure

Some paragraphs are very short, and some ideas are repeated. Try to give each paragraph one main point.

A possible structure:

Introduction: introduce the stereotype and state your thesis
Body paragraph 1: pink in advertising for women
Body paragraph 2: pink and girls/babies
Body paragraph 3: blue and boys/babies
Conclusion: explain what these stereotypes mean socially

This will make your essay feel more organized and less repetitive.

Use a more formal academic tone

Some phrases sound too conversational or too absolute.

Examples:
“you would not catch a baby boy with pink on” → too informal
“always show this stereotype” → too absolute
“automatically assume” → possible, but should be softened

Try using more careful language:
“many advertisements reinforce this stereotype”
“baby boys are often dressed in blue”
“people may assume the baby is a boy”

Academic writing is usually stronger when it avoids overgeneralization.

Develop your analysis more

Right now, the essay gives many examples, but sometimes it does not explain enough why they matter.

For instance, after mentioning pink razors or pink diapers, explain:
what message the product sends
how companies use color to target consumers
how these color codes teach children and adults about gender roles

This will make your essay more analytical, not just descriptive.

Grammar and sentence issues

There are several grammar, capitalization, and word-choice problems. These are common and can be fixed in revision.

Capitalization
Capitalize the first word of every sentence:
“On the Secret deodorant commercial...”
“Once a baby has developed...”
“Of course...”

Sentence fragments / punctuation
Example:
“Why? because it is a very soft color...”
Better:
“Why is this the case? Because pink is often seen as a soft color, and women are stereotypically portrayed as soft and tender.”

Word choice
“pictured as being soft and tender” → better: “portrayed as soft and tender”
“get away with it” → better: “are more socially accepted”
“its looks” for a baby girl is awkward → better: “Once a baby’s sex is visually recognizable...”

Agreement / article use
“commercials for feminine products... also have” is okay, but some sentences need smoother grammar.
“Females can wear masculine colors” → more natural: “Women and girls can wear traditionally masculine colors...”

Spelling/typing
“jam referring to babies” is likely a typo.
  - Probably: “I am referring to babies.”

Avoid repetition

You repeat the same point many times:
pink = female
blue = male
soft colors = feminine

Instead of repeating, combine ideas and add deeper explanation. For example, rather than saying several times that baby girls wear pink, mention it once clearly and then explain how this influences public perception.

Strengthen the conclusion

Your ending currently introduces a new idea about what is socially accepted for older males and females, but it feels unfinished. A conclusion should summarize the argument and show why it matters.

For example:

“Overall, the repeated use of pink for girls and blue for boys in advertising, clothing, and baby products helps maintain traditional gender stereotypes. Although these associations may seem natural, they are socially constructed and reinforced through everyday consumer culture.”

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize the essay into clear body paragraphs.
Reduce repetition.
Explain your examples more deeply.
Correct grammar, capitalization, and formal style.

You have a solid topic and good supporting examples. With stronger organization and more formal analysis, this could become a much more effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position from the beginning: you argue that drug legalization would benefit the United States. This is a good start for an argumentative essay because the reader immediately understands your claim. You also mention several supporting points—reduced crime, lower prison populations, tax revenue, and public health—which gives your essay a clear overall direction.

What is working well

Clear thesis and strong opinion  
   Your introduction makes your position obvious. An argumentative essay needs a debatable claim, and you provide one.

Several relevant arguments  
   You do not rely on only one reason. Instead, you discuss economic, social, legal, and health-related effects. This gives your essay breadth.

Attempt to use sources  
   The quotation from Duke shows that you are trying to support your ideas with outside evidence. That is important in academic writing.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen structure and paragraph development

At the moment, many body paragraphs begin with a good topic sentence, but the ideas inside are underdeveloped or incomplete. Some sentences are marked with , which suggests missing evidence or explanation. Each paragraph should follow this pattern:

Topic sentence: state one benefit clearly
Explanation: explain why this benefit would happen
Evidence: facts, statistics, or source-based support
Analysis: explain how the evidence supports your claim
Link: connect back to the thesis

For example, your paragraph about crime says legalization would decrease crime, but you need to explain how and provide evidence. Would crime decrease because black markets disappear? Because users would not need to steal to buy expensive drugs? Be precise.

Improve logic and avoid unsupported claims

Some claims are too broad or not clearly accurate. For instance:

“Americans as a whole have shown…”
“Legalizing heroin, cocaine, and marijuana would produce a reduction in the use of tobacco and alcohol…”

These are very large claims and need strong evidence. In academic writing, avoid overgeneralizations unless you can prove them. Use more careful language such as:

“Many Americans believe…”
“Some researchers argue that…”
“Legalization may reduce…”

This makes your argument sound more credible.

Use evidence more effectively

Your essay needs more source support. Right now, most paragraphs contain opinions but not enough evidence. Add:

statistics
expert opinions
research studies
historical or policy examples

Also, when you use a source, explain it. Do not just insert a quotation—show the reader why it matters.

Work on clarity and sentence accuracy

There are frequent grammar and wording problems that sometimes make your meaning unclear. As a German-speaking writer, you may want to pay special attention to word choice, article use, and sentence flow.

Here are some examples:

“They have come to reality” → They have come to realize
“beneficiary to America” → beneficial to America
“imprisonmemt” → imprisonment
“it' s” → it’s
“the society of America” → American society
“drug users would be responsible for their own debts” is unclear. Do you mean the government would no longer pay enforcement costs?

Some sentences are also awkward or incomplete, for example:
“Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers.”
This sentence is not grammatical and must be rewritten completely.

Use a more academic tone

Some phrases sound too emotional or informal for an academic essay, for example:

“it’s time we stop wasting money”
“face the truth”
“big money”
“hooked up in the drug business”

Try more formal alternatives:

“the government should reconsider current spending priorities”
“the evidence suggests”
“high profits”
“become involved in the drug trade”

Address counterarguments

A strong argumentative essay usually considers the other side. Your essay would improve if you briefly acknowledged an opposing argument, such as:

Legalization might increase drug use
Some people fear greater health risks

Then respond to that point with evidence. This will make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Suggested revision priorities

Complete all unfinished parts marked with .
Create a clear paragraph structure: one main idea per paragraph.
Add evidence from reliable sources to each body paragraph.
Rewrite unclear or ungrammatical sentences.
Use more cautious, academic language.
Add one counterargument and rebuttal.

Example of a stronger sentence

Original:  
“Another benefit of legalizing drugs would be the decrease in crime.”

Improved:  
“Another major argument for drug legalization is that it could reduce crime by weakening black markets and lowering the inflated prices that often drive drug-related theft and violence.”

This version is more specific and analytical.

Final comment

Your essay has a strong central opinion and several promising ideas, but it needs deeper development, clearer evidence, and more careful language to become convincing as an academic argument. Focus especially on clarity, paragraph development, and support for your claims. The foundation is there; now your task is to make the argument more precise, structured, and credible.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have chosen an important topic and written with real conviction. Your essay’s strongest feature is its personal engagement: the examples from South Carolina and from your own family make the issue feel urgent and human. You also clearly state your position early, which is good for an argumentative essay.

To improve the essay, focus on organization, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Main argument and focus
Your thesis is clear, but it can be made more precise. At the moment, the essay argues that gun control is needed because guns are misused and people die. That is understandable, but the claim would be stronger if you explain what kind of gun control you support.

For example, instead of saying:
“Gun Control needs to implemented because it would mainly reduce crime, save lives and lessen the fears of innocent ones.”

you could write:
“South Carolina should adopt stricter gun control measures, such as background checks, waiting periods, and limits on access for minors, because these policies could reduce violent crime and save lives.”

This makes your argument more specific and easier to support.

Organization and paragraph structure
The essay has many examples, but sometimes it reads more like a list of incidents than a structured argument. Try this pattern:

Introduction: define gun control, give background, state thesis.  
Body paragraph 1: youth access to guns.  
Body paragraph 2: guns and robbery/public safety.  
Body paragraph 3: personal/community impact.  
Body paragraph 4: response to opposing views.  
Conclusion: restate argument and propose solutions.

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects clearly to the thesis. For example:
“One reason stricter gun laws are necessary is that minors can gain access to firearms too easily.”
“A second reason for stronger regulation is that guns make robbery and public violence more deadly.”

Use of evidence
You include newspaper quotations, personal recollections, and an interview. This shows effort, but your evidence needs more explanation and sometimes more reliability.

What works:
Local examples make the issue concrete.
Personal examples create emotional impact.

What to improve:
After each quotation, explain how it supports your argument.
Be careful not to assume that one event automatically proves that gun control would have prevented it.
Some sources are vague or incomplete, especially “Multimedia Resources-CD-Rom” and “Personal Recollection.” In academic writing, readers need clear, credible sources.

Also, one statistical claim seems questionable:
“robbery being approximately 2.5% … and murder being approximately 16.3%”
These numbers may be inaccurate or misunderstood. Murder is usually much less common than robbery. Recheck the source carefully.

Addressing the opposing side
You mention that some groups oppose gun control, especially the NRA, but the treatment is too brief and dismissive:
“This opposing organization doesn't realize that gun-related crimes are steadily rising.”

A stronger argument would briefly explain why some people oppose gun control (for example, self-defense or constitutional rights), and then answer that argument respectfully. This will make your essay sound more balanced and persuasive.

Tone and style
Your tone is passionate, which suits the topic, but some sentences are too emotional or too general for academic argument. Examples:
“people killing others over nonsense”
“commit insane acts”
“for no reason”

Try to use more formal wording:
“over minor conflicts”
“commit violent acts”
“without justifiable cause”

Also avoid capitalizing “Gun Control” in the middle of sentences unless it begins a sentence.

Grammar and language
As a German speaker, you may want to pay special attention to word choice, article use, and sentence structure.

Common corrections from your essay:
“goverment” → government
“needs to implemented” → needs to be implemented
“techers” → teachers
“memebers” → members
“misue” → misuse
“acquaintace” → acquaintance
“fill it with it money” → fill it with money

Sentence-level issues:
Run-on sentence:
  - “There are some segments of society that are not taking gun control seriously, in light of these obstacles…”
  - Better: “Some segments of society do not take gun control seriously. In light of these obstacles, stricter laws are still necessary.”

Awkward phrasing:
  - “From the use of guns, people are dying at an alarming rate, daily.”
  - Better: “Gun violence causes deaths at an alarming rate every day.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion introduces a new strong claim—only police and military should carry guns. Because this idea appears only at the end, it feels sudden. Either develop this position earlier in the essay or keep the conclusion closer to the thesis by summarizing the need for stricter laws.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis to make it more specific.  
Organize the body paragraphs by clear reasons.  
Use fewer examples, but explain them more fully.  
Add a fair counterargument and response.  
Correct grammar, spelling, and source details.

You already have strong material and genuine commitment to the topic. With clearer structure and more careful language, this could become a much more persuasive academic argument.
