Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position and strong conviction: you argue consistently against capital punishment and organize the body around three main counterarguments—cost, retribution, and deterrence. This gives the essay a solid basic structure. For revision, focus especially on tone, accuracy, grammar, and evidence. Right now, the main weakness is not your ideas, but how they are presented.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your introduction makes your stance unmistakable.
Logical organization: Each body paragraph addresses one argument used by supporters of capital punishment.
Relevant argumentative strategy: Refuting opposing points is effective in an argumentative essay.
Strong engagement: The reader can see that you care about the topic.

Main areas to improve

A. Use a more academic tone
At the moment, the essay sounds too emotional and conversational for academic writing. Phrases such as:

“these reasons are as ridiculous as the source”
“Let me throw a couple of statistics at you”
“simply pull their plug”
“Correct me if I’m wrong”

make the essay sound informal or aggressive. In academic argumentation, your credibility increases when your language is calm, precise, and respectful.

Try instead:
“These arguments appear persuasive at first, but closer examination reveals important weaknesses.”
“The following statistics suggest…”
“execute the prisoner” or “carry out the death penalty”

B. Support claims with credible evidence
You mention “statistics” and “studies,” but you do not identify the source. In academic writing, readers need to know where the evidence comes from.

For example:
“it costs five times the amount” → according to whom?
“They have done studies in states…” → which studies? which states?

Without sources, your points sound unsupported. In revision, add:
names of organizations, researchers, or reports
dates if possible
more precise wording

For example:
“A 2011 report by the Death Penalty Information Center found that death penalty cases often cost more than life imprisonment because of lengthy trials and appeals.”

C. Improve logic and precision
Some claims are too broad or overstated. For instance:
“justice and capital punishment can't coexist” is a very absolute statement.
“murders don’t consider the form of punishment” may be true in some cases, but not all.

Academic argument is usually stronger when it is careful and precise, not absolute.

Better approach:
“These findings suggest that capital punishment does not reliably promote justice.”
“In many cases, potential offenders do not appear to weigh the legal consequences before acting.”

Language and grammar issues

There are frequent spelling, word choice, and grammar problems. Many seem to come from writing quickly or from sound-based spelling. Examples:

untill → *until*
it’s existence → *its existence*  
  (*it’s* = *it is*; *its* = possessive)
Ounce society → *Once society*
inprison → *imprison*
recieves → *receives*
midevil → *medieval*
deterent → *deterrent*
murdeers / murdeer → *murders / murder*
deference → *deterrence*
detramental → *detrimental*

Also check sentence structure. Some sentences are too long and unclear. For example:

> “How this statement can be valid is due to the reasons that…”

This is awkward. A clearer version:
> “This is largely because death penalty cases involve long appeals and high court costs.”

As a German speaker, also pay attention to apostrophes and word choice. English uses fewer commas and fewer capital letters than German, but apostrophe errors are common.

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction
Your introduction clearly states your position, but it should be more formal and concise. Avoid attacking supporters. Present the issue neutrally, then state your thesis.

Body paragraph 1: Cost
This is potentially your strongest paragraph because it uses practical reasoning. Strengthen it with specific, cited evidence and more precise phrasing.

Body paragraph 2: Retribution
The main idea is interesting: punishment should not become revenge. However, this paragraph becomes emotional and rhetorical. Reduce the number of rhetorical questions and explain your reasoning more directly.

Body paragraph 3: Deterrence
Good topic choice, but again you need sources. Also, “they have done studies” is too vague. Name the research.

Conclusion
The conclusion summarizes your points well, but avoid saying things are “proven” unless your evidence is very strong. “Suggests” or “indicates” is safer in academic writing.

Suggested revision priorities

Correct grammar and spelling
Replace informal/emotional expressions with academic language
Add real sources for all statistics and studies
Reduce rhetorical questions
Make claims more precise and balanced

Final comment

You have a strong foundation: clear position, clear structure, and relevant main points. Your next step is to make the essay sound more scholarly. If you revise for formal tone, evidence, and language accuracy, your argument will become much more convincing.

If you want, I can also help you by:
rewriting one body paragraph in a more academic style, or  
correcting the whole essay sentence by sentence.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0012.2.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay addresses an important ethical issue and shows clear engagement with different perspectives on euthanasia. You include definitions, types of euthanasia, social opposition, and the case of Dr. Kevorkian. This gives your essay a strong base of material. For revision, the main areas to improve are: clarity of argument, organization, use of sources, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Relevant topic and clear interest: You focus on a controversial issue and try to present both background and argument.
Use of examples: The discussion of Dr. Kevorkian helps make the issue concrete.
Awareness of opposing views: You mention religious, legal, technological, and medical objections, which is good for an argumentative essay.

Main problem: unclear thesis and position

Your essay raises the question of whether physician-assisted suicide should be legalized, but your own position is not fully clear until the final paragraph. Even there, the conclusion seems somewhat contradictory:

> “it is not the right of a doctor to decide to initiate or suggest euthanasia as an option, but it is one's option as a physician to cooperate with a patient's decision.”

This sounds as if you partly support physician cooperation, but earlier you emphasize objections. Try to make your thesis direct and specific in the introduction.

For example, you could write something like:

Against legalization: “Although terminally ill patients deserve compassion and pain relief, physicians should not be allowed to assist in suicide because it conflicts with medical ethics and gives doctors too much power over life and death.”
In favor with limits: “Physician-assisted suicide should be legally available for terminally ill patients under strict safeguards, because patients should have the right to avoid unbearable suffering.”

Then make sure each body paragraph supports that thesis.

Organization and structure

At the moment, the essay reads partly as background information and partly as argument. To improve coherence, use a clearer structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the issue
   - Define euthanasia/physician-assisted suicide carefully
   - State your thesis

Body paragraph 1: Definitions and distinctions
   - Different forms of euthanasia
   - Clarify active/passive euthanasia, refusal of treatment, assisted suicide

Body paragraph 2: Arguments against
   - Religious, legal, and medical objections
   - Hippocratic Oath

Body paragraph 3: Arguments for
   - Patient autonomy
   - Relief of suffering
   - Kevorkian case or legal examples

Conclusion
   - Restate your position clearly
   - Explain what doctors should or should not be allowed to do

Also, some paragraphs are very long. Break them into shorter paragraphs with one main idea each.

Use of evidence and sources

You refer to William F. May and Joseph Fletcher, which is good, but the use of sources needs revision.

Several quotations are incomplete and marked with . These must be filled in or removed.
When mentioning a source, explain how it supports your argument.
Be careful not to rely too much on summary. In an argumentative essay, evidence should be connected to your own claim.

For example, after presenting May’s “four forces,” explain whether you agree with them and why they matter for your thesis.

Language and style

Your meaning is generally understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word-choice problems. Below are some important examples:

Grammar/correct forms
“Those patient's” → “Those patients”
“would rather die than to continue” → “would rather die than continue”
“Many people leave in their will expressed wishes” → “Many people express in their wills their wish”
“Christains” → “Christians”
“modern societies outlook” → “modern society’s outlook”
“break throughs” → “breakthroughs”
“one's ending of one's life” → awkward; better: “a person ending his or her own life”
“helping patients end their own life” → “helping patients end their own lives”

Word choice
Some expressions sound too informal or imprecise for academic writing:
“easy way out” is too colloquial and judgmental.
  - Better: “an escape from suffering” or “a way to end unbearable pain.”
“vegetable” is inappropriate in academic writing, even in quotation marks.
  - Better: “persistent vegetative state” or “permanently unconscious state.”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Try to shorten them and avoid repeating “one” too often. For example:

Original:
> “It is not the right of a doctor to decide to initiate or suggest euthanasia as an option, but it is one's option as a physician to cooperate with a patient's decision.”

Clearer:
> “Doctors should not initiate or recommend euthanasia. However, if the law permits it, they may choose whether to cooperate with a competent patient’s decision.”

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize the essay so that each paragraph has one purpose.
Complete or remove missing quotations.
Define key terms carefully and consistently.
Edit grammar, especially possessives, plural forms, articles, and sentence structure.
Use a more formal academic tone.

You have strong material here, but the essay will become much more effective if your argument is clearer and your evidence is more carefully organized.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: the wild card is good for baseball. You also give several reasons and use concrete examples from the 1995 season. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. The main areas to revise are focus, organization, sentence clarity, grammar, and source integration.

Strengths

Clear thesis: In the first paragraph, you state that the wild card is good for baseball because it adds teams, creates another race, and provides more competition.
Good use of evidence: You include specific teams, players, and playoff scenarios. This makes the essay feel informed and persuasive.
Consistent argument: Most paragraphs support your central claim.

Main revision priority: improve focus

A major problem is that the essay spends too much time explaining the playoff system before arguing why the wild card is beneficial. This creates a weak middle section and delays the argument.

For example, the long explanation of the Division Series and home-field advantage is interesting, but much of it does not directly prove that the wild card is good for baseball. Ask yourself: Does this paragraph support my thesis, or is it only background?

Suggestion
Condense the background to 1 short paragraph. Then organize the body around your three main arguments:

More teams and star players in the playoffs
More excitement and fan interest at the end of the season
Stronger competition in the postseason

This structure will make the essay more persuasive.

Organization and paragraphing

Some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas. Try to give each paragraph one main point.

A possible outline:

Introduction: background + thesis
Body 1: more teams/players/cities involved
Body 2: more excitement in the pennant race / wild card race
Body 3: more competition in the playoffs
Conclusion: restate argument and significance for baseball

Also, transitions can be smoother. Instead of jumping from rules to opinion, use topic sentences such as:

“First, the wild card improves baseball by allowing more talented teams and players to reach the postseason.”
“Second, the wild card keeps more fans interested until the end of the regular season.”
“Finally, the wild card increases competition by making the road to the World Series more difficult.”

Use of evidence

You have many examples, but sometimes you list too many names or details without explaining their importance. For instance, the long list of star players is impressive, but it would be stronger if you selected fewer examples and connected them more clearly to fan interest and television appeal.

The detailed playoff scenarios for the final day are also probably too long for this essay. Summarize them instead. Your reader does not need every possibility; they only need enough evidence to see that the wild card created excitement.

Style and tone

Your tone is often energetic, which is good, but at times it becomes too informal or biased for academic writing. For example:

“Baseball, unfortunately, feels the need to be different...”
“when all is said and done...”

Try to sound more analytical and less conversational. Academic argument is strongest when the writer sounds controlled and precise.

Grammar and sentence-level issues

There are several grammar and wording problems. Many seem influenced by German sentence structure or by trying to write very long sentences. In English, shorter sentences are often clearer.

Examples

“...the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners.”  
  → Missing word: “besides” or “among non-division winners.”

“who plays who”  
  → In formal writing: “which teams play each other” or “who plays whom.”

“The answer is not that simple.”  
  → Fine grammatically, but a more formal version would be: “The answer is more complicated.”

“The Most Valuable Player from each league, Barry Larkin from the National League Cincinnati Reds and Mo Vaughn of the American League Boston Red Sox...”  
  → This is hard to read. Use clearer apposition:  
  “The Most Valuable Players from each league—Barry Larkin of the Cincinnati Reds and Mo Vaughn of the Boston Red Sox—would not have appeared in the postseason.”

“as made evident”  
  → Better: “as is evident” or “as shown by”

“The wild card teams were clearly very competitive in the playoffs which was good for baseball...”  
  → Add comma or divide sentence:  
  “The wild card teams were clearly competitive in the playoffs, which was good for baseball...”

Also check:
article use (the/a)
plural/singular consistency
commas after introductory phrases
spelling: Leagued Championship Series and Galaragga should be corrected

Source integration and citation

You include quotations and source references, but citation is inconsistent. Make sure all sources are introduced clearly and formatted in one style. Also, avoid dropping in long block-like material unless necessary. Summarize when possible.

Final advice

For revision, focus on these steps:

Cut unnecessary playoff-rule explanations
Reorganize around your three main reasons
Shorten long lists and summarize evidence
Use a more formal academic tone
Edit carefully for grammar and sentence clarity

You already have strong material and a clear opinion. If you tighten the structure and make the language more precise, this essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and strong personal voice. You are clearly engaged with the topic, and that energy gives the paper conviction. You also raise an important question: when does the public’s right to know conflict with privacy, fairness, and responsibility? That is a strong basis for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear stance
   - Your position is never in doubt: you believe the media often oversteps ethical boundaries and sacrifices quality for profit.

Specific examples
   - You refer to Grenada, Desert Storm, Bosnia, the Surgeon General nomination, the O.J. Simpson trial, and Colin Powell. These examples give the essay substance.

Effective use of rhetorical questions
   - Questions such as “When does the media’s ‘right to know’ start to tread on a person’s right to privacy?” help focus the central issue.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen your thesis
At the moment, your main argument appears gradually, but it should be stated clearly in the introduction. Right now, the opening focuses on your experience with *The State*, which is interesting, but it delays the main claim.

A stronger thesis could be something like:

> Although the media plays an essential role in informing the public, today’s news organizations too often invade privacy, sensationalize events, and prioritize profit over responsible journalism.

This gives your reader a roadmap.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads as a series of examples connected by frustration, but the argument would be stronger if grouped into clear categories.

Possible structure:
Introduction: personal anecdote + thesis
Body paragraph 1: media and military/security risks
Body paragraph 2: media influence on political/public figures
Body paragraph 3: sensationalism in trials and entertainment-style news
Body paragraph 4: privacy and ethics
Conclusion: restate argument and call for responsible journalism

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that states the point of that paragraph.

Use more precise and formal academic language
Your voice is strong, but some phrases are too conversational for an academic essay:
“How dare they…”
“Watch out, future warriors…”
“Let it be.”
“get off this merry-go-round”

These create emotion, but in academic argumentation, it is usually better to sound controlled and analytical. Try replacing emotional language with more precise claims.

For example:
Instead of “How dare they endanger lives…”
  - write: “Such reporting may endanger military personnel by revealing sensitive information.”

Avoid overgeneralization
Some claims are too broad:
“we don’t see any [good news] anymore”
“the media’s freedom of speech will be the downfall of personal liberty”
“most military leaders have been [terrific presidents]”

These statements sound exaggerated and may weaken your credibility. Try qualifying them:
“much contemporary coverage seems to prioritize sensational stories over constructive reporting.”
“in extreme cases, irresponsible reporting can threaten individual privacy and public trust.”

Add balance and acknowledge counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should show that you understand the other side. For example, you could briefly admit:
the media can expose corruption,
the public sometimes does need information about public officials,
freedom of the press is essential in a democracy.

Then explain that your argument is not against journalism itself, but against irresponsible or sensational journalism. This will make your essay more nuanced and persuasive.

Language and grammar issues

There are quite a few mechanical errors, probably from typing or proofreading problems. Examples include:
successfiil → *successful*
fives → *lives*
inunune → *immune*
hwnburgers → *hamburgers*
ofv → *off*
medi&s → *media’s*
gwne → *game*
dodt → *don’t*

Also watch for punctuation and quotation marks:
Keep quotation marks consistent: “right to know”
Avoid comma splices and run-on sentences.
Example:
  - “It seems to me, this was the job…”
  - Better: “It seems to me that this was the job…”

Article/preposition style
As a German speaker, you may want to pay attention to article use and idiomatic phrasing:
“on this reason” → “for this reason”
“take a subscription” → “subscribe” / “take out a subscription”
“come under the people’s right to know” → “be justified under the public’s right to know”

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize the essay into logical body paragraphs with topic sentences.
Replace emotional or conversational wording with formal analytical language.
Reduce exaggeration and add qualifications.
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and word choice.

Overall
This essay has a strong core argument, vivid examples, and genuine conviction. Its main weakness is not lack of ideas, but lack of structure and polish. With clearer organization, more formal tone, and careful editing, this could become a persuasive and effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you believe marijuana should be legalized. That strong opinion gives the paper energy, and your personal voice is very noticeable. You also try to engage the reader with questions and examples, which is useful in argumentative writing. For revision, the main goal is to make the essay sound more academic, more organized, and better supported with evidence.

Main strengths
Clear thesis/topic: From the beginning, the reader understands your general opinion.
Strong personal voice: The essay feels sincere and engaged.
Relevant topic: Legalization is a good subject for an argument essay because there are multiple sides and much evidence available.

Main areas to improve

A. Use a more academic tone
At the moment, the essay sounds very conversational and personal. Phrases like “weed,” “mega bucks,” “talking out of his head,” or “little man” fit informal speech, but not formal academic writing.

Try to replace informal language with more neutral vocabulary:
weed → marijuana or cannabis
cops → police
mega bucks → large sums of money
hustlers → drug dealers or public figures, depending on meaning

Also avoid direct accusations unless you can prove them. For example, claims that government officials keep marijuana illegal for personal profit are very serious and need strong evidence. Without evidence, they weaken your argument.

B. Organize the essay more clearly
Right now, the essay moves from childhood memories to politics, then to Europe, then to medical use, then to celebrities. The reader needs a clearer structure.

A possible structure:
Introduction
   - Introduce the issue
   - Give your thesis: marijuana should be legalized because of medical benefits, limited harm compared with other substances, and the failure of criminalization
Body paragraph 1: Medical uses
   - Eye diseases, pain relief, other medical benefits
   - Use reliable sources
Body paragraph 2: Social/legal argument
   - Criminalization does not stop use
   - It may create black markets and unnecessary arrests
Body paragraph 3: Comparison with legal substances
   - Compare risks of marijuana with alcohol or tobacco
Counterargument and response
   - Acknowledge concerns: addiction, youth use, impaired driving
   - Explain why regulation is better than prohibition
Conclusion
   - Restate your argument clearly and formally

C. Use stronger evidence
Much of your support is based on personal experience or things you have heard from others. In academic argumentation, this is not enough. For example:
“I have a lot of friends who do…”
“former class mates… aced their tests…”
“a Jamaican man… explained it all to me…”

These examples may be interesting, but they are not reliable evidence. Use:
academic studies
government reports
medical research
statistics from trustworthy organizations

For example, instead of saying marijuana helps eye ailments, cite a medical source that explains when and how it is used.

D. Avoid weak or illogical arguments
Some points are not convincing:
Celebrities or presidents using marijuana does not prove legalization is right.
Students smoking before studying and then doing well on tests is anecdotal, not evidence.
Saying marijuana is not a drug because it is a plant is inaccurate. Many drugs come from plants. A better claim would be that marijuana may be less harmful than some other drugs.

Try to make your reasoning more precise and logical.

Language corrections to focus on

Because you are a German speaker, pay special attention to word choice, punctuation, and sentence boundaries.

Common issues in this essay:
Sentence fragments / run-on sentences
  - Example: “In my mind weed was a drug I had seen it as a young kid…”
  - Better: “In my mind, marijuana was a drug. I had seen it as a young child, but I had never examined it closely.”
Capitalization
  - European, Colombian, Marley
Possessives and contractions
  - it’s = it is
  - its = possessive
  - You wrote: “in it's leafy form” → in its leafy form
There / their
  - “their are” → there are
Spelling
  - criticize, classmates, session
Article use
  - “I had a conversation” (not “I a conversation”)

Thesis improvement
Your opening could be more formal and direct. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Marijuana should be legalized because it has recognized medical uses, its criminalization creates unnecessary social harm, and a regulated system would be more effective than prohibition.*

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

Final advice
When revising, focus on these three priorities:
Make the structure clearer
Replace personal anecdotes with evidence
Use a formal academic style

You have a strong opinion and a clear interest in the topic. If you support your ideas with reliable sources and improve organization, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You clearly take a strong position and show genuine engagement with the topic. Your essay argues consistently that journalists should be able to protect confidential sources, and you support this view with historical background, legal examples, and famous cases. This gives your paper substance. For revision, focus mainly on sharpening the argument, improving organization, and correcting sentence-level issues.

Strengths

Clear overall position: Your opinion is easy to identify from the beginning.
Relevant evidence: You use examples from Maryland, South Carolina, Watergate, and California to show that this is a real legal and ethical issue.
Awareness of complexity: You do mention that shield laws do not always provide full protection, which is important for an argumentative essay.

Main area to improve: stronger thesis and argument

At the moment, the essay sometimes becomes descriptive rather than argumentative. You give many examples, but you do not always explain clearly how each example proves your claim.

What to revise
Create a more precise thesis in the introduction. For example:

> Journalists should be legally protected from revealing confidential sources because source protection is essential to press freedom, encourages whistleblowers to speak, and helps the public receive important information.

This kind of thesis gives you 3 clear argument points, which can guide the whole essay.

Then organize body paragraphs around those points:
Trust between reporter and source
Public interest and freedom of the press
Problems caused when courts force disclosure

After each example, add 2–3 sentences of analysis:
Why does this case matter?
What does it show about shield laws?
How does it support your position?

Organization

Right now, the essay includes many examples, but the structure is loose. Some paragraphs are very long, and transitions are weak.

Suggestions
Write one clear introduction ending with a thesis.
Group related examples together instead of moving from case to case without a clear category.
Use topic sentences at the beginning of each paragraph.

For example:
*Confidentiality is necessary because sources will not speak without trust.*
*Legal pressure on reporters can damage the public’s access to information.*
*Court cases show that weak shield laws create inconsistency and unfairness.*

Your conclusion should not only repeat your opinion. It should summarize the main reasons and end with a strong final statement.

Use of evidence

You include many citations, which is good, but some quotations are too long or dropped into the paper without enough introduction or explanation.

Improve by:
Paraphrasing more often instead of copying long legal language.
Introducing quotations clearly.
Explaining the quotation after using it.

For example, after the Maryland law quotation, explain briefly what it shows: that early lawmakers already recognized the importance of source confidentiality.

Also, some examples feel outdated or only partially explained. If possible, focus on fewer examples but discuss them more deeply.

Style and academic tone

Sometimes the tone becomes too emotional or informal, especially near the end:
“If the justice system were better…”
“Don’t make people scared to talk.”
“The stories will be much more interesting.”

These weaken your academic credibility. Try to sound more formal and precise.

For example:
Instead of *“Don’t make people scared to talk,”* write:
  > Forcing reporters to reveal sources may discourage individuals from sharing information of public importance.

Also be careful with absolute statements such as:
“A journalist’s job is to get the story no matter what.”
This sounds too broad and ignores ethical limits. A more academic version would be:
> Journalists have a professional duty to investigate matters of public interest while maintaining ethical standards.

Language issues to correct

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are several grammar and punctuation problems.

Common issues
Comma errors:  
  “Keeping the sources secret, especially in a courtroom setting has always…”  
  → comma needed after *setting* only if the phrase is parenthetical; better:  
  *Keeping sources secret, especially in a courtroom setting, has always been a problem…*

Word choice / grammar:  
  “the way other states handle cases… is varied greatly”  
  → *varies greatly*

Double punctuation / typing issues:  
  “example,,” / “Swasy,,” / strange quotation marks and symbols

Capitalization:  
  *deep Throat* → *Deep Throat*

Possessives:  
  “reporters right” → *reporter’s right*

Sentence clarity:  
  Some sentences are too long and should be divided into two shorter ones.

Because you are a German speaker, pay special attention to English word order and to writing shorter, clearer sentences. English academic style often prefers less sentence length and less repetition than German.

Priorities for revision

Write a more specific thesis.
Reorganize the essay by main argument points, not just by examples.
Add analysis after each example.
Make the tone more formal and academic.
Proofread carefully for punctuation, grammar, and formatting.

Final comment

This essay has a solid foundation: you care about the topic, and you already have useful material. Your next step is to turn the paper from a collection of examples into a focused academic argument. If you improve structure and analysis, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position: women should be allowed to attend The Citadel. Your essay also includes several relevant arguments, such as equality, public funding, and preparation for real life. These are strong ideas for an argumentative essay. To revise successfully, focus on three main areas: thesis and organization, development of arguments, and language accuracy.

Thesis and overall argument
Your essay shows your opinion clearly, but the main thesis should be stated more directly in the introduction. Right now, the opening is too general and has grammar problems that make it less effective.

For example, instead of:
“Over the past few years there has been one question that appearred in the courts.”

you could write:
“In recent years, courts have debated whether women should be allowed to attend The Citadel. Women should be admitted because excluding them is unfair, unconstitutional for a publicly funded institution, and based on stereotypes rather than ability.”

This kind of thesis tells the reader exactly what your position is and what your main reasons will be.

Organization and paragraph structure
Your body paragraphs contain good points, but some ideas are mixed together. Try to give one main idea per paragraph.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Tradition is not a sufficient reason  
Women are capable / individuals should be judged individually  
Public funding and equal access  
Coeducation does not create harmful distraction  
Conclusion

Also, use clear topic sentences. For example:
“Tradition alone is not a valid reason to exclude women.”
“The argument that women cannot meet The Citadel’s demands unfairly generalizes about ability.”
“Because The Citadel is publicly funded, it should provide equal access to both men and women.”

Development of ideas
You have strong claims, but some need more explanation or evidence.

Strong points:
tradition should not determine policy
not all men are equally capable, and not all women are incapable
public institutions should not discriminate
men and women must work together in society

What to improve:
Some examples are too extreme, such as “you would still have black slaves.” This weakens your tone because it sounds emotional rather than carefully reasoned.
Explain why each point matters. For instance, if you argue that The Citadel is publicly funded, connect that clearly to equal protection or fairness.
The paragraph about “chaos” in the school system is interesting, but the logic is not fully clear. Make sure every sentence directly supports your argument.

For example, you could revise that section to:
“Because The Citadel receives public funding, it should not exclude students on the basis of gender. Public institutions are expected to serve all qualified citizens equally.”

Tone and academic style
Your essay has conviction, which is good, but some phrases are too informal or emotional for academic writing.

Examples:
“traditions are meant to be broken” → too conversational
“and so on” → too vague
“as a women I especially know that is not true” → personal opinion can be used, but in formal argumentation it is usually stronger to focus on evidence and reasoning

A more academic style would sound like:
“Although The Citadel has historically enrolled only men, tradition by itself does not justify exclusion.”

Grammar and language issues
There are many grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors. Because you are a German speaker, pay special attention to subject-verb agreement, articles, plural forms, and word choice.

Common corrections from your essay:
“appearred” → appeared
“That questions is should women” → That question is whether women
“There has been many objections” → There have been many objections
“a women” → a woman
“publically funded” → publicly funded
“desciniinate” → discriminate
“pubfically” → publicly
“poliish” → Polish
“shouln’t” → shouldn’t
“Every deserves” → Everyone deserves
“eqaul” → equal
“there may a few cases” → there may be a few cases
“learening” → learning
“work place” → workplace
“less superior” → this is incorrect because superior already expresses comparison. Better: “inferior” or “less important.”

Sentence-level grammar
Watch especially for:
Subject-verb agreement:  
  “There has been many objections” → “There have been many objections”
Singular/plural:  
  “That questions” → “That question”  
  “as a women” → “as a woman”
Punctuation:  
  Some sentences are run-ons or joined incorrectly with commas.

Final advice for revision
When revising:
Write a stronger thesis in the introduction.
Separate your ideas into clearer paragraphs.
Use more formal academic language.
Remove vague or exaggerated examples.
Carefully proofread grammar and spelling.

A possible revised thesis
You might use something like this:
“Women should be allowed to attend The Citadel because tradition is not a sufficient reason for exclusion, publicly funded schools should provide equal access, and women are just as capable as men of meeting academic and professional challenges.”

You already have the basis for a persuasive essay. With clearer structure and more careful editing, your argument will become much stronger.

the television program contains missing quotations (),

unpaid labor saves the family money,

“watch the soaps”

“duties,his wife” → “duties, his wife”

Consistency

Final advice for revision

Reorganize the body paragraphs so each paragraph develops one main idea.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows clear engagement with a controversial topic and includes several relevant areas of discussion: history, medical use, physical effects, social consequences, and comparison with alcohol. You also try to address both sides before moving toward a conclusion, which is a good basis for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: thesis and structure, evidence and argument quality, and language accuracy/style.

Thesis and overall purpose

Your introduction says the essay is not trying to persuade the reader, but only to inform them. However, the conclusion clearly argues that legalization is the “only logical answer.” This creates a contradiction.

What to revise:
Decide whether this is an informative essay or an argumentative essay.
Since your conclusion takes a position, make your thesis clear from the start.

Possible revision of thesis:
“Although marijuana has risks, it should be legalized because criminalization causes greater social harm, medical use is already recognized, and regulation would make its use safer.”

That would give your essay a clear direction.

Organization and paragraph structure

At the moment, the essay moves through several topics, but the logic is sometimes loose. Some paragraphs begin with broad statements such as “the next step” or “after the physical effects are realized,” which sounds informal and mechanical.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: background + clear thesis  
History and legal context  
Medical and physical effects  
Comparison with alcohol/tobacco  
Benefits of legalization: crime reduction, regulation, taxation  
Counterarguments and response  
Conclusion

Each body paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence linked to your thesis.

For example:
“One reason to support legalization is that marijuana already has recognized medical uses.”
“Another argument for legalization is that regulation could reduce black-market crime.”

Evidence and use of sources

You include many factual claims, but several need stronger support, clearer citation, or more precision. In academic writing, unsupported claims weaken your credibility.

Problems to address:
Some statements seem too absolute:
  - “NO ONE has ever died from marijuana.”
  - “any doctor will inform you…”
  - “there can only be one logical answer.”
Some facts may be inaccurate or unclear:
  - Glaucoma is not treated “in order to increase appetite.”
  - “Marijuana cigarettes to suffers of glaucoma” needs correction and verification.
  - “Most of what people smoke nowadays is… laced with crack or PCP” is a very strong claim and needs reliable evidence.

Advice:
Use credible, up-to-date sources.
Integrate them properly.
Avoid exaggeration.

Instead of writing:
“any doctor will inform you...”

write:
“Some medical researchers argue that alcohol causes greater long-term harm than marijuana.”

Instead of:
“NO ONE has ever died from marijuana,”

write:
“Fatal overdose from marijuana alone appears to be extremely rare, especially compared with alcohol and tobacco.”

This sounds more academic and more defensible.

Argumentation and balance

Your essay would become stronger if you addressed the opposing side more fairly. At the moment, the risks of marijuana are mentioned, but not fully discussed. A good argumentative essay acknowledges counterarguments and responds to them.

Possible counterarguments to include:
risk of dependency
impaired driving
effects on adolescents
mental health concerns in heavy users

Then explain why, despite these concerns, legalization with regulation is preferable to prohibition.

That would make your essay more persuasive and mature.

Language and grammar

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are many grammar, punctuation, spelling, and word-choice problems. These reduce the academic quality of the essay.

Frequent issues:
a) Capitalization
“marijuana” should usually not be capitalized in the middle of a sentence.
Also check consistency.

b) Confusion of “its” and “it’s”
“it’s purpose” → its purpose

c) Sentence structure
Some sentences are fragments or missing words:
“The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States.”
  - Better: “The first thing to consider when forming a position on marijuana is its history in the United States.”

d) Comma use
You often place commas where they should not be:
“Some short term effects of smoking Marijuana include, the ‘high’...”
“Most agree, however that prolonged usage...”

e) Spelling/typing errors
Examples:
“govermnent” → government
“suffers” → sufferers
“Pgychology” → Psychology

“is office now” → is in office now

f) Formal style

Use a more academic tone:

Feedback Report

You have a clear argumentative purpose: society has applied unfair double standards to women, and feminism has challenged those standards. Your essay also shows strong personal engagement, which gives it energy and conviction. For revision, focus on three main areas: organization, evidence and tone, and language accuracy.

Biggest revision priorities

Your first paragraph introduces the topic, but the central claim is still broad. End the introduction with a more precise thesis.

This gives your essay a clear roadmap.

B. Improve organization

A possible structure:
Introduction: topic + thesis  
Historical background: traditional gender roles and early inequality  
Double standards in family/social life: daughters vs. sons, dating, behavior  
Cultural messages: fairy tales, childhood socialization  
Media representations: beauty standards and sexualization

This will make your reasoning easier to follow.

C. Use a more academic tone
Some sentences sound sarcastic or conversational, such as:
“Things were fine and dandy…”
“women started getting a little ‘uppity’”
“now what kind of idea was that?”

I understand that you are trying to show the attitude of men in the past, but in an academic essay, this tone can weaken credibility. Instead, explain those attitudes directly and formally.

You make many important claims, but often they need more support or explanation. For instance:

article use: “the equality of warm”

fragments and run-ons

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear and passionate position: you argue that banning books harms education and freedom of expression. This is a strong topic for an argumentative essay, and your use of examples, court cases, and references to specific groups shows that you tried to support your opinion with evidence. Your voice is engaged and persuasive. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis and organization, clarity of argument, grammar and word choice, and formal academic style.

Strengths

Clear opinion: Your position against censorship is obvious from the beginning.
Relevant examples: You include real organizations, legal cases, and public controversies.
Strong engagement: The essay sounds committed and emotionally invested, which helps persuasion.
Good conclusion idea: You end by returning to the importance of education and free expression.

Main issues to revise

A. Create a clearer thesis
Your introduction asks several rhetorical questions, but it does not give one strong thesis statement early enough. In an argumentative essay, readers need a direct claim.

For example, you could end the introduction with something like:

“School boards, parents, and political pressure groups should not ban books because censorship limits students’ education, violates freedom of expression, and prevents readers from engaging critically with difficult ideas.”

This would make your argument more focused.

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from one example to another without always explaining how each paragraph supports the main argument. Try this structure:

Introduction – present the issue and thesis  
Body paragraph 1 – censorship in schools  
Body paragraph 2 – legal cases showing the problem  
Body paragraph 3 – censorship in public libraries  
Body paragraph 4 – why exposure to difficult topics cannot be prevented  
Conclusion – restate argument and significance

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence. For example:

“Book censorship in schools limits students’ access to important ideas.”
“Several court cases show that book bans are often based on weak reasoning.”
“Public libraries also face pressure from groups who want to restrict reading materials.”

C. Explain your evidence more fully
You mention statistics and court cases, which is good, but often you do not explain them enough. For example, after giving the numbers from PAW, explain why those numbers matter. After discussing a court case, explain how it proves your point.

A useful pattern is:
example → explanation → connection to thesis

Language and grammar points

Because your first language is German, some sentence patterns seem influenced by German structure. Try to focus on word order, article use, singular/plural, and idiomatic phrasing.

Examples of sentences to revise

“Who is provided with the authority…”  
  Better: “Who is given the authority…”

“As I last recalled the Constitution…”  
  Better: “As I recall, the Constitution…”

“fireedom” → “freedom”

“the go as far as to bum them”  
  Better: “they go so far as to burn them”

“high students”  
  Better: “high school students”

“speaking openingly”  
  Better: “speaking openly”

“provided the court”  
  Better: “provide the court”

“an previous agreement”  
  Better: “a previous agreement”

“constant attempt of groups”  
  Better: “constant attempts by groups”

“stop the world for turning”  
  Better: “stop the world from turning”

“putting a restrain of the voices of authors”  
  Better: “restricting the voices of authors”

Also check:
capitalization: First Amendment, Board of Education
apostrophes: students’ education, authors’ rights
spelling of names/titles: Johannes Gutenberg, burn, curriculum, I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings

Style and tone

Your essay uses emotional language effectively, but some phrases are too informal or too dramatic for academic writing. For example:

“try to stop the world from turning” is vivid, but not very academic.
“they will see it” is understandable, but needs fuller explanation.

Try to balance passion with precise language. Instead of attacking people directly, focus on the consequences of censorship. For example:

Less academic: “They think that banning books will stop violence and sin.”  
More academic: “Supporters of censorship often believe that restricting books will protect children from harmful influences; however, this assumption ignores the fact that students encounter such themes in many other forms of media and daily life.”

Punctuation and sentence control

You have many punctuation problems: double commas, misplaced apostrophes, extra spaces, and sentence fragments. Read the essay carefully for editing. Also avoid overusing rhetorical questions. One or two are fine, but too many weaken the formal argument.

Final advice for revision

When you rewrite:
state your thesis clearly
organize paragraphs by one main idea each
explain every example
use a more formal academic tone
proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation

You have a strong foundation because your argument is clear and your topic is important. With better structure and language control, this essay could become much more convincing and polished.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You choose a clear and emotionally charged topic, and you include many sources and quotations. That shows effort in research. Your main position also becomes visible by the end: you argue that the Confederate flag should not be removed because it represents heritage for some people. For revision, the biggest needs are a clearer thesis, stronger organization, better explanation of evidence, and careful editing of language and mechanics.

Main argument and thesis
Your introduction gives historical background, but your exact claim is not clear until the conclusion. In an argumentative essay, the reader should understand your position early.

Current problem:  
The essay spends several paragraphs reporting opinions, but it does not clearly guide the reader toward your own argument.

Revise by adding a direct thesis at the end of the introduction, for example:
*Although many Americans see the Confederate flag as a racist symbol, it should not be removed from public memory because some view it as heritage and because history should be preserved rather than erased.*

If that is truly your position, you then need to defend it consistently.

Organization
Right now, the paper reads like a series of quotations and examples rather than a structured argument.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: explain why supporters see the flag as heritage  
Body paragraph 2: present the opposing argument that the flag is tied to slavery and racism  
Body paragraph 3: respond to that opposition with your own reasoning  
Body paragraph 4: discuss public opinion carefully and explain what the polls do or do not prove  
Conclusion: restate thesis and explain why your argument matters

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects to your thesis.

Use of evidence
You use many quotations, but often they are dropped into the paragraph without explanation. This weakens your argument.

For each quotation, do three things:
Introduce it
Quote or summarize it
Explain how it supports your point

For example, after the Alexander Stephens quotation, you need analysis. That quotation strongly suggests that the Confederacy was explicitly founded on white supremacy. Since your essay argues in defense of the flag, you must address this evidence directly, not just include it. Otherwise, it seems to undermine your own position.

You should also be careful with the poll paragraph. The claim that the NAACP is not speaking for most African Americans is too broad based on one poll. Also, the numbers you provide are surprising, so they need clearer context and interpretation.

Balance and logic
You include both pro-flag and anti-flag views, which is good. However, your own reasoning is sometimes too general.

For example:
*“There is always going to be controversy…”* is too broad.
*“There is no reason to fight over something that was established so many years ago.”* is not fully convincing, because old symbols can still have present-day effects.

Try to develop stronger reasoning:
Why should historical symbols remain in public places?
Is there a difference between preserving history in museums and displaying symbols on government buildings?
How do you respond to people who say the flag represents slavery and racial oppression?

These questions can help deepen your argument.

Style and tone
Your tone is mostly formal, but sometimes wording is unclear or too conversational.

Examples:
*“Those are some different views of people around the USA.”* → too informal
*“There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world.”* → awkward phrasing

Use a more academic style:
*These responses illustrate the range of public opinion across the United States.*
*Because historical symbols carry different meanings, public controversy over them is likely to continue.*

Grammar, spelling, and mechanics
This essay needs careful proofreading. There are many errors in capitalization, spelling, punctuation, and word form. Some may come from typing or encoding problems, but they still need correction.

Common issues:
Capitalization: *confederate* → *Confederate*; *united Daughter* → *United Daughters*
Spelling: *fory* → *forty*; *siad* → *said*; *todl* → *told*; *unacceptabe* → *unacceptable*
Word choice: *grand father* → *grandfather*
Punctuation: several comma splices and missing periods
Possessives: *ancestors lives* → *ancestors’ lives*
Quotation formatting: punctuation should be consistent, and citations should be complete

Example revision
Original:  
*The flag was, in a sense, a part of our ancestors lives.; They fought under those colors to what they thought were their rights.*

Better:  
*The flag was, in a sense, part of our ancestors’ lives. They fought under those colors for what they believed were their rights.*

Source integration
You cite sources, but the formatting is inconsistent. Make sure each citation follows the style your teacher requires (likely MLA or APA). Also, some quotations are very long. Try paraphrasing more often and quoting only the most important phrases.

Final advice
Your revision should focus on argument, not just information. Right now, you have material for an essay, but you need to shape it into a clear and logical case. Start by writing a precise thesis, reorganize the body around key reasons, explain every source, and proofread carefully.

You have a strong topic and plenty of evidence. The next step is to make your own voice and reasoning much clearer.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a strong sense of urgency. You argue consistently that frivolous lawsuits create costs for society, and you end with concrete proposals. This gives the essay a recognizable argumentative structure. For revision, the main areas to improve are tone, evidence, organization, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Clear position: Your thesis is easy to identify: too many unnecessary lawsuits burden the legal system and lawmakers should respond.
Relevant examples: You mention prisoner lawsuits, fake accidents, workers’ compensation, and school discipline. These examples help show the range of the problem.
Action-oriented conclusion: Your final paragraph offers specific solutions, which is effective in an argumentative essay.

Main issue: tone and academic style

At the moment, the essay sounds more like a spoken complaint or editorial than an academic argument. Expressions such as “Seems like everybody is suing everybody,” “ridiculous cases,” “begging for people to call them,” and “you and I” make the style emotional and informal.

In academic writing, try to sound measured, precise, and fair. You can still criticize the system, but avoid exaggeration and sweeping statements.

Example revision
Original:  
“Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days.”

Better:  
“The number of civil lawsuits appears to have increased, raising concerns about the efficiency and cost of the judicial system.”

Original:  
“These ridiculous claims”

Better:  
“claims that may lack sufficient legal merit”

Need for evidence and balance

Your argument depends heavily on assertion. You make many claims about costs, delays, false lawsuits, inmate behavior, insurance companies, and public schools, but you do not provide statistics, studies, legal examples, or sources. In university writing, this is essential.

For example, if you say:
civil cases take three to five years,
frivolous lawsuits cost millions of dollars,
prisoner lawsuits are frequently trivial,

you should support these points with evidence.

Also, the essay would be stronger if you briefly acknowledged the other side. Not all lawsuits are unnecessary; civil courts also protect injured people, workers, consumers, and prisoners whose rights are genuinely violated. If you recognize this, your essay will seem more credible.

Possible counterargument
“Although civil litigation is essential for protecting legitimate rights, the system should include stronger mechanisms for dismissing clearly unfounded claims.”

Organization and development

Your essay has a general structure, but the body paragraphs could be more focused. Right now, several different topics are mixed together.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction  
   Present the issue and thesis.
Economic costs of excessive litigation  
   Court expenses, administrative staff, insurance costs.
Examples of allegedly frivolous claims  
   Prisoner suits, staged accidents.
Consequences for society  
   Delays in legitimate civil cases, effects on criminal courts.
Proposed reforms  
   Faster dismissal, penalties for bad-faith claims, better fraud investigation.
Conclusion

Use a topic sentence at the beginning of each paragraph so the reader knows its main idea.

Word choice and precision

Some of your wording is too broad or imprecise. For example:
“taxpayers dollars” → “taxpayers’ dollars”
“payoff” should often be “pay off” as a verb
“then” should be “than” in comparisons
“corporation of lawyers” should be “cooperation of lawyers”
“worker’s compensation” is usually “workers’ compensation”

Be careful with legal terms. For instance, calling all such cases frivolous is a legal judgment, so use that term carefully unless you can support it.

Grammar and mechanics

There are many spelling and typing problems, some of which make the text hard to read. Examples include:
1990's → 1990s
govermnent → government
cems → seems
wn t~g → am talking
fiLI1 → fall
wnount → amount
ffivolous → frivolous

Also revise sentence structure. Some sentences are too long and should be divided for clarity.

Example revision
Original:  
“If there were fewer civil cases to be dealt with, it would free up courts to deal with more criminal cases, reducing the backlog of criminal cases, which would bring the criminal cases to trial quicker.”

Better:  
“If courts had fewer civil cases to process, they could devote more resources to criminal trials. This could reduce delays in the criminal justice system.”

Suggestions for revision

State your thesis more formally in the introduction.
Replace emotional language with neutral academic language.
Add evidence: statistics, laws, or scholarly sources.
Distinguish between legitimate lawsuits and clearly abusive or fraudulent claims.
Organize each paragraph around one main point.
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and grammar.

Final thought

You have a strong starting point because your essay has a definite claim and a practical purpose. To make it persuasive at university level, focus on evidence, fairness, and formal style. If you revise in that direction, your argument will become much stronger and more credible.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: partisan conflict harms government and the 1995 shutdown shows why. You also use a real historical event to support your argument, which is a strong choice. The paper shows engagement with politics and a willingness to take a clear stance. For revision, focus on accuracy, organization, and style.

Main strengths
Clear argument: You consistently argue that party conflict damages effective government.
Relevant example: The 1995 government shutdown is a useful case study.
Strong sense of purpose: The reader can tell what you believe and why it matters.

Most important revision priorities

A. Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, the essay begins with Jefferson’s quotation and then moves into the shutdown. However, your exact claim should be stated more directly in your own words.

For example, you could write:
> The 1995 U.S. government shutdown shows that extreme partisanship can prevent elected officials from governing effectively and can harm ordinary citizens.

This is clearer than repeating “I feel” throughout the essay.

B. Improve organization
The essay sometimes repeats ideas or moves suddenly from one point to another. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce Jefferson’s warning about factions.
   - Present your thesis.

Background
   - Explain briefly how the budget process and fiscal year work.
   - Explain why failure to pass funding bills can cause a shutdown.

The 1995 shutdown
   - Describe what happened.
   - Explain the conflict between Congress and President Clinton.

Effects on citizens
   - Workers furloughed
   - Public services interrupted
   - Economic and social consequences

Conclusion
   - Return to your main claim about partisanship and government responsibility.

C. Be careful with factual accuracy
Some historical details need checking. In an argumentative essay, factual accuracy is essential because it affects credibility.

Examples:
The date appears incorrect: the shutdown began in November 1995, not “Tuesday, November 13, 1995” in the way you describe it.
The U.S. government does not pass “thirteen essential bills” in exactly the simple way stated here; this should be explained more accurately as appropriations/funding legislation.
“Ending with the president of the United States” is not accurate; the president is not “released” like other employees.
“This could cause a depression” is too extreme and not supported by evidence.

Before revising, verify dates, names, and political procedures.

Style and language

A. Use a more formal academic tone
Avoid conversational phrases such as:
“So who’s right?”
“Both sides were stubborn like two kids fighting on a playground.”
“I say no.”

Instead, use formal analysis:
> Neither side demonstrated enough willingness to compromise, and the result was harmful to the public.

B. Reduce repetition of “I feel”
Frequent use of “I feel” weakens the authority of your argument. In academic writing, state claims directly.

Instead of:
> I feel the American people elect representatives...

Write:
> The American people elect representatives to prevent this kind of political failure.

C. Use precise wording
Examples:
“partisan” → better: partisanship or political parties
“government began shutdown” → the government began to shut down / the shutdown began
“effect” vs. “affect”:  
  - affect = verb  
  - effect = noun  
  Example: “How does this affect the ordinary person?”

Grammar and sentence-level issues

Because your first language is German, pay special attention to word order, capitalization, punctuation, and article use.

Common issues in your essay:
Sentence fragments:
  > “This statement that was made by Thomas Jefferson...”
  Better:
  > This statement was made by Thomas Jefferson, the third president of the United States.

Capitalization:
  - Congress
  - President
  - United States
  - August
  - not Should

Punctuation:
  > “government officials. decide”
  Remove the period in the middle.

Agreement and plural forms:
  > “more point or views”
  Better:
  > more points of view

Spelling:
  - government
  - fighting
  - situation
  - Strom Thurmond
  - nonessential

Example of a revised passage

Original:
> Both sides were stubborn like two kids figthing on a playground.

Revised:

This version is more formal, precise, and persuasive.

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on these three goals:

Write a direct thesis in your own words.
Reorganize the essay into clear paragraphs with topic sentences.
Check all historical facts and revise for formal academic style.

You have a solid central idea and a meaningful topic. With better structure, more precise language, and stronger factual support, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that abortion should remain legal and that individual choice should be protected. This is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. You also raise several relevant issues, such as rape, poverty, government interference, unsafe illegal abortions, and adoption/foster care. These give you useful material for revision.

Main strengths

Direct style: Your writing is easy to follow at the sentence level when the wording is correct.

Most important areas to improve

Introduction
   - Introduce the issue briefly
   - State your thesis clearly

Body paragraph 1: Bodily autonomy / personal choice
   - Explain why the decision should belong to the pregnant person
   - Give evidence or reasoning

Body paragraph 2: Cases of rape
   - Explain the emotional and psychological impact
   - Support with evidence

Body paragraph 3: Dangers of illegal abortion
   - Explain that banning abortion does not end it
   - Show how unsafe procedures increase harm

Body paragraph 4: Social and economic consequences
   - Discuss poverty, support systems, adoption/foster care
   - Use careful, ethical wording

Conclusion
   - Restate your main claim
   - Summarize your strongest reasons

B. Use stronger evidence
Many claims are stated as facts but are not supported. In an academic argumentative essay, you need reliable evidence:
statistics from trusted sources
court cases
medical or psychological research
government or NGO reports

Examples of unsupported claims:
“One in four women experience rape or attempted rape…”
“Lower income families also tend to have five children on average…”
“The average cost to support a child is one hundred twenty-five thousand dollars…”

You should either provide a source or avoid precise numbers.

C. Be careful with logic and ethics
Some arguments may weaken your essay because they sound insensitive or unfair. For example:

> “By offering abortion to the lower income people you are helping to maintain a balance…”

This sounds as if poor people should have fewer children for social control. That is ethically problematic and may offend readers. A better argument would be:
People living in poverty may face greater barriers to healthcare, childcare, education, and financial stability.
Therefore, access to reproductive healthcare helps people make decisions appropriate to their circumstances.

Similarly, “ignorant and uncaring parents” is too harsh and generalizing. Use neutral academic language.

Language corrections

A. Word choice and spelling
There are several spelling and typing errors:
“Hapeo povertyg” → this seems accidental and should be deleted/rewritten
“life time” → lifetime
“force” → forced
“that they would already be receiving” → than they would already be receiving
“Rowe versus Way” → Roe v. Wade
“if the remove” → if they remove
“eightteen” → eighteen
“sky racket” → skyrocket
“alsop” → also

B. Capitalization and punctuation
“they won this right…” → They
“Would be able to love him or her?” → Would you be able…?
“No many women…” → No, many women…
Avoid random apostrophes: “practice'?” should be practice?

C. Grammar
Examples:
“examples why people should maintain their right”  
  → examples of why people should maintain their right
“would require more time to recover and more counseling that they would already be receiving”  
  → would require more time to recover and more counseling than they would otherwise need
“remain in that socioeconomic level their entire life”  
  → remain at that socioeconomic level for their entire lives

Improve your tone
An academic essay should sound formal and precise. Avoid:
emotional questions used too often
exaggerated wording
sweeping generalizations

For example, instead of:
> “Would be able to love him or her?”

Write:
> “For some survivors of rape, being forced to continue such a pregnancy may intensify trauma and make psychological recovery more difficult.”

This sounds more academic and persuasive.

Possible revised thesis
Your current thesis is understandable, but it can be sharper. For example:

Abortion should remain legal because individuals must have control over their own bodies, banning abortion does not stop it but makes it more dangerous, and access to abortion is especially important in cases of rape and severe economic hardship.

Final advice

Correct grammar, spelling, and sentence errors.
Reorganize the essay into clear paragraphs with one main idea each.
Replace weak or offensive arguments with fairer, more precise reasoning.

You have a clear opinion and several usable ideas. With better structure, evidence, and language accuracy, this essay can become much more convincing.
